MEMOIR OF REV. ROBERT STORY. 


PART I. 


1790—1820. 


CHAPTER I. 
BIRTHPLACE—EARLY LIFE—COLLEGE. 


ApouT seven and a half miles to the south-west of 
Kelso lie the twin villages of Town Yetholm and Kirk 
Yetholm. After leaving the wide rolling country round 
Kelso, with the three wizard-piled Eildons on its 
horizon, you approach the green slopes and hollows 
of the Cheviots. Between two of their lower ridges is 
the valley of the Beaumont. On either hand the hills 
rise round and bare; between is a rich, well-tilled 
flat, with the Beaumont in the middle of it—a thin 
stream, feeling its way over and among bars of brown 
gravel, to the south, where its waters, merged at last in 
those of the Till, join the Tweed. Northward, the 
valley is gradually folded up among the softly-swelling 
hills ; to the south it opens out on the Northumbrian 
fells like the mouth of a trumpet, much as the gorge 
of the Neckar opens on the plain of the Rhine. 

Halfway up the valley a raised causeway advances 
from either side, and the broad, rambling channel of 
the stream is spanned by the arches of a bridge con- 


necting the two villages. On the right stands Kirk 


vy: B 
cr & 


2 YETHOLM. 


Yetholm, with its Gipsy huts, its black church of very 
modern Gothic, and its manse, the home of no common 
worth and culture*: on the left is the larger village of 
Town Yetholm. 

It consists of one wide unpaven street or place, 
with irregular offshoots in the rear of its two lines 
of dwellings. At the lower extremity of the street 
stands the house—now altered and repaired—where 
the subject of this memoir was born. 

The two villages have no manufactures—no great 
interests—no great population. They le there among 
their green hills, and beside their gliding little river, 
like “grey-haired Saturn, quiet asastone.” The great, 
noisy world is at a distance. No shriek of railway- 
whistle is heard among these tranquil meadows and 
braes. The Past here is greater and nearer than the 
Present. Every hill, and moor, and glen has its memory 
and its tradition. From the height above Kirk Yet- 
holm one can mark the site of many a well-known 
haunt of moss-trooper, and once famous border-keep, 
or scene of heady fight in the rude times of old. Away 
beyond the mouth of the valley is the fatal field of 
Flodden; and further still sharp eyes can trace the 
blue outline of the sea at Berwick, and even detect 
the white sails of ships and the smoky trail of steamers 
passing up and down the coast. 

It was amid these scenes that Robert Story entered 
life. He was born on the 8rd of March, 1790, and was 
his parents’ seventh child. 

His father, George Story, was one of the leading 
personages in this sequestered spot, taught the parish 
school, and acted as factor or agent for Mr. Wauchope, 


* Written before the death of the late excellent minister, Mr. 
Baird. 


FAMILY. 3 


of Niddrie. His mother was Margaret Herbert, of 
a Northumbrian family. Robert survived the last 
member of his family by the space of twenty years. 
With the exception of a sister, Robina, they all died 
ere he had attained middle age. He had but very 
few near relatives, and none of them have left behind 
them any record of his early years. ‘These were times 
to which he himself seldom alluded, and of which he 
seemed to retain only fragmentary reminiscences; so 
that we can obtain no distinct picture of his childhood 
and of his home. What we can gather from his own 
occasional allusions, and from the contents of old let- 
ters, seems to indicate a household homely, tranquilly 
happy, and well ordered, and around it a simple and 
primitive society, marked with some of those pic- 
turesque features which the march of civilization has 
now almost everywhere effaced. 

We hear of the village game of football, at which 
Mr. Story, as the most important citizen, administered 
the initiatory kick; of the Gipsies, their camp, their 
king, and their own code of morals and manners; and 
much, too, of the superstitions and goblin lore of the 
worthy peasants of “ Beaumont’s broomy dale.” To 
them evidently the “march dyke” between the natural 
and the supernatural was in a very dilapidated condi- 
tion, and the invisible world not seldom disclosed its 
wonders to their eye of faith. 

One well-remembered incident of the boyish era was 
the false alarm of French invasion, in June, 1804. At 
the long-looked-for signal every volunteer in the county 
was at his rendezvous, save one ill-starred tailor who 
failed to appear, and whose life was thereafter for ever 
embittered. The men in the neighbourhood of Yet- 
holm flocked to Mr. Story’s, to ask his advice as to 

B 2 


4 ; CHILDHOOD. 


the course they should adopt. He counselled them 
to repair to the general rendezvous at Kelso. Among 
the crowd was a woman who had two of her sons 
in the ranks of the volunteers. ‘ Well, Bella,” 
said Mr. S., “what are you going for?” ‘“ Well,” 
answered she, with Spartan spirit, “I'll aye slacken a 
bullet afore my bairns.” 

Field-preaching seems to have been then, as in some 
places it is still, a favourite mode of conducting worship 
and imparting instruction to those who either were 
unable or unwilling to attend the regular services of 
the parish church, or of other places of worship. Robert 
Story, even when quite a child, delighted in attending 
these meetings; probably, although too young to enter 
much into the merits of what he heard, he found interest 
and entertainment enough in the combination of reli- 
gion and of excitement which they afforded. One 
quaint sentence, which he had heard from an old 
burgher field-preacher, he used to quote: it had stuck 
in his memory, like a bur on a coat, when much more 
valuable no doubt slipt past unheeded. 

The old man had touched upon some doctrinal dis- 
cussion which was at the time agitating the society of 
which he was a member, and assured his hearers that 
they “micht as weel think to feed their bodies on 
whun stanes and shinders, as their sauls on thae meta- 
pheesical subtleties.” 

In a small volume, dated 1808, and entitled ‘“‘ Diary,” 
I find a variety of entries, the earlier among which throw 
some light upon this period of the life whose course we 
are about to trace. 

The following was probably written in the diarist’s 
eighteenth year :—‘‘ There was nothing remarkable in 
my youth—nothing which ad the spirit of adventure 


DIARY. 5 


—nothing which Piety could applaud. It was spent in 
that unmeaning innocence whose existence depends on 
the inability to commit evil, not on a love of purity or 
holiness. I remember nothing remarkable until the 
arrival of an awful period; it was a season of deep 
trial, when the feelings of the heart were to receive a 
lasting wound—the death of my oldest brother, whom 
of all I loved most dearly, and in whose life my greatest — 
bliss was found. The Angel of Death summoned him 
home while bathing; and the spirit which was life, 
gentleness, sweetness, majesty, and virtue, from the 
bosom of the lake ascends to its Father. This melan- 
choly event, remarkable in the annals of my life, hap- 
pened on July 27th, 1805, about five o’clock in the 
afternoon. I was then about fifteen years old—young 
enough to suffer unutterable agony—old enough to 
sustain the awful trial without a mortal injury. 

“This perhaps gave my mind a melancholy cast, which 
it yet has not entirely thrown off; but any other effect 
was, to my shame, but momentary. My resolutions 
then indeed, were to be strong in the faith and to serve 
the Lord; but human weakness was too powerful, 
and I yielded to the illusions of the tempter.” 
Despite the youthfulness of the style here, there is 
a tone of greater gravity and reflectiveness than one 
would expect to find in the writings of a lad of eigh- 
teen. The event to which he alludes seemed to 
impress him more deeply than any other occurrence in 
his early years; and it was most natural it should do 
so. His eldest brother was his senior by twelve years, 
and seems to have been one well-fitted to attract sincere 
affection and esteem. Between the two brothers there 
existed a devoted attachment. For the formation and 
continuance of such, the nature of the younger was 


6 HIS ELDEST BROTHER. 


specially endowed with a depth and tenderness of 
affection that to colder beings would appear almost 
extravagant. “Aye,” said one of his humble friends, 
after he was gone, speaking of “the Minister ”—“aye, 
the Love that was in him was extraordinary.” Another, 
and a well-known friend, in a far different walk of life, 
recalling the same aspect of his character, wrote after his 
death, thus—“ The moral nobleness of nature which 
one ever recognized in him, didn’t in the least chill you, 
or make you feel his superiority a restraint, for it was 
so tempered with all that was genial, and open, and 
benignant, that you felt at once attracted to him, and 
more at ease in his presence than in that of many afar 
inferior man. I have been reminded, in thinking of 
him, of that combination of qualities so seldom united 
which Robertson* depicts as so beautifully blended in 
the character of Christ—the grandeur and moral 
strength of manhood, with the purity and gentleness of 
true womanhood.” 

This combination evidently characterized his earliest, 
as it did his latest years. His affections seem from 
the first to have been strong and intense, and the 
object round which they twined most closely was 
this brother. Long after this time, when revisiting 
Yetholm, and writing to his friend Dr. Robertson, 
he says, “Although I have a happy home in Dunbar- 
tonshire, fountains of grief that seemed all dried -up, 
‘flow with most sorrowful remembrance of sore and 
bitter bereavements in a domestic circle, of which I was 
for many a year too much anidol. O! I cannot tell 
the agony of that hour which, after a long period of 
years and many vicissitudes, stands forth in my mind 


* F. W. Robertson, of Brighton. 


EARLY DAYS. 7 


still like no other hour of grief, when I heard a voice 
echo through my father’s garden, ‘ George is drowned !’” 
The nature which could receive and retain so strong 
and vivid an impression, and whose affections were 80 
ardent and tender, could not but possess a powerful 
influence over the affections of others—an influence 
“ working through love.” An intensity—an enthusiasm 
in whatever he undertook—a warmth of love for what- 
ever was loveable, and a reality of disgust at whatever 
seemed to him false and mean, characterized his life 
throughout all its stages. As illustrating this “ per- 
fervidum ingenium,” which pervading all his words 
and works gave him no common power over the 
hearts and minds of others, one friend who knew him 
well, used to say, “ I like to propose a thing to Story— 
for if I say, ‘ Will you take a trip to St.’ Petersburg ?’ 
he'll answer, ‘ Oh yes—and we'll go on to Siberia.’”’ 
In another volume than that already quoted, appear 
some additional entries, regarding his young days at 
Yetholm. The following is under date, “ Ballindal- 
loch, January Ist, 1812.”—‘‘ The period between my 
birth and the time I commenced the study of huma- 
nity, may be considered as the fabulous—the golden 
or the fairy age. . . . I was extremely super- 
stitious and wildly religious, about the commence- 
ment of my learned studies. Every night I dreamed 
of evil or happy spirits, and my days were spent 
in thinking about them. . . In those days I 
heard not a minister but I could imitate him; and 
as I was intended for the church, I remember well 
that I anticipated often the rapture I would inspire by 
a tone similar to that of an old screeching minister in 
the neighbourhood. To the fields I would take my 
Bible, retiring into some lonely place, choose a text, and 


8 EARLY DAYS. 


preach extempore with great energy and pathos. My 
two favourite passages are stillin my mind, ‘ Behold 
He cometh with clouds,’ &c.; and ‘ Blessed are they 
who know the joyful sound.’ Above all days, a windy 
day was my delight, for then I could speak loudest ; 
and after all the notions which I have since gained of 
sublimity and beauty, I cannot yet help thinking that 
there was something very interesting and wildly grand 
in a little boy retiring into a wood during a storm of 
wind—opening the Bible, or selecting a text from me- 
mory, and with the ardour of his young fancy, launch- 
ing out into all the earnestness, pathos, and piety of a 
Christian orator. About the same time, young as I 
was, I was a great politician—a great friend to the 
constitution of my country, and a determined enemy 
to the French. . . I studied Latin without a com- 
petitor. I learned it with my father, and I alone had 
the benefit of his classical instructions. My prosecu- 
tion of it was not very regular, owing to many things 
of inferior moment which, from peculiar circumstances, 
became incumbent upon me to perform. These re- 
quired me often in the open field; but perhaps I inhaled 
as much fancy and taste from the broad book of nature, 
as from the ‘ Metamorphoses’ or the ‘Aineid.’ ” 

The only relic of the lost brother seems to be a 
letter addressed by him to Robert about a year before 
the latter went to college, wherein, after some very 
sound advice regarding his classical studies and bodily 
exercise, he thus proceeds: “ As it appears from obser- 
vation and experience, every one, or at least the greatest 
number are blind to their own faults and improprieties, 
they without ceremony make their remarks upon the 
appearance, peculiarities, and conduct of those who, 
are perhaps far superior to themselves. Not being 


LETTER FROM HI8 BROTHER. 9 


aware of the danger to which they are exposed, they 
are, and with justice, doubly censured in their turn. 
However awkward any one may appear to you, either 
in point of speaking or acting, or in any respect what- 
ever, make no observation. If there is any peculiarity 
which you may perceive, don’t attempt to laugh at it: 
this may lead you on to act the mimic, which is a mean 
and detestable thing, abhorred by every generous mind. 
Be sure never to expose the weakness of your fellow 
mortals—bury a blemish if you observe it, and on the 
other hand, expose to full view their praiseworthy 
qualities. ‘There is another detestable practice which 
I shall mention, and that is, telling wonderful things 
which never happened, in order to impose upon a 
sincere and unsuspecting mind. Upon perceiving 
that what you relate as a simple fact is fairly 
believed, you go and boast of the success you have 
met with; but if you would seriously consider, you 
act unlike the gentleman. You deceive yourself, 
and only prove the object of your merriment to be 
nothing less than a sincere and honourable man. 
He takes you to be a man of your word, and from good 
example shown, and the purest precepts given to you 
by the best of parents, besides a polite education, 
would never be led to believe that you would be guilty 
of such a diabolical practice.” 

It is to be hoped that this warning had due effect, 
and that the imaginative younger brother took care to 
avoid the “diabolical practice” of drawing the long 
bow. In after life, his mimetic powers, although of 
the finest order, were never called into play. He 
never yielded to the fascinating temptation of indulg- 
ing in caricature; and it was only from an occasional 
and unconscious flash of the hidden power, that one 


10 GOES TO COLLEGE. 


could judge how strict was the control exercised over 
it, and what a wealth of mimicry and satire that con- 
scientious control restrained. 

In November, .1805, about three months after 
his brother’s death, he left home to enter the 
University of Edinburgh—then as now the most 
distinguished in Scotland, and one of the most 
renowned seats of learning in Europe. The most 
illustrious of its professors was at that time Dugald 
Stewart ; and among its students were not a few who 
have since risen to the very loftiest eminence. Many 
have passed away; some yet remain. Among those 
who are still living, and who then were Robert 
Story’s fellow-students, is Earl Russell. Long after- 
wards, when his lordship came to spend a month or 
two at Rosneath Castle, his old class-fellow recalled 
to his mind a forgotten incident of those college days, 
the presentation of an address on behalf of the students 
of moral philosophy to Dr. Thomas Brown, while he 
was acting as assistant to Professor Stewart, and which 
Lord John Russell, as the student of highest rank, was 
deputed to read. 

Mr. Story had arranged with his friend, Mr. 
Pringle, who then held the farm of Easterstead, in 
Roxburghshire, that Robert and Mr. Pringle’s son 
Thomas, should proceed to Edinburgh together, and 
occupy the same lodgings. Young Pringle was about 
a year older than he—a lad whose earnest, enthusiastic 
nature was to him a congenial one. They had never 
previously met. Of the first night they spent together 
when on their way to Edinburgh, and of the impres- 
sion the elder produced upon the younger of the two, 
we find the following account from the pen of the 
latter in Mr. Leitch Ritchie’s Memoir of Thomas 


THOMAS PRINGLE. 11 


Pringle: ‘“ Among the remembrances of the first even- 
ing we spent together, it may deserve notice that on = 
comparing our attainments in literature, he mentioned’ 
with peculiar delight Park’s ‘ Travels,’ and Camp- 
bell’s ‘ Pleasures of Hope,’ quoting that fine passage 
in the latter which ends with the line, ‘ And Freedom 
shrieked when Kosciusko fell.’ It must have seemed 
very unlikely at that time that a young man suffering 
from incurable lameness should become a traveller ; 
but the congenial enthusiasm which the adventures of 
the African traveller awakened in his mind, peculiarly 
fitted him for assisting in laying the foundations of a 
new colony in the wilds of Southern Africa; while in 
his admiration of Campbell’s verse may be traced 
the germinating love of freedom and abhorrence of 
oppression, which became the ruling passion and 
determining motive of his future life.” Young Story 
was happy in entering on college and on city life 
with such a friend and companion as this. The 
warm and.intimate friendship then formed between 
them grew only more true and steadfast as life rolled 
on; and wide apart as were the spheres of the 
African colonist and the parish minister of Ros- 
neath, it never knew an interruption. 

We have no very distinct record of the events 
of the first winter in Edinburgh. Pringle and 
he lived together. “The two lads,” says Mr. 
Ritchie, in the memoir already referred to, “lodged 
in the same room, where, for a long time, amidst 
the novelties of a capital, they still continued to 
‘remember their Creator in the days of their youth.’ 
They performed religious service regularly as they had 
been accustomed to see it done at home, taking the 
duty alternately. The Sabbath they kept holy, as they 


12 FIRST YEAR AT COLLEGE. 


had been taught to do; avoiding so much as opening a 
book on that day which was not of a directly religious 
character.” Notwithstanding this strict and pious 
conduct, we find Story writing thus, with reference to 
his moral state during the first session at college: 
“Whether virtue had the ascendency I am afraid to 
decide. This is one thing, that although piety lost 
none of its beauty or apparent worth, vice appeared 
arrayed in garments less disgusting. It is needless to 
detail the circumstances which produced the effect. 
It is an awful truth, which I shall never think of 
without horror.” Only the most delicate sensitiveness 
of conscience could prompt him thus to express 
himself. Of this sensitiveness, this extract gives 
another instance: “I have not been unhappy to-day, 
nor have I committed any great iniquity. I have, 
however, been guilty, and, in particular, have been 
betrayed into some unguarded expressions about the 
character and dignity of the legislator Moses. Oh! 
that I might have the security of an angel,-for I often 
err in thought and conversation.” One cannot help 
wondering what this offence against Moses could have 
been. 

In one of the Diaries already referred to, he 
says, ““I went to college with little knowledge and 
considerable vanity. I had read but little, and 
what I had read consisted only of religious books 
or old novels. ‘Tis true I had read Milton and 
Thomson, but I did not understand them. The 
‘Pilgrim’s Progress, ‘ Blind MHarry’s Wallace,’ 
‘Nature Delineated, ‘Hervey’s Meditations,’ and 
such like books, I had indiscriminately devoured, but 
had received little advantage from any. 

My first session was without much honour, indeed 


DAVID LANDSBOROUGH. 13 


with none, for in the linguist classes I never distin- 
guished myself. My dreams of grandeur were far too 
sublime for the acquisition of syntax rules, or the 
ability of answering with ease a preceptor’s queries. . . 
I then formed my friendship with Cannan’’—(He else- 
where mentions this friend with great enthusiasm, 
‘“‘as dearer to him than ten thousand worlds’)—“ and, 
became acquainted with my brother’s most intimate 
friends. Mr. Landsborough found me out, whom I 
love, and who, in my opinion, is a perfect model of the 
gentle, virtuous man.” 

The person here spoken of was afterwards the Rev. 
David Landsborough, minister of Stevenston, in Ayr- 
shire, and latterly minister of a “‘ Free” Kirk at Salt- 
coats. His name has been rendered familiar to many by 
his well known and much esteemed writings on Natural 
History, and especially by his ‘‘ History of British Zoo- 
phytes.” In one of his graceful and interesting works, 
“Arran: its Natural History,’ Mr. Landsborough, 
writing of a period forty years after that at which he 
met his friend, describes an excursion to his favourite 
island in the yacht of Mr. James Smith, of Jordanhill; 
on which occasion he was, he says, accompanied by “the 
Rev. Robert Story, the kind-hearted parish minister of 
Rosneath, almost the oldest of my friends.” It is 
worthy of record, in a time when friendship is often 
sacrificed to the violence of party feeling and sectarian 
dispute, that the intimacy thus early formed stood the 
test of all changes of time and circumstance, and that 
the correspondence begun in the first year of college 
life continued uninterrupted till the hand of death 
suddenly arrested it. Another companion who is 
mentioned in the Diaries in terms of highest praise 
and esteem, was Thomas Wright, afterwards the Rev. 


14 PROGRESS AT COLLEGE. 


Thomas Wright,* minister of Borthwick. “To him,” 
he says, “I am indebted for my taste; at least for its 
more rapid development. I cannot detail what obliga- 
tions I am under to him for the advicés he gave me 
about innumerable topics and opinions.” During this _ 
winter he seems to have assisted in originating a small 
literary and debating society, wherein, he says, “ we 
spouted, and to whose members I recited an address 
to Satan, of my own composition, in poetry;” not an 
invocation, let us hope, in the style of “ Descend from 
Heaven, Urania ;” but, whatever its tendency, rather a 
peculiar subject for the youthful muse. This, he says, 
was his second attempt in poetry; his first was “‘ An 
Elegy on the Death of an Old Woman, the fifth wife 
of a very Old Man about ninety ; with his lamentation 
and desire to supply her place by another selection.” 

We may infer from the following extract from a 
letter of Landsborough’s, that during his second ses- 
sion, his progress had been highly distinguished. 
Mr. L. says, “I hope you are preparing to add fresh 
wreaths to those laurels which you so deservedly 
obtained last winter. Few during their whole course 
at college make themselves so conspicuous as you have 
already done.” He himself says of this session that it 
was “‘a season of honour,’ won principally in the 
logic class, then presided over by Dr. Finlayson. 
That, however, which especially marked him as a 
rising genius, was the delivery, before one of the deba- 
ting societies, of an essay on “The Causes of the 
Athenian Grandeur.” The society. was the Philale- 
thic, afterwards absorbed in the still existing Dia- 
gnostic. The essay produced a vivid impression on 


* Deposed in 1841 on a charge of heresy. 


POETIC EFFORTS. 15 


those who heard it. One of the circle present on 
the night on which it was read, writing in 1835, 
amid the stress of care and business that pressed 
upon him, in a prominent and useful sphere, says, 
** Often and often have I mused on bygone days—on 
those halcyon hours we used to spend together at the 
Philalethic Society and elsewhere. I verily believe I 
am more indebted to you than to any man living for 
having given my principles a right direction in morals, 
politics, and literature, at a time when the youthful 
mind was ductile and easily biassed. Even at this 
distance of time I remember with delight your essay 
on ‘The Causes of the Athenian Grandeur,’ and the 
many instructive conversations arising out of that 
and similar topics.” 

After his third session he remained in Edinburgh 
to superintend the education of the only son of the 
Honourable David Hume, one of the Barons of H.M.’s 
Exchequer, and nephew of the philosopher. He con- 
tinued in Baron Hume’s family for three years, till 
July, 1811, carrying on at the same time his own 
studies, and in winter attending the University. 
During this period he became more and more addicted 
' to the sin of rhyming, and his muse took higher, 
though perhaps not bolder, flights than the “ Address 
to Satan” and “ The Widower'’s Lament.” He became 
acquainted in the summer of 1810 with Dr. Thomas 
Brown; and at the request of one of Dr. Brown’s 
sisters, undertook to write an “ Ode to Music.” 
“From that moment to this,” says he, writing two or 
three years afterwards, “I have become so enamoured 
of poetry, that in my poetical attainments must I 
look for the honour and much of the happiness of my 
life.” Many a one has im like manner, during the 


16 THE “ INSTITUTE.” 


first freshness of youthful fancy, dreamed of poetic 
fame, and, looking back long afterwards, has pro- 
bably been surprised to note how gently the dream, 
which seemed erewhile so intensely real, has faded 
away into oblivion. One tangible result his aspira- 
tions had, however, and that was the publication, 
early in 1811, of the “ Institute,” a heroic poem in 
four cantos. Pringle was understood to be his partner 
in the authorship of this satire—for such the produc- 
tion was, and levelled apparently at the members of a 
society called the “‘ Philomathic.” No doubt it pos- 
sessed point and interest enough to amuse contempo- 
raries, but its date is fled now. Here is a specimen of 
the style of it:— 


“ A snug apartment thirteen feet by eight, 
O’ercanopied with mossy-mantled slate. 
Here crazy seats around the walls extend, 
And tatter’d mats the well-worn floor defend. 
Its cushion’d rostrum too had once been green— 
And dirt and dulness sanctify the scene ; 
Here bugs from time unknown a fortress held 
And many a tribe of orators expell’d ; 
Here cobweb tap’stry o’er the dusky wall 
Waves black and dismal like a sable pall ; 
And seldom lighted lamps with sickly glow 
O’er the dim gloom a dreadful glimmering throw. 
Within the covert of this foul retreat 
A secret junto oft were wont to meet, 
By peril long and tribulation proved, 
And Philomathic was the name they loved.” 


The “Institute” was not the only satirical effort, 
though probably the only one that was introduced to 
the public. He began and carried on for some time, 
but never finished, another, entitled “ Edinburgh, or. 
the Holy City;” and if Edinburgh in the teens of this 
century exhibited, as much as it exhibits now, of godly 
profession with ungodly practice, he had a broad target 
for his shafts. 


DR. BARCLAY. 17 


I have two volumes, containing about five hundred 
pages, filled with metrical compositions, all fairly 
copied out in his own hand. The labour implied in 
accomplishing this shows how much pleasure he must 
have found in versifying, and how much value he 
must have attached to these poetic offspring. As far — 
as one can judge, there seems to be in them a good 
deal of delicate fancy, correct taste, and elegant 
thought. There is not, however, the high poetic genius 
which alone would justify the reproduction of any of 
them in these pages. Poetry is like wine—a luxury : 
if we are to have it at all, let us have it of the best. 
There is also not a little of that mannerism—that 
buskined tread which was agreeable to the statelier 
taste of a generation taught to reverence Pope as the 
Prince of the “ Ars Poetica,” but which to us has lost 
its charm, and seems stilted and unnatural. 

The publication of the “Institute’”’ brought him 
acquainted with Dr. John Barclay, then a well-known 
extra-academical lecturer on one of the branches of 
medical science. The Doctor, who is described as ‘a 
fellow of infinite jest,” fanned the flame of poetic fer- 
vour in the student’s breast, urging him, he says, “to 
undertake a great poetical work, which should perform 
for Theism what Lucretius has. done for Atheism.” 
* Altius ibunt qui ad summa nituntur,” says the old 
proverb; so perhaps we should not find fault with Dr. 
Barclay’s suggestion; though it certainly does not seem 
a very wise counsel, to urge a young student in his 
twenty-first year to write a “‘counterblast” to the 
greatest didactic poem the world has seen—not to 
speak of a refutation of the profound old Epicurean’s 
materialistic philosophy. Bold as was the project, 
however, it was with characteristic enthusiasm adopted, 

0 


18 BALLINDALLOCH. 


and for some time pursued; and there are among his 
books and papers several pieces entitled “ Fragments 
for the Great Work.”” Another subject, suggested to 
_ him by another friend, was one probably more adapted 
to his genius— “The Enthusiast;” and upon this, 
too, he seems to have entered with energy and spirit. 
With his intensity, however, he combined a certain 
vacillation, which often sapped the foundation of a 
plan ere he had time to build. Perhaps it were truer 
to say, that each successive vision burst upon him in 
colours so bright as to eclipse its predecessor, and was 
destined to be in its turn eclipsed. So “The Enthu- 
siast” and “ Anti-Lucretius” floated in his fancy gor- 
geously, parts of them now and then taking form and 
fashion, but never developing themselves into complete 
and articulate poems. 

In July, 1811, he left Edinburgh for Ballindalloch, 
in Morayshire, the seat of Mr. M‘Pherson Grant, in 
whose family he had accepted the situation of tutor. 
At this time the appointment of his friend Lands- 
borough to the church of Stevenston opened to him 
the opportunity of becoming tutor in the family of 
Lord Glenlee. Understanding, however, that Cannan 
wished to obtain the same situation, he took no steps 
to secure it. The result was, that neither got it; 
“but,” says he in his diary, “I had the satisfaction 
of making a sacrifice to a friend who is dearer to me 
than my own soul.” 

With his former pupil, Joseph Hume, he kept up a 
regular correspondence. Hume’s letters are lively and. 
frank, and are evidently written to the friend, not to 
the tutor. He was a lad of fine talents and high pro- 
mise. Some of his letters give interesting glimpses 
into the Edinburgh of that day, as, for instance, this 


CORRESPONDENTS. 19 


one, the first written to Ballindalloch. He had been 
at the theatre, and “ruffed a little too much, as I 
was told when I came home. I, however, erred on the 
right side, because I had’ been before told that Kemble 
complained of the want of applause. .'. Jeffrey called 
me a ‘petulant boy’ to a friend of mine, who told 
me of it. Wait a few years, and he shall find what 
sort of a ‘ petulant boy’ he has to deal with. I hope 
to match him soon.” Alas! hope for him was never 
to be fulfilled: “‘ Abstulit atra dies et funere mersit 


acerbo.” 
The three chief friends with whom at this time he was 


most intimate, and kept up a constant correspondence, 
were Pringle, Cannan, and Donaldson. Cannan was, 
like himself, a student of theology; Donaldson was a 
limb of the law in Edinburgh. 

Their communications were not of that rapid, 
sketchy, curt description, to which penny-postage, 
daily newspapers, electric telegraphs, and other vehi- 
cles for the universal and perpetual diffusion and 
interchange of ideas, have reduced letter-writing too 
generally in our days. They wrote to each other on 
large folio sheets, and wrote closely too. Nor was 
the matter of their correspondence any passing baga- 
telle that might suggest itself. They discussed set 
subjects with the candid object before them of mutual 
edification. These long, dim, withered sheets no doubt 
contain many a fair fancy and acute thought; they 
seem, however, to bear on subjects—metaphysical and 
other—which the current of general interest and 
speculation has ere this swept past: yet here and 
there in the midst of arid pages there gleams a less 
scholastic and more genial light, and we become aware 
that these young sages were not so sternly philoso- 

0 2 


20 BALLINDALLOCH. | 


phical as to forget the Beautiful in their thoughts and 
theories about the True. 

‘‘T sometimes sigh,” he writes from Ballindalloch, 
“over the days that are past, and the friends of my 
youth that I have left at a distance. I bless, however, 
my God, that my lines are yet cast into a pleasant 
place. 

“Mr. and Mrs. Grant are fine people: Mrs. Grant is 
a delightful woman. JI am completely my own master. 
There is no restraint but what I appoint to myself, and 
there is no one to interfere with any plan of improve- 
ment or happiness which I may form. My boy is a 
delightful charge.* I have a commodious and hand- 
some apartment, which adjoins Mr. Grant’s library: 
an arm-chair which is broad and capacious, and soft 
as a bed of down. It moves upon wheels like the 
chariot of the sun. O then figure my delights! 
No, I will not give you a disgust with your troubled 
world: I will not make you die of the maladie du 
pays, or rather the maladie de elbow chair. One of 
my windows looks to the policies of the front, and 
from my bed there I view the comet in the morning. 
The other looks into a beautiful garden, and I might 
pluck the wall-fruit which is spread round it: beyond 
this garden is a lawn, which is bounded by the 
oblique winding of the river.” 

In this retreat he settled down, and began with 
his usual impulsiveness divers poetic works, which 
were to be the steps in his ladder of fame. 

The “ Enthusiast,” the “Age of Women” (“ Saturnia 
regna,” this, surely), the “ Celestial Vestals,” &c., seem 
to have been more or less in train. But self-criticism 


* The late Sir John M‘Pherson Grant, Bart. 


THE AGONISTIC PERIOD. 21 


not seldom dogged too closely the heels of poesy: thus 
of the ‘“ Celestial Vestals” he writes to Donaldson—“ I 
wrote a poem consisting of thirty-six Spenserian 
stanzas. Its title is the “ Celestial Vestals,” and is the 
fulfilment of a promise which I made to two young 
ladies. I thought at first it was something like 
Spenser; now I see it is a long drawling yawning 
haver.” This decisive change of opinion regarding his 
poem was but a type of many a vicissitude of feeling 
on matters more important than verses. Over the 
heights and depths of his moral and spiritual being, 
dark shadow often chased bright sunshine. ‘“ The 
restless, unsatisfied longing” seems to have been often 
his—the craving after the Ideal—the self-distrust and 
self-abhorrence of one seeing the distant goal, and yet 
feeling that he came “far short.” Hence we trace in 
his correspondence at one time the merriest, and at 
another the saddest spirit, evoked by some change 
within or without—in the actual world around him, or 
in that “ world not realized,” wherein he often wan- 
dered. Pringle’s temperament seems to have been 
somewhat similar. Pringle’s letters to him, and his to 
Pringle, reflect more clearly than any other branch of 
his correspondence, this sensibility of feeling and quick 
susceptibility to joy and sorrow. The following ex- 
tract may serve to illustrate this. Pringle had not 
written for some little time ; and in nervous dread lest 
some evil should have befallen him, his friend thus 
writes— 

“© heavens, if you are wandering on the confines of 
the dark valley—if you are indulging other hopes than 
those of human life, and anticipating other enjoyments 
than those of mortal friendship, write, write, tell me 
of your intimate condition that I may sympathize ; 


Q2 THE AGONISTIC PERIOD. 


that I may mourn, or hope, or rejoice. Oh Pringle, — 
Pringle—there are just three whose names to me are 
enrolled in white—who are virtuous and noble—whose 
sentiments are those of moral beings, and whose hopes 
look to distant and indefinite perfection. Thou art 
one; and if our connection is now to separate—if my 
eyes are now to behold scenes of desolation, where 
there was beauty, and a cemetery of death where there 
was life and hope—where shall my heart rest, or my 
sorrow find repose ? Cannan and Donaldson remain ; 
but is Pringle gone ? 

**In these three, O heavenly father, was the happiness 
of my life: how many hopes—delightful hopes I had 
formed of protracted happiness—of enjoyment to which I 
willingly, though perhaps foolishly, prescribed no limits! 
How often the three brethren mingled in the dreams 
of felicity, and how often they sanctified the scenes of 
imaginary glory! O ruinous prospects—O disap- 
pointed hopes—O shadowy glory ! I will soon follow, and 
you will recognize your Story in the fields of Elysium 
before you have taken possession of your grotto. We 
will live together till our brethren come. We will pre- 
pare for them a banquet, and invite them with tender- 
ness and love.” 

His diaries during the time of his residence in the 
North tell the frequent tale of darkness and distress ; 
partly, no doubt, the result of solitude and separation 
from his former friends; partly, in all likelihood, the 
evidence of that agonistic period which most lives ex- 
perience, as they pass from the negative happiness and 
tranquillity of youth to the knowledge, and wisdom, 
and firmness of manhood—a period wherein many 
problems arise that must be solved, many foes have to 
be vanquished—a season, as Carlyle says, of “ Fer- 


ABERDEEN. 23 


mentation, wherefrom the fiercer it is, the clearer pro- 
duct will one day evolve itself.” Those perpetual 
schemes, too—half accomplished, visions half-realized 
—must have helped to weigh down heart and fancy. 
Fragmentary poems and unfinished designs are com- 
fortless spectacles evento the most sedate and self- 
reliant: they are spectres to the young and ardent and 
impressible. Here is an entry dated April 4th, 1812, 
after his return to Ballindalloch from Aberdeen, 
whither he had gone to read a discourse in the Di- 
vinity Hall, as prescribed by the Church to those giving 
a partial attendance during one of their Theological 
Sessions :—‘ I have now arrived from Aberdeen, after 
an absence of three weeks, and with still darker pros- 
pects than ever I had in my life before. Oh, what 
most miserable and dreadful fatality is this which hangs 
overme? Happy and innocent I appeared to all with 
whom I associated in Aberdeen, and from the atten- 
tion and behaviour of many of the students, indeed of 
all the students who were respectable in abilities or 
conduct, I perceived that they thought well of me— 
but I perceived it with secret anguish and indescri- 
bable sorrow. O that I were cast into some desert, 
some wretched desert far from the sight of every 
human being, that, amid storms and tempests, I might 
lose the remembrance of the past, or acquire better 
hopes of the future. O, yes, I am indeed the chief of 
sinners, nor has there been one with a more perverted 
soul than poor wretched I.” 

Almost all the entries in his diary during his resi- 
dence at Ballindalloch, in 1812, are in terms of similar 
dejection ; sometimes the distress is less vehement, at 
other times it is more so, but it is always in some 
shape expressed. At length he resolved ‘to quit the 


24. LEAVES BALLINDALLOCH. 


situation he held in Mr. Grant’s family. He seems to 
have been led to this by a variety of considerations ; 
a wish to be near his father, whose health was failing, 
the idea of entering on a literary life, and others; but 
probably his choice was in no small degree determined 
by his restless unhappiness, which, in the retirement 
and even tenor of his life in Morayshire, became more 
and more unbearable. He communicated his views, 
before his final decision, to Dr. Thomas Brown, who 
wrote in reply, strongly dissuading him from the pro- 
posed step. But he was not to be dissuaded: in the 
autumn, he left Ballindalloch ; and, after a tour through 
the Highlands, went home to Yetholm. 


CHAPTER ILI. 


FISHWICK MAINS—COALSTOUN—ENTERS THE 
CHURCH. 


THe mental doubts and struggles which harassed 
him at Ballindalloch, and finally hastened his depar- 
ture thence, still continued to trouble him when he had 
settled down to a winter’s study and literary work at 
Yetholm. His resolution was to remain quietly in the 
country at his father’s house, and to employ his time 
in reading, and in the accomplishment of some of the 
many designs, by means of which he hoped to gain both 
emolument and fame. He seems, however, to have 
been too restless and ill at ease to be able for much 
regular intellectual exertion. The entries in his diary 
at this time are still “sicklied o’er” with the air of 
dejection and unrest. In some lines, written appa- 
rently after his return to Yetholm, he pictures a youth, 
after the lapse of years, coming back to his early home 
and mournfully looking from the green hill-top, at his 
father’s dwelling in the valley, as he thinks of “the 
universe of change” that intervenes between the care- 
laden Present and the Past. His own image, probably, 
was the original of the picture. 

Landsborough writes to him from Stevenston, in 
answer to a letter which must have been penned in a 
very sombre mood :—“ I was enraged because I could 
not read your letter. I was nearly red wud mad at 


26 FISH WICK MAINS. 


discovering from what I made a shift to interpret,* 
that you had allowed a legion of blue Hielan’ deils to 
possess you. I have not patience to hear a person who 
is so eminently fitted to be the joy and delight and 
ornament of society, speaking of retiring from the 
world to spend his life like a hermit in his cave, or a 
monk in his cell. Resist the devil (I speak with reve- 
rence, my dear fellow), and he will fly from you. In- 
stead of shunning, court society, even though you 
should be doing violence to yourself. Be much with 
your dear and worthy parents ; visit your friends, and 
invite them to visit you; and, after a few weeks, go to 
Edinburgh, where you can find so much society to your 
mind.” 
This was very sensible advice. It was not, how- 
ever, acted upon; and in April, 18138, having remained 
all winter at home, Mr. Story accepted the situation of 
tutor in the family of Mr. Burton Grieve, residing at 
Fishwick Mains, on the Tweed, opposite Norham. 
This was the prosaic and practical termination of a 


* In another letter Landsborough says, “ Your fine figures are quite 
lost by your cramped hand. Instead of ‘striding in fairy boots,’ I 
read ‘riding in a ferry boat,’—what a falling off.” AJl Mr. Story’s 
correspondents had their faith and patience sorely exercised by his 
extraordinarily bad handwriting, and made many bitter complaints, 
which he was inclined to treat with considerable disdain, asserting that 
to any person of ordinary penetration his writing was perfectly legible. 
It consisted of a system of symbols, among which the letters of the 
alphabet had by no means their proper place; when once this system 
was mastered, and all the signs and contractions were understood, inter- 
pretation followed easily enough; and one worthy parishioner used to 
ray apologetically—“ The minister writes a verra undistink haun, but 
yell never catch him wi’ an ill-spelt word.” The illegibility of the 
letter culminated in the signature, which, as written, looked exactly 
like Robaty, or as Principal Scott suggested, like Rabshakeh. Professor 
Pillans—a correspondent in later years—used to grumble much at 
the “ illegibility so famed in Story,” and maintained that many of 
his letters looked as if an insane spider had dipt its feet in the ink- 
bottle, and then danced up and down the page. 


PROJECTS. a7 


number of projects which seem to have floated before 
him during the dull winter months at Yetholm, among 
which we can trace divers literary schemes, the chief 
one being the introduction to the world of a poem en- 
titled the “ Amazon;” educational enterprises also— 
one being a Professorship, for which he made unsuc- 
cessful application—and another, the establishment of 
an Academy in Alnwick ; and, finally, the leading idea 
which, at this period, he entertained, viz., that of taking 
Orders in the English Church. His studies had 
hitherto been pursued according to the curriculum pre- 
scribed to candidates for Orders in the Church of Scot- 
land. He was, however, by no means an enthusiastic 
Presbyterian, and it needed very little persuasion from 
his friend James Morton*—then tutor in Earl Grey’s 
family, and now Vicar of Holbeach, in Lincolnshire, 
and Prebendary of Lincoln—to induce him seriously 
to contemplate transferring his allegiance from the 
Scottish to the English Establishment. Morton offered 
to secure a title for him from a clergyman ni the North 
of England, and to forward his wishes by every means 
in his power. He was strongly inclined to avail him- 
self of this offer, and to apply for ordination to the 
Bishop of Durham; but was deterred by the invincible 
repugnance of his parents, and especially of his mother, 
to his alienating himself from Scotland, and entering 
an Episcopal Church. 

It was with no common pain he sacrificed his own 
desires and hopes to his sense of filial duty, and saw 
the prospect of a useful and happy career, within the 
pale of a Church whose traditions and whose service 
had peculiar attractions for a mind like his, fade away, 


* Editor of “Leyden’s Life and Remains.” 


28 FISH WICK MAINS. 


as many another vision had already faded. And so 
this “high aim abandoned,” he betakes himself to 
Fishwick Mains, and to the training of “the young 
idea.” It was no doubt a wholesome change for him; 
it brought him out of Cloud Land into the workday 
world—gave him a plain daily duty and said, “ Do 
it.” His vague and enthusiastic fancies had not, in 
the leisure and stillness of Yetholm, shaped themselves 
into anything stable or coherent; nor had the restless 
unhappiness which oppressed him at Ballindalloch 
been soothed by the familiar tranquillity of home. 
Solitude and self-communion had no nepenthe for 
him: he was surely ready then for new labour, and 
disciplined by the past, prepared to find in that labour 
the secret of happier days. It is always good for a 
man in his young days to be brought down from the 
sunny realm of Imagination to the stony district of 
Fact, and to be told to work out his Ideal there as 
best he may, and often from rough material, instead 
of vainly trying to reach it in some visionary Utopia. 
So, instead of impossible professorships, and curacies, 
and rectorships of academies, and wreaths of laurel 
sprinkled with the dews of Hippocrene, appear two 
boys at Fishwick Mains—hopeful and tractable boys, 
it seems—asking for guidance and instruction. They 
are not Ideals in any way; not even the name of their 
residence is at all romantic; but with them lie the 
present work and duty,—a better work and a clearer 
duty than poetizing about ‘“ Amazons,” or any other 
females, ancient or modern. 

This rescue from the still atmosphere of Yetholm, 
and recommencement of active life among strangers, 
and in a new scene, seems to have been a turning- 
point in his history. His intellect is clearer now, his 


“ MISS P.” 29 


judgment sounder, and his spirits rise. He writes 
to Pringle after he has been a month or two in his 
new sphere. ‘“ My present residence is a monument 
of ancient hopes: consider it as a token of the com- 
plete abandonment of those vain and unsatisfactory 
schemes which I impiously framed, and which I ele- 
vated against the Highest. What is a name or im- 
mortality? Before I desire that of genius, I have 
resolved to secure that of virtue. When I am good, 
then shall I be great... . I have two boys, one of 
sixteen, the other of fourteen: of them I shall say 
nothing but that all is well, and exactly to my mind.” 
There seems to have been something else than his 
relation to his two pupils “ exactly to his mind ” at his 
present abode. Among the visitors at Fishwick Mains 
there was a “ beautiful and ingenious Miss P.,” with 
whom he was wont to hold high discourse on all manner 
of literary and sentimental topics. A most beneficial, 
but withal somewhat dangerous companion she must 
have been to a handsome romantic young genius, full 
of poetry and enthusiasm. The diary, which seems 
very honestly to reflect his daily thoughts and feel- 
ings, begins now to present a much more cheerful 
record than formerly; and even when Miss P. retires 
from the scene, she seems to leave behind her a good 
influence, so that the tone of the journal is by no 
means in the “De Profundis” key, which our previous 
acquaintance with the writer’s quickly varying moods. 
would have led us to expect. In October, he chronicles 
“ the lovely visitor has gone for a season with her rela- 
tions to B—. We had commenced Italian together, 
and Iam sorry that it should have met so speedily with 
such an unavoidable interruption.” Nodoubt. “ In- 
deed this new study has interrupted my other avoca- 


30 COALSTOUN. 


tions, and during the whole week my study has 
been that of poetry.” The study of Italian and of 
poetry clearly indicates a position of affairs, in which 
it was judicious that Miss P. should pay a visit else- 
where. | 

He did not remain long at Fishwick Mains. Lady 
Dalhousie had heard from Baron Hume of the excel- 
lent abilities and distinguished qualifications of Mr. 
Story ; and her Ladyship was accordingly very desirous 
of obtaining his services as tutor to her two eldest 
sons, Lord Ramsay and the Hon. Charles Ramsay.* 
With great regret he made up his mind to leave 
Mr. Grieve’s, but the situation now placed at his 
disposal was one so very desirable, that both he and 
the friends whom he was leaving saw it to be his 
duty to accept of it, which he forthwith did—quitting 
Fishwick Mains for Coalstoun, the seat of the Earl 
of Dalhousie, in November. 

His residence with this noble family seems to have 
been one of the happiest periods of his life. His 
pupils were most interesting boys,—affectionate, with 
good dispositions, and excellent talents. His rela- 
tions with the Earl and Countess were of the most 
satisfactory and friendly nature. 

The following expression of Lord Dalhousie’s feel- 
ings with regard to him cannot but have been most 
gratifying to his own :— 

“ Corziprrest, 4th February, 1814. 


“Itake this opportunity of assuring you that I go 
abroad at this time with feelings of the greatest con- 
fidence and comfort, in knowing my boys so well placed 


* Lord Dalhousie being about this time frequently absent on military 
duty, such arrangements as the above devolved upon his wife. 


LORD DALHOUSIE. 31 


both as to their education, and care of them as to 
morals, health, and manners. A weight of anxiety is 
taken off my mind and off Lady D.’s beyond what I can 
express, and all that I feel wanting is that you should 
know such to be the case. While Lady D. is in this 
part of the world, I beg you will do me the favour to 
write me every second Saturday, as that exactly meets the 
packet mail from London on Tuesday. Ramsay and 
Charles may put in a little letter for me alternately,— 
that will show me their writing. Tell Ramsay, when 
I was in London I appointed him to be one of my 
three Esquires of the Bath. Major Couper and Cap- 
tain Sparrow are the others, so that he will always 
walk with me in the processions of Knights of the 
Bath. My love to both of them, and believe me to be, 
‘‘ Dear sir, yours faithfully, 
“ DaLHousIE.” 


And so the gallant father goes off to the stormy 
war-worn Continent, thinking of his boys at home. 

During his residence at Coalstoun, there seems to 
have been a marked growth and progress in his mind 
and life—an increasing solidity and earnestness, with 
but little of the old dissatisfaction and misery of heart. 
The Scottish Church rises more distinctly before him 
as his proper goal, and, with this in view, there is 
more purpose and consistency in his studies. Still, 
though by his own showing he must be tolerably dili- 
gent and busy, he is by no means content with his 
own performances. “I do not even play a game at 
chess regularly,” he writes. ‘‘ My whole life is a bloated 
mass of irregular and frivolous occupation.” Com- 
bined with a great inability to observe a stated order 
and strict method, he had always the most eager desire 
to do so—always a kind of fruitless striving after an 


32 SELF-JUDGMENT. 


ideal regularity never attained ; and so he was apt to 
hold very cheap any work, which, though considerable 
in quantity, was not performed in a manner sufficiently 
methodical to please his fastidious taste. His own 
work, consequently, was generally very lightly esteemed. 
Similarly, his own pure and lofty standard of moral 
worth, not less than a tremulous sensitiveness of con- 
science, and a self-forgetful humility of heart, which 
characterized him through all his life, rendered him a 
most exacting observer and rigid judge of his own inner 
condition, and of his own conduct. He, who was to 
all others most indulgent, was to himself most severe, 
inexorably detecting and condemning his own faults 
and shortcomings. While he is at this time the one 
apparently most popular, most admired and respected 
among a large circle of friends, many of them dis- 
tinguished for moral excellence and mental accom- 
plishment, he is never oblivious of those imperfections 
which, he believes, make him unworthy of the warm 
regard and esteem in which they hold him. ‘“ Oh!” 
he writes in his diary, “Oh! that Divine grace would 
in some fortunate moment eternize the emotions I 
sometimes feel, that I might be for ever happy, and that 
these effusions might be the token of everlasting purity.” 
The triple correspondence between him, Donaldson, 
and Cannan,” had by this time died a natural death. 
The three friends, as might have been expected, found, 
as the sterner business of life claimed their attention, 
that they had no time for such elaborate and philo- 
sophic disquisitions as those which used to “ circulate 
through the little system of their friendship,” between 
Edinburgh and Ballindalloch. 


* Afterwards the Rev. Thomas Cannan, minister of Carsphairn. 


HIS FRIENDS AT COALSTOUN. 33 


Story had now more important work to look after 
than he had had then, and a much wider company of 
friends and longer list of correspondents, among whom 
was one whose name was in coming years to make 
the church grow pale, Edward Irving, at this time 
Rector of Kirkcaldy Academy. 


“There about the beach he wander’d nourishing a youth sublime 
With the fairy tales of Science and the long results of Time.” 


And with Irving may also be mentioned the Rev. Dr. 
Balmer, at Berwick, and Mr. (now Bishop) Terrot, then 
the Episcopal minister at Haddington. 

To Pringle, who was toiling laboriously amidst the 
old records in the Register Office in Edinburgh, he 
wrote regularly, and to him he transmitted such of his 
poetical compositions as he deemed deserving of pub- 
licity. Where or when these appeared in print, it 1s 
impossible now to ascertain. It is obvious, however, 
that they were given to the public, and that the author's 
name became known as that of a useful collaborateur 
in literary enterprise. Pringle bespeaks his co-opera- 
tion in the designs for the establishment of a perio- 
dical, which he was attempting to carry out; and Hogg 
also desires to enlist him as a contributor to his 
“Miscellany.” This he declined to become, owing 
to the pressure of his necessary avocations. Pringle, 
however, writes to his friend that he had taken it upon 
him to entrust one of the poems which he had placed 
in his hands to the Ettrick Shepherd. He goes on to 
say, ‘ Upon giving him this, I read to him some passages 
addressed to you long since, upon our tour through 
Teviotdale and Ettrick Forest, which he strongly 
pressed me to print, and flattered me so well, that I 
have set to and finished them; and the said poem, 


D 


34 THE “AUTUMNAL EXCURSION.” 


yet without a name, extends to no less than sixteen 
pages.’”* 


* The poem here alluded to by Pringle is the one he afterwards 
published under the title of the “Autumnal Excursion.” It is dedicated 
to “R. §.,” and commemorates a Border excursion which the two 
friends bad made together in their younger days. It first appeared in 
the “ Poetic Mirror” in 1816. It was meant, we are told by Mr. 
Leitch Ritchie in his life of Pringle, “as an imitation of the strains of 
the Wizard of the North :—the said wizard however (in whose hands it 
was placed for revision) declaring that he wished the original notes had 
always been as fine as their echo.” This poem was republished along 
with others in a collection which he entitled ‘‘ Ephemerides,” in 1828, 
when the author appended to it the following note :—“ The friend to 
whom the ‘ Autumnal Excursion’ is addressed, is a native of the Vale of 
Beaumont. The author and he were born in adjoining parishes, amid 
the secluded glens of Cheviot, and were inseparable associates in early 
youth ; and though our pursuits in maturer life have been widely 
different, it is not the less pleasing to look back over a twenty years’ 
friendship, which no selfish jealousy has ever disturbed or coldness inter- 
rupted, or even long separation impaired. My old companion and 
valued friend will, I trust, excuse this slight expression of affectionate 
remembrance, and forgive me for adding that the ‘R.8.’ of my little 
poem, though not the Poet Laureate of England (as the ‘ Quarterly 
Review’ once supposed), is a person who fears God and loves mankind 
not less sincerely, namely, the Rev. Robert Story, Minister of Ros- 
neath.” The poem begins thus :— 

“ Dear Story, while the southern breeze 
Floats fragrant from the upland leas, 
Whispering of Autumn’s mellow spoils 
And jovial sports and grateful toils— 
Awakening in the softened breast 
Regrets and wishes long supprest— 

O, come with me, once more to hail 
The scented heath, the sheafy vale, 

The hills and streams of Teviotdale. 
"Tis but a parting pilgrimage, 

To save from Time’s destroying rage 
And changeful Fortune’s withering blast 
The pictured relics of the Past. 

Then come, dear comrade, welcome still 
In every change of good or ill; 

Whom young affection’s wishes claim, 
And friendship ever finds the same ; 
Awake with all thy flow of mind, 

With fancy bright and feelings kind, 
And tune with me the rambling lay, 
To cheer us on our mountain way.” 


MASQUE AT COALSTOUN. 35 


Meanwhile his pupils were making great progress 
under his instructions. Their father, on his return 
from abroad, was charmed with their advancement. 
His lordship’s safe restoration to his home was cele- 
brated by the performance of a Masque, composed by 
the tutor and performed by the boys, with some co- 
operation of more experienced actors. ‘“ Comus” 
has suggested an idea here and there; but the 
chief model has been found in the ‘“ Midsummer 
Night’s Dream.’ The dramatis persone are, as in the 
legitimate drama, Oberon, Titania, and Puck, with the 
addition of Ariel and Orson, The author and manager 
used to tell that all went off with perfect éclat, and 
everyone did his part dutifully, except the Honourable 
James; and he who was in due time to sway the 
sceptre over a hundred and eighty millions of men, 
could not be induced to carry his wand like a discreet 
and benevolent fairy, but would insist on poking it into 
the coloured lamps which lighted up the elfin grove, in 
order to discover the peculiar properties of luminaries 
of so novel an aspect. 

It was the Earl’s wish that, during the winter of 
1815-16, his boys should be lodged in Edinburgh with 
Mr. Story, and attend the High School, then flourishing 
under the masterly control of Mr. Pillans. This, how- . 
ever, was not to be: before the winter came, Mr. S.’s 
superintendence of their education had ceased. 

A vacancy seeming to be imminent in the parish 
of Ladykirk, in Berwickshire, Lord Dalhousie and 
his father’s friend, Mr. Wauchope, of Niddrie, applied 
to the Government on his behalf, and secured for him 
the eventual succession to the living. Meanwhile he 
pushes on as expeditiously as possible his Presbyterial 


examinations. He was rather apprehensive of the 
D2 


36 THE DIVINITY HALL 


result of these; for his last appearance in the Di- 
vinity Hall of Edinburgh (Feb., 1815) had provoked 
criticisms from the Professor of a not very assuring 
nature: and if the Presbytery was to be as captious 
as Dr. Ritchie, his case would be pitiable indeed. 
That reverend doctor had fallen foul of his theology, 
as he tells Morton :—‘ Convicted of heretically and 
gnostically philosophizing—denounced as being un- 
qualified for the office of a useful preacher—reprobated 
for pretending to new discoveries in religion, was I 
uneasy under all these imputations? No; I assure 
you my mind reposed upon the egis of eternal truth ; 
and the dark, sophisticated prejudices of a super- 
annuated necromantic theologian stormed upon it 
without avail. Take this as a precious sentence from 
the infallible Chair, ‘Sir, I give you all due credit for 
talent and learning—for your acquaintance with the 
ancient and modern philosophy. Sir, Irespect your 
abilities, and I respect your virtues; but beyond this I 
cannot go, for you have left out of your discourse the 
Lord Jesus.’”’ 

The discourse was on the whole creation being made 
“subject to vanity,” and took some view not found in 
the adust pages of the commentators; hence, it would 
. seem, the Doctor’s wrath, his maxim being that what- 
ever is new is unsound, or, at least, dangerous : a good 
enough axiom, no doubt, provided it were proved that 
all that is old 1s sound, or at least safe. 

“The Hall,” he says, with bitter emphasis, “is a 
miserable place ;” an opinion to which only too many 
will say “Amen.” Perhaps some readers may think 
the following sentences not without a more modern 
anplication than to the grave and reverend seignior of 

a they were first spoken :—“ He is destroying the 


IS LICENSED. 37 


literary character of the Church. He has a most 
Gothic antipathy to everything that is elegant, inge- 
nious, or new. He is affected with a most feverish 
dread of some lurking evil whenever it appears: in 
this manner he watches like an angel of desolation at 
the portal of the Church. I am speaking undutifully, 
however, for it is a sin to revile the anointed of our 
fathers.” 

The Presbytery (that of Haddington) took no excep- 
tion to his doctrines, and found him, in their opinion, 
fully qualified to discharge the duties of a preacher of 
the Gospel; and he consequently received licence as a 
preacher at their hands in July, 1815. He then quitted 
Coalstoun, carrying with him the sincere esteem and 
affection of its noble inmates. 

The decisive step was now taken: he was alicentiate 
of the Scottish Church: his tutorial engagements were 
at an end, and his career as a preacher begun. But he 
had scarcely quitted his former avocations and entered 
on his new life, ere he repented of the course he had 
adopted. 

Mr. Tod, of Ladykirk, still tottered along the 
broken arches above the “ prodigious tide,” and there 
was accordingly no vacancy in that parish. Of 
course the expectant presentee is restless and des- 
pondent: there seems no promise of any opening 
for him in the Church. He wishes to go away to 
England and take a tutorship there—to be off to the 
Continent if possible: he would do anything in fact 
that an honourable man might, to employ his quick, 
and as he thinks—wasted energies. Meantime, how- 
ever, he must give some proof of his capability of 
discharging the duty which the Church has entrusted 
to him; he must conduct his first service, and preach
