FIRST SERMON. 39 


shall not mention, that you may be induced to pay 
an ‘angel visit’ to your Bible) . . . Im the 
evening I went out to our romantic and beautiful 
Linton, where another day was destined to fix my 
popularity and fame as a Border orator. In the night 
I had some fearful visions. I heard the cock crow, 
and saw the sun rise, and the eight o'clock bell tolled 
with an import which a Kirk steeple never emitted to 
my ear before. At twelve the little temple was crowded 
to the door, and .as I went in all the people looked at 
me askance—fearful lest the minutest noise should 
discompose the virgin oration, with which my mind was 
in great travail. The first object I noticed was the 
fiery, immense circumference of “ the Philistine’s” face, 
who had come home drunk from St. James’s Fair on the 
Saturday night, but was resolved to charm away his 
headache by listening to my eloquence. With a firm 
and unfaltering voice I read out the Psalm. My prayer, 
will you believe it—my first prayer was, in the strictest 
sense of the word, extempore. Fora time I lost myself 
and my head began to grow dizzy, but I gradually re- 
turned, and terminated with avery good grace. During 
the first part of the discourse I was solemn, calm, and 
serious. The people gaped as I described the nature of 
the angels, and before I had finished the introduction 
were all attentive to the wonders that were to follow. 
I proceeded without stopping—sometimes, however, 
wandering from my notes, but always possessing a suffi- 
cient command over myself to use fit and becoming 
expressions on my way back. In the last part of the, 
discourse, however, I became animated. My rotund 
sentences flowed delightfully. I had towards the con- 
clusion some awful imagery, which produced a deep 
silence, and when I concluded, one universal act of 


40 ROSNEATH. 


inspiration succeeded to the breathless stillness which 
hung upon my lips! Not one of you fastidious critics 
ever dreamed of my becoming a popular preacher ; but 
it seems that I gained the hearts of them all. ‘“ The 
Philistine” returned home rejoicing in spirit. An old 
woman from Eckford parish told as she returned, that 
I was just her heart’s desire; and some of the tailors 
and ditchers were so edified that they are declaring 
to follow me wherever I go, if I but preach in the 
bounds.” 

The tone of this letter indicates none of that deeply 
earnest spirit, which afterwards pervaded all his minis- 
trations in the Church and parish over which he was 
ordained. He does not seem to have fully realized the 
serious nature of the charge entrusted to him, in his 
being authorized by the Church, to preach the Gospel 
of the Grace of God; nor is it likely that he would 
duly feel it, until he came to stand in a definite and 
permanent relation to a congregation. 

He had not to wait long before this relation was 
formed. He was offered in the end of autumn the 
assistantship at Rosneath, and after some wavering, 
determined to accept it. In December he set out for 
Dunbartonshire. On his way he writes to Morton: “At 
Rosneath I shall be devoted to my duty. I have too 
long trifled with the things of undying interest, and thy 
heart faints through very satiety of the contemptible 
frivolities of life. In my new occupations I trust I 
shall find new feelings, and while I endeavour to teach, 
be myself taught.” While he was thus forming the 
resolutions which were to direct his conduct in his 
new sphere of action, his arrival there was anxiously 
expected. The incumbent was the Rev. George 
Drummond, D.D., during the latter years of whose 


ROSNEATH. 41 


incumbency the parish had got into sad disorder, while 
the pulpit had been supplied in a manner extremely 
inefficient. The consequence had been that the church 
was in a very forlorn condition: its influence was weak ; 
its public services were generally neglected. This state 
of matters gave great offence to all who were really 
concerned for the welfare of the parish; and to none 
more than to the Duke of Argyll’s factor, Mr. Robert 
Campbell, a most excellent, warm-hearted, honourable, 
Highland gentleman, who zealously attended to the 
interests of the duke, the patron and principal heritor 
of Rosneath; and who, indeed, to tell the truth, was dis- 
posed to exert a very prompt and lofty control over all 
persons and affairs within the parochial boundaries. 
Mr. Campbell’s correspondence enlightens us as to the 
way of conducting affairs at this time in the fair penin- 
sula, towards which Mr. Story was directing his steps. 
He writes to Lord John Campbell * in July—‘ The 
poor infirm body, Brown, who assisted Dr. Drummond, 
died on Friday last. The old Doctor, whose mind is 
almost gone, has entered into some engagement with 
our idiotical schoolmaster, Graham, to make him his 
assistant, although he wants two years of his divinity 
studies, and was on that account refused a licence by 
the Presbytery of Dunbarton. He is, however, going 
to try to get himself licensed by the Presbytery of 
Dumfries.” . 

By the end of December, Mr. Campbell’s appre- 
hensions as to the “ idiotical schoolmaster” are 
dissipated; and he writes to another correspondent, 
“We have got a most delightful young man as Dr. 
Drummond’s assistant, named Robert Story, who has 


_ * Late Duke of Argyll: brother of the then Duke, (George). 


42 ROSNEATH. 


one of the best livings in Scotland secured to him at 
the death of an infirm old gentleman at Ladykirk. He 
has preached twice here to the great satisfaction of his 
hearers.” 

The peninsula of Rosneath les between the Gair- 
loch and Loch Long: at its northern extremity a high 
and narrow ridge, it slopes gradually down to the low 
well-wooded shores past which the Clyde flows on the 
south. Each of the lochs has its own peculiar scenery. 
That of Loch Long is stern and desolate ; the long dun 
gorge is aptly termed by Rogers a “ vast and awful 
depth of hills.” That of the Gairloch is calm and beau- 
tiful, and viewed in the serene light of a summer even- 
ing, reminds one of the soft and dreamy loveliness of 
the Campanian shore. Beyond the Rosneath Ferry 
the loch stretches out its ample sheet of water, till itis | 
barred by the low-browed heights which separate it from 
Loch Long, and beyond which rises the bold ridge of 
the Argyllshire hills, tracing their rugged outline on the 
sky. ‘These wild and lonely mountains contrast well 
with the wooded slopes and fair meadows of the lower 
portion of the loch, and enhance the charm that always 
lingers there. It isa charm that is felt most strongly, 
perhaps, in early autumn, when “a fiery finger’ here 
and there has touched the leaves, and the fields are 
yellow to the harvest. The weariest Pilgrim of the 
Beautiful could wish to gaze on no lovelier scene than 
then meets the eye, as the westering sun gradually 
leaving the slopes of Rosneath, with the leafy glens and 
deep rich woods around its nestled bay, in gathering 
shadow, still shines upon the winding shore of Row, 
and sparkles on the roofs and windows of the villas that 
rise amongst their trees and gardens, while the rough 
mountains in the airy distance assume a deeper purple. 


FIRST IMPRESSIONS, 43 


But no such prospect as this—no such autumnal 
richness and colour and serenity greeted the new as- 
sistant, as on the afternoon of Saturday, the 17th Dec., 
1815, he approached the shore of his future parish in 
the open packet-boat from Greenock. Where now the 
villas cluster along the hill-side at Row, there was 
then an unbroken solitude. Ardencaple and the Inn 
and the Manse were the only habitations with roofs 
of slate between Helensburgh and the Church. The 
Rosneath braes looked equally dreary. From hill-top 
to water’s edge a shroud of snow stretched cold and 
grim. Between the white and silent shores the loch 
lay black and sullen. 

He landed as the early twilight was deepening into 
night, and went upto the Manse. 

The house was old, mouldy, and out of repair. Dr. 
Drummond was quite in his dotage. When the new- 
comer retired for the night, and went comfortless to 
bed, the sheets were damp and clammy; from the 
bed-room walls the discoloured paper hung in tatters. 
Altogether, the place, and house, and things in general, 
looked so hopelessly dismal and dispiriting that he re- 
solved he would bid them all a long farewell, as soon 
as Sunday was over. He had only two sermons; 
he would preach them next day, and make off on the 
Monday morning; it having been part of his engage- 
ment with Dr. D. that he should be free to leave 
when he chose. 

He did preach them to a congregation of about forty 
persons assembled in the dingy barn-like Kirk, and 
came back to the Manse after service, no less dis- 
gusted with his new circumstances than when he had 
quitted it in the forenoon; clearly he could never 
imprison himself here. 


é 


44 THE INHABITANTS. 


One or two pleasant neighbours came in, however, 
after church, to introduce themselves to the new 
minister—Mr. Robert Campbell, of whom we have 
already spoken, and Mr. Lorne Campbell, a frank and 
hearty young fellow, about Mr. Story’s own age. Their 
warm welcome and kindness and interest cheered his 
drooping spirits, and “made a sunshine in the shady 
place,” like the fair face of Una in the visions of Ed- 
mund Spenser. Mr. Robert, as he was called (for 
Campbells were, and are, so common in the district as 
to need distinction by the Christian name rather than 
the surname), took him up to his kindly and hospitable 
home at the Clachan House, and introduced him there 
toa family circle, whose true and genial friendship was 
to be, for many a long year, one of the closest ties 
binding him to Rosneath. The weather, too, im- 
proved, and his prospects seemed to brighten with 
the sun: in a day or two he had made up his mind 
not to beat quite so hasty a retreat, as he had contem- 
plated in the first sharpness of his disappointment. 

The society of Rosneath at this time was very limited, 
as, indeed, it is still; but, upon the whole, agreeable 
and intelligent. 

The old castle (since taken down) was inhabited by 
Mr. James Smith,* of Jordanhill, who, with Mrs. Smith, 
was among Mr. Story’s most intimate and valued 
friends. Mr. Lorne Campbell lived at Portkiln with his 
excellent mother. At the Clachan House, resided the 
family of Campbells already mentioned; at Barremman, 
two miles farther up the loch, Mr. Cumming; and at 
Peatoun, on Loch Longside, Mrs. Campbell, widow of 


* Author of the work on the “ Voyage and Shipwreck of St. Paul,” 
which has settled the vexed question of the identity of our Malta with 
St. Luke’s Melita. 


THE INHABITANTS, 45 


the late Laird of Peatoun, and sister of Dr. Drum- 
mond. These formed the “ gentry” of the parish. In 
a letter to Pringle, written a few months after his 
arrival, we have the young curate’s impressions of some 
of them. “I have only to walk along a most solemn 
avenue of ancient yews to a family where there is as 
much heart as in any, house in Christendom. Argyll’s 
factor, a Mr. (Robert) Campbell, is its inhabitant; his 
lady, high-spirited, ardent, and affectionate, is a native 
of Mull. His brother, Sir Colin, resides with him ; he 
was above forty years abroad, and is a sublime and 
interesting Stoic. He was t’other day afflicted with a 
disorder that is attended with excruciating torment, 
and he laughed and was merry, like an Indian on his 
funeral pile. There are two sisters, both of a romantic 
disposition—friendly, warm, and generous. The old 
mother is still alive, near ninety, a splendid beauty in 
her time. She reads the whole day, and is still in the 
habit of regularly getting by heart a hymn or a passage 
of Scripture, like any of her grandchildren. You know 
my pliability, and you may well conceive that, long 
before this, I am like one of the family. There is, 
again, on the other side of the ‘Island’ (for you must 
know such is the designation of my parish) an old lady, 
with whom, in a dowager mansion, resides a beautiful 
young virgin—a fine singer, and performer on the piano. 
I have only time for one trait of the former. I dare 
not trust myself with any observation upon the latter. 
She got Simpson’s ‘ Visit to Waterloo,’ in which honour- 
able mention is made of her nephew’s regiment. She 
sat down and read it from beginning to end, without 
shifting her position: her handkerchief, which lay 
upon her lap, was as completely drenched with her 
' tears as if it had been steeped in the loeh before her 


46 THE CLACHAN FAMILY. 


door. Ina holy frenzy she took it in her hands, and 
vowing that it no more should be used for an ordinary 
purpose, she consigned it to the flames, that the holy 
oil with which it was so richly anointed might never be 
polluted with corruptible emunctions.” 

Among such persons as those spoken of in this 
letter there could not but remain a good deal of the 
primitive individuality, that gradually disappears under 
that advancing tide of modern society which produces 
a level uniformity by obliterating all that is pic- 
turesque ; the “social mill” had not yet rubbed each 
angle down. 

The trait which marked the Clachan family dis- 
tinctively was their devotion to the house of Argyll. 
Loyalty to the great head of their clan was, with 
them, the ruling principle, and seemed to possess the 
solemn obligations of a, religious duty. ‘Time did not 
efface, nor vicissitude impair it.* At a period long 
after that at which the above letter was written, and 
when one of the two sisters alone surviving was mourn- 
ing her last brother’s death, an heir was born to the 
house of Argyll in circumstances that called forth very 
general rejoicing among all interested in its welfare. 
“Miss Betsy” was indignant that her nephews were 
not invited to a festivity held in honour of the occasion 


* The reverence for Mac Callenmor’s name and authority seemed, in 
another case, to invest a gentleman much identified with the ducal 
interests with a certain ducal dignity of his own. On one occasion, 
when debating about some repairs to be made in a mansion belonging to 
the Duke, its occupant suggested the propriety of delay till they could 
learn the Duke’s opinion. ‘The Duke !” cried the other, “Z’m the 
Duke.” And although his Grace could have no worthier representative, 
there still was a measure of truth, for Rosneath as well as for Inverary, in 
the words of a parish “ bauldie,” who, meeting the Duke as he was once 
quitting the latter for London, begged him to return as soon as possible, 
“for we hae owre mony Dukes whan yee awa.” 


MR. BROWN. 47 


at the house of a gentleman in the neighbourhood. 
It was suggested to her that perhaps his wife thought 
it would be inappropriate to invite them to a merry- 
making so soon after their father’s death. ‘“‘She may 
_ think what she likes,” she replied, “ but her husband’s 
mother’s son should have known better. He should 
have known that no affliction could ever visit the 
Clachan that would make us indifferent to the pro- 
sperity of the house of Argyll. My brother would have 
rejoiced to-day if he had buried his last child yester- 
day, and his sons would not be worthy of his name if 
they could not do the same.” 

Mr. Story used to relate with great zest an instance 
of the veneration in which the natives of his parish 
held “the majesty that hedged” the Duke. A man 
came to the “bailie,’” Mr. Campbell, with a long 
complaint of contumelious usage received at the hands 
of some adversary or other; “and mair nor that,” 
said he, coming, after a detail of his grievances, to 
the crisis of his charge, “he had the impiddence to 
strike me in the presence o’ His Grace’s Horse.’ 
“The force of clanship could no further go.” 

The Rev. Dr. Drummond had in his best days been 
a man of considerable ability and energy, and had dis- 
charged his duties efficiently, though his ideas of the 
dignity and responsibility of his position seem to have 
been somewhat lax. But for the seven years pre- 
ceding 1815, he had been almost entirely unfit for 
duty. Mr. Story’s immediate predecessor as his 
assistant, was Mr. Brown, described by an old parish- 
ioner who remembers him, as “a gran’ preacher, but a 
wofu’ drucken body.” On one occasion Mr. B. fell 
down in the pulpit: Mr. Robert Campbell hastened 
up to see what was wrong— Oh! Mr. Campbell,” he 


48 «SMUGGLING. 


groaned pathetically, “you know there’s only one thing 
that'll do me good.” Mr. Campbell accordingly retired 
and procured this elixir, and after a pretty strong dram, 
Mr. Brown revived and regained his upright position. 
This desirable consummation having been attained, 
Mr. Robert was descending the pulpit-stairs to return 
to his own pew, when Dr. Drummond, from the Manse 
seat, addressed him in a stage-whisper, “ Tell him to 
cut it short—tell him to cut it short.” Mr. Brown, 
whose sense of hearing was in no wise impaired by the 
unpleasant little attack he had had, gave no time for 
the transmission of this injunction through Mr. Robert, 
but darting a vicious glance down at his principal, said 
loud enough for all to hear: “ Tell him I will not—tell: 
him I will not;” then resuming his interrupted dis- 
course, he preached for an hour and a-half. 

Under the auspices of this worthy, religion could not 
but wither, and the parish became sadly deteriorated. 
There were six public houses in it. Drunkenness was 
very general, rows frequent, smuggling universal. 
There was a still in every glen, and the illicit manu- 
facture and traffic were carried on with very little 
concealment. So blunted was the moral sense of the 
community, that it was considered no stigma on any 
man’s character that he should be a smuggler; and 
persons even making a decidedly religious profession 
perceived no inconsistency in combining therewith the 
avocation of the unlicensed distiller. Nor did they 
recognize any reason why the minister should look 
coldly on such a profitable employment. The smug- 
gler felt no delicacy, and no apprehension in bringing 
to the Manse a gallon or two which he could recom- 
mend as “nane o yer clatty muckle-still whusky, 
but a wee drap that I ha’e jist made mysel’,” and 


DRINKING USAGES. 49 


in the old Doctor’s days he found most probably a 
ready purchaser. “I pay the duty on the maut,” said 
one man in reply to Mr. S.’s remonstrances—“ I alloo 
nae sweerin’ at the still, and everything’s dune dacently 
and in order. I canna’ see ony hairm in’t.” 

A libation accompanied every transaction either at 
kirk or market. Dr. Drummond, in conformity with 
the recommendation of the Directory,* now but little 
observed, used to assemble his flock in the Church in 
order to be catechized. If the day were a cold one, 
the Elders would gently propose that they should see 
if “Jean” had anything that would warm them; 
upon which hint all would retire to the neighbouring 
public-house, the Doctor taking his dram, and paying 
his “lawing” with the rest. At christenings also, an 
adjournment to Jean’s generally took place, on which 
auspicious occasions the minister would provide the 
means of drinking to the infant’s health. Marriages 
and funerals, however, afforded the opportunity of 
the severest drinking usages. ‘‘ At marriages,” says 
Mr. Story himself, in the Statistical Account of 
the Parish of Rosneath (1841), “‘numerous crowds 
assembled, and generally they were very boisterous 
festivities. On the intermediate days before the Kirk- 
ing, the young pair, with their attendants, preceded by 
the bagpipe, perambulated the parish, visiting the 
cottages that had furnished their quota to the carnival. 
The ceremonies were closed by the whole party, after 
Divine Service on Sabbath, adjourning for refresh- 
ment, to the contiguous tavern.” 


* “Form of Church Government—Pastors. To catechize, which 
is a plain laying down the first principles of the Oracles of God, or of 
the Doctrine of Christ, and is a part of preaching.”—Durectury of 
Public Worship, appended to Confession of Faith. 

8 


50 COMMUNION SERVICES. 


At funerals, four rounds of whisky were considered 
due to wounded affection and departed worth, and 
respect was shown to the dead by the intoxication of 
the living. A company, each of whose component 
members had imbibed four glasses of raw whisky, 
could hardly be expected to offer a very staid and 
orderly escort to the grave; and it not unfrequently 
happened that a good deal of squabbling and fighting 
took place among the mourners on their way to the 
churchyard. On one ‘occasion the funeral procession 
halted half-way to the place of interment, in order 
that the bearers might be relieved. At this juncture 


' a dispute arose, on the question who should carry 


the head, and who the feet. Each claimant for the 
post of honour had his bereaved and inebriated sup- 
porters; the coffin was laid down, and physical force 
instead of moral suasion was quickly resorted to for 
the settlement of the discussion, and there was 
much din and dust of battle ere the quarrel was 
adjusted. 

The religious aspect of the district in which Ros: 
neath was situated was very cloudy. Sunday-schools 
and the like agencies were unknown. The Holy 
Communion was in all the parishes dispensed only 
once a year; and when it was administered in one 
parish, the churches of the neighbouring parishes were 
left vacant for the day, so that minister and flock 
might resort to the “Holy Fair.” It was a point of 
honour, in which each parish tried to rival its neigh- 
bour, that the Communion services should be pro- 
longed as much as possible, and it was by no means 
rare that beginning at 11 a.m. they should “ drag their 
slow length along” till 6 or 7 in the evening. While 
“+= communicants were receiving the Sacrament at the 


THE “MONDAY DINNER.” 51 


successive tables in the Church, the people outside 
clustered round the “tent” (an erection exactly like a 
bathing box) in the Churchyard, and listened to pro- 
tracted preachings; one minister rising as soon as 
another sat down exhausted, with as dauntless a devo- 
tion as that of Knight or Squire in the “desperate 
ring” at Flodden. The religious exercises of the day, 
however, were varied by a good deal of eating and 
drinking, either in the open air or in the nearest public- 
houses. An old clergyman of a parish near Rosneath 
used to remark with pride, when an extra gathering 
attended his Communion, “ It was a creditable crood, 
there was fourteen stane o’ saumon eaten in the 
village.” 

The Communion at Arrochar was usually graced by 
an excursion party, which came down in a steamer from 
Glasgow. It was a “very superior occasion,” as the 
Ettrick Shepherd would say. No exception was taken 
to the Sunday travelling; and when the excursionists 
steamed out of the bay in the evening, they saluted 
their fellow-worshippers on shore with three cheers 
“ for the tent.” 

The “Monday Dinner”* was then an institution 
at the zenith of its glory. A glimpse of its usual 
characteristics may be obtained through the follow- 
ing extract from a letter, addressed by a friend in 
Argylishire to Mr. Story, before he came to the west 
country: ‘“ Hero of the dark rolling eye! The roar- 
ing and bowsing of last night has been succeeded 
by a stillness and uneasiness which the temperate 
and virtuous never know,—and even among the 


* A banquet on the Monday following the Communion Sunday, at 
which all the elders, lairds, and ministers that could be collected 
“ assisted.” ; 

E 


52 THE “MONDAY DINNER.” 


heathy wilds of Caledonia, where temperance and 
purity might seem to have fixed their abode, there 
is to be found one languid pulse—one aching head 
—one remorse-struck soul. Perhaps you think that 
I have been in some infamous company which 
prudence and honour conspired in exhorting me to 
shun ; and that with the loss of my innocence, I have 
thrown aside also my respectability. Never once 
imagine it, sir, The bowsing match which I am 
lamenting was nothing else than the clerical dinner 
which usually follows the celebration of the Sacra- 
ment, and my fellows in iniquity were blameless priests 
and holy elders, high-minded heritors and High- 
land Chieftains. We drank, and roared, and sang, in 
a style which would have shamed our most illustrious 
meetings at Turnbull’s, even when roared ‘ Jolly 
Beggars.’ We fired our grape shot (nothing less than 
royal port), sometimes three in a minute. We bum- 
pered every young lady in the country. We gave three 
times three to the Spanish patriots. I, in a style of 
matchless expression, sung ‘ Swigg’d it,’ and ‘ A High- 
land lad my love was born.’ About eight in the 
evening, some were sick, and others were groaning ; 
some mounted their horses to ride home many miles 
over the hills, and your humble servant was completely 
done up.” This is a specimen of the entertainment 
that was considered in those days a seemly termination 
of a solemn religious ordinance ! 

It was on the charge of a parish thus neglected, and 
into the midst of a community content with the low 
religious standard which the facts I have alluded to 
 “eate, that Mr. Story now entered,—a young man 

of life and energy,—without indeed any very pro- 
| convictions or strong personal hold of spiritual 


ENTERS ON HIS WORK. 53 


truth, but with a deep desire to do his duty,—with 
high aspirations after possible excellence in himself 
and others, and with a refinement of taste, and a purity 
of moral feeling that revolted from the disorders and 
improprieties which he saw around him. He had 
poetized a good deal about the Ideal; and, in rhyme 
had embodied his hankerings after the Beautiful, 
True, and Good—loving the two latter perhaps more, 
because they were the elements that went to make up 
the first, than for their own sakes only. He now found 
himself in a Poet's Corner, where the exquisite love- 
liness of nature seemed to plead with low and plaintive 
tones, for a moral beauty in the heart of man that should 
answer to her own. To the heart of the young priest 
the voice of the great Mother did not utter itself in 
vain. He resolved he would do with his might what 
his hand found to do, and as far as his single effort 
could accomplish it, would try to set this little portion 
of the great world right; to “make a clearer faith, 
and manhood shine in the untutored heart ;” to instil 
some hearty reverence for God, and Law, and Order. 
Thus resolving, he, with poetic enthusiasm, and Chris- 
tian earnestness, bent all his energies to his appointed 
work. 


CHAPTER III. 
PARISH WORK—DECLINE—REVIVAL. 


How to tend and cultivate this neglected vineyard 
was now, of course, the problem. He pondered it 
thoughtfully, and resolved to begin operations by 
making a domiciliary visitation of the whole parish. 
As he thus went among them, he spoke to the people 
in his own kindly familiar way, of the duties of which 
they had too long been careless, and the responsi- 
bilities to which they had been indifferent. The effect 
was soon seen. Their attendance at church became 
regular and full; and a desire to be instructed was thus, 
as well as in other ways, manifested. He then com- 
menced meetings from week to week, for the purpose 
of catechizing; and, with few exceptions, all the parish- 
ioners, high and low, attended in their respective dis- 
tricts. The Shorter Catechism was in great request ; and 
in his forenoon calls, now and then, the minister, as he 
entered a room, would catch a glimpse of the slender 
volume vanishing into some place of concealment. He 
next began to bring the children into order. He sum- 
moned them to assemble after service one Sunday. A 
large number came, whom he organized into a Sabbath 
School— quite a new institution in Dunbartonshire; and 
after it had been for some time fairly in working order, he 
established, in connection with it, a juvenile library. 


SONDAY SCHOOL. 55 


No part of his ministerial duty was performed with 
more hearty interest than the instruction of the young, 
and none produced a more living and lasting impres- 
sion. His nature was so genial, and his manner so 
kind,—his earnestness so obvious, that children always 
turned to him with spontaneous affection and confi- 
dence. Few forgot in after years, even if they forgot 
all else, the voice and look of their early teacher; while 
many who have traced to the hours spent in the Sab- 
bath classes, the beginning of all that was true, and 
useful, and hopeful in their lives, look back to him as 
to their spiritual father, with “ blessings on his kindly 
heart and on his silver head.” A few years before his 
death, one of these, an elderly woman, arrived at the 
Manse, bringing two boys with her. She had brought 
her two sons, she said, that they might see the minister 
that had instructed her in her youth. She had often 
told them of his Sabbath school, and of the first day it 
met, when all the children in the parish were collected 
in the middle of the kirk, while the minister spoke to 
them, and then read out for singing the 11th verse of 
the 34th Psalm :— 

“Q! children, hither do ye come, 
And unto me give ear— 


I shall you teach to understand 
How ye the Lord should fear.” 


Thus labouring for the enlightenment and reforma- 
tion of both old and young, he gained a strong ascendency 
over the hearts of all. Their faults were the errors of 
& primitive, and somewhat lawless and ignorant com- 
munity—not the sordid vices of those whom civi- 
lization has corrupted, while it has smoothed. Their 
instincts of right and wrong were dormant, not dead- 
ened; and so, when they were appealed to, they awoke 


56 GOOD PROGRESS. 


and asserted their authority. And he who thus roused 
them was respected for his work’s sake, as well as loved 
for his own. The man who, true to his own duty, 
animates others with the consciousness of theirs, will 
always be respected by all who have not lost the very 
capacity of moral life. 

Mr. Robert’s satisfaction (and when Mr. Robert was 
pleased, who should be discontented ?) was more em- 
phatic than ever. “Mr. Story,” he writes to the Duke 
of Argyll, “‘is, in my humble opinion, likely to turn 
out one of the best preachers in Scotland, and is most 
indefatigable and wonderfully successful in bringing 
the people, so long neglected, to a due sense of their 
duty to God and man, by visiting them in their 
families, and collecting them in their villages, for the 
purposes of religious instruction on week days, and 
bestowing an hour each Sabbath day in instructing the 
children in Church, where sometimes more than one 
hundred of these children attend.” 

That the feelings of regard and goodwill which he 
and others of the flock entertained for the young 
minister were fully reciprocated may be inferred from 
what Dr. Thomas Brown writes in July, 1816:—* I 
have seldom been more interested than by the picture 
your letter drew of your pastoral happiness. You have 
indeed made a noble beginning, and after such begin- 
nings, there is no fear of the middle and the end. I 
had at one time the intention of finding my way to 
Rosneath, and casting my moral, as well as physical 
eyes on the different sorts of beauty so well described 
by you; but I must, I believe, bend my steps in a dif- 
ferent direction; and before I can hope to see it, you 
will, if you go on as you have begun, be an Arcadian 
~ “4 of the Golden Age, and one may go to your 


. RESOLVES TO STAY AT ROSNEATH. 57 


parish instead of reading over again, my good friend 
Ovid’s picture of the times of that best of kings, old 
Saturn. Our Professor of Mineralogy, you know, some- 
times takes out his pupils to the hills and shores that 
they may examine with their own eyes what he has been 
talking of in his lectures. I, who have to talk about 
the virtues, should like very much to be able to do the 
same thing; and I really think there is some chance 
that Rosneath may be exactly such a place as a 
moralist with his pupils should visit peripatetically. 
Do not be alarmed therefore next year, if you see me 
invading your domain with my little army of sages.” 
The living of Rosneath had been promised by the 
Duke of Argyll toa Mr. M‘Kellar, who was tutor in 
the family of Mr. Campbell, of Skipness. This 
arrangement, about which the parishioners had origi- 
nally known little and cared less, became a matter of 
considerable importance to them, as Dr. Drummond’s 
health grew worse, and as their attachment to his 
assistant increased. They accordingly petitioned the 
Duke to alter the destination of the living, and to appoint 
Mr. Story the assistant and successor. Aftersome nego- 
tiation, the desired change was effected. Mr. Story felt, 
however, on his own part, some difficulty in sanctioning 
the measure so ardently desired by the people ; for the 
living of Ladykirk, which was already secured to him, 
was at once the more valuable of the two, and offered 
him a residence in a part of the country, where he would 
be nearer to his relatives and most of his friends, than 
he could be in Dunbartonshire. But he was at the 
same time reluctant to quit the sphere of duty on which 
he had so successfully entered, and to thwart the 
unanimous wish of the parish ; and he therefore deter- 
mined to accept the position offered to him at Rosneath, 


58 ORDINATION. 


and to receive ordination there ;* and though he would be 
thus bound more closely to his present abode, he still 
would be free to move to Ladykirk on the death of Mr. 
Tod. His own inclination, as well as the advice of all 
his friends, pointed to Ladykirk as his ultimate destina- 
tion; and Hume writes to him that, “ converting smug- 
glers is all very well,” but that he should not, to that 
sort of thing, sacrifice his future prospects and the pos- 
session of a valuable living. In the meantime, how- 
ever, he resolves to abide by Rosneath, and his ordina- 
tion was accordingly fixed for the 26th of March, 1818. 

That day found him overwhelmed with a crowd of 
conflicting emotions, predominant among which was 
the self-distrusting conviction of his unworthiness for 
the sacred office of the ministry. 

His feelings, as recorded by his own pen, would, to 
those who did not know him, appear to have been 
morbid and exaggerated. He thus writes of his mental 
struggles on the eventful morning :—“‘I knew not what 
todo. To proceed, I seemed to feel, exposed me to 
the vengeance of the insulted Husbandman—to retreat 
treasured up for me the scorn and contempt of the 
society I lived in. I then revolved the possibility of a 
precipitate flight to some district or country, where my 
name and profession could be easily concealed. But 
this again seemed a desperate alternative, as the very 


forlorn hope of a man in some sore extremity of doubt 


regarding his duty to God or to his fellow creatures. 
Then, again, I seemed shrouded in the thick darkness, 
and listening to those awful thunders whence trembling 
Israel heard the Divine voice, ‘Cursed be every one 


* He had already declined a proposal of Lord Dalhousie’s, who was 
now Lieutenant-Governor of Nova Scotia, that he should resume the 
direction of Lord Ramsay’s studies, and go out with him to Halifax. 


ORDINATION. 59 


that abideth not in all things written in the Book of 
the Law to do them.’ I wept bitterly, flung myself on 
the ground, and a prayer, vehement as was ever framed, 
burst from my lips.” 

The Presbytery is constituted, and proceeds to the 
Church. He continues :—‘ There is something glo- 
riously elevating in the simultaneous burst of solemn 
music: from the hearts of a multitude of worshippers, 
where a deficient harmony is lost in the intense zeal of 
emotion which it so powerfully expresses. At the 
moment the people burst forth with the voice of Psalms 
I was borne along with it, and without resistance I 
yielded to that overflowing flood of devotion. The feel- 
ings, personal to me or peculiar to my situation, were 
lost in the general sentiment; 1 became as one of the 
people—all relations forgotten, saving that in which 
we all stood to our common Father, the object of that 
solemn adoration. . . . While I stood before 
them all, professing my faith, and taking my vows, I 
was assailed by temptations in a more tangible and 
definite form than I had experienced for many years. 
Thou believest! Admirable sophistry—denial or 
belief of unintelligible mysteries is an equal honour to 
thy understanding, for they are not the objects of human 
thought at all. Or, let the mysteries pass, canst thou 
admit the monstrous paradoxes of Calvinistic necessity, 
so repugnant to the moral feeling of humanity, so 
inconsistent with the every conception ofa responsible 
agency ? Thou veriest slave to the errors and preju- 
dices of the age of thy birth, and not only so, but per- 
jured devotee to the dogmas of an illiberal and ignorant 
ancestry, pity but a glimpse of martyrdom would startle 
thy hardened front in the very face of Heaven, and 
before all this multitude, sacrificing thine own mental 


— eo ee 8 ee 


60 ORDINATION. 


integrity, and panting for the functions of an apostle of 
error and deceitfulness. ‘Thou givest to hell-torments. 
too, without a sigh, the myriads of unbaptized infants— 
of unillumined and reprobate heathens; cannot the 
loud shriek of their concentrated agony rouse thy 
apathy ? Down, thou bold and bloody assassin of the 
hopes of those vast multitudes that have wept by the 
tombs of their guiltless babes. Yes; such were the 
suggestions of those awful moments.” 

The presiding minister was the Rev. Duncan 
M‘Farlan, D.D., then minister of Drymen; after- 
wards the well-known Principal of the University, and 
minister of the Cathedral, of Glasgow. The solemn rite 
was administered, as it had been to Timothy of old, 
by the Apostolic “‘ laying on of the hands of the Presby- 
tery.” ‘‘ While their hands pressed on me I felt as if, 
by a tangible cohesion, I was the object of some pre- 
ternatural and mysterious influence. The presence of 
that Spirit that bloweth where he listeth, invisible, 
intangible, uncontrollable, like the winds of Heaven, 
seemed to assume a formal and substantial reality, 
which came in contact with my whole body and soul, 
searching to the very depths of my amazed, and 
elevated, and enraptured spirit. When the reverend 
father prayed that, as in ancient times, by the laying on 
of hands a power might be conveyed commensurate with 
the necessities of my holy functions, my mind went 
back to those first days of the Christian Church, when 
by the same simple but sublime ceremony, Matthias 
was selected and set apart from the family of converts to 
supply the vacant Apostleship. No parade of hierarchic 
ceremonial could have so deeply affected my soul, as 
the assemblage of Presbyters, so accordant with the 
primitive simplicity—so representative of the Eleven 


DR. CHALMERS. 61 


selecting a ecoadjutor m the glonous achievement of 
illuminating and reforming a world.” 

On the Sunday following, he was introduced to his 
charge by Dr. Chalmers. 

His acquaintance with Dr. Chalmers had begun 
since his coming to Rosneath, and had already npened 
into hearty mutual friendship. The Doctor had been 
much struck with his preaching, and it seems to 
have been contemplated that he should become the 
assistant minister of the Tron parish in Glasgow; 
this idea, however, never took definite shape, although 
it was desired by some of the Tron congregation, 
and would have been welcome to Mr. Story himself. 
During the years when Chalmers was overwhelming 
his audiences in Glasgow, with his rugged and resist- 
less eloquence, and was working out in his parish there 
those theories of parochial and civic economy, which 
are the least showy, but the most substantial founda- 
tions of his fame, a holiday at Rosneath was always a 
welcome break in the noisy and laborious round of his 
town life. On one of his visits, on which he was 
accompanied by Irving (at the time his assistant), Miss 
Helen Campbell, Mr. Robert’s sister, gave an enter- 
tainment in his honour, at her bower in the Clachan 
Glen. The Glen is a narrow wooded ravine that winds 
back into the hill lying behind the level shorelands of 
Rosneath ; the bower was a romantic retreat in its 
deepest and shadiest hollow, where the steep bank has 
drawn back a little from the brawling “ burn,” and left 
as much of smooth and gently sloping sward as might 
serve for the silvan revels of the court of Oberon and 
Titania. A large and merry party took breakfast in 
the bower; after which one of the gentlemen assumed 
the fiddle, and Mr, Irving performed the Highland 


62 TAMNAHARRA. 


Fling among the children, on the turf in front. 
“What shall we do now?” said Mr. Story, when the 
festivities of the morning were over. ‘ Oh,” answered 
Chalmers in his impetuous way, “come and let us 
abandon ourselves to miscellaneous impulses!” 

One of the miscellaneous impulses, on this or 
another occasion, was to climb to the top of Tamna- 
harra, the highest point in the parish, and 800 feet 
above the level of the sea. The panorama from 
the summit is magnificent. Away to the left swells - 
the broad bosom of the Firth of Clyde, with the 
' blue cone of Ailsa rising on the horizon, and nearer 
the towering crests of Arran. On the night stretch 
the billowy Argyllshire Highlands, hill beyond hill. 
Looking down, on the one side you see the Gairloch, 
lying between its wooded and cultivated banks; on the 
other, is the sublimest view of all—Loch Long, deep 
and dark, amid its impending mountains, which throw 
solemn shadows even in the brightest summer day 
upon the sombre waters. Right in front of Tamna- 
harra, one huge rocky giant standing out abruptly 
from the others, divides the channel of Loch Long 
from that of Loch Goil. Only the entrance to the 
“‘dark and stormy water” can be seen, guarded on the 
one hand by the mountain which hides the rest of it 
from sight, and on the other by the ruined Castle 
of Carrick, which stands weather-beaten and gloomy, 
on a strip of meadow at the base of the hills. 
The effect of the view is heightened by the imme- 
diate foreground—a bare hill-side, with one sullen 
tarn in a stony hollow; but the grand and impressive 
feature of it is the strength and majesty of the ever- 
lasting hills, whether grim white with winter snow, 

* purple with summer heather, or scowling amid 


“A SABBATH AMONG THE MOUNTAINS.” 63 


drifts of mist and rain, or sleeping in the cloudless 
sunshine. “ Never,” said the Doctor, after gazing for 
a while, “never did I see such specimens of the 
statuary of nature.” 

_ On another of the Doctor’s visits to Rosneath, 
he met at the Manse the Rev. Legh Richmond, 
and the Rev. James Gray; the one the well-known 
author of the “‘ Dairyman’s Daughter ;” the other one 
of the masters of the High School, Edinburgh, after- 
wards Rector of Belfast Academy, and finally one 
of the chaplains of the H.E.I-C., and tutor to the 
young King of Kutch. They spent the Sunday with 
Mr. Story; Dr. Chalmers took part of the service, 
and Mr. Richmond addressed the Sabbath school. 
The genial company and the glorious scenery kindled 
the poetic flame in Mr. Gray’s breast; and ina few 
months after the pleasant meeting at Rosneath, a 
poem issued from the Edinburgh press, bearing the 
title, “‘A Sabbath among the Mountains.” It de- 
scribed the Sunday he had spent at Rosneath, with 
no little warmth of feeling and gracefulness of diction ; 


it took the public taste, and in a short time reached 
its third edition. Here is the author’s portraiture of 


the minister : 


“The Pastor of that people was beloved 
In cottage and in hall, where’er he moved, 
The aged loved him as a favourite son, 
The young a brother—all a faithful one. 
Of manners simple and devoid of guile, 
Even infants brightened in his kindly smile ; 
Of active fancy, and of judgment clear, 
Of life unspotted, and of truth sincere ; 
Of heart by nature formed for all to feel, 
Of ardent spirit, Apostolic zeal, 
Aright the weary wayfarer to lead, 
To clothe the naked and the hungry feed, 
To mingle with the mourner balmy tears, 
To quench the Christian’s doubts and quell his fears

POSITION AMONG CHURCH PARTIES. 65 


most beautiful spot in the world.” Thus, surren- 
dering at what he deemed the call of duty, the richer 
benefice on Tweedside, he pitched his tent by the 
shores of the quiet Gairloch. He assumed, of course, 
all the functions of a parish minister, and took his 
seat in the presbytery of Dunbarton. He never at any 
period took much interest in the ordinary business of 
the Church courts, nor did he set store by the differ- 
ences of party which from time to time agitated them. 
The Church was to him the Father’s family, and the 
Redeemer’s witness on earth. He could not take part 
in proceedings which too often tended only to violate 
this relation, and to obscure this character. Perhaps 
he allowed himself to stand too much aloof when, by 
addressing himself to the ecclesiastical politics of his 
day, he might have done solid service to the Church. 
He did not find it easy, however, to attach himself 
positively to either of the parties which divided the 
ecclesiastical body when he joined it. In_ political 
questions, he would have been disposed to side with 
the ‘‘ Moderate” party. In his religious feelings and 
- practice he differed utterly from them, and was even 
more earnest and devoted than the generality of those 
who called themselves the ‘‘ Evangelical ;’ while he pos- 
sessed a catholic charity which they not seldom sadly 
lacked. Before the time of his ordination, Lands- 
borough had written to him as follows, and the words 
express pretty accurately the common opinion and 
feeling of his clerical friends: “I was amused to hear 
that you are claimed by both parties. You ask to 
which I belong. If you would head a party I would say 
that I belong to yours. I am moderate, as I think you 
are, in the true sense of the word. In most cases, I 


would from conscience vote with the Moderates, but I

itis first and severest struggle was with the smug- 
zlers, but by degrees he succeeded in eradicating their 
pernicious traffic. He preached, he remonstrated, he 
excommunicated, he brought all the moral artillery at 
his command to bear upon the stills, until at last they 
were overthrown. One would have expected that the 
private and illicit manufacture of whisky being thus 
extinguished, its public and legitimate sale and con- 
sumption would increase. But it was not so. He 
succeeded in checking not only the smuggling, but the 
drinking in the parish. And he did so, because he 
strove to gain his point not through mere restrictive 
measures, but by the diffusion of sound principles, 
and by elevating the moral standard of his people. 
And thus, though there was no such thing as a temper- 
ance society known in those days, the drinking usages 
gradually gave way; and of the six public-houses 
which existed and drove a flourishing trade in the 
parish when he came to it, only two remained, one at 
Rosneath Ferry, and the other at Kilcreggan. The 

¥2 


68 AMELIORATION. — 


repeated rounds of whisky at funerals dwindled down 
to one, at which minimum the untimely and unmean- 
ing libation remained. One old Highlandman made 
an effort to revive the abolished custom after it had 
been for some time in abeyance. ‘“ You know that has 
been given up, Duncan,” said Mr. Story, as Duncan 
was preparing to hand round the replenished glasses. 
“Surely,” replied Duncan, “ye wad na hinder me 
payin’ respec’ to the deid.” Mr. Story said he did not 
think the proposed measure would manifest that laud- 
able sentiment; he would return thanks for what they 
had got, and those who were inclined to drink might 
stay behind. All followed him from the house. 

New Year’s Day had been of old a day notorious 
for drinking and disorder, which generally accumulated 
around the great annual game of shinty played on that 
anniversary. The game used to be accompanied with 
banners, bagpipes, and drink ad libitum: the players 
(such of them at least as were sober enough and had 
not got their heads broken) adjourned from the field to a 
ball at the tavern, where they kept up the festivity till 
next morning. The game in itself was perfectly inno- 
cent, but its accompaniments, and these seemed insepa- 
rable from it, were decidedly bad. It was gradually 
“shorn of its beams,” and the observance of the great 
annual holiday came to be quiet and respectable; the 
church was opened for a short New Year’s service, and 
the congregation decently attended. 

Previous to Mr. Story's incumbency, the Sacrament 
of the Lord’s Supper had been dispensed only once a 
year. He introduced the half-yearly observance of it, 
and for some time before his death, it was dispensed 
thrice a year. He also introduced what was con- 
sidered the offensive innovation, of never leaving his 


a 


INTERCOURSE WITH HIS PEOPLE. 69 


own pulpit vacant on the occasion of the Communion 
being observed in the neighbouring parishes. Rather 
than do this, he entailed upon himself the fatigue of 
performing service at home on these occasions, and 
then travelling, often many rough miles, to assist in 
the services elsewhere. But though the change was 
thus irksome to himself and unpopular with his 
clerical brethren, he persevered in adhering to it; and 
had at last the satisfaction of seeing his practice 
adopted throughout the whole district, and the Com- 
munion Sundays no longer the scenes of noisy crowd 
and excitement which they once had been. 

These changes, when we look back to them and re- 
late them now, seem simple and unimportant matters. 
No one, however, who knows how deeply rooted old and 
evil customs always are, will believe they were easily 
effected; and none, contrasting the present state of the 
country in which they #ook place, with its condition 
before they were contemplated, will be disposed to 
underrate their value, or to think lightly of the manly 
energy and moral influence by which they were brought 
about. 

Mr. Story’s personal knowledge of his parishioners, 
of their circumstances, wants, capacities, characters, 
feelings, was most minute, his interest in them cordial 
and deep, his sympathy unbounded. 

Before the withering influence of schism disturbed 
in later years the harmony of the parish, it exhibited 
a spectacle of peace and union among its inhabitants, 
and happy confidence and good will between them 
and their fatherly benignant pastor, that recalled the 
golden visions of patriarchal times, and presented as 
fair an ideal of a parish and its priest, as any poet could 
have found in his day-dreams of a Christian Arcadia, 


70 “ WHEN DID YE TURN DOCTOR ?” 


They well might love and trust him; for, apart alto- 
gether from his efforts for their spiritual good, few could 
have so completely identified himself with their interests 
in every way, and made himself so thoroughly “all things 
to all men,’ as he. 

Some of the incidents of his parochial experience 
indicate this in a somewhat ludicrous light. 

For instance, one of his panshioners, an old woman, 
was dying of cancer, and was ordered to take lauda- 
num. This she would never consent to, unless the 
minister took a similar dose first—this he used to do, 
and then she submitted to take it herself-* Old 
Donald McNicoll, who was insane, was at one time 
under medical treatment, but refused all medicine, 
under the impression that it would poison him. To 
convince him of his mistake, Mr. Story volunteered to 
take one or two of the fatal pills, provided Donald 
would no longer reject the prescribed dose. To this 
paction the latter agreed; and Mr. Story swallowed his 
share of the pills in all good faith. Not so the patient; 
he kept the unsavoury morsels in his mouth until the 
taste began to tell upon his palate, then spitting them 
out, he sprang up in a great rage, and flourishing his 
stick over the minister’s head, roared, ‘“ Whan did 
ye turn a doctor? Ye’ll hae quat the preachin’ noo.’ t

FRAY IN THE VILLAGE. vl 


Late one Saturday nicht, a noise of wrangling and 
fighting was heard near the Manse gate. It was a 
clear moonlight micht, and the ground covered with 
snow. Mr. Story, who was sitting by the fire, attired 
in his dressing-gown and slippers, started up and went 
to the window. A crowd, and two men fighting, were 
distinctly visible in the bright moonlight. Out he 
rushed, the dressing-gown flying behind him hke John 
Gilpin’s cloak, and in a moment was in the thick of 
the fray, and attempting to seize a combatant with 
each hand. One he did succeed in collaring. Him 
he handed over to a bystander, to be kept in retentis, 
while he gave chase to the other, who had made off. 
It was a fruitless pursuit, however; he had effected 
his escape, and the whole company of assembled vil- 
lagers, two minutes before spectators of the heady fight, 
had vanished as quickly and utterly as did Homer's 
interposing deities of old upon the plains of Troy. 
When the minister returned to the scene of the battle, 
no one was on the ground, except the big man he had 
caught, and the little man who held him. The former, 
who was what Dr. Carlyle calls ‘“ somewhat disguised,” 
begged, with many elaborate bows, permission to put 
on his coat and to depart in peace, which accordingly 
he was allowed to do. Next day, before pronouncing 
the benediction, Mr. Story said that a very disgraceful 
scene had occurred last night at his gate, and that he 
desired that those who had taken part in it should come 


“Na, na! I’m for nane o’ yer dookin’.”” The same day his wife was 
complaining she did not know what to do about giving him a drink, 
for he always would bite a piece out of the dish to choke himeclf 
withal ; upon which a woman standing by remarked, with great appa- 
rent seriousness, “It’s needless for him to be spiling dishes trying to 
choke hissel. He micht jist gang doun there to Archie Turncr’s 
muckle bull, an’ it ’l] sune tear oot his thrapple for him,” 


72 | MORAL POLICE. 


to the Manse after service. ‘“ Do you really imagine 
they will come ?” one of his hearers asked, very incre- 
dulously. ‘“‘I am sure they will,” said he; “ but not 
till all the rest are out of sight.” When the coast was 
quite clear, accordingly, the captive of the previous 
evening and his unknown antagonist duly made their 
appearance. The result of his interview with them 
was, that they promised not to taste whisky for a 
twelvemonth, which promise, he had every reason to 
believe, they kept. Whisky was the remote cause of 
the conflict they had engaged in; but the immediate 
occasion of it was the one having remarked to the 
other, on their leaving the tavern, that the road he 
was taking was the longest way; upon which his 
companion made answer, “ An’ div ye think I wad 
gang thro’ your clatty Clachan Hole again?” a 
taunt which the Clachan man instantly pronounced a 
casus belli. Many years after, one of the two, who 
had in the meantime become a “ Free” Kirk man, 
when on his death-bed sent for Mr. Story, and 
thanked him for his efforts to arrest him in a course 
of life which he now looked back on with bitterness, 
though he had neglected the warning voice at the time. 
He was in the habit, if he saw a light in the village 
tavern, on his return late at night from his perambula- 
tions in the parish, of going in and dismissing the 
company. ‘No one in Rosneath,’ he used to say, 
“ drunk or sober, would injure me;” nor did any one 
ever attempt resistance. Whenever he entered, there 
was a universal scuffle at back door and window, for 
the privilege of being the first out of sight. 
“ot long after his only daughter’s birth a malignant 
‘oke out at one of the farms on Loch Longside. 
so fatal that no one would enter the infected 


CASE OF FEVER. 73 


house, where all the inmates had it in succession. At 
last there were none to wait on the dying, but those 
who were themselves just recovering from the disease. 
Mr. Story went across the hill to them daily: one day he 
returned, sadly worn out, and at three o’clock the next 
morning he was sent for by the father, who thought him- 
self dying. He set off across the hill, and when, after 
an hour’s hard walking, he reached the house, he found 
the two daughters, only half recovered from the fever, 
wailing over the corpse. He said to one of the sons, 
“Your father is gone away.” ‘The lad was raving, 
and answered ‘ Whaur?” The mother, who was in 
possession of her senses, said, “ Then I'll never hear 
his bonnie prayers ony mair, that he used to mak’ wi’ 
me in the morning afore I went oot to the kye.” Mr. 
Story dressed the body, and, with the help of a neigh- 
bour who consented to enter the house, laid it in the 
coffin. 

Among his papers the following was found, written 
before this visit to the house of Death :— 

‘“‘T have such peculiar feelings regarding my intended 
visit to , that I cannot resist the inclination I feel 
to record them, as they may possibly cast ight on the 
providence of God regardjng me and mine. The fever 
there is of a virulent kind. Bryce* advised me not to 
go, on account of my family; but J., the father of the 
diseased family, desires me to visit him, and his wife 
was prayed for to-day as in great distress. I have an 
impression that I am either to be seized myself, or to 
communicate it to others, or both. Yet all my maxims 
of ministerial duty conspire in the conclusion that it is 
my duty to go at all hazards. My wife, my daughter, 


* The Doctor. 


' 74 PERSONAL HISTORY. 


dearest Helen, and her child, I commit to God, and 
pray that I may so exercise faith in His promises of 
protection, that no evil may befall them or myself, acting 
according to what I believe, without any suspicion or 
doubt whatever, to be according to His holy will. Re- 
cording my feelings regarding my purpose, I hereby sub- 
scribe myself, the unworthy servant of the most High 
God, and follower of Him who went about doing good. 
—R. Story.” 

He did, on his return, feel all the symptoms of the in- 
cipient fever—sore throat, &c.; but these were checked by 
the prompt use of his favourite remedy, the vapour bath.* 

One might fill many a page with incidents illustrative 
of his intercourse with the members of his rustic flock, 
but these “ Annals of the Parish” would unduly pro- 
long the course of our narrative. What, let us rather 
ask, of his own personal history in those days that 
followed his ordination? At first, not much distinc- 
tively marked in any way. He seems to have felt the 
lack of literary intercourse in his immediate circle of 
neighbours, and thrown a good deal upon himself, 
to have yielded from time to time to his old gloom 
and despondency. His diaries bear many records of 
days and nights of mental pain, and doubt, and rest- 
lessness. So also do his letters :—‘‘ I have had griefs 
and sorrows of my own; and often you must have felt 
a kind of selfishness when overwhelmed by the strength 
of your own painful sensations. You cannot fly from 
them, and you become the very victim of a fearful

incubus. But, indeed, mine are often imaginary. They 
haunt me, however, hke an evil spirit, and cast a dim, 
disastrous light over the most favourable moments of 
my solitary existence.” Solitary! it is said bitterly, 
no doubt, for he had just been foiled in an attempt to 
exchange his solitude for the blessed duality of married 
life. His friend Cannan, by this time married, must 
needs write, advising him to turn Benedick too, and get 
a wife “to sit at the helm of his quick-sailing imagina- 
tion.” The counsel was not needed, apparently, for he 
had already fallen wonderfully in love, having come in 
his appointed season to the knowledge of the truth 
that 
“the love of All 
Is but a small thing to the love of One.” 

Of course he says he thought he had loved before, but 
of the “deep intensity of the emotion till now he had 
had no experience;” and so, being unsuccessful, he 
forthwith likens himself to “a solitary spirit that haunts 
some unholy grave till the birth of the Resurrection” 
—the old, old tale. 

One of his friends writes him a very sensible epistle 
on the subject; for his distresses seem to have been 
perfectly well known to them all:—‘“ Most happy or 
most miserable of men—or, what is more probable, 
something between the one and the other!—lI sus- 
pect you went to on a fool’s errand. ‘The 
lady was really much surprised; your acquaintance 
had been so short; as a friend, she should always 
esteem Mr. Story; but, &c., &c.’ Oh, curas hominum ! 
What foolery does being in love seem to me now. Only 
to think of the os sublime, nay, even the gray* hairs

of a man who possesses ‘discourse of reason, hum-

Really it is too absurd. But love is like the small-pox 
and the measles, we must all have it once in our lives; 
nay, 1t sometimes recurs in what the doctors call a 
mitigated form. Mind, if you marry a wife without 
money you are ruined for life; itis all stuff about ‘a 
treasure in herself,’ and so forth. You are not a 
money-making man, but you are sufficiently inclined 
to be an expensive one, and therefore you must get

read your letter yet, and never shall, because I cannot 
read it. I wish you would take a few lessons from 
your daft schoolmaster. If you continue to write 
such a detestable hand, I shall not know whether to 
congratulate or condole in my next.” 

The failure of his addresses to Miss was fol- 
lowed by another event, which to one with his keen 
sympathies and strong friendships, was the occasion of 
no small disappointment and regret. He had always 
lamented Pringle’s choice of literature as his profession, 
maintaining, with Sir Walter, that “‘it 1s a good enough 
staff, but a very bad crutch.” But Pringle was him- 
self so hopeful that he had inspired his friend with 
some of his own high anticipations of his success as a 
littérateur. These anticipations had been doomed to 
disappointment; and in February, 1820, after a fitful and 


nearly blind with fatigue, and next morning his hair was here and there 
streaked with gray. By the time he reached the meridian of life, his 
“locks, divinely spreading,” were “ white as snow in Salmon.” 


PRINGLE’S EMIGRATION. 74 


chequered career in Edinburgh, where his residence 
latterly was embittered by the results of his disputes 
with the publishers, whose rivalries gave piquancy to 
the coarse satire of the ‘“‘ Chaldee MS.,” poor Pringle 
emigrated to the Cape of Good Hope. Before his 
earliest friend quitted his fatherland, Mr. Story wrote 
to Mrs. Pringle, who was a somewhat morbid religionist, 
a farewell letter, from which the following is an ex- 
tract: “Iknow youthink I have but slender prospects 
of Salvation. I have calculated with great anxiety the 
probabilities of final happiness, and perhaps I have a 
far more humbling idea of myself and of my prospects 
than even your severity will allow; but it is a trans- 
action between my Maker and my soul, and silently do 
I ponder on the awful realities of eternal enjoyment 
and eternal suffering. Now it is the same with your 
excellent husband perhaps. You are anxious, you are 
unhappy, when the idea of his being deficient in religious 
principle or feeling agitates your mind, and you ob- 
trude the subject upon him when necessary cares may 
occupy his attention. New, my dear madam, this is 
not prudent; it may have an opposite effect, as I have 
seen often in other instances. It may present to his 
contemplation the loveliest and divinest of all senti- 
ments in somewhat a repulsive form, arrayed indeed 
with the anxieties of an affectionate heart, but nct or- 
namented with the delicacies of a conciliatory wisdom. 
We are now to be far from each other. But our 
thoughts will submit to no restraint save that of affec- 
tion, and they shall meet together often on the great 
deep. I feelin the departure of my early friend; I am 
ignorant whether there be anywhere in the world so 
much affectionate fidelity left. Never since we knew 
each other did a day or an hour of coldness interrupt 


78 SOCIAL TEMPTATIONS. 


the vehement interchange of friendly regard,* and the 
profoundest secrets of our spirits, without hesitation 
and without fear, were deposited in each other’s 
bosoms.” 

The want of stimulus in the society at and around 
Rosneath told unfavourably upon him. He was unfor- 
tunate also in being remote from all his relatives and 
intimate friends, and in meeting among the clergy of 
his district with but little ardent piety and zeal. In 
every company his social gifts and graces made him a 
welcome guest; and it is evident that when the first 
enthusiastic burst of his work in his parish was over, 
he allowed his time to be too much occupied with the 
amusements of a society to which he no doubt made a 
valuable addition, but from which he could in turn gain 
but little. He was too pliable, and had too strongly 
woven in his being the “one touch of nature” that 
makes the whole world kin, to be able to maintain that 
self isolation, which is not only expedient but necessary, 
if we would act up to our ideal among those whose 
moral and intellectual stand-point is lower than our own. 
His time, too, was constantly broken up by the advent 
of visitors coming to explore the beauties of Rosneath, 
-and who all flocked to the Manse to find within its 
ever open door a ready guide and hospitable enter- 
tainer. <A friendt who often visited him in these days, 
writes: “I look back to my intercourse with him 
during my sessions in Glasgow as an interesting and 
profitable time in my history. I well recollect how 
devoted he was to the interests of all in the parish, 


* It was a saying among his College friends that “Story was the 
firat to form a friendship and the last to break it.” 

+ The Rev. William Stobbs, a native of Yetholm, and now minister 
ofa U. P. congregation at Stromness. 


BACKSLIDING. 79 


but I could not help regretting the frequent interrup- 
tions to which he was subjected, by parties ever and 
anon calling on the minister to show them the glen, 
&c. While admiring his courtesy and gentlemanly . 
bearing to all who thus drew thoughtlessly upon his 
valuable time, I was grieved to think that his fine 
talents were not permitted to have more leisure for 
consecutive study.” His consciousness of his own 
lapse, which probably was fully realized by none but 
himself, though we learn it was in some sort per- 
ceptible to at least one other, is expressed in a letter 
to Morton, written in the end of December, 1820: 

“ I cannot say for my own part that I am doing any- 
thing, but writing regularly two sermons a week, which 
even still is hard labour. I had for three years ex-, 
temporized chiefly, framing a discourse only at distant 
intervals; but qualms of conscience some months ago 
seized upon me, and I now write with more care and 
labour than ever before. My conscience was wounded 
(not my heart) by a lady the most admirable and lovely 
of her kind (but married), who out of a great regard 
for me, assumed the task of reclaiming me from in- 
evitable ruin. I had yielded from time to time to the 
fascinations of society, society always of the polite and 
fashionable order, but rapidly tending to distract and 
enervate the mind. Although the duties of the parish 
were regularly performed, nothing neglected that had 
ever been done for its benefit, my heart was gradually 
wearing away from delight in them, and far too 
frequently was I seen quaffing the champagne or 
claret of the great and wealthy.* I felt myself be- 


* He alludes here chiefly, if not altogether, to the society which lay 
beyond the bounds of his own parish. We gather from his diaries 
that, at this time, he was always dashing about the country to one 


80 THE LADY’S LETTER. 


coming a voluptuary, in solitude restless, requiring the 
artificial stimulant of society to procure enjoyment, 
incapable or careless of study, selfish in my feelings, 
even seeming to forget my friends. I was, in fact, on 
the high road to intellectual and moral destruction, 
when an intrepid lady, perhaps the loveliest woman of 
all Clydesdale, not my own age, but the mother of 
lovely children, whom I have often kissed through 
love and admiration of the image they bore, interfered 
to deliver her friend; and she was as an angel staying 
the current of shame and dishonour. I first defended 
myself ina long letter of about twenty pages, an admir- 
able special pleading of one who was anxious for her 
esteem, yet conscious of not deserving it. This pro- 
duced a reply which humbled me to the dust, and 
produced a confession of ‘ guilty’ to all her charges. * * * 
O! how I loved her at that moment, with a pure 
Platonic devotion, for I did regard her as a messenger 
from on high. Her remonstrance had a miraculous 
effect. I changed my habits instantly, began writing 
sermons, arose in the morning at six, and felt distaste 
for the society I had so frequently gladly enjoyed. 
All this was about ten weeks ago, and I have been 
persevering tolerably, happier in myself and with all 
around me, than for many a year. I correspond often 
with her. She is withal a lady of the purest and most 
elevated devotion, such as St. John would have chosen 
to address another epistle to. She is my polar star, 
and often I sigh and ask if it be possible there be any 
other such in the world.” 

This is a touching and instructive confession : few 


point or another in the wide circle of his acquaintance. Rosneath was 
*-» quiet and contracted a sphere to afford full scope to his social 
ies. 


ITS EFFECTS. 81 


cotld or would have interfered as this “elect lady” 
did, and with the like felicitous result. The effect 
produced was not a spasmodic and transitory one. 
The tendency which she had detected was not only at 
once checked but permanently eradicated; while the 
feelings which on the one hand had earnestly prompted, 
and on the other had generously responded to her ' 
counsels, consolidated into a life-long friendship. 

Her words marked indeed a crisis in his life, which in 
this record of its course seems fitly to close a period. 

He had passed morally unscathed, though often 
stricken and bowed down with intellectual difficulties 
and spiritual darkness, through the dangerous age in 
which youth is free from the restraint of authority, and 
is not yet invested with the duties and responsibilities 
of an office or a profession. He had after many 
doubts and debatings, entered the Church of his fathers, 
and found within her pale a congenial and suitable 
sphere of labour. He had bent himself to that labour 
heartily ; but when the first keenness of interest and 
effort was over, he had found that his heart was still 
unsatisfied, still “a hungry heart” longing for old 
companionships which he could not regain, and then 
seeking a new love which was denied to him. And so 
emerged before him the great peril of his life, the 
possibility of his falling back from energy and earnest- 
ness and lofty aspiration towards “the high regions 
where the pure forms dwell,” into mere formal obser- 
vance of a routine of duty, and goodnatured acquiescence 
in the usages, and compliance with the standards of 
a Society, invested with no deep religious earnestness, 
nor possessed of shining moral or intellectual charac- 
teristics. Led by his own character and constitution to 
seek the substance and the gladness of his life without
