PART II. 
1827—1835. 


CHAPTER I. 


JOHN CHALMERS—ISABELLA CAMPBELL—ENGLAND 
—CHANGE OF VIEWS—PASTORAL ADDRESS— — 
RETURN. 


“THE greater part,” says good Sir Thomas Browne, 
“ must be content to be as though they had not been— 
to be found in the register of God—not in the’ 
record of man;” and among the many so destined to 
pass away into the Silent Land, few have been humbler 
_and less noticed in their brief day than John Chalmers. 
A poor man, and the son of a poor man, he lived a 
life of privation and suffering, and died and was 
buried; and for many a long year his dust has been 
mouldering away in his nameless grave in Rosneath 
Churchyard. But “not a worm is cloven in vain,” 
far less is any human life quenched in the great dark- 
ness until it has served some well-ordered end. This 
poor man’s life was—as far as we can trace the 
gequence—the first link in a chain of many lives 
whose record is on high. He was a man of singular 
faith, patience, and devotion, with no little insight into 
the mystery of godliness. His spiritual life does not 
seem to have been awakened by Mr. Story’s ministra- 
tions, but to have developed of itself into a fulness of 


86 JOHN CHALMERS. 


stature to which his minister had probably not 
attained. The latter describes himself as taught, 
rather than teaching, at the sick man’s bedside; and 
was wont often to revert to the remarkable expressions 
of faith, hope, and love which he had listened to there. 
One of Chalmers’s sentences he used to quote: “Every 
pang,” said he, in the midst of excessive agonies, “ that 
pierces through this corrupted body, gives me a new 
glimpse of the goodness of God.” In an address 
to his people, after Chalmers had been released from 
his sufferings,* Mr. Story wrote: “His image must 
remain with me so long as I retain the knowledge of 
my own being, or any memory of the past. His 
absence is a blank amid the objects which interest 
me, beyond expression, or what any can conceive; he 
had existed so long as a fixed and permanent anxiety 
in my thoughts, and in his society I had experienced 
many of the best and noblest enjoyments of my being. 
I have only now to regret—not that I can see him no 


more, for he is for ever removed from the alternations _— 


of human hopes and fears, the circle of human anxie 
ties and sorrows, but that while he was here I enjoyed 


* The manner of his death was strange. Two men came hurriedly 
at midnight to summon the minister to his cottage, two miles from 
the Manse. He went with them. All the way they were silent, pre- 
occupied, mysterious; could not, or would not, give any account of what 
was the matter. When Mr. Story entered the cottage he went near the 
bed and engaged in prayer; while he was praying the Devil seemed to 
enter into the prostrate sufferer. He began to yell out the most terrific 
blasphemies in the voice of a Stentor. His bodily strength returned, 
he swung his arms wildly about, and in so doing slapped Mr. Story 
across the face, and the blow, he said, was like that of a strong man in 
his prime. Under the influence of continued prayer he gradually 
calmed however, and by-and-bye the fit was over, and he lay weak as 
before, and peaceful asachild. Presently he breathed his last with 
the name of “‘ Jesus” on his lips. It seemed impossible not to recog- 
nize in this dreadful paroxysm, the Satanic agency entering into the 
worn-out body, and making one final rush upon the fainting spirit. 


JOHN CHALMERS. . 87 


so little of his society. And to you, O my people, he 
hath not gone away, without leaving what is far 
better than the wealth of the rich, the power of the 
mighty, or the glory of the noble; he hath bequeathed 
the blessing of a dying believer. One of the last 
times I parted from him, ‘ Now,’ said he, ‘ farewell. 
Although we should never meet again, I have offered up, 
and shall offer up, my prayers for you, and all your peo- 
ple, that the Gospel may flourish among young and old.’” 

But a year or two passed away ere the minister, 
whose growing earnestness made him readier now to 
detect the quickening of the hidden life in any of his 
well-known flock, believed he saw an answer to the 
many prayers of the departed saint. ‘‘ In Isabella 
Campbell,” he writes, “there seemed an answer to his 
prayers, the first fruits of the vehement wrestlings of 
his holy soul in his latter days, for his fellow parish- 
ioners before the throne of God.” 

To many, no doubt, the tracing of this connection 
between “the effectual fervent prayer of a righteous 
man,” and the development of the divine life in 
another’s spirit may appear a mere rope of sand; but— 

‘¢ More things are wrought by prayer 
Than this world dreams of . 


For so the whole round earth is every way 
Bound by gold chains about the feet of God.” 


—And behind the Veil, that agent, for aught we know, 
may be the most prevailing, which is least recked of in 
this selfish and sinful world. One of the strongest 
features in the life which we are tracing, was its own 
prayerfulness and reliance on the power of prayer. 
We need not wonder, then, that he traced a divine 
succession, linking the life of Isabella Campbell to 
that of poor John Chalmers. This young woman 


88 ISABELLA CAMPBELL, 


grew up under Mr. Story’s eye, was one of his Sunday 
school scholars, and was by him admitted to the Holy 
Communion, and after many a long and painful strug- 
gle, she was able in God’s light to see light, and to 
enter into the glorious liberty of his children. While 
yet a girl in her teens a pulmonary disease attacked 
her, and gradually reduced her so low that at last she 
was chained almost entirely to her home at Fernicarry. 
While there her minister saw her constantly, and while 
he helped to instruct and comfort her, his own spirit 
was much quickened by its intercourse with hers; for 
during this illness she seemed step by step to enter 
into the very Holy of Hohes, and to enjoy a most rapt 
and intense communion with her God and Saviour, to the 
peace and joy of which she bore perpetual witness by the 
exalted utterances of her faith and love. Her life was 
indeed hid with Christ in God ; its fountain was within 
the Veil; there, she felt as few are able to realize, were 
the realities, here the illusion. ‘* 1am not conscious,” 
says one who went, as many did for the truth’s sake, 
to visit her on her dying bed, “that I ever was made 
so to feel the reality of eternal things; their being had 
an possession. When I rode away from the house, I 
actually felt as if the firmament overhead, and your 
mountains and the lake at our feet, and the very ground 
over which we were yet sensibly passing, were all illu- 
sive, and as if I could have put up my hand to push 
them all aside, to make room again for the great reali- 
ties which I seemed to have left with that wonderful girl.” 

It is not, however, with the intention of enlarging 
upon Isabella’s character or history, that I now mention 
her name, but because it connects itself pretty closely, 
“or a considerable period, with the course of Mr .Story’s 

70 life. 


MR. STORY'S ILLNESS. 89 


The warm friendship and pious communion which 
united so closely the pastor and this member of his 
flock, were not suffered to continue uninterrupted. 
During the winter of 1826-7 he seriously injured his 
health by frequent exposure to wet and stormy weather 
during his visits throughout the parish, but especially 
to Fernicarry, which was six miles from the Manse. 
At last, one wild day, he crossed in an open boat from 
Greenock,* got drenched with spray, and went in his 
dripping clothes to marry a couple in-a barn. The 
- next Sunday he rose from bed, after bleeding and 
blistering, to perform service; by the time it was over, 
he could scarcely crawl home to the Manse, agonized 
with an onslaught of sciatica. Formonths he was the 
victim of this disorder; his general health could not 
but suffer, the more so that his mind and heart were at 
this time much perplexed and saddened by concerns of 
his own, which were full of trouble for him. At length 
it became absolutely necessary, both for body and 
mind, that he should try the healing and soothing in- 
~ fluence of change of climate and scene. So in the end of 
the summer of 1827, he went to England. While there, 
as we shall see, a great change passed upon him. When 
he returned to Rosneath, there was in him no longer 
the wavering, the restlessness, the dissatisfaction which 
had hitherto, more or less (though latterly, since the 
lady’s letter, in ever lessening ratio), clung to him, and 
oppressed him. He was “stablished, strengthened, 
settled ;” he had gained the truth, and the truth had 
made him free. 


* There were no regular steamers in those days. Whenever there 
was a day specially stormy, old Dr. Steele, of the West Church, used to 
Bay to Mrs. Steele, “ My: dear, look out and you'll be sure to see Mr. Story 
in the middle of the river.” He delighted in the excitement of the 
crossing when winds were high and waves white. 


90 MR. CAMPBELL OF ROW. 


“You gratify me,” writes the Rev. Dr. Scott, of 
Greenock, some time before he quitted Rosneath, “ by 
mentioning the minister of Row’s regard forme. I wish 
I deserved it as much asI covetit. I esteem him highly, 
and do indeed expect that He whois ‘working in him 
to will and to do of His good pleasure,’ will spare him 
to perform eminent service in His Church. I bear him 
affectionately on my heart when I pray for the young 
servants of God, nor think I am wanting in affectionate 
regard or prayer for the minister of Rosneath, especially 
that he may be settled.” 

The minister of Row here spoken of, and one or two 
others who were Mr. Story’s correspondents during his 
absence from his parish, require some mention here, as 
we shall owe much to his letters to them, and theirs to 
him. 

The minister of Row was the Rev. John McLeod 
Campbell, son of the Rev. Dr. Campbell, minister of 
Kilninver ; he had been ordained to his charge in the 
autumn of 1825. The acquaintance, which necessarily 
resulted from the fact that Mr. Story and he were the 
ministers of parishes adjoining each other, speedily 
developed into friendship: they had much in common, 
and were both young and hopeful workers in their 
Master’s vineyard. Their intercourse was regular and 
frequent, and sometimes they arranged that their meet- 
ing should be at the point where the two parishes met, 
and where stood the home of Isabella Campbell. “ To- 
day,” writes Mr. Story, after one of these meetings, 
‘“‘we met at the extremity of the parish, to see a dying 
saint, and we parted in comfort, and peace, and joy ; 
transitory feelings often, but to taste of them even for 
a moment is worth a whole eternity of other emotions.” 
During his absence from home, Mr, Campbell, as his 


MR. CAMPBELL OF ROW. 91 


nearest neighbour acted, to a certain extent, as Mr. 
Story’s substitute in performing those duties which 
required the offices of an ordained clergyman, and in 
attending to persons in sickness and affliction. In 
these circumstances their correspondence naturally was 
constant and ample; but it was much more than a 
mere chronicle of parochial matters on the one hand, 
or of the absent invalid’s anxieties and arrangements 
on the other. The nine months during which the 
latter was absent from his charge saw a great change, 
as has just been said, pass upon his views of truth; 
and they were also the period of a growing fulness and 
distinctness in those of Mr. Campbell, of which his 
letters are an invaluable record. 

One of the latest works in which the subject of these 
Memoirs was engaged, was the drawing up of a brief 
biography of another friend and correspondent, who 
will be mentioned anon, Mr. Alan Ker; and, in the 
course of that work (left incomplete), there occurs’ the 
following passage regarding the minister of Row, which 
may appropriately be introduced here: “ His prede- 
cessor was a man of superior gifts, a good scholar, and 
of more than the average accomplishment of the minis- 
ters of his day. He was, however, a cold intellectual 
preacher merely, rather inclined to repress anything in 
the form of enthusiasm of feeling, than attempt to stir 
up any great earnestness in the religious tone of the 
people. With few exceptions, indeed, it may be stated, 
that religion was at a low ebb throughout the families 
of the parish. Mr. Campbell having entered upon his 
parochial charge with a deep sense of the responsibility 
of his office, and with a single-minded desire to devote 
all his energies to the accomplishment of its duties, 
felt, upon surveying the condition of the parish, that 


92 MR. CAMPBELL OF ROW. 


every effort must be made to awaken an interest in the 
great things that belonged to his people’s peace, and to 
rouse them from a state of what seemed such spiritual 
carelessness and insensibility. He had been thoroughly 
educated, and was equal to any other of his age and 
standing in knowledge of the principles of the doctrine 
of Christ; at least, such as is attained from the dog- 
matic teachings of our theological faculty, and the 
works recommended in illustration of these prelections.* 
He was led to feel, however, that something more was 
necessary than acquaintance with what others had taught, 
to reach the hearts of the people; especially as it seemed 
that they were familiar with the phraseology employed 
in the systems of theology, which yet appeared to have 
no power whatever in exciting any personal interest in 
the truths they embodied. His resolution was formed 
promptly : he shut himself up in his study with his Bible 
and Concordance alone; and with weapons drawn from 
that armoury only, in the strength and by the counsel 
of his Master, determined to make war upon the enemy 
of truth, and rouse the immortal souls committed to his 
pastoral charge from their deep and perilous slumber. 
Week after week, so prepared for his public ministra- 
tions, it may be easily imagined what a freshness and 
vitality there must have been in his presentation of the 
truth to his audience, urged, as it was, with intense 
earnestness of manner. The contrast between this and 
what they had been accustomed to, even the most care- 


* In his “Memoirs of Dr. Chalmers” (1st ed., vol. iii., p. 246), Dr. 
Hanna rather flippantly describes Mr. Campbell as a “young minister of 
ardent piety, but of slender theological discrimination.” One does not 
know Dr. Hanna’s standard of theological acquirements; but if he 
measures Mr. Campbell by that current among Scotch theologians, he 
has no title whatever to call his “discrimination” (which, I presume, 
"* the result and application of his erudition) ‘“ slender,” 


MR. CAMPBELL'S PREACHING. 93 


less and indifferent could not but notice as something 
very remarkable, especially when they witnessed during 
the week, wherever their minister happened to be 
in their society, the same deep earnestness of spirit, 
the same yearning for their souls’ well-being. He 
chose such subjects for his pulpit ministrations as 
seemed to have for their object the awakening of just 
conceptions of sin, the necessity of being born again, 
of fleeing from the wrath to come, and finding refuge 
in the cross of Christ ; embracing such topics indeed as, 
in the first days of their ministry, the Apostles selected 
in order to turn men from the bondage of sin and 
Satan to the service of the living God. He taught 
that for this end, God himself was throughout the 
Scriptures revealed as longing with a father’s heart ; 
that for this He had given up His Son to die for them, 
and had exalted Him to His own right hand to give 
them repentance and remission of sin; in one word 
to bless every one of them by turning them away from 
their iniquities. Whatever subject he selected for the 
purpose of convincing them of sin, was followed by a 
declaration of the freeness of the Gospel, while he 
showed that the rejection of the blessings it conveyed 
arose from their own unbelieving scorn; that whoso- 
ever turned to Him who was the God of pardons, would 
be received at once, like the Prodigal Son of the parable; 
and that, according to the great principle of the New 
Covenant, his sins and his iniquities, in the day that he 
turned to righteousness, would be remembered no more. 
His ministrations at this time* were all designed to 
shut up those whom he addressed into the faith, leaving 

* The time of Mr. Story’s illness, 1827-8. The remark, though 


originally used with reference to that period, holds good of Mr. Camp- 
bell’s whole ministry. 


94 THE KERS. 


no alternative but receiving the message of the Gospel, 
and so being rescued from the certain destruction 
awaiting all who continue in their natural condition.” — 

Such was the ardent and well-equipped young apostle 
who was labouring on the other side of the quiet 
Gairloch, when Mr. Story left Rosneath—such was the 
character of his ministry, which, ere his friend returned, 
was to make the loch and its romantic shores notable 
in the annals of religious history. 

I have mentioned Alan Ker. The Kers were a 
Greenock family, to whom Mr. Story had been intro- 
duced by Landsborough on his first arrival in the 
West. Their door was ever open, and he always a fa- 
voured guest, and, sooth to say, a frequent one ; for the 
Greenock society of thosé days was very genial and 
attractive to the solitary priest across the river. 

His visits to the Kers seem to have been very merry 
and gay,—the pent-up vitality escaping then with a 
rush; for we find Mrs. Ker on one occasion, previous 
to the dispensation of the Holy Sacrament, telling him 
that she cannot at such a solemn season ask him to 
stay at their house, as she always feels in his presence 
too strongly disposed to indulge in a levity which would 
be reprehensible on the eve of the Communion. She 
was a large-hearted motherly woman, as clear and 
just in mind as she was warm and sincere in affection. 
Mr. Ker was a man of singular simplicity, mingled 
with considerable shrewdness, and a great deal of ster- 
ling worth. He was much respected in the town of 
Greenock, and his opinion regarded with no little de- 
“rence. Apropos of this, Mr. Story used to tell an anec- 

e of him which was very characteristic. A person 
1e to him in great perplexity. His daughter wanted 
narry a man whom he did not at all choose to have as’ 


. ALAN KER. 93 


his son-in-law. What was he to do, for the pair were 
quite dogged? Mr. Ker ascertained, by a few pointed 
queries, that their fixed determination had been most 
unequivocally announced,—they would marry,—and, 
moreover, that the young man, though distasteful to the 
“leathery Herr Papa,” was really a respectable and 
well-doing personage. “ Well, then,” said he, giving 
his deliverance after a full hearing of the case, “ Just 
let them marry, or they'll do’t in spite of ye, for if 
you put her in a barrel, he’ll kiss her thro’ the bung.” 
“Sic vetuere patres quod non potuere vetare.” In 
the days of the old volunteers, Mr. Ker commanded a 
company, which he duly drilled and paraded, but his 
recruits were but an awkward squad ; they never would 
draw up in a straight line, do what he might. “Oh!” 
he cried one day, holding up his hands in horror as he 
looked along the front rank, ‘“‘Oh! what a bent row; 
just come out, lads, and look at it yourselves!”* Alan 
Ker was a cripple and an invalid; but his mental 
powers were of the finest order. Though incapacitated 
by ill health from pursuing any regular course of study, 
or attending the University, he acquired at his own 
hand a varied and most liberal education. He was an 
excellent classical scholar, (many are the notes and 
squibs in Latin prose and verse, which he used to 
indite to his friend at Rosneath), read French and 
Italian, was a scientific musician, and had a wide 
acquaintance with the facts and theories of physical 
science, not nearly so generally studied then as now. 


* A body of Greenock volunteers was reviewed in these days by the 
then Duke of Montrose. His Grace complimented their commanding 
officer very highly, saying he was quite surprised at the steadiness of 
his men. “Indeed, my Lord Duke,” replied the worthy Major, 
‘I’m very much surprised at it mysel’, for I don’t think half a dizzen 
o’ them ever saw a Duke afore.” 


96 ALAN KER. 


His chief delight in life, however, was in going about 
(on his crutches) doing good, while his health allowed 
him to do so. He was Secretary to the Sabbath School 
Society, and laboured indefatigably to reclaim the 
* Arabs of the streets;’’ and the Arabs of “the Sten- 
nars” and “the Vennel” were more thoroughly Ish- 
maelite in their tendencies, than teachers and mis- 
sionaries now-a-days in well superintended districts 
would readily believe. With all this active well-doing, 
and the earnest piety and Christian love which ani- 
mated it all, he combined a disposition brimming over 
with fun and humour. ‘‘ While friend and favourite 
were one by one being withdrawn from me,” says one 
of the most useful, and since these days, most widely 
known among his coadjutors, ‘““he was ready to receive 
me with all his multifarious attractions, his array of 
ancient and modern literature for discussion, his prac- 
tical knowledge of the physical sciences for infor- 
mation, his shrewd and comprehensive views on politics 
and philanthropy, his fresh and original landscapes of 
the journey of human life, his extraordinary appre- 
hension of character, his mimicry and gaiety, his 
boundless, active, buoyant, happy benevolence, and 
progressive holiness and growth in grace, that kept me 
in a perpetual astonishment. And the frequent and 
sudden flashes of wit, and peals of laughter, that some- 
times came from a bed some minutes before shaken 
with an agony of pain, lent an unheard-of charm and 
fascination to his sick chamber.” His humorous 
bent in his earlier days sometimes displayed itself in 
a practical shape; and he and his friend Motherwell, 
the poet, liked nothing better than concocting a “ trot” 
as they called it. The origin of this term was Alan’s 
having published in the Greenock papers an elaborate 


ALAN KER. 97 


description of the “ Trottle Plant,” a member of the 
British Flora, totally unknown to botanists: the de- 
scription was copied into the contemporary journals, 
and gave rise to much speculation; when some ac- 
quaintances of Alan’s, who discovered the parentage 
of the vegetable, inserted an advertisement in the 
newspapers, to the effect that he was the sole agent 
for it. Immediately orders for specimens came in by 
the dozen, and at last a cheque arrived from the Duke 
of Devonshire’s gardener, with a request that Trottle 
Plants for the amount might be forwarded to Chats- 
worth. It was by this time necessary to undeceive 
the public, and to acknowledge that the tables were 
fairly turned on the original plotter; and a trot became 
synonymous in his vocabulary with a practical joke. 
Many, no doubt, would be inclined to question the 
stability and depth of religious principle and feeling, 
which could coexist with such real and hearty enjoy- 
ment as he had in any joke, or whim, or ludicrous 
escapade; but the living, devoted piety of the man was 
as sincere as his delight in wit and laughter, and what, 
perhaps, some staid people would have called “tom- 
foolery,” was genuine. His nature was too free and 
pure, his heart too wide in its sympathy with many- 
sided humanity, to be able to detect any incongruity in 
the keen sense and ample fund of humour, and light- 
hearted enjoyment of life, despite all its sufferings, 
which played around the deeper, stronger, sterner 
citadel of his faith and devotion, and self-sacrifice. 

His intercourse with Alan Ker had been of great use 
to Mr. Story. He was drawn to him by his singularly 
winning and attractive character; while his inner life 
could not but be quickened by the contemplation of 


Ker’s earnest and unaffected godliness, which was not 
H 


98 QUESTION OF * ASSURANCE.” 


“of his life a thing apart,” but pervaded and harmo- 
nized his whole being. His strong intellectual powers, 
too, guided by his deep spiritual insight and experience, 
enabled him to clear up many a misty point in the 
horizon of truth, which, to Story’s eager, but less 
piercing gaze, seemed lost in Cloud-land. Especially, 
one disposed too often to take the shadowed, and not 
the sunlit view of life, found in Ker a perpetual and 
unshaken witness to the heartfelt peace and the strength 
to suffer, found in believing on God as revealed in Jesus 
Christ. This, too, he beheld no less beautifully and 
constantly shown forth in Isabella Campbell; though 
in her the influence of the example of heroic endurance 
and unswerving faith was not enforced by such manifold 
power of character, and by the spectacle of sufferings 
so keen and bitter as those of Alan Ker. 

Lhe theological subject which had for some time pre- 
vious to his leaving home chiefly occupied Mr. Story’s 
mind, was that of “ Assurance.” His attention seems to 
have been directed to this by the preaching of Dr. Malan 
(of Geneva), whom he had met during the latter's visit 
to Scotland. The subject had presented many diffi- 
culties to him. The popular teaching regarding it was 
of such a nature, as to lead the inquirer to look for the 
ground of his assurance in himself rather than in God— 
to examine into his own heart, and, from the feelings 
and convictions he found there, to decide whether or 
not he had a right to this assurance. It made, in short, 
the ground of it subjective—not objective. This, the 
commonly-received view of the matter, Mr. Story was 
anxious to maintain. He accordingly plunged with his 
wonted rapid energy into the sea of the theological 
literature circling around this point. He read assid- 

usly all that bore upon it in the Fathers—in the 


“ ASSURANCE.” 99 


writings of the Reformers, Continental and English, 
with which his acquaintance was intimate and exten- 
sive; and from his reading he made copious extracts, 
apparently with some (subsequently abandoned) in- 
tention of writing a treatise on the subject. But all 
that he found failed to satisfy his mind: he was 
unable to rest in the popular teaching; nor does he 
seem to have got a firm foothold elsewhere till after 
much searching. The final solution of his difficulties 
and enlightening of his belief came mainly from his 
intercourse with Alan Ker and Dr. Gordon.* To the 
latter Alan had himself owed much of his clear appre- 
hension of Divine truth; and from his devout, wise, and 
liberal views of the true meaning and scope of the Glad 
Tidings, Mr. Story also gathered not a little. Thus, 
by their aid, and by his own reflection, he was brought 
to the distinct understanding of the nature of Assur- 
ance—as being man’s conviction of the truth of God’s 
testimony concerning God, which brings with it the 
certainty of his salvation (if he believe), not because of 
what he is, but because of what God is.t ‘This ques- 
tion had not, at this time connected itself in his mind 
with that of the Atonement, to the importance of which 
he was awakened during this visit to England. 

There was still another correspondent, Mary Dunlop, 
one of the daughters of Mr. Dunlop, of Keppoch. She 
was endowed with many rare and noble qualities of head

100 GOES TO ENGLAND. 


and heart, and seldom did a purer and saintlier spirit 
worship in a fairer shrine. Mr. Story’s relations with 
Mr. Dunlop’s family were such as fully to account for 
his unreserved and affectionate correspondence with 
this member of it; and few of those then witnessing 
and rejoicing in the light which began to brighten in 
the West, could have written to him such interesting 
details of its progress and influence as she, or with 
as clear an intelligence and quick a sympathy as hers, 
could have entered into his thoughts, feelings, and 
anxieties. 

On quitting Rosneath, Mr. Story went to Liverpool, 
where he placed himself under the care of his friend, 
Dr. Macrorie, from whose medical skill he derived much 
benefit; so that, ere long, he reports himself to his 
friends at Rosneath as able to “walk without halting, 
and ride* without any uneasiness.” 

He writes to Mr. Robert Campbell :—“ T’other day I 
rode so far as the seat of the Earl of Derby, and returned 
by Earl Sefton’s—both exceedingly interesting and beau- 
tiful. Dr. Macrorie and a clergyman were with me, 
and in our circuit we must have travelled about twenty 
miles. The Derby family are at present in the country ; 
and before we left this we were aware of the rigid rule 
they lay down of admitting nobody to see the interior 
of the Hall when they are at home. I had heard so 
much, however, of some of the paintings, that I pro- 
posed that we should make the attempt; and although 
there was no admission for Liverpool people, I was a 
visitor and a stranger, and by every principle of polite-

KNOWSLEY—BUXTON. 101 


ness I thought they could not refuse us. I rang, and 
asked the servant if we could be admitted to see the 
paintings. He shook his head, and said, owing to the 
populousness of the neighbourhood and nearness to 
Liverpool, all were excluded during the residence of 
the family in the country; ‘but your name, Sir?’ I 
gave it, saying that I was a clergyman from Dunbarton- 
shire, and these were my two friends. An answer was 
delivered from her ladyship, that if we would wait for 
a little in the library, everything would be ready for 
our inspection. We went accordingly through the 
apartments, all studded with paintings by the great 
masters ; and when we came to the dining-room, which 
was the last we entered, we found an elegant collation, 
with varieties of fruits and wines, &c., which our atten- 
dant said it was the particular request of the Countess 
that we should partake of. We, of course, availed 
ourselves of her noble hospitality, and resolved that we 
should not fail to make mention of it to her praise.” 
From Liverpool he went to Buxton. Here he 
found a sphere of usefulness which he had not ex- 
pected. At first he performed service on the Sunday 
evening, merely for the benefit of those inmates of the 
boarding establishment where he resided, who chose to 
attend ; but finding a deep and growing interest taken 
in his expositions of scripture, he made arrangements 
for having service daily in the morning and evening. 
The room was generally crowded; all listened with 
fixed attention. Sometimes not a few were moved to 
tears by his earnest and powerful addresses, and he 
had the joy of knowing that these morning and evening 
meetings were the means of opening the eyes of some 
of those who heard him to the Light of the World. 
After leaving Buxton, where he derived considerable 


102 ALBURY. 


benefit from the waters, he proceeded leisurely to 
London, where he visited Mr. Angas,* and then Mr. 
Irving. With the latter he went to Albury Park, to 
attend the Prophetical Conference held under the aus- 
pices of Mr. Henry Drummond, “who,” said Irving, 
in the dedication of one of his works, “hath taken us 
poor despised interpreters of prophecy under your wing, 
and made the halls of your house like unto the ancient 
schools of the prophets.” 

It may be questioned if, in so doing, Mr. Drummond 
performed any real service for the cause of religion 
in his day and generation. The speculations and in- 
quiries which this conference greatly encouraged, and 
in part originated, do not seem to have produced a 
very salutary effect on the religious thought of Eng- 
land and Scotland, and have too often run into most 
foolish and profane extravagance. In the case of 
Mr. Irving, few who knew his power as a preacher 
of righteousness and as a witness for his absent Lord, 
during the earlier years of his incumbency in London, 
will deny that he lost much of all that was noblest in 
his ministry, when he came to devote his time and 
gifts chiefly to the unravelling of the intricate web of 
Biblical prophecy. He, and others, seem to have 
altogether lost sight of the fact that the prophet of old 
was not necessarily a foreteller, but always an out- 
speaker, a revealer of the mind of God; and that 
much that modern interpreters set themselves to 
elucidate as predictions, may have been intended 
merely as symbolic representations or illustrations 
of hidden truths, showing forth the great laws of God’s 

* George Fife Angas, Esq., one of the chief promoters of a very 
useful institution, “The British and Foreign Sailors’ Society,” whose 


object is to afford religious instruction to sailors in British and Foreign 
ports. 


PROPHETICAL CONFERENCE. 103 


government asserting themselves everywhere and 
always, and not pointing to special times and seasons. 
Mr. Story speaks of these twenty-four Albury sages as 
endeavouring “ to ascertain the mind of God regarding 
the things that shall shortly come to pass.” This 
obviously begs the whole question. What are the 
things that were shortly to come to pass in 1827, and 
how did these investigators know these things were 
shortly to come to pass? Probably no two answers 
precisely similar would have been obtained from the 
Albury Conclave ; and in any answer one may be certain 
either prophecy would be twisted to meet fact, or fact 
mutilated to fit prophecy. One instance of this im- 
pressed Mr. Story vividly, and no doubt tended to lessen 
his sympathy with the “school of the prophets.” At 
a sitting of the conference, the news was announced 
of the death of the King of Rome. ‘“ That can’t be 
true,” cried one, springing from his seat, “that would 
overturn this whole interpretation.” 

Mr. Story’s thoughts about this singular meeting 
may be ascertained from the following extract from a 
letter to Alan Ker, in which, after speaking of Albury, 
he goes on to the subject of his own widening and 
consolidating views: “ The meeting was numerous, 
consisting of a great variety of talent and accomplish- 
ment, and, upon the whole, of singular unity of spirit 
and feeling. Its prominent character was at once 
devotional and rational. Freedom of judgment, ear- 
nestness of inquiry, willingness of persuasion, en- 
thusiasm without rashness, zeal without intolerance, 
speculation without levity, prediction without bold 
presumption, unravelling without the destroying of 
mystery, examination without irreverence of the Divine 
decrees,—are so many attributes expressive of the 


104 PROPHETICAL CONFERENCE. 


proceedings of those memorable days. I saw and 
heard so much, that I cannot rest till fairly im- 
mersed in the study. Nor do I see how we can 
explain Scripture in many places, without a closer 
consideration of its prophetical character. You are 
- aware that Irving and Maclean are regularly preach- 
ing of the Advent as at hand, and preparing the 
minds of their people for the coming judgment. I 
myself as yet consider of still greater importance the 
settlement of the previous question, whether their 
people have admitted in reality the fact of Christ’s 
first coming to save them from their sins. O! yes, my 
friend, unless they bear about with them his dying, 
they cannot love his glorious appearing; can feel no 
interest whatever in the anticipation of those wonderful 
things that are, according to the Adventites, shortly to 
come to pass. Alan! Alan! I confess to you that my 
own life has been spent in delusion concerning the 
foundation. I am, therefore, distrustful; while I do not 
approve of their exclusive way of preaching the advent. 
Yet I feel confident that if once roused to ascertain 
their relationship to God by the reception of his 
testimony concerning Christ crucified and risen, they 
will not be permitted to hold any essential error 
regarding his return for the gathering of his people. 
I regret that my weakness forbids the attempt to 
preach in London, otherwise I long much to present 
the truth fully before the Scotch brethren and people. 
It is too simple, too comprehensive, too sublime in its 
simplicity to harmonize with the tortuous perplexities 
of mere discussionary and technical theologians. It 
is called the ‘new doctrine,’ and being found so simple, 
having nothing of the wonderfulness of a discovery or 
an invention about it (since in truth the primitive sim- 


WIDENING VIEWS. 105 


plicity of the faith once delivered to the saints), it 
excites the half scornful observation, ‘What meaneth 
it?’ ‘There is nothing in it but what we know. Is 
this all? the babe can understand this; but faith is a 
mystery and a gift of God; and the great truth is 
forgotten that the advancement of the divine life is not 
measured by the knowledge of mysteries, but by the 
intenseness of love; not by deeming that we are wise 
like the ancients, but ignorant as a very babe.” 

The truth, upon the possession of which he was now 
entering, and which he valued far above anything that 
the Albury Congress could teach him, was that to which 
he was afterwards called to bear witness before his 
brethren in the Scottish Church—what he held to be 
the truth of the Atonement. The “doctrine of evi- 
dences” which he had discarded, was that set forth 
in the popular statements regarding assurance, which 
sent men to seek in themselves for the proof of their 
being in a “ gracious state,” instead of simply accept- 
ing God’s word that whosoever believes is saved.* 
From this system, and its torturing self inspection, he 
had now set himself free, and he was advancing 
steadily towards the full apprehension of what appeared 
to him to be the goal of all inquiry—the fact of 
Christ’s death being a death in which all men are - 
alike interested, and whereby all have received the 
name of the sons of God. And this connected itself 
with his previous line of thought; for this he came to 


* The preaching of this search for “ Evidences” was most perplexing. 
Thus one minister who used sometimes to have a sixteenth section in a 
discourse on Assurance would state that, if you loved God, you might 
be sure you were in “a gracious state ;” and if you did not love Him, 
still if you loved his people, you were safe: or, again, if you did not 
love his people, but regretted that you did not love them, and so on, 
and 80 on, ad libitum. 


106 WIDENING VIEWS. 


see was the only true foundation of all assurance. ‘The 
individual could feel assured of his own salvation only 
through his belief in the universal offer and promise 
of it. 

To this truth, in all its manifold relations, he made 
his way, as far as we can trace it, alone. Although in 
constant correspondence with Mr. Campbell, he does 
not seem to have been led to this by him, nor in his 
turn did he lead Mr. Campbell. Their mutual influ- 
ence was that chiefly of an ever deepening and 
strengthening earnestness and piety; and both beheld 
the truth which they valued as their life, breaking upon 
them ever more and more clearly in the growing light 
of an intenser communion with their Lord, and a more 
earnest study of His written word. Nor were his views 
of the Atonement affected in any way by his intercourse 
with Mr. Irving, whose opinions on the subject did not at 
this time coincide with his, and whose attention was prin- 
cipally directed to branches of speculation quite apart 
from those which were to him most interesting. Two 
books, besides his Greek Testament, are named as hay- 
ing been about this period much in his thoughts : “ Dor- 
ney’s Contemplations ” and ‘ Gambold’s Works.” To 
the latter Mr. Erskine * had written an Essay which had 
attracted Mr. Story’s attention to the works (chiefly 
poems) themselves. Neither of these books can be called 
dogmatic; but a profoundly devout and loving spirit 
breathes through each—a spirit of childlike faith and 
devotion to the Divine Father and Son. In his copy of 
Gambold, one can trace in the passages, his pencil has 
marked, how his heart rejoiced to find in the author's 


* Thomas Erskine, Esq., of Linlathen, Advocate, Author of the 
‘‘ Internal Evidence of Revealed Religion,” “ Freeness of the Gospel,” 
&c., &e. 


WIDENING VIEWS. 107 


language an indirect expression for its own love, and 
trust, and peace. And this helps us to discover 
clearly enough the impulse which drove him from his 
old theological moorings, where he had cast anchor 
at the first just because others did so—not because 
he had proved all things for himself. That impulse 
was the growing reflection in his own heart of the 
great central heart of love. The love that was in 
himself was the power that enabled him to appre- 
hend the love of God to all, not to “an elect world,” 
which was the garbled phrase of evangelical divines 
in those days, but to the whole world lying in wicked- 
ness. “ Quicquid recipitur ad modum recipientis, recipi- 
tur ;” his nature was formed to receive the full reve- 
lation of the Divine love, nor could one so loving 
rest in any conception of the Almighty’s character, 
which denied his possession of a love infinite and — 
almighty. 

It is best that his thoughts and feelings should be 
portrayed in his own language. He writes in December 
to Mr. Robert Campbell :— 

“‘T am more and more persuaded that there is only 
one way of preaching that is effectual unto life and 
salvation, and that for a great period of my own 
ministry I have but little wielded it ; indeed I feel that 
one of my first labours when I return, must be to com- 
mit to the flames every line of what I have written 
upon most fundamental points of the Christian faith. 
I daresay you are a little amazed at this, considering I 
was wild enough already; but be this as it may, it is a 
fearful thing to resist the light, and I feel I should do so, 
did I not considerably modify some opinions I formerly 
held.” 

“‘T am really wearying to tell my good people how in 


108 LETTER TO MR. R. CAMPBELL. 


many respects, it appears to me now, that I have with- 
held from them not a little of the counsel of God. 
But what a responsibility there is upon poor ministers! 
and what a woe there is upon them if they preach not 
the Gospel. Alas, for the poor people in the hands of 
many of us! Instead of a message of heart-stirring 
joy and gladness, a principle of life and peace, of holy - 
and blessed activity in all heavenly pursuits, it is made 
an embassage of perplexity, of negotiation, of disputation; 
it either alarms falsely; or it stupifies and relaxes the 
whole soul, blunts its sensibilities into perfect apathy, or 
whets them into a feverish acuteness that converts even 
declarations of love into sounds of wrath and terror. 
How simple in reality it is and full of Divine benignity ! 
and what a strange evil it is under the sun that any 
refuse to rejoice in it, or doubt that it contains in 
it to all who will, the most blessed assurance of an 
interest in the complete salvation of its all-sufficient 
Saviour. 

O! may you and I realize this great blessing, not 
transiently, or at intervals, but for ever.” 

The good friend to whom the above was addressed, 
did not at all discover that necessity for altered teaching 
and augmented pastoral diligence, which was only too 
obvious to Mr. Story. In his opinion, the minister 
was already far more active than there was any call 
for; and it seemed to him the height of folly and fana- 
ticism that an invalid should forget his ailments so 
completely, as to travel about in winter to attend Con- 
gresses at Albury, or to visit Mr. Irving in London. 
To Mr. Robert’s remonstrances he replies in a sub- 
sequent letter. ‘You seem to think that my recovery 
“ag been retarded by plunging into the haunts of 

fics and enthusiasts, and thereby exerting myself 


LETTER TO MR. R. CAMPBELL. 109 


beyond my power. It is not, however, so. I did 
attend, as you know, the Albury Conference in No- 
vember, but change of air and other inducements com- 
bined to render it of no inconsiderable importance that 
I should be there. Perhaps I did at that time suffer 
a little, but I am persuaded certain compensations far 
more than overbalanced the temporary inconvenience. 
Since, I have been living in the greatest retirement. 
So severe I am in my rules, that even Irving (whom, 
of course, you consider as the prince of enthusiasts) 
and I have seen each other only once since the middle 
of December. Peatoun sees me; but surely, my dear 
friend, you cannot, in good earnest, recommend a game 
of chess as in any way to be thought of, if we could, 
to each other’s edification, converse on the things of 
eternity. The great evil is, that immortal beings who 
must all stand before the judgment seat of Christ, will 
resort to any occupation, and any amusement, rather 
than deeply and seriously consider what preparation 
ought to be made for that fatal trial, upon which depend 
everlasting and irrevocable issues. I look back upon 
the past, and it seems to me that in what most would 
call, and what I myself thought pastoral fidelity, I dis- 
cover very little indeed of that apostolical devotedness 
which, whosoever does not imitate, is chargeable with 
the guilt of neglecting the souls of his people. I re- 
member hearing you express yourself in some such 
way as this,—that the Duke of Argyll had a right to 
whatever service of body or mind you could render; 
and truly no one was ever served with more zealous 
fidelity. But, surely, if all agree it was right in you 
so to feel towards an earthly creature, a fellow worm, 
how much more ought ordained ministers, with vows 
upon their souls, so to feel towards a heavenly Master. 


110 PAUL'S CRAY. 


It has always seemed a great mystery to me how that 
you, so diligent a reader of your Bible—so familiarly 
acquainted with the zeal and activity of the prophets 
and apostles—so lofty and pure in your ideas of de- 
votedness to the interests of an earthly employer, yet 
should always incline to denounce, as fanaticism in 
ministers of religion, anything like a more earnest 
anxiety to win souls to their Lord. Now, with regard 
to our friend of Row, what is hiscrime? Merely 
that he pours his whole soul into the service of his 
Master. He was never a pupil of mine, nor I of his. 
I know not whether our opinions agree. If they do, it 
is so because each, independently, and far from the 
other, has reached the same conclusion, because equally 
earnest in the pursuit of the truth. From what I hear, 
the clergy have raised a most intemperate clamour 
against him. Jam prepared to show that Calvin and 
Luther, with all the greatest names of Protestantism, 
held similar views,—that he teaches no new, but revives 
an old commandment.” 

This letter seems to have been written in January, 
from Paul’s Cray, in Kent, where the writer was on a 
visit to the Rev. John Simons, a well-known clergyman 
of the English Church, and a leader of the “ Evan- 
gelical” party, when that name was less known, and 
less popular than it is now. He had been introduced 
at Paul’s Cray by Mrs. Wales,* a friend of Mr. Simons, 
and a woman of uncommon mental power, with whom 
he had become acquainted at Mr. Whitlaw’s establish- 
ment at Bayswater. After a course of vapour-bathing 
there, from which, along with Mr. Whitlaw’s general 
treatment, he derived considerable advantage, he went 


* For an extract from a letter to Mrs. Wales, see Appendix II. 


MR. SIMONS. 111 


to Paul’s Cray. On his arrival, he found Mr. Simons 
apparently, as he believed himself really to be, at the 
point of death. The family was gathered round his 
bed to receive his final blessing, and he bade Mr. Story 
kneel down, that he might put his hands on his head, 
like Jacob, and invoke a benediction on him likewise. 
The venerable old gentleman, however, rallied after 
this ; by-and-bye he was able to move about, and Mr. 
Story’s visit was prolonged for some time. 

There was much about Mr. Simons that was, apart 
from his sincerely religious character and influence as 
an evangelical leader, attractive and interesting to a 
stranger,—often much that was amusing too. 

One of his strongest dogmas was an expansion of 
St. Paul’s “in the flesh dwelleth no good thing ;” and 
he laid with a naive frankness, all his little frailties and 
the ebullitions of a temper as warm as his heart, to 
the charge of the ill-disposed flesh. On one occasion 
he became much irritated at table about some joint 
that was underdone. He ordered up the cook, and 
then and there administered a vehement reproof, 
asking her if she was not ashamed to abuse God’s 
gifts as she had abused that piece of meat. Then 
when he had thus relieved his mind, he looked at his 
guests and shook his head sadly, “‘ Ah, gentlemen, it 
is the flesh, not I—the flesh, not I.” * 

He had conceived an affection for Scotch porridge, 
or at least for a viand he called by that honoured name. 
“ That's not what we would call porridge in Scotland,” 
said Mr. Story, the first morning the accustomed mess 
was introduced. “ Not porridge!” exclaimed Mr. Simons, 


* Mr. Simons’s grace before meat was, “John Simons, lackest thou 
anything? Lord, thou knowest that I lack nothing.” 


112 MR. SIMONS'S PORRIDGE. 


indignantly. “‘ Why, I have eaten it for twenty years, 
made of the best oatmeal, from the great Scotch meal 
merchant in the Strand; if it is not porridge, what would 
you call it,sir?” Mr. Story replied, that “drammock” 
would probably be considered the fitter name in the 
North; and the upshot of the dispute was, that he was led 
off to the kitchen by his host, who insisted, that since 
that was not porridge, he should let him see what was. 
The cook, however, drove them ignominiously from 
the lower regions: She knew her place and her work, 
and she would have no gentlemen meddling with her 
business—not she. They retired discomfited to the 
breakfast-room ; but Mr. Story affirming that he could 
make the porridge himself if he had but the materials, 
Mrs. Wales smuggled in a pot, meal and water, and 
the operation began; Mr. Simons fidgeting about, and 
watching the process with intense anxiety. At length 
the porridge was ready, and duly poured into his plate. 
With solemn eagerness he lifted a spoonful, tasted, 
paused a moment, then starting up, exclaimed enthu- 
siastically, ‘‘ You’ve given me a new sensation, sir! 
You've given me a new sensation!” The triumph of 
the real porridge was complete. 

Mr. Story’s time after this was spent chiefly in Lon- 
don, with an occasional visit elsewhere ; to Mrs. Wales, 
at her residence at Bury Green, &c. Pringle, returned 
from South Africa, was this winter in town, and the two 
early friends saw much of each other. The state of Mr. 
Story’s health prevented his mingling in society, but he 
was visited by many to whom his name and his doctrine 
had become known, and who sought from him advice 
and enlightenment. In the spring of 1828, his health 
being materially improved, he came down to Liverpool 
to be again with his friend Macrorie. Meanwhile, the 


LETTER FROM MR. CAMPBELL. 118 


change in his theological opinions, which had been 
gradually taking place, had reached such maturity and 
distinctness, that he considered it his duty to commu- 
nicate to his people the views which he now held, 
Unable to appear among them personally, he wrote a 
Pastoral Letter, which he begged Mr. Campbell to 
read from the pulpit of Rosneath Church. The 
address* appeared to Mr. Campbell an injudicious 
document ; and unwilling to read it publicly as it stood, 
he wrote to Mr. Story as follows, after a careful 
perusal of it :— 

“My dearest Brother,—I have indeed rejoiced 
before God in seeing the firmness of your tone and the 
simplicity of your perceptions, and your clear views of 
the nature of the long established delusion as to 
Evidences. I have also rejoiced at your guarding of the 
system from practical abuse by the inseparable con- 
nection established between grace and holiness, they 
being both made to arise out of the same perception 
of God, and growing and waning together; and when 
I proceed to ask you to reconsider the form of expres- 
sion which you select as that which you say you will 
employ, ‘Believe that your sins are forgiven,’ I am 
aware that I shall seem to recall statements you have 
heard me make on the subject, for I think I have used 
the same or nearly the same words. Yet itis not that 
my views are in the least changed, nor so far as I can 
see different from yours; but that this expression, 
besides being I think without apostolic sanction, is 
calculated to convey something else than the truth. 
When we say that faith is the simple belief of facts 
resting upon the authority of God’s word, we mean 


* With reference to this Address and other documents, see Appendix 
III, 


I 


114 MR. CAMPBELI’S LETTER. 


that we press men to believe only what are facts, 
whether they believe or not. Certain other facts will 
arise in their believing, but the facts which we ask 
them to believe are already facts, otherwise the 
doubting of them 1s not making God a liar. Accord- 
ingly I am wont to say, ‘I do not ask you to believe 
anything, but what I know to be true concerning you; 
and if you believed concerning what God has done for 
you what I believe, you would be rejoicing in the Lord.’ 
Now, dearest, do you believe that the sins of men are 
forgiven before they believe—and this is a fact con- 
cerning every man whom you address, although he 
should never believe? If so, so far as I yet see, I 
could not go along with you. I believe that Christ 
has suffered for all, and that therefore each has for- 
giveness in Christ in the same sense that he has 
eternal life in Christ, and this, whether he believes or 
not. But out of Christ there is neither life nor for- 
giveness. God has given us eternal life, and pardon 
as the first consciousness of that life, but this eternal 
life is in his Son, and so in Him as to be inseparable 
from the knowledge or belief of Him. God is revealed 
in Christ reconciling the world unto Himself, not 
imputing unto men their sins; which I thus under- 
stand :—Sin has interposed a curtain between us and 
God; while this curtain remains, God is misconceived 
of, thought of as our enemy, because we are his enemies, 
andsoon. Revelation removes this curtain and discovers 
God in Christ having accommodated Himself to our 
case as sinners, and in infinite love assumed a character 
or wrought a change on his own aspect which makes 
' Him as accessible to us sinners as He would out of 
Christ have been had we never sinned. This change 
is, that through the finished work of Christ, we have 


MR. CAMPBELL'S LETTER. 115 


revealed to us in God Himself that righteousness which 
we sought for in vain in ourselves, and the want of 
which kept us far from Him. It is discovered that in 
the Lord we have righteousness and strength. This 
is the fact, whether we know it or not. But by the 
knowledge of it is Salvation, because the knowledge of 
it draws us towards God in the way which He desires. 
Therefore men are not told simply that their sins are 
pardoned, but pardon is proclaimed through Christ ; 
and they are not told that they are justified, but that 
in believing they are justified, see Acts xiii. 88, 39. 
Every man has righteousness, and every man has 
pardon in Christ, but it is only in knowing or believ- 
ing that this is the case that righteousness is imputed 
to him, and he actually a justified person; and this by 
the righteous appointment of God, because it is only 
in the knowledge that he has these in God, that he is 
drawn towards God in that holy way of rejoicing in 
the Lord his righteousness, and having no confidence 
in the flesh, which is according to God’s will. I know 
that it sounds like a contradiction to say that a man 
has not absolutely what he is said to have in Christ. 
Yet this objection lies equally against the statement 
that God has given eternal life to those who have no 
life inthem. I remember being much nonplussed by, 
the answer, ‘then if you say I am to believe as a fact 
that I am pardoned, I am pardoned whether I believe 
it or not;’ and I was subsequently brought to see (as I 
thought) the truth in the words ‘I am the way, the 
truth and the life, no man cometh unto the Father but 
by me.’ I also felt a similar view conveyed by the 
words, ‘that he might be just, and the justifier of 
those who believe ;’ also by the words ‘He made Him to 


be sin for us who knew no sin, that we might be made 
12 


116 MR. CAMPBELL’S LETTER. 


the righteousness of God in Him.’ Here I saw some-. 
thing done by God, with a view to something to be 
accomplished through the belief or knowledge that it | 
had been done, according to Acts xiii. 88, 39, 40, 41. 
It arises from the nature of the work wrought by 
God that in it is eternal life for the world, and that 
the knowledge or belief of it is the enjoyment of 
eternal life; to know God as Love to me, and to have 
free access to Him as a child to a Father—this know- 
‘ledge is mine, and this access I feel that I have when 
I see Christ crucified for my sins, and God in Christ 
not’ imputing sin. But while I continue in unbelief 
that is in ignorance of the fact because I will not 
believe the statement of God’s word, I am a stranger 
to eternal life which exists in the consciousness of 
God’s love, and I am without pardon or righteousness, 
and still an unjustified person, because I am not going 
to God in Christ. Yet while thus without life and 
without pardon the facts are equally certain, in the 
knowledge of which I should have both, viz., that the 
love of God to me is as Christ’s sufferings, and that 
in Christ I have free access to God. The facts that 
are prior to belief, true, and which are properly the 
objects of belief, are that Christ died for the sins of 
every man, and that therefore every man has access 
to God through Him; coming in which way a man 
comes sinless, and not only sinless but clothed with 
the righteousness of God. The facts that emerge or 
arise, or become existences in believing, are that the 
soul becomes alive in Christ, and is pardoned and 
justified. I therefore do not say ‘ believe that you are 
pardoned or justified,’ any more than ‘ believe that you 
are alive to God,’ because these are not yet facts. But 
I say, ‘believe that Christ died for your sins and rose 


PASTORAL ADDRESS. 711 


again for your justification, and that in Him you have 
pardon and righteousness.’ And if the person whom I 
address believes this, then will he have confidence 
towards God and rejoice in the Lord. 

“T here see how salvation should be in God's holy 
wisdom connected with the knowledge of a fact, 
because it is a fact that the power of love disarms 
the hostility of the heart and reconciles it to God, and 
causes it to rejoice in God. I see also how he that 
believes has the witness in himself, for what witness 
ean there be to the fact that God has given us eternal 
life in Christ, compared to the experience of the 
present enjoyment of eternal life in Him; and I see 
how he that believeth not maketh God a liar, because 
I see the fact, in the knowledge of which is eternal life, 
vouched by the word of God.” 

Mr. Campbell’s representations had the effect he 
desired; Mr. Story consented to certain alterations 
in the wording of the address, and to the omis- 
sion of a reference he had made to the character 
of his own former preaching, and of the ordinary 
discoursing of other clergymen, and the amended 
edition was read from the pulpit by his friend. 
It was listened to by a crowded congregation, and 
sank deep into many hearts. ‘“O!” said one humble 
- hearer on leaving the Church, “this has been a 
great hairst* day. I hope there have been mony 
sheaves the day.” The hostile and indifferent were 
inclined to cavil, the more so as opposition was now 
beginning to be shown to Mr. Campbell’s teaching, 
and it had been rumoured in the district that Mr. 
Story had been “ converted” by the minister of Row,

and had written 2 declaration of his conversion which 
the latter was to read to his people m his absence. 

In Jane he returned home m good health. 

While in Liverpool he had been well enough to 
preach, and had with keen delight declared publicly 
for the first time the truths of Christ's having died for 
ali men; of man’s ground of assurance being found 
sitnply in God’s own word; of faith im Christ as the 
fountain of remission of sins and of eternal life. His 
preaching, we are told, caused great speculation in the 
religious world of Liverpool; “the presumption is,” 
he says himself, “ that I had been preaching something 
very like the Bible, since all parties seemed to find 
something very like their own systems in what I said.” 
With the following extract from a letter to Alan Ker, 
this chapter must conclude. 

He writes from Liverpool: “ Latterly (in London), 
I was under the necessity of living chiefly in seclusion. 
I saw Irving and others occasionally, and you will 
rejoice to learn, that I think with but little difference 
he now sees the power of faith as we see it. It is 
possible, however, that some new speculation may 
start up in sume of the outer fields of contemplation, 
and may lead him in full pursuit along most devious 
tracks where the feet of Christians seldom stray. 

“It is melancholy how little of simple Christianity 
is to bo found even among what are called Evangelical 
Christians. Something else than what God has said 
ur proscribed, often resorted to as tests and evidence 
of a state of grace and salvation, which cannot exist 
without the settlement of the previous question upon 
which it and all its tests depend. Mr. Tarbet has just 

“alled, and wo had a conversation upon the Lord’s 
and ohiofly on the spirit which it has gene- 


LETTER TO ALAN KER. 119 


rated, or rather which it ought to generate: It is a 
noble and blessed hope, the Lord’s coming; but it is a 
greater blessedness to think of Him as the adminis- 
trator of peace of conscience, than as the inflictor of 
judgment upon the fiercest persecutors and slanderers 
of His most holy name. Of these matters, while yet in 
the flesh, I trust we may have occasion to speak—but 
I feel as if this day were sufficient forme. I cannot 
think of to-morrow without fearing I should doubt of 
the present reality with which the fulness of God’s 
compassion fills the soul. Looking forward is only 
safe and sinless when it proceeds from triumphant 
faith. If to aid its weakness and infirmity, the soul 
seeks for what it does not at present enjoy, it is of the 
essence of doubt and unthankfulness. There is a 
divine fulness in the blessedness of feeling that it is 
enough—‘ there is nothing lacking of all that Thou 
hast promised.’ What lively faith would there be 
often where all is coldness and insensibility, were the 
believer to feel that the only knowledge he can pos- 
sibly have of his safety arises from the appropriation 
of the fact, that the Saviour died for him; in doubt of 
its reality he is possessed of the spirit of infidelity, 
inclusive of all disobedience and misery. The world 
is in a fearful dream, and I am very doubtful whether 
what is called Protestantism be not as needful of a 
purgation, as the Augean Stable of Catholic Christen- 
dom in the days of Luther. But let all be done in love. 
Nothing is sovereign but love in the feelings of 
those who receive the faith fresh from the Cross of 
Christ ; which is the way by which the good gifts come 
from the Father of Lights .. .. I hope the address 
has not been lost upon my dear people; I heard 
that they were very attentive. The day before I left 


120 | LETTER TO ALAN KER. 


London, General N— called for me to hear what he 
understood to be the truth, and upon going, he assured 
me he saw many things in a light he never did before. 
On my way home, I had occasion to converse with some 
clergymen of the Establishment upon the matter, and 
I trust some good was done. 

“O, my dear dear friend, I need not ask your 
prayers. I know you pray for me—so unworthy—so 
undeserving of the honour of speaking even one word 
in season to weary and miserable souls. I have been 
in my time weary and miserable; and what office is so 
fitted either to express my habitual joy to my Heavenly 
Father, or to remind me of the deep waters which, 
but for his immutableness, would again overwhelm me, 
as to seek’ for agonized spirits, and in my Master’s 
name cast out the devil that possesses them. 

“T avoid all contentious disputations as much as 
possible. To form merely correct notions of faith is. 
not believing.” 


CHAPTER ILI. 


PASTORAL ADDRESS—PAROCHIAL LIFE—ISABELLA 
CAMPBELL. 


THe reading of the Pastoral Address made an impres- 
sion which was felt far beyond the bounds of Rosneath 
and its immediate neighbourhood. 

People began to understand that there was some- 
thing real and positive in the doctrines which, whether 
new or old, were now for the first time beginning to 
spread along the shores of the Clyde, and that these were 
not the notions of one solitary man merely, but seemed 
indeed to possess the leavening power of the eternal 
truth. All Mr. Story’s friends were interested, many 
of them alarmed by the news of the change’ that had 
passed upon his belief; and those who had no clear 
understanding of what the change was, were most irri- 
tated at the mention of it, or of any like deviation 
from the ancient landmarks which their fathers had 
not set. He got many letters of inquiry, remon- 
Btrance, and advice. Landsborough writes, “ You are 
aware that your own views and those of your most 
excellent friend, Mr. Campbell, are by many regarded . 
as rather heretical. It is not often I own that heresy 
is found united with so much fervent piety towards 
God, and so much holy zeal for the welfare of immortal 
souls, and therefore I will not pronounce you here- 


122 LETTER FROM DR. CHALMERS. 


tics, at least till I know better what your new tenets 
are ....I see that I will not have room to advert 
even to what you have said, ‘The root of the evil, 
perhaps lies in our thinking that we may remain 
satisfied that we are believers, though we are not 
certain that Christ died for us.’ I am not aware that 
it is common to say that we may remain satisfied, 
though I fear many do remain satisfied, and though 
many ministers I fear seek too little to disturb those 
who are thus at ease in Zion.” Another friend writes, 
‘“‘Mrs. — and I were quite delighted with your letter: 
she exclaimed, ‘The wild romance of life is done— 
the real history is begun’—yes, Story, I am more than 
delighted when I hear you say that in the midst of all 
your troubles and trials you found Him whom your 
soul loveth.” 

Some time later Dr. Chalmers wrote, “ My very 
dear sir, I cannot say how much I felt interested by 
the momentary glance I had of you from the steam- 
vessel on the shore of Greenock—though I felt the 
extreme awkwardness of the contrast between the cor- 
diality I felt within, and the cramped or apparently 
cold expression to which one was constrained by the 
hundred eyes on all sides of us. It is more for the 
purpose of doing justice to my own emotions than for 
anything else, that I now write you, and to assure you 
of the unabated regard of one, who looks back with 
pleasing and tender recollections on all the intercourse 
he had with you in other days. And it has served 
greatly to confirm and augment that regard, to have 
heard of your growing interest in the great work of 
your Lord and Master. I will not enter into the pecu- 

‘as of that doctrine which I have heard ascribed 
, but I rejoice in the devotedness which you 


POPULAR RUMOURS. 123 


manifest to the best and greatest of causes, and I 
accept of this as one of the most hopeful symptoms 
that all is, or will be, right. ‘If thine eye be single, 
thy whole body shall be full of light.’ ‘If any will (or 
is willing to) do the will of God, he shall know of my 
doctrine, whether it is of God.’ ‘If any man keep my 
sayings, to him will I manifest myself.’” 

Any influence from without, however, could but 
slightly and transiently affect him, for he felt that he 
had now, what he knew to be the mind of Christ. It 
was as impossible for him to go back to the old for- 
mulas which he had left, as for the full-grown man, 
who can read what Plato spoke, or what Homer sang, 
to return to the lessons of his childish primer. 

As a specimen of the idle reports that were flying 
about the country after he returned, take the following 
from a clergyman far off in one of the Western Islands. 
“It was reported that you declared from the pulpit that 
you had been preaching false doctrines to your flock 
hitherto, and that you now recanted. It was said that 
you committed to the flames all the books procured for 
the children of the parish ; then from the books it came 
to be said it was the sermons you burned ; and from 
the actual burning it has come lately to the intention of 
doing so. It is said that you reject all human aid and 
knowledge, and deem it impious, not only to write ser- 
mons, but even to make any preparation before entering 
the pulpit. I heard it said a few days ago, that you 
completely did away with the doctrine of election, and 
taught, or at least, implied in your preaching the 
doctrine of Universal Salvation; that you discarded 
everything like duty altogether, and many such 
things.”* Meanwhile, utterly indifferent to the cla- 


* It should be mentioned that, at the time of Mr. Campbell’s trial 


124 RENOVATED TEACHING. 


mour of the religious world, he entered with a new 
‘ spirit and keener energy into his long suspended work. 
Diligent and faithful as he had always been, there was 
now a life in what he said and did that had been un- 
known before, and that quickened the life of all to 
whom he ministered. In the pulpit, in private, in 
constant meetings for prayer and instruction at his own 
home, and throughout the parish, he laboured with all 
earnestness and love to diffuse the knowledge of the 
truth “ as it is inJesus”—the sum and substance of his 
teaching being simply this, That God loved the world 
and gave His Son to die for it; that to believe the 
record He has given concerning this Son, is Faith; 
that this record is, that God hath given us eternal life, 
and this life is in His Son; that to believe that record 
as God would have us to believe it, is immediate joy 
and peace in believing; that he who lives in sin has not 
received this record, and therefore abides in sin; that 
any peace grounded on mere evidences, save as these 
themselves prove God to be Love in Christ, is a false 
peace; that to believe that Christ died for some 
only, and that the way to find out whether we are of the 
number, is to watch, and labour, and pray, till we are 
good enough to feel convinced that we have the Spirit, 
is another Gospel. 

Thus he taught, and not in vain. God gave the 
increase. Many were aroused—many .changed—led 
from darkness and bondage into light and freedom. 


neither Mr. Story nor he rejected the doctrine of Election, but held it 
always along with their belief in the truth of Christ’s death for all. 
They did so with this idea, that no man could believe in Christ 
~ sat the direct choice or election of God. Afterwards they came 
that Christ was truly the Elect of God, and that all likewise

HIS MARRIAGE. | 125 


What we popularly term a Revival, began in Rosneath ; 
a revival, for which Mr. Campbell’s labours of love in 
Mr. Story’s absence, had prepared the way, and which 
was now all the more thorough and lasting, that it 
entirely lacked the accompaniment of those painful and 
disorderly demonstrations, which have of late marked 
the progress of the so-called religious movements. 
There were no prostrations of the body, no outcries, 
no crowded midnight meetings, no public confessions 
and details of conviction and conversion; there was 
a quiet, earnest, prayerful seeking after God’s truth— 
a vivid enlightenment—a living reformation. Nor was 
this confined to the members of his own flock merely 
—others elsewhere hearing of the work that was going 
on, came to witness it and to share the blessing. From 
letters, that cannot well be reproduced here, we learn 
how he, whose words had given this movement impulse, 
rejoiced in its progress; how wisely and unweariedly 
he directed it—how abundantly he laboured—how 
many there were, who for his work’s sake, had good 
cause to call him blessed. 

His toils were now cheered, and in a measure 
shared, as they had not been before. Shortly after 
his return from England, his marriage—long con- 
templated—was accomplished.* His wife, “set” to 
him, “like perfect music unto noble words,” was 
Helen Boyle Dunlop, one of the daughters of Mr. 
Dunlop of Keppoch; of her sister Mary we have already 
heard. Perhaps not the least of the blessings he real- 
ized through this union, was the sense of relief he 
experienced in obtaining the aid of one who could 


* When he was in England Irving had -been bent on his paying his

than any woman in England, except my wife.” 


126 HIS MOTHER'S DEATH. 


manage his pecuniary and domestic affairs for him. 
For the conduct of the former he was totally unfitted, 
by the want of anything resembling business habits ; 
as regarded the latter, he had long groaned under the 
dominion of a succession of housekeepers, with whose 
tactics he was as unable to cope, as he was to keep 
accounts, or make the two ends of his yearly income 
meet.* He needed, too, a companion to cheer his home 
and to bind up his affections with new links, all the 
more intensely that he had now lost both his father and 
mother. His mother was the last survivor, and she died 
in 1824. He wrote after her death to Alan Ker: “I had 
gone to spend a day or two at Jordanhill, and returning 
on the Saturday evening, found in my dreary parlour 
the letters that gave me little prospect of seeing her 
alive. It was a dark and stormy night, but it was a 
feeble emblem of my mind racked with many a melan- 
choly imagining. . . . When at last thus separated 
from the Past, and her, I felt a desolateness which no 
words can convey.” | 

I have been desirous to produce, at this stage of our 
history, a picture of what he was as a preacher,—as a 
clergyman, and of the impression he produced, as such. 
My own knowledge and recollection fail me here. At 
the time at which I can with any intelligence recall his 
preaching, his oratorical powers were on their wane; 
not his mental powers, but the physical energy and 
animation which used to wing so powerfully what he

SACRAMENT SUNDAY. 127 


said. He had then, also, formed a habit of reading his 
sermons very closely, which, as he could not always 
decypher his own writing, greatly marred the effect they 
were calculated to produce. ‘Till the latest days of his 
ministry, however, one occasion never failed to draw forth 
all he was, and all he had, in freest and fullest force; 
and that was the dispensation of the Holy Sacrament. 
Few who heard his words and saw his countenance 
as he addressed the communicants, and put into their 
hands the bread that is meat indeed, and the wine that 
is drink indeed, can have forgotten the impression that 
the tone and aspect of the man produced. Through 
the memories of many years, and the recollections of 
many another church and scene, and priestly ministrant, 
I look back and see as clearly as if it were only yes- 
terday that my eyes had rested on it, the old Church, with 
the laurel shadowing the tall window beside the pulpit, 
the open door flooded with the Autumn sunshine; and 
just within it, at the head of the long table, my father 
standing with the consecrated bread in his hand, adding 
one more sentence of love and adoration ere giving it 
to the Elders at his side; while on his face there 
brightened such a light of joy and heavenly communion 
with the Father and the Son, as helped one to realize 
the spectacle beheld of old in the Council-chamber 
in Jerusalem, when all that sat in the Council looking 
steadfastly on the first martyr of the church, “saw 
His face as it had been the face of an angel.” * 

* Since writing the above I have read Dr. John Brown's pleasing

have made one feel what it is so impossible otherwise to convey—the
