128 LETTER FROM REV, A. YOUNG. ' 


' What from personal knowledgeI am unable to supply, 
will be more fitly conveyed than I could have hoped to 
convey it, by the extracts that follow; they are from 
letters of friends, whose opinion is valuable as that of 
men, who not only were intimately acquainted with his 
opinions and his history, but who loved him truly, and 
therefore knew him, and understood him best, for 
“that only which we love we know.” The first is 
from a letter from the Rev. Alexander Young, M.A., 
Minister of Mochrum, dated Mochrum, 5 Feb., 1861: 
He says, “In regard to my beloved departed 
brother’s preaching, I had the privilege of hearing him 
but seldom, and many years have elapsed since last I 
heard him. For although some ten or twelve years ago 
I heard him at Luss, it was not the Mr. Story who 
used to rivet the attention and draw forth the love of my 
people. It was here, indeed, that to most advantage 
he stood forth the minister of God’s word, the repre- 
sentative of the manifested love. Not that there was 
anything very marked in his argument. His division 
of the subject was plain and simple, and his reasoning 
fair and to the point; but certainly this was not his 
forte. It was the illustration, and specially (what I 
would call) the episodical illustrations, wherein he 
greatly excelled. His whole heart was poured forth in 
them, and his love-beaming eye and noble frame in 
fervent animation, beautifully harmonized with the 
guiding spirit, and added to the effect. Shall I say, 
that it was poetry embodied in and expressed by the 
preacher? I had intended to tell you this immediately 
on receiving your letter, but I waited for a leisure time 
(which has not come) to state more fully my thoughts 
of one with whom I had unreserved heart-communion, 
and whom I much loved.” | 


LATTER OF REV. DR. WYLIE. 129 © 


The next is from the Rev. Dr. Wylie, of Carluke: 
—‘ Clifton, 8 Feb:, 1861. You persist in asking 
from me ‘a labour of love,’ but what FE feel to be 
‘a hard thing;’ I so shrink from fearing to fail 
in it. You ask me to indicate my impression of 
your late dear father’s teaching. There can scarce, 
I presume, be dubiety as to the substance of it, 
embodied, as it might be said to be, in a text he 
often spoke from (Hosea xii., 9), ‘O! Israel, thou 
hast destroyed thyself, but in Me is thine help;’ ever 
justifying God in his judgment as infinitely removing 
from Himself the blame of the sinner’s destruction, 
and leaving wholly on the sinner the guilt and burden 
of his refusal and condemnation. It was not more 
in prayer a pleading with God for the sinner, than 
in preaching a pleading with the sinner to believe and 
receive his God. How he now warmed—aye, wept 
in his yearnings, ‘O Jerusalem, Jerusalem, &c.,’ and 
now rose and remonstrated against the hindering and 
destroying self-delusions of the sinner, as did they who 
(Acts xiv., 15) ran in and cried out, ‘ saying, Sirs, why 
do ye these things?’ But I must be more minute. 
1. His preaching was textual preaching. He dealt 
the more safely and effectually with large texts, taking 
(a frequent and favourite theme with him) a whole type 
from Leviticus to bring home and rivet the Gospel, its 
deliverance, its cleansing power, its life; or such 
passages as Isaiah vi., 1—10; John xv., 1—11; Ephes. 
v1., 18—20; 2 Peter i.,1—12. From all these texts I 
have heard him teach ; and he did not stop hesitat- 
ingly, distrustfully, to qualify and pare away, like the 
poor grudging systematizers, to show how this could be 
made to fit into that; he was conscious only that he 
spoke for God. So, 2. It was scriptural teaching ; I 

K 


130 LETTER OF REV. DR. WYLIE. 


mean, it was of no school but his own, as drawn from 
the fountain. If at any time.a tone and taste seemed 
to come from elsewhere, it was from those old reformers, 
Luther, Becon, Tyndale, &c., of whom his mind and 
heart were so full—from these certainly, rather than 
from any of the Puritan school. 3. So, also, it was 
wise teaching, eminently wise. We had fallen on a 
time when, as ever with truth and life reviving, there 
was no slowness to ‘make a man an offender for a 
word.’ In this he saw sooner and farther than others, 
and he was very jealous of the words he should use, 
often at the cost of great misconstruction ; very faithful 
with his dearest, most valued friends, ‘ withstanding 
them to the face,’ if they erred inthis. At times, 
quoting Ps. xu., 6, he assumed quite a startling and 
solemn tone, demanding for God’s own truth that it 
be declared in God’s own words, and bidding them to 
forget this at their peril. Oh, that all had remembered 
and weighed his counsel.* 4. To be honest in con- 
veymg my own full impression—the teaching was 
Pauline. I was amusingly confirmed in this by the 
remark of an intelligent lady, who, as I afterwards 
learned, would persist in assimilating the respective 
teachings of three friends whom she frequently heard 
at the same season, to Paul, Peter, and John. Who 
the other two were, does not matter; your father, she 
said, was ‘ Pauline all over.’ 

“I must not omit that at its grextest—for at seasons 
it was great teaching, my beloved friend, from his 
very nature, required sympathy. Where he felt this 
was not, he was, at times, as if struck dumb—extin-

guished: so ‘ grieved for the hardness of their hearts,’

“IT cannot but recall two especial occasions of his 
preaching. 

“On the Saturday of that May, 1831, when his— 
dearest friend was ‘cast out,’ he was utterly pros- 
trated in body and spirit. Bearing my own burden, I 
trembled for the issue to him. But I knew what would 
relieve; some ready vent for his overcharged spirit in 
God’s service and for the good of souls. I urged his 
arranging for his own pulpit at Rosneath on the mor- 
row, and his returning with me to Carluke: and so it 
was. The preliminary worship—in which there was 
still great oppression, even to hindrance—over, he read 
out Romans viii. 1—18; and such a burst followed, 
almost instantaneously, of power, and love, and life! 
His whole man was lightened. Surely God had praise 
in that day. 

“‘ The other occasion was, on a glorious July evening, 
the closing sermon of the Communion at Mochrum, in 
the tent, from which the entire previous service had 
been conducted, (the act of communicating alone having 
been within the Church). From the place of the tent, 
a low corner in the Churchyard, rose, tier upon tier, 
up to the Church wall, closely planted graves and flat 
tombstones, densely packed with eager, waiting lis- 
teners—piled as you can conceive would be the Colos- 
seum if filled with worshippers. Among others, I 
seem to see now that grand old soldier, the late Sir 
Wm. Maxwell, of Monreith, take his place again on a 
stone which he had occupied for already many hours 
during the day. It was now seven o’clock. After 
praise and prayer, Josh. xxiv. 14—27, was read. And 
there he fixed for yet two hours more (notwithstanding

182 LETTER OF REV. DR. WYLIE. 


the distance many had to travel) that large assemblage ; 
some even gathered from distant parts of Wigtonshire : 
in his burning closing appeal to those who had once 
more that day said ‘ We are witnesses, summoning as 
one day destined to appear for or against them, not 
only the living, but the dead—the graves they rested 
on—the stones that told of those dear ‘dead in the 
Lord’ who had gone before—the walls of that Church, 
the scene of their so recent vow—the grand old elms 
that shadowed from the sinking sun the resting-places 
of their fathers, brothers, children,—all nature above 
and around,—waxing, as he thus appealed, louder and 
louder, growing grander and grander, till one felt as if 
earth and life had but one use, one end—to proclaim 
God’s Son, God’s love and glory—spending and being 
spent in ‘beseeching men as ambassadors for God in 
Christ’s stead to be reconciled to God.’—Yours affec- 
tionately, JOHN Wye.” 


The same valued friend says, in another letter :— 
“Very eminently his nobleness, to those who really 
knew him, was in himself, far more even than in what 
he said or did. How much I ever felt was below and 
behind! Your tribute should speak to many of one 
‘whose praise was of God,’ in this poor day of craving 
for ‘the praise which is of men.’ Is there faith any 
more of the ‘Father which seeth in secret ?’”

LETTER OF ARCHDEACON GOOLD. 138 


of your late dear father, I have thought that perhaps

prove unacceptable. To those of a catholic spirit, my 
humble testimony to his worth may appear the more 
impartial, as it comes from one who, by circumstances 
and convictions, has been led to labour in another and 
a different section of his Master’s vineyard from that in 
which your dear father toiled so long and so faithfully. 
“Tt was in the spring of 1831 that I was led, as some 
would say, by chance, but, as I believe, by the special 
providence of God, to reside for some months in a then 
remote and retired district of the parish of Rosneath. 
I was at that time suffering under great depression 
of spirits, produced by causes to which it is needless to 
revert. My mind, I believe, was opening to the import- 
ance of religion and the value of the soul ; but I needed 
the guidance and counsel of one who was not only 
faithful, but sympathizing and tender. Such a mes- 
senger—one in ten thousand—I met in your father. 
‘“‘T had not attended the Church many Sundays when 
he visited our humble cottage, and introduced himself 
to Mrs. Goold and myself as the pastor of the parish. 
“From early associations and prejudices, I should 
have been very much indisposed indeed to cordially 
respond to or welcome the visit of any minister, whose 
manners and appearance were not frank and gentle- 
manly. With his singularly dignified and _ attractive 
appearance I was nearly as much impressed at first 
sight, as a poor countryman of mine own who, it is said, 
on meeting him one hot summer’s day walking by the 
Gairloch, with, as was his wont, hat in hand,* was so 


* He was very impatient of the pressure of the conventional round 
hat, and, whenever he could, carried his hat in his hand and his 


134 LETTER OF ARCHDEACON GOOLD. 


struck by the snowy-white hair, the large, well-defined 
black brows, the lustrous eyes that shone beneath, and 
the general mien, so apostolic, that he could not disas- 
sociate the figure he saw from his idea of the ‘ Head 
of the Church.’ He paused, and gazing on him for a 
moment, reverently bent the knee, doffed his hat, and 
gave vent to his feelings with an ‘Och, thin, may the 
Lord’s blessing, and that of the Holy Vargin, be for 
ever upon your Holiness.’* But, to return; from that 
day our acquaintance gradually ripened into an inti- 
macy which lasted to the end of his life. As long as I 
remained in the parish, the hospitable doors of the 
Manse were ever open to me, and no hour was deemed 
unseasonable for a visit ; and no opportunity on his part, 
or that of your dear mother and her precious sister, 
Mrs. S. Ker,t was let slip of recommending the truth as 
it is in Jesus, not only by precept and counsel, but by 
the practical exhibition of whatever was pure, lovely, 
and of good report. At this distance of time, coupled 
with the fact of my great ignorance and inexperience 
at the period to which I refer, I do not feel myself 
qualified to give any decided opinion on his merits as 
a preacher. From what I do remember of him, I think 
his style of preaching was far less doctrinal in sub- 
stance, and less methodical in arrangement, than most 
of the clergy of Scotland whose ministry I have occa- 
umbrella over his head. Where he was not so well known as in his 


own immediate neighbourhood, this was sometimes supposed to be 
in order to exhibit his locks.

LETTER OF ARCHDEACON GOOLD. 135 


sionally attended. He appeared to me to be most 
efficient, or, if I may use a homely phrase, most at 
home, when he selected for his text a passage of scrip- 
ture full of pathos, tenderness, and a yearning after 
souls. Such a text I now vividly recall to mind: it 
impressed me much at the time, and still dwells on my 
memory. I mean that touching passage, Hosea x1., 8, 
9. He was, indeed, a man of exquisite sensibilities. 
He seemed to me constitutionally to have been imbued 
with a great portion of that spirit which led his Master 
to exclaim, ‘ Reproach hath broken my heart.’ Had 
he been cast 1n a more iron mould he would, no doubt, 
have been saved from much mental and bodily suffering. 
Few, and only those who were very intimate friends, 
could know what an ordeal he passed through, not only 
during the period of the unhappy ‘ Disruption,’ but fora 
considerable time after. The ties that were then so 
rudely snapped—the unkind censures and surmises 
so industriously circulated—the chill that was thrown 
around pastoral intercourse once so social and confiding 
—all conspired to wound and lacerate a heart whose 
leading elements were love, peace, and joy; but I knew 
that he was enabled to look upon these sore trials as 
part of that moral discipline which was destined by his 
Heavenly Father to train him for that kingdom, which 
cannot be reached but through the path of great 
tribulation. 

“T have often seen him suffering bodily pain of no 
ordinary intensity, and I never heard a murmur escape 
his lips. I was privileged to see him a few weeks 
before his death: the same meekness, gentleness and 
patience were as prominent as ever. His trust and 
confidence in that peace-speaking blood ‘ which cleans- 
eth from all sin’ were undiminished, and he looked 


136 LETTER OF ARCHDEACON GOOLD. 


calmly to the crisis fast approaching, knowing whom 
the had believed, and persuaded that He was able to 
keep that which was committed to him against that 
day. And now I feel that in penning these few hasty 
lines—a feeble tribute to the memory of one I loved, 
and ought to love—I am in some measure acting 
inconsistently, for I own I do not in general admire 
religious biographies. They often paint men more 
as they ought to be than as they really are, and 
thus become not only unedifying to the living, but 
positively hurtful and depressing. Faults your dear 
father had in common with all the fallen sons of 
Adam ; but whether it arises from the impulsiveness 
of my own individual idiosyncrasy, or from the power 
exerted by the new nature in him in keeping down the 
evil propensities of the old, I cannot say; but this I 
know, that I am not the man who could either detect or 
expose them. My recollections may appear to some too 
highly charged with encomium: be it so; to me, indi- 
vidually, his memory is connected with everything 
that was elevated, holy, and pure. 


‘* Believe me to be, yours, very affectionately, 
“ FRED. GOOLD, 
“ Archdeacon of Raphoe.”

“TI got into the spirit of the day, and although very 
languid ” (he had been unwell), “I became at last full 
of the power of the truth and felt very happy; many 
of the people I saw shedding tears; strong, iron faces, 
ashamed to weep, half hid as they heard what I had 
to say on the words, ‘Having yet, therefore, one

HIS SUNDAY SERVICES. 137 


glancing at the watch to see it was past three.* Of course 
I stopped immediately, as it was far beyond the time, yet 
i was loath to part from them. Then after service five 
came in, that had not appeared before, and wished to 
be examined privately for admission to the Communion. 
I had said, that if any were like the Son in the parable 
who refused at first, and afterwards repented and went, 
I should be ready to converse with them this evening 
as I had to go from home early in the morning. Then 
after being occupied with them till nearly nine o’clock, 
I went to Clynder and saw poor widow Campbell.” 
The unconsciousness of the flight of time which is 
indieated in this letter was owing in a great measure 
to the fact that his sermons were only partially written. 
The latter portion of them, in his best days, was spoken 
from the thoughts and feelings of the hour, and was 
frequently expanded to an undue length—the service 
not seldom extending over more than three hours. 
Long as it was, he expected all to remain to the 
close; and anyone attempting to escape in the middle 
(as in summer strangers would do), was sure to bring 
down on himself a reproof sharp and decisive. He 
kept in fact a tight rein of discipline over his congre- 
gation: any smiles, whispers, or apparent inattention 
were promptly checked, the delinquent, if a juvenile, 
being oecasionally ordered to come out of his pew and 
sit on the form in the aisle; al] sleepers were vigilantly 
wakened up by a loud rap on the pulpit, and a “ Don’t 
sleep, I beseech you;” a call to order sure to be effectu- 
ally seconded by a silent dig from some neighbouring 
friendly elbow. 

Besides the parochial duties which so engrossed him 
after his return from England, he had another occupa- 


“ Having begun at 12 o'clock. 


138 MEMOIR OF ISABELLA CAMPBELL. 


tion which absorbed much of his time and thought. 
This was the drawing up of the Memoir of Isabella 
Campbell. Isabella had died during his absence; and 
So Impressed was he with what he had seen of her holy 
life, and what he had heard of her joyful death, that 
he determined to give some account of her and her 
godly example to the world. He accordingly wrote a 
Memoir, in the form of an address to the people of 
Rosneath. It was published in 1829. The first edition 
was published by subscription, and consisted of 3500 
copies. It was quickly dispersed, and a second of 
2000 followed, and was sold off in a fortnight. A third 
and fourth were then brought out simultaneously, as also 
another smaller work, a ‘Memoir of Mary Mackay,” 
one of his Sunday scholars; but the sale of these 
had scarcely made any way, when it was checked by 
an outcry of popular alarm and prejudice—ignorant 
‘ and illogical as these commonly are—which chose to 
connect the memory of Isabella and the name of Mr. 
Story with certain religious phenomena which, in the 
year 1880, began to create excitement in the West of 
Scotland. We shall have occasion to advert to these 
anon, and to mark their progress, from small and 
abnormal beginnings to a somewhat imposing develop- 
ment in a new order, ritual, and belief. Meantime, it 
is enough to say, that neither Mr. Story nor Mr. Camp- 
bell was in any way connected with the first stirrings 
_ of that strange religious movement, which ended in the 
erection of the “ Holy Catholic Apostolic Church: ”* 
they stood aloof. 


* To call this body “Irvingite” is absurd, since Irving was not its 
founder. To call it Holy Catholic and Apostolic, without the sign of 
quotation, would be to admit that it had a peculiar claim to these titles. 
For convenience and distinctness’ sake, I give it the name it assumes to 


MEMOIR OF ISABELLA CAMPBELL. 139 


The Memoir of Isabella, though almost studiously 
divested of literary grace, or any interest external to 
herself, depicted a life so consecrated by suffering, by 
faith, by prayer, by rapt, almost mystic, communion 
with its Divine source, that it produced a very vivid 
Impression. It was immensely read in England and 
Scotland, and was promptly reprinted in America, 
where it likewise had a wide circulation. To how 
many it was made by God’s Spirit a messenger of 
peace and salvation, His day shall declare; but even 
had it produced no effect, save in those cases which 
became personally known to its author, the result 
would have been richly ample.* 

The avidity with which this work was received and 
read indicated a state of religious feeling pregnant 
with no little change. It testified to a thirst for a 
deeper spiritual teaching than was then to be found 
generally in Scottish churches. The theology of Scot- 
land, as might have been expected of theology reared 
on so purely dogmatic a foundation, had gradually 


ceased to have much living influence on the popular . 


conscience, though it had graven its outlines deeply 
on the popular understanding. The doctrines of the 
Confession, as commonly set forth in all the accuracy of 


itself, but within inverted commas. For a like reason, in speaking of 
the Seceders who left the Church in 1843, I give them the title they 
arrogate, with the same mark of quotation. 

* Of the many letters of thankful acknowledgment for the lessons of 
Isabella's life, the following from William Wilberforce may be given as 
an interesting specimen :—“I have been wishing to tell you with what 
reverence and admiration I have been filled by the latter part of the life 
of Isabella Campbell. I hope also to be edified by it. I believe it often 
pleases God to grant a larger measure of spiritual peace and joy to those 
of his children who are experiencing the sharpest pangs of bodily and 
temporal suffering. But I fear we almost all live below our privileges, 
and might be cheered with a far larger measure of peace and joy in 
believing.” °

140 STATE OF SCOTTISH THEOLOGY. 


their irrefragable logic, were not felt to have any very 
close connection with existing thought and action. As 
a natural result, the preaching of the national pulpits, 
diverging into two branches, became either a mere 
ethical or practical discoursing without any doctrinal 


_ or spiritual basis; or a formal exposition of Calvinistic 


doctrine, tending rather to foster a morbid self-con- 
sciousness, than a free’ and loving development of 
Christian life. The “Moderate” preacher was a 
preacher of morals, who too seldom made any reference 
to their root and sanction in Christ : the “ Evangelical ” 
preacher was a preacher of Genevan doctrine, who was 
too generally rather engrossed with the harmonies of 
his system, than conscious of those wants and ques- 
tionings of humanity, which it was insufficient to meet, 
and unable to answer. The mode of preaching known 
as “ Moderate,” arose in many cases, it is probable, from 
the honest consciousness of inability to deal with the 
doctrines of the “Confession of Faith” without appear- 
ing to controvertthem. Unless, however, practical and 
ethical preaching draw its authority from some true 
spiritual fountain, it will soon become dry and lifeless, 
and will be associated with, and, at last, merged in, the 
dull indifferentism which has neither will nor capacity 
to encounter the difficulties that surround all truth. 
‘Thus, “‘ Moderatism ” and “ Moderates” became terms 
of reproach, often too truly indicating a barren un- 
spiritual practice and profession. The tendency of 


. the “ Evangelical” preaching, on the other hand, was 


to foster the growth of a religion which dealt too much in 
grim and narrow self-inspection, and lacked broad and 
healthy principle and sympathy, being but little interested 
in catholic truth, unless that truth were moulded into the 
special form which had been stamped with its approval. 


READINESS FOR CHANGE. 14] 


A religion of this type begot too marked an isolation - 
of the individual, and obscured the great central fact 
of God’s fatherly relation to All, by teaching each man 
to regard his relation to God as affected or determined 
by his own personal condition and belief. But neither 
of these modes of setting forth the truth was exhaustive 
or satisfactory. Neither Moderatism nor Evangeli- 
calism fully occupied the minds, or met the spiritual 
wants, of earnest men within the Church. Much 
of the most zealous Christian life gradually with- 
drew itself from the pale of the Establishment; not 
a few of those who were most thoughtful and pious 
seceded from its communion, in the hope of finding 
elsewhere a more living spirit of Christianity. 

The eagerness with which the Memoir of Isabella 
Campbell was read, bore witness to the restless and 
keen anxiety with which any tidings of new religious 
light and truth were looked and longed for—especially 
any that professed, as that book did, to tell of a direct 
and simple “ peace in believing;” and that portrayed 
a life which seemed to rest on foundations of wider 
truth, and more unhesitating faith in God as a Father, 
revealed and reconciled in Christ His Son, than those 
exhibited in the ordinary religious teaching of the day. 
The same unrest, the same ‘consciousness of some- 
thing lacking in that teaching, served to render men’s 
minds readily and frankly receptive of those forms 
of doctrine, more intensely spiritual than Moderatism , 
dreamed of, and more broadly catholic than Evan- 
gelicalism reckoned safe or orthodox, which now 
began to centre the interest and attention of Scottish 
thinkers and seekers after truth, in the preaching of 
Mr. Campbell at Row. The times were ripe for a 
new expansion and development of Gospel teaching, 


142 READINESS FOR CHANGE. 


which should be less trammelled by the old formula, 
and more in sympathy with a deepening Christian con- 
sciousness; which should, if possible, bring religious 
truth out of the fetters of its polemical relations, into 
real contact with growing and earnest life; which 
should regard as of less importance dogmatic accuracy 
of opinion, than the development of a pure and earnest 
character out of @ living faith in, and knowledge of, 
God the Father. 


CHAPTER III. 
ROW CASE—PRESBYTERIAL PROCEEDINGS. 


LET us now turn our eyes to Row, and follow out to 
its close the history of this ministry of Mr. Campbell’s, 
which has been mentioned at the end of the last 
chapter. In what follows, I desire simply to relate 
events as they occurred, without entering on any argu- 
ment on the one side or the other. Before begin- 
ning, however, the narrative of the proceedings which 
terminated in Mr. Campbell’s deposition; it will be 
well to give in his own words an account of the way 
by which he was led to hold the opinions, for main- 
taining which he was expelled from the ministry of the 
National Church. 

In the following letter (prefixed to the lithographed 
collection of his sermons, and addressed to a member 
of his family, now no more), we trace the progress and 
learn the nature of his views more clearly than any 
other statement would enable us to do: —“ Two circum- 
stances—my exclusive study of the Word of God, and 
my exclusive intercourse as the servant of God with 
my people, increased rapidly my acquaintance with the 
extent of the demands for personal religion on the part 
of God, and with the little measure of compliance with 
these on the part of men, and rendered my meditations 
chiefly researches into the reasonableness of the former, 


144 DEVELOPMENT OF MR. CAMPBELL’S VIEWS. 


and the consequent sin in not meeting them; and the 
various devices of Satan, by which men were enabled 
to live at peace in an evil way; and my discourses 
contained the exposition of the discoveries on these 
subjects which were daily making to me, along with 
such personal warnings and practical exhortation to 
my people as these discoveries suggested. The first 
doctrinal and practical subject on which I remember to 
have felt that much light was given me was repentance. 
I was led to see how much of that little repentance 
which was to be found, was merely regret for the per- 
sonal evil consequences of having exposed one’s self to 
the wrath of God; and how large a proportion of the 
confessions of sin, with which even the most serious 
were acquainted, were more truly speaking, confessions 
of folly and imprudence, and not of guilt; and how 
much of the acknowledgment as to the superiority of 
religion to irreligion was rather the acknowledgment of 
the greater importance of eternal interests as compared 
with temporal interests, and not the acknowledgment 
of the intrinsic excellence and beauty of holiness and 
love, and the intrinsic deformity and hatefulness of 
sin and selfishness. 

“At the same time when this subject was opened 
up to me, I remember that the hollowness and hypo- 
crisy of the professions made of repentance in men’s 
approaches to God—their hollowness and hypocrisy as 
seen by Him—appeared to me most awful; though 
through the error in which they were on the subject, 
men felt a measure of consciousness of sincerity, and 
took to themselves the comfort of thinking that they 
were complying with God’s call-upon them to repent. 

“A similar mockery of God, I was at that time and 
in connection with the same subject, made to see in 


SELF-DECEPTION. 145 


those verbal tributes of admiration to God as just, 
holy, and good, which men offer in their prayers, not 
because their hearts are full of His excellence, and 
enamoured of His beauty, but because they think it 
will please Him and recommend themselves to Him. 
At this time I laboured hard to make those distinctions 
visible to my people, and to give them a true standard 
by which to measure themselves, and tests by which 
to detect the deceptions of their own hearts. Idid not _ 
at that time, however, see any great fruit of these 

labours, and was often subjected to the pain of hearing 
persons whose demeanor indicated that they were not 
new creatures in Christ Jesus, expressing the strongest 
approbation of sermons which, it appeared to me, were 
peculiarly fitted to disturb their false peace. And I 
found the higher I raised the standard, I only the more 


stimulated the efforts of a self-righteous and self- 


deceiving spirit to personate, in the way of acting as 
in a dramatic exhibition, the character of excellence 
held forth. 

“Meditating with prayer on this painful ministerial 
experience, I was gradually taught to see, that so long 
as the individual is uncertain of being the object of 
love to his God, and is still without any sure hold of his 
personal safety, in the prospect of eternity, it is in vain 
to attempt to mduce him to serve God under the power 
of any purer motive than the desire to win God’s love 
for himself, and so to secure his own happiness; conse- _ 
quently, however high the standard, correspondence 
with it was sought under the influence of unmingled 
selfishness, making every apparent success only a 
deeper deception. And thus I was gradually led to 
entertain the doctrine commonly expressed by the 


words, ‘ Assurance of Faith ;’ having first seen that the 
L 


146 “ ASSURANCE OF FAITH.” 


want of it precluded singleness of heart and eye in the 
service of God—and then having found, in studying the 
Epistles to the first Christian Churches, that its exist- 
ence, in those addressed, was in them taken for granted, 
and in every practical exhortation was presupposed, I 
accordingly began to urge on my people, that in order 
to their being in a condition to act purely, under the 
influence of love to Him, and delight in what He is, 
their first step in religion would require to be, resting 
assured of His love in Christ to them as individuals, and 
of their individually having eternal life given to them 
in Christ. 

“‘T think this was the character of my preaching in 
the latter part of the year 1826, but I cannot easily 
fix; and in the summer of 1827, I think, it first was 
that I understood that offence was taken with what I 
taught. This, however, for some time, amounted 
merely to the complaint ‘that I carried the subject of 
assurance too far, and no one ventured then to 
advance the charge of heresy. It was at the same 
time also that I first enjoyed the happiness of seeing 
many awakened from their false security, and not a 
few to delight themselves in the Lord; and what my 
pressing of high attainments as the fruits of faith had 
been unable to accomplish, I now found produced by 
the earnest demand for the true faith itself. Towards 
the end of 1827, at the time when my summer parish- 
loners all returned home, the report carried up to 
Glasgow of what they had been hearing at Row, pro- 
duced a considerable sensation there, in what is called 
‘the religious world. In consequence of this, a 
minister of Glasgow selected as the subject of essay 
in a theological society, ‘The Assurance of Faith.’ 

10Wing that many misrepresentations of what I had 


x 
SUBJECT DISCUSSED IN GLA8GOW. 147 


taught on the subject reached my brethren, I felt it my 
duty to attend and hear this essay read; and if the 
opportunity was given (for I was not then a member 
of the society), to reply or explain, as it might appear 
right. The essay was exceedingly temperate, but full 
of what I knew to be false principles. I was asked, 
and gladly accepted the invitation, to speak ; and after 
they had all spoken, and very courteously, I was again 
permitted to reply. I went away thankful for the 
indulgence which I had experienced, and full of expec- 
tation as to the result of their meditating upon what 
I had been enabled to say. 

“The following week I preached a public sermon on 
a week-day, for one of the Glasgow charitable insti- 
tutions ; and I remember, in order to preclude the 
charge of Antinomianism, while at the same time as 
affording the opportunity of setting forth the practical 
importance of the assurance of faith, I selected as 
my text, John xvi. 17, ‘Sanctify them through thy 
truth.’ Most of the ministers of Glasgow were pre- 
sent, and from this occasion I date the opposition of 
my brethren. I had fondly hoped that the explanations 
given in the clerical society would have removed pre- 
judices and commended the truth. They, however, 
had calculated on my being changed by what had come 
from them, and in consequence were much offended to 
hear me so shortly after state so fully what they had 
condemned; and for many Sabbaths most of the 
ministers of Glasgow were preaching with pointed 
reference (and with a view to confutation) to what I 
taught. There was as yet, however, no organised 
opposition in the parish of Row. 

‘* The controversy in which I was constantly engaged 


in almost all my intercourse with my brethren, obliged 
L 2 


148 UNIVERSAL ATONEMENT AND 


me to examine narrowly the foundation furnished, by 
the communications made in the Gospel for the Assur- 
ance of Faith. This led directly to the closer con- 
sideration of the extent of the atonement, and of the 
circumstances in which mankind had been placed by 
the shedding of the blood of Christ. And it soon 
appeared to me manifest, that unless Christ had died 
for all, and unless the Gospel announced him as the 
gift of God to every human being, so that there remained 
nothing to be done to give the individual a title to 
rejoice in Christ as his Saviour; there was no founda- 
tion in the record of God for the Assurance which I 
demanded, and which I saw to be essential to true 
holiness. The next step therefore was my teaching, as 
the subject matter of the Gospel, universal Atonement 
and Pardon through the blood of Christ. 

‘“It may appear strange to one unacquainted with 
these matters, that those who had been hitherto most 
satisfied with my teaching on the subject of assurance, 
viz., some sects that urge this subject much, now became 
opposed to me, and strangely held, that if I regarded 
the Atonement as universal, I deprived the individual 
Christian of all assurance ; while those who objected 
to the urgency with which I pressed Assurance, now 
held that doctrine to become still more dangerous 
when connected with that of Universal Pardon. 

“Such opposition made more and more apparent to 
me the want of true religion in the land. I was made 
to mourn over many whose strong confidence, along 
with their outward propriety of conduct, had made me 
hope well of them, when I saw by their opposition to 
the universality of the Atonement that their assurance 
had been very much its own basis—that their faith had 
not been a belief of a record of God—true, whether 


PARDON THROUGH CHRIST'S BLOOD. 149 


believed or not—but had been the assumption of a fact, 
for which they had no other proof but that they 
assumed it; and that instead of resting on the 
character of God as revealed in Christ, they looked 
upon the death of Christ as so much suffering—the 
purchase-money of heaven to a certain number to 
whom it infallibly secured heaven. And I was made 
to mourn over the opposition to the doctrine of 
universal pardon, taking as it did such forms, ‘if all 
are forgiven, then we need not repent, or be sorry 
for our sins, or think of a future judgment, and we 
may do what we please: for it was thus apparent 
beyond all my previous fears, that what men called 
Repentance, was not a real sorrow for sin, but merely 
something offered in exchange for safety; and I was 
shocked to hear men avow, that if they were certain 
that their Heavenly Father forgave them their sin, 
they would feel it unnecessary to grieve because they 
had offended Him; and instead of being led to repent- 
ance by the knowledge of this His goodness, would be 
encouraged by it to sin yet more and more. It was, 
however, rather, as what others would be likely to say, 
than as what they would say themselves, that men 
uttered such things; but it was manifest that, though 
speaking in the third person, they spoke of themselves.* 

“While I was labouring to remove objections, the 
bringing of which made such painful discoveries, it 
became more and more apparant to me that men 

* “The knowledge of God’s love in Christ is the cruse of salt which 
alone can turn the bitter and barren-making streams of remorse into the 
healing waters of repentance. And thus the truest and best repentance 
follows, and does not precede the sense of forgiveness ; and thus, too, will 
repentance be a thing of the whole life long, for every new insight into 
that forgiving love is as a new reason why the sinner should mourn 


that he ever sinned against it."—Compare Ezekiel xxxvi., 25—81. 
Dean Trench’s Notes on the Parables: “ The Prodigal Son,” 


150 OBJECTIONS BROUGHT. 


objected, because they wished to object; that they hated 
the light, and only sought to excuse themselves to 
themselves,—I mean, tofind apologies of a specious kind 
for rejecting a doctrine which they felt was searching ; 
and I was struck to find thus illustrated our Saviour’s 
declaration, that while it was for righteousness’ sake 
that his followers would be hated, the form in which 
that hatred would be manifested, would be speaking 
evil of them falsely. No man will admit a thing to be 
holy and at the same time object to it. He must first 
call it, and find some excuse for calling it, unholy, and 
then he can condemn it (see Isaiah, chapter 66, verse 5 ; 
John, chapter 16, verse 2); and thus while rejoicing at 
being taught of God the secret of the prevailing 
ungodliness, that it was the simple unbelief of God's 
manifested character in Christ; and while urging the 
faith of forgiving love as that which purifieth, I found 
myself charged with Antinomianism, and with setting 
forth doctrines leading to licentiousness; and as if to 
stamp the character of the opposition awakened, it first 
took an active form in the parish in the persons of some 
individuals of much practical ungodliness.” 

The leaven of the doctrines of which Mr. Campbell 
was, at the time we speak of, the most noted exponent, 
has now 80 penetrated the popular mind, that it is diff- 
cult for us to realise the power and vivid freshness with 
which his preaching, breaking through traditionary 
trammels, came to the hearts of his hearers. It was as 
the voice of one crying in the desert, “‘ Prepare ye the 
way of the Lord, make his paths straight.’ Truth had 
long lain bound hand and foot in the dungeons of cold 
and rigid “‘ Moderatism,” or maimed and blinded in the 
“‘ wandering mazes lost” of ‘“ Evangelicalism” falsely 
socalled. It wasa wondrous novelty and revolution to 


PROGRESS OF MR. CAMPBELL'S TEACHING. 151 


hear from apostolic lips a message of simple glad 
tidings; not a spiritless inculcation of mere morality, 
nor a complex exposition of the nature of salvation, 
and the ground of assurance, and the evidences of 
faith; but the plain statement, “ Christ died for all: 
faith is the believing that if so, then he died for me; 
assurance is the confidence towards God, as forgiv- 
ing my iniquities and saving me from sin and death, 
which I have when I come to believe this.” 

The effect of this preaching was deeply felt and widely 
spread. Not only in the parish of Row, but wherever 
-Mr. Campbell’s voice was heard, many were led to 
receive this Gospel and to rejoice in it. Even while 
Mr. Story was in England, in 1828, his earnest plead- 
ings and instructions had begun to bear rich fruit, and 
to provoke angry hostility. Even then Alan Ker could 
write, ‘our beloved Campbell has been the happy 
almoner of his Lord’s munificence. Of him, his flaming 
love, unbaffled zeal, and singular power of commending 
the truth to the acceptance of inquiring souls, I could 
write pages. He has been often here, and goes from 
house to house, feeding and refreshing from one well- 
spring of delight. One says, ‘It is quite a new Gospel 
to me;’ another, ‘I never understood the Scriptures 
before ;’ another, ‘The same Scripture in this: light 
has a melting and novel influence, which, without 
it, never was felt nor thought of. . . . Yet,as the 
opposition chiefly comes from the Evangelical clergy, 
whose influence is so extensive, it is formidable.” 
And Mr. Campbell himself then wrote, ‘‘O, you do 
not know how the idea of peace toward God, from the 
simple belief of his words, without waiting for evi- 
dences, is resisted by those who have long been leading 
their flocks through the dark and desolate places of 


152 OPPOSITION SPRINGS UP. 


their own hearts, instead of leading them to Jesus, and 
causing them to look unto Him and be saved;” and 
again “I pray for you, dearest, that you may abound 
in wide love towards those that are yet in the dark. I 
wish our part in the fast approaching conflict to be 
characterized by love, and the Spirit of Him, who 
spoke weeping of the enemies of the Cross of Christ. 
By love God proposes to subdue his enemies, and we 
are allowed no other weapon.” 

Meanwhile, amid all opposition, his words had free 
course. The pulpits of the neighbouring clergy, one 
after another, were closed against Mr. Campbell. 
The Greenock ministers (not including Dr. Scott) 
refused to take part in the services of the Seaman’s 
Chapel, in that port, if Messrs. Campbell and Story 
were allowed at any time to officiate, and their names 
accordingly were struck off the list of officiating min- 
isters. In every way distrust and resistance were dis- 
played; but still his teaching prevailed. Row became 
a centre of light to which many came—often from a 
great distance—to learn the way of the Lord more per- 
fectly. Not a few of the more earnest and inquiring 
among the young students of Divinity, and others inter- 
ested in theological questions, gathered round Mr. 
Campbell’s Church, among whom two of the most 
welcome, whose interest was deepest, and whose sym- 
pathy most full and ready, were Mr. Erskine, of Lin- 
lathen, and Mr. A. J. Scott.* 

At last the open and ecclesiastical warfare fairly

began. After one or two irregular and abortive

was presented, entreating the Presbytery to investigate 
and deliver the parish from the oppression of such per- 
nicious errors as were taught by the minister. Into 
the competency of the memorialists there was no in- 
quiry, although one of them was the notorious smug- 
gler, and another the drunken tailor. It was moved 
and seconded, that a committee be appointed to con- 
verse with Mr. Campbell ; rose, and in a furious 
speech, moved that they should proceed at once 
to root out the pestilential heresy. He was not 
seconded. The committee was appointed, and for a 
few minutes Mr. Campbell had been looking into 
Pardovan.* Hearing the clerk say that the business 
was concluded, he started up and begged to be heard : 
‘No, no, it is over, the time is past,’ echoed from 
around the Presbytery table. ‘At least, if in point of 
justice, I cannot be heard,’ said he—‘in point of cour- 
tesy.. ‘You ought to have been listening,’ cried they. 
‘From the Presbytery,’ cried one voice, ‘you deserve 
no courtesy; and thus was it ended.”

GENERAL ASSEMBLY’S PRELIMINARY DECISION. 155 


tived; and the Presbytery adopted a motion to the 
effect, that their memorial being found to contain a 
serious charge against Mr. Campbell, the memorialists 
be asked ‘ Whether they are willing to convert their 
memorial into a libel under all the pains thereof.” 
Mr. Story was the only minister who voted against 
this finding; along with Mr. Dunlop he dissented, and 
complained to the ensuing General Assembly, on the 
ground that the Church’s form of process had not been 
duly complied with by the memorialists. This com- 
plaint, however, when the Assembly met, was by the 
advice of their counsel, Mr. Cockburn,* withdrawn. 
Mr. Story agreed to its withdrawal at the time, but 
afterwards greatly regretted it. ‘It was,’ he writes, 
“an evil counsel in an evil hour, yielded to in a moment 
when the result of a discussion was confidently antici- 
pated to be the calling of Campbell to the bar, or the 
appointment of a committee of conference, requiring 
immediate explanations, which might have been followed 
by instant suspension.” The judgment of the Assembly 
was to allow the withdrawal of the dissent and com- 
plaint; and seeing that the doctrines which the memo- 
rialists imputed to Mr. Campbell, had been condemned 
by the General Assembly in 1720,+ to remit to the 
Presbytery to proceed with the investigation of the 
case (instructing them to receive any libel presented 
by parishioners of Row), and to “carry on their pro- 
ceedings till the cause is ripe for a final judgment, 
notwithstanding any appeal or complaint on prelimi-

* During these discussions there seems to have Been, from time to 
time, some confusion arising between the Assurance of Faith and the 
Assurance of Salvation. The libel charged Mr. Campbell with teaching 
that no man was a Christian unless he was assured of his own salvation 
(Assurance of Salvation): whereas he maintained that his teaching 
simply was, that a conviction of the possibility of his own salvation was 
of the essence of each man’s faith—that a general faith was vain, but a

following from Calvin’s Catechism :—* What is farther required besides 
our placing confidence in God, and having an assured knowledge that He 
is Almighty and perfectly good 1—Answer: That every one of us be 
Jully assured in his conscience that he ta beloved of God, and that He 
will be both his Father and his Saviour.”

158 MR. STORY'S SPEECH. 


was therefore necessary for Mr. Campbell to prove that 
his doctrines were consistent with Scripture. I have 
been surprised that none of the brethren have adverted 
to the candour with which he has treated the Presby- 
tery; instead of putting them to trouble, he has come 
forward and candidly confessed what he does hold. 
This should have drawn forth the warmest admiration. 
Of the nature of his defence, sir, I was ignorant, until 
I heard it read this day. Had I been his counsellor, 
I should not have given him such counsel as he has 
followed. Some of his parishioners have brought a 
charge against him; as a pastor it became him to 
require a specific charge. I for one would have 
listened for hours, had you entered into the matter 
[and required more definite charges from his libel- 
lers.) Iwill therefore claim what I would have will- 
ingly granted to another; for to say a little is to say 
nothing .... The fundamental question is, Does 
God hate or love his creatures? A reverend father 
(Rev. Dr. Hamilton of Strathblane) well qualified to 
speak on this subject, alluded to the subject of repro- 
bation. Is this decisive of God hating his creatures ? 
If so, then I hesitate not to say that I would re- 
nounce the Standards as contrary to the Gospel. I 
say that it is contrary to all natural religion to say 
that the Infinite Mind hates any of its creatures. Has 
God expressed His love to all His creatures? Is 
there a man, woman, or child, to whom God has not 
exhibited some kindness? Is there a man here who 
could venture to say that there has existed a being to 
whom God has not exhibited some kindness? ‘The 
question then is, what is the kindness God is ever 
manifesting? And I adopt Dr. Graham’s* rule of inter- 


* Minister of Killearn. 


MR. STORY’S SPEECH. 159 


pretation, that Scripture cannot contradict itself: ‘ If 
God endured with much long-suffering the vessels of 
wrath fitted to destruction,’ did those vessels of wrath 
‘ never experience his kindness? I hold that no sinner 
has a right to exist in the universe of God. I hold 
that Adam, the moment he sinned, did not merit a 
moment’s existence; why then was he permitted to 
exist, but in virtue of the mediation of Christ? There 
must then be some connection between Adam and the 
whole human race;—in his body were contained the 
elements of all future generations. I say more; I say 
that in him was contained that which entered into the 
constitution of our Lord’s humanity. There then is a 
fundamental point; for the question to which I am 
speaking is not, Are all men pardoned? but, Is 
there any man, woman, or child who has no con- 
nection with Christ? Is there any one that has 
merited kindness from God ? most unquestionably not. 
The only channel, then, through which such kind- 
ness could flow, is through Christ. I beseech you to 
observe that the very existence of Adam, after sinning, 
depended on the fact that the Son of God should die 
in his nature. .... As it is late, I must refer but 
shortly to the Standards. . . . Itis a first principle that 
the Holy Ghost holds communication with the sons of 
men only through the work of the Lord Jesus; and if 
His work be limited to the elect, there could be no 
communication between the Holy Spirit and the non- 
elect. Now the Confession is quite explicit on this 
point, that there are influences of the Spirit exerted on 
those that are non-elect.* Now you will observe that 
I hold this principle, described in this portion of the 
Confession, to be identical with the truth that our Saviour

DECISION OF THE PRESBYTERY. 161 


Catechism. ‘ What is the right faith?’ ‘Itis a sure 
persuasion and stedfast knowledge of God’s tender love 
towards us:’ Calvin’s Catechism, 111th question. ‘A 
sure persuasion of the heart that Christ, by his death, 
hath taken away our sins,’ is the answer given to the 
same question in Davidson’s Catechism, which was 
approved by an Assembly of this Church.” He 
then alluded to the Act of 1720, the authority of 
which, as bearing on the present case, he declined 
to admit; and said, in conclusion, “ Seeing, then, 
that there does exist a connexion between Christ 
and the children of men previous to their believing 
—‘for God commendeth his love toward us in that 
while we were yet sinners Christ died for us ’—this 
connexion must have existed anterior to our recog- 
nition of it; there is, therefore, nothing contained in 
this libel contrary to any fundamental principle 
of religion or to our Standards. And seeing that 
this is the principle laid down in Mr. Campbell's 
Answer, and also that a» man must be assured of 
what God says, I would entreat the House, before 
coming to any resolution, to investigate the matter 
more thoroughly.” 

On a division, the relevancy of the major proposition 
of the libel was affirmed, Mr. Story and Mr. Dunlop 
alone forming the minority. From this decision the 
former dissented and appealed. Next day the Presby- 
tery again met, and took into consideration the several 
counts making up the minor proposition of the libel, 
which were also, with some trifling exceptions, declared 
to be relevant. Against this Mr. Story again entered 
his dissent, and appealed for leave to complain to the 
Synod of Glasgow and Ayr. Mr. Campbell also, as a 
matter of course, took a similar step. Mr. Story’s

“Reasons of Dissent and Complaint” will be ascer-

The Presbytery having thus decided as to the rele- 
vancy, resolved to proceed to the probation of the libel. 
Some days were occupied with the examination of the 
witnesses, among whom were the late Rev. Patrick 
Brewster and Rev. Dr. R. Burns, of Paisley; Rev. W. 
Cunningham, then assistant to Dr. Scott, of Greenock, 
late Principal of the Edinburgh “Free” Church Col- 
lege ; and the Rev. John Arthur, Independent Minister 
at Helensburgh, for the prosecution: and James Whit- 
shed Hawkins, Esq., of Dunnichen, advocate ; Wiliam 
Bonar, Esq., banker, Edinburgh; Captain Stirling, of 
Glentyne, R.N.; and Mr. Lusk, bookseller, Greenock, 
for the defence. During the progress of the trial Mr. 
Story wrote almost hourly bulletins for the friends at a 
distance, who were eagerly waiting for the result, and 
praying without ceasing that it might be one favourable 
to Mr. Campbell's cause. Here is an extract :—‘ There 
is, outwardly at least, a fair spirit in the Presbytery, and 
nothing irritating. I do not think the case is made 
stronger since Mr. Hawkins’ testimony, although there 
are various points brought out to fill up the outlines of 
that most memorable witnessing. I am quite sure that 
at this present moment prayer is offering up from many 
faithful hearts. It is very remarkable that yesterday I 
had no such feeling or impression ; this may have been 
owing to my own hardness of spirit, which so often is 
a great bitterness tome. Truth is coming out beauti- 
fully from the lips of Barclay,* whom I am loving very 
much as the investigation proceeds. Alas! how God 
can humble us when we rest on any human instrumen- 
tality ; for he has just given a most perplexing answer, 


* Captain Barclay, R.N. 


TESTIMONY OF MR. HAWKINS. 163 


but I doubt not light will arise amid the darkness. It 
is even so. He had stated that God’s condemnation 
of sin had been removed by the death of Christ; but 
now he has answered that the wrath of God had not 
ceased to be manifested against all unrighteousness 
and ungodliness of men. But these clouds are distress- 
ing, and I feel that my own unbelief may be the cause 
of much of that dear man’s transient obscurity.” 

The most distinct statement of Mr. Campbell’s teach- 
ing Mr. Story held to be given in the testimony of Mr. 
Hawkins, and from the evidence of this witness he 
deemed it to be clearly enough made out that Mr. 
Campbell was condemned by the Presbytery, and after- 
wards by the Assembly, for doctrines which he did not 
inculcate. He believed that the repeated popular asser- 
tion, that the so-called “heretical” teaching was identical 
with that condemned in 1720, was erroneous, and that 
this error was demonstrated by Mr. Hawkins’ testimony. 

The libelled clergyman, according to this witness, 
taught that the death of Christ as a sacrifice was for 
all men without distinction, and was a manifestation of 
the love of God to all; that this manifestation did not 
appear, from his teaching, to indicate any toleration 
of iniquity; but that he was in the habit of urging that 
men were to find the greatest manifestation of God’s 
hatred of sin in the sacrifice of the Lord Jesus Christ. 
Mr. Hawkins stated that he always understood Mr. 
Campbell to teach that God gave His Son to die for 
- us—the just for the unjust—that He might bring us 
to God, meaning by ws, all mankind; that Mr. Campbell 
always seemed perfectly to recognize the election of 
God the Father, and spoke of it as a purpose eternal, 
not originated in time; that God’s desire is that all 
men should be saved, and that as a manifestation of 

mM 2 


164 TESTIMONY OF MR. HAWKINS. 


that desire he had opened a way of access to Himself 
by which he desired all to come to Him; that God 
had removed the barrier which His holiness interposed 
to the access of the sinner to Him, not by laying 
His holiness aside, but by a work which enabled Him 
consistently with His Holiness to receive the vilest 
sinner who came to Him by the way which He Himself 
had opened up: that Mr. Campbell taught that the 
sacrifice of Christ has procured forgiveness for all 
men, by which he did not mean deliverance from 
judgment to come, but non-imputation of trespasses 
now—the privilege of coming to God as a reconciled 
Father: that he referred a man’s coming to God to 
the work of the Spirit bringing home the Gospel to his 
heart; and that the condemnation of men was that 
while they received the witness of men they rejected that 
of God, which is greater. Mr. Hawkins went on to 
explain Mr. Campbell’s teaching that the future judg- 
ment would be executed by the Son ‘of God, and that 
the condition of men at the day of judgment will 
be according as they have believed or rejected the 
Gospel; that those, therefore, who are condemned, 
will be condemned for unbelief: that he never 
taught that the belief of the love of God was calcu- 
lated to make men easy in sin, but that the reason 
why a man will not believe that love is that he will 
rather keep his sin: that a knowledge of the cha- 
racter of God was essential to a belief in the for- 
giveness of sins, and that no man would believe in 
that forgiveness who did not see God’s righteousness 
therein, and that, consequently, no one could receive 
the remission of sins except by believing in Christ: 
that in reference to self examination before par- 
taking of the sacrament, Mr. Campbell called upon 


TESTIMONY OF MR. HAWKINS. 165 


believers not to examine whether they were believers 
or not, but whether they had acted as believers should 
act: that he called upon unbelievers to repent and 
believe the Gospel; no other precept could he address 
to them who were, as he believed, without the spirit of 
obedience, by which alone men offer acceptable service. 

According to the witness Mr. Campbell taught that 
Christ was the gift of God to all men, that in 
Him were contained all things pertaining to life and 
godliness, and that in Christ they had the Holy 
Ghost: that all men were concluded under Christ 
as a righteous Head, through whom the forgiveness 
of sins flowed to all who believed in His name: 
that all men were redeemed from the curse of the 
Law by Christ, but not from subjection to itas a rule 
of life; the work of Christ being for the purpose 
that the righteousness of the Law might be fulfilled 
in us, not in the flesh but in the spirit. 

Mr. Hawkins further testified that he never heard 
Mr. Campbell preach on Assurance particularly, but 
that he always understood him to teach that to believe 
in the love of God and to be assured of it is the 
same thing, and that no man is believing it who is 
not assured of it as applicable to himself: that in 
Mr. Campbell’s preaching the forgiveness of sins, 
through the blood of Christ to every creature, he 
understood him to mean that personal guilt during 
the day of grace does not debar sinners from access 
to God as the fountain of life, and that the work of 
Christ alone is sufficient to give the sinner confidence 
toward God as a father—to enable him to serve 
Him with the spirit of a child instead of that of a 
slave: that the charge that Mr. Campbell did not 
pray for forgiveness was false, inasmuch as he used 


166 THE LIBEL “PROVEN.” 


the very words “Lord forgive us,” and his prayer 
implied a confession of sin and an entreaty for its re- 
moval, because a thing hateful in God’s sight: that Mr. 
Campbell explained the nature of faith to be the belief 
of a testimony, that testimony being that God had so 
loved the world as to give His only begotten Son that 
whosoever believeth on Him should have eternal life. 

From this précis of the principal testimony for the 
defender, which it has seemed advisable to give as much 
as possible in the witness’s own words, it will be at 
once seen that the charge of Antinomianism preferred 
against Mr. Campbell had no valid foundation. It will 
be especially evident that there was no ground for this 
charge where it was alleged chiefly to exist—in his 
teaching regarding pardon. The accused preached 
‘universal pardon,” not in the justly condemned Anti- 
nomian sense of an universal act of indemnity to sinners 
irrespective of their moral character; but in the sense 
of the removal in every case of the judicial barrier inter- 
posed by guilt between God and Man—God declaring 
that barrier to be taken away by Christ. 

After a prolonged examination of witnesses the 
Presbytery pronounced the libel “Proven” by the 
usual majority, and in respect thereof “Find that 

-the defender has entertained and promulgated the 
doctrine of Universal Atonement and Pardon through 
the death of Christ, and also the doctrine that Assur- 
ance is of the essence of faith, and necessary to Sal- 
vation.” Appeals to the Synod were of course taken 
against this finding, and to the Synod the case ac- 
cordingly was referred.* 

* The proceedings in Mr. Campbell’s trial in Presbytery, Synod, and 


Assembly, were collected and published in an interesting volume by 
R. B. Lusk, Greenock, in 1831. 


CHAPTER IV. 


ROW CASE—SYNOD—ASSEMBLY. 


THE case came before the Synod on the 13th April, 
and was discussed on that and the following day. 

Mr. Story appeared at the bar, and in a long and 
able speech, maintained the conformity of Mr. Camp- 
bell’s teaching with the Scriptures, and the Standards 
of the Church ; disproved its identity with the doctrine 
condemned in 1720, and fortified his position with 
numerous extracts from the Confessions of the Pro- 
" testant Churches, and the writings of Scottish Church- 
men from the time of Knox to the present century. 
He then went over the counts in the libel seriatim, 
showing that they ought to have been held irrelevant, 
either because they consisted of detached sentences, 
the meaning of which could not be ascertained without 
the context, or, because understanding the terms agree- 
ably to Mr. Campbell’s own understanding of them, 
they contained nothing contrary to Scripture or to the 
Confession of Faith. It appeared to him unjust to 
condemn a man for teaching what the accused asserted 
he never taught, and he wished to apply to Mr. Camp- 
bell’s case the rule laid down by the Assembly of 1722, 
with reference to the “ Marrow of Modern Divinity.” 
“The passages from the said book,” says the Act of 


168 CASE GOES BEFORE THE SYNOD. 


Assembly, “are to be taken and understood in the sense 
and meaning of its author.’* 

Mr. Campbell followed, and in the longest and most 
comprehensive statement he had yet made, he defended 
his position and his teaching. 

In the whole Synod, however, only one voice was raised 
on his behalf—that of the Rev. John Wylie, minister 
of Carluke; and on the motion of Dr. Hill, minister of 
Dailly, now Professor of Theology in the University 
of Glasgow, the sentence of the Presbytery on the 
relevancy of the libel was affirmed. 

On the following day the discussion on the proof 
was proceeded with. Mr. Story again spoke in support 
of his appeal, but of the speech he delivered, no record 
has been preserved. Thomas Carlyle, Esq.,t Advocate, 
appeared as counsel for Mr. Campbell; Dr. Fleming, 
minister of Old Kilpatrick, now Professor of Moral 
Philosophy in the University of Glasgow, and Dr. 
Hamilton, on behalf of the Presbytery of Dunbarton ; 
and after a hearing, the Synod proceeded to debate the 
case. 

The question awaiting decision had created intense 
interest among thoughtful and pious persons through- 


* The Rev. Dr. Hetherington, in his “ History of the Church of Scot- 
land,’ remarks upon the unfair and partial way in which the “ Marrow” 
was treated, and to which this Act of 1722 had a retrospective reference. 
“In vain did its defenders attempt to procure a fair and thorough 
investigation of the work for the purpose of showing that some un- 
guarded expressions were so modified by others, and by the general 
spirit of the book, that, taken collectively, the doctrine of the book was 
orthodox and scriptural. This, which is the only fair and candid mode 
of ascertaining what are really an author’s sentiments, was refused, and 
the attention of the Assembly was rigidly confined to the expressions 
selected by the accusers.” . 

+ Afterwards a well-known member of the “ Holy Catholic Apostolic 
Church,” and author of “Pleadings with my Mother, the Church in 
Scotland.” 


TONE OF THE SYNOD. 169 


out Scotland. The gallery of the Church in which the 
court sat was thronged with spettators, many of 
whom had travelled far to be present on an occasion, 
felt to be one full of momentous issues to the Scottish 
Church. 

The speeches of the members of the Synod must 
have fallen heavily on the hearts of the many who 
prized as their life the doctrines for which Mr. Camp- 
bell was then arraigned. They too generally indicated 
less of a candid and generous impartiality, than of a 
sheer abhorrence of the teaching ascribed to the 
defender, and were in their tone polemical rather than 
judicial. “If,” said Dr. Begg, of New Monkland, 
“if Mr. Campbell were an honest man, if he were a 
true Christian, if he had the fear of God before his 
eyes, he would have come forward and said, ‘I have 
changed my opinions; I swore to and subscribed with 
my hand the Confession of Faith; but I am now con- 
vinced that the doctrines contained in it are not accord- 
ing to the word of God, and I give up my charge.” 
This is hardly the language one would have expected 
from a Christian minister, sitting as a judge at a 
brother’s trial. Dr. McLean, of the Gorbals, used 
language still more reprehensible. ‘I have no parti- 
cular interest in the question, other than a Christian 
minister ought to have; but I am vexed to the bottom 
of my heart, that whilst in London we have an indivi- 
dual of our Church searching after the Millennium and 
other extravagances, and in our own Synod others 
uttering such doctrines as we have heard in this court, 
our neighbours should have to say that the Church of 
Scotland was going about with a fool’s coat upon its 
back, and thus exposing our Church to the scorn and 
contempt of all rational people. . . . . I] am 


170 DECISION OF THE 8YNOD. 


shocked that in a complaint and dissent of this kind 
we are bearded by the appellants, who say to us, ‘ You 
know nothing at all about the matter; you must come 
to us to learn what truth is;’ and I understand that 
there were some attempts to pray that we should be 
enlightened on the subject. The thing is perfectly 
shocking—there is nonsense on the face of it.” This 
clergyman who was so horrified at being prayed for, 
adds a devout wish thatif there be any other individuals 
desirous of joining in the heretical notions of Messrs. 
Campbell and Story, they “may all go in the same 
boat,”—to the haven of deposition, it is to be presumed. 
Dr. McFarlan, of St. Enoch’s, Glasgow, afterwards 
“Free” Church minister, in Greenock, spoke of the 
defender as having “polluted the whole country with 
heresy.” Alas, was not such hard, unbrotherly lan- 
guage in a court of Christ’s Church an indelible 
pollution ? 

On the motion of Principal McFarlan, the case 
was referred, without any formal expression of the 
Synod’s opinion, to the ensuing General Assembly, 
from which judgment Mr. Wylie alone dissented. 

The decision of the Assembly was awaited by Mr. 
Story with little hopefulness. He felt the question 
was prejudged. Prejudice, and evil feeling, and igno- 
rant hostility had been excited by a war of pamphlets 
on the controverted points, and by a great deal of 
declamation from “evangelical” pulpits. He had 
striven to stem the current of clerical opposition, and 
to throw some useful light on the subject by the publi- 
cation of a few tracts entitled “ Light from a cloud of 
witnesses,” in which he collected a great number of 
declarations regarding the atonement, faith, and assur- 
ance, “from the writings of the reformers and 


MEETING OF ASSEMBLY. 171 


martyrs.” This, however, did not seem to produce 
much effect; the voice of the Church—in public at 
least—was all but unanimous against Mr. Campbell, 
and what, with singular unfairness, one of his brethren 
in the Synod of Glasgow and Ayr had—(ere the case 
was tried)—styled in a popular and violent pamphlet, 
the “ Row Heresy.” 

Mr. Story looked forward to the Assembly’s deci- 
sion as likely to affect himself hardly less than his 
friend. ‘Soon, in all probability,” he writes, antici- 
pating his own deposition, ‘we must become pil- 
grims from the home that we love; but I remember 
Elijah and the ravens. I remember Him who car- 
ries the lambs in his bosom. I hear the voice of that 
good Shepherd, ‘ when the poor and needy seek water, 
and their tongue faileth for thirst, I the Lord will 
lead them, I, the God of Jacob will not forsake them ;’ 
and what a simple matter it seems to be to bid 
adieu to all outward felicities that the truth may be 
glorified.” 

The time of the Assembly arrived, and he went into 
‘Edinburgh. He writes on his reaching town, “I have 
not heard particularly as yet of the state of feeling 
here, Some ministers that are not members have ex- 
pressed themselves with great feeling and interest; but 
then they are not members; they go like sheep in 
bodies, one after the other, even although it be to leap 
into the water. Poor Proudfoot* came to me to-day 
and entreated me to be careful in my selection of words, 
for they were all watching to seize upon me. I think I 
have ceased very much from men; but I am deeply 
grieved often to feel that mine is too much the moral 


* Minister of Arrochar, in the Presbytery of Dunbarton. 


172 PROCEEDINGS IN THE ASSEMBLY. 


indignation of the mere flesh, rather than the holy 
aversion of the spirit.” 

The proceedings in the Assembly began on Tues- 
day, May 25th. Seldom had a General Assembly 
met to decide a question of profounder importance ; 
for apart from all peculiarities, and, one may add, 
objectionable forms of diction, the only issue before 
the Venerable House really was, whether by the death 
of Christ a way was opened up to all men to return to 
their Father, and whether the power of serving Him 
aright depended on their knowing and believing Him 
to be the Forgiver of their sins through the shed- 
ding of the blood of His Son. Mr. Campbell had 
maintained the affirmative, and his teaching had been 
branded as “serpent doctrines,” by a Rev. Dr. in an 
inferior court; was the supreme court of the Church 
about to ratify the previous condemnation? To all 
observers it was evident that it was to be so. Many 
events had been occurring—as we shall by and bye 
have occasion to notice—in the neighbourhood of Row, 
of a nature calculated to shock and disturb most men’s 
minds; and these, though utterly unconnected with 
Mr. Campbell and his teaching, had been either wil- 
fully or in foolish ignorance represented as associated 
therewith ; so that now to all formerly existing causes 
of distrust and hostility, was added a new one, as pow- 
erful perhaps as all the rest. The prejudice against 
Mr. Campbell and his doctrine, and these supposed 
results or accompaniments of his preaching, grew 
stronger and stronger, and seemed at last to culminate 
in the very sphere where its influence should have been 
totally unfelt. 

What follows is from Mr. Story’s own pen—a frag- 
ment from his unfinished Memoir of Alan Ker. “In 


PROCEEDINGS IN THE ASSEMBLY. — 173 


the inferior court there had been the appearance of 
impartiality—of patient inquiry; day after day the 
members had met to examine witnesses for the de- 
fence as well as the prosecution; and no doubt many 
of them did conscientiously weigh the evidence before 
they recorded their decision. But Mr. Campbell was 
not only deposed by the General Assembly, but deposed 
in such circumstances of indelicacy and precipitation, 
as to be without precedent in the annals of the Church. 
The Presbytery, I have stated, had engaged in a 
laborious inquiry, and the evidence formed a large 
volume of 400 or 500 pages. The inferior had thus 
provided for the Supreme Court sufficient premises on 
which to found their judgment; but could any man have 
anticipated such a procedure as this,—that long after 
midnight the Assembly should enter upon the merits 
of the doctrines impugned, about which, under the great 
eye of the universe, and in foro conscientie, they could 
alone be qualified to decide by weighing the testimo- 
nies of the witnesses carefully and impartially ? It is 
manifest, that unless they had been gifted with an 
intuitive discernment that should supersede the neces- 
sity of laborious investigation, they were utterly unfitted 
tojudge of the contents of the evidence between the dark- 
ness of midnight and the light of the morning; indeed 
the greater portion of those reverend and learned 
judges regarded any knowledge, howsoever imperfect, 
as an utterly superfluous acquisition ; for, no sooner 
was the volume put into their hands than they trans- 
ferred it to their pockets, as if they had some super- 
stitious dread lest the light which shone through all 
its pages should startle them in the perpetration of 
the deed which they meditated. Some of them, indeed, 
assigned as a reason for conduct so abhorrent to the 


174 PROCEEDINGS IN THE ASSEMBLY. 


moral sense of men, and still more repulsive in pro- 
fessed teachers of the truth, and judges between right and 
wrong, that they had read a good deal about the matter 
in the newspapers and magazines for months past; as 
if the gossip and lying inventions of the scorners of 
all truth had authority superior to the solemn declara- 
tions of men upon oath examined by a court of their 
own brethren. What seemed fitted to cover with dis- 
grace that Assembly, or those members of it who 
recorded their judgment was this, that only a small 
minority was present. The greater number of the 
members had retired at the close of the discussion on 
the relevancy of the libel, never dreaming that ere the 
light of another day, the question of the guilt of the 
accused should be entered upon. When they returned 
on the following morning they were astonished to dis- 
cover what had been done.. One member, distinguished 
for his talents and acquirements, addressed the Mode- 
rator, and thus gave vent to his indignant surprise: 
‘Why sir, I should as soon have expected upon my 
return to this house, to find yourself deposed as Mr. 
Campbell of Row.’ In proceeding with the case at such 
an untimely hour, as if emulous of the zeal of an ancient 
Sanhedrim, it was indeed stated, that as there was such 
@ press of business before the Assembly, its considera- 
tion could not be postponed till the following day, and 
therefore it was absolutely necessary they should pro- 
ceed at once toa final judgment. Will such a reason 
not be regarded as the most shallow of all devices in 
the judgment of any impartial historian, when con- 
strained to record that the whole of the following day 
was occupied in discussing the admissibility of a witness 
in a case of adultery? That venerable Assembly of 
‘the purest Church of Christendom,’ the zealous 


PROCEEDINGS IN THE ASSEMBLY. 175 


guardian of the faith, exhibited this strange anomaly 
in their judicial proceedings—the bestowal of double 
the time in weighing the testimony of a single witness 
on whose testimony depended the reputation for good 
morals of one brother, that they devoted to consider- 
ing the testimonies of a numerous list of witnesses 
upon which they could alone judge of the true or false 
teaching of another regarding the most important of 
all the principles of the doctrine of Christ; upon the 
just conception of which, indeed, and of their power 
in regulating the lives of men, depends the pure morality 
of the ministers and people of all Churches. 

One striking circumstance connected with the public 
proceedings in the Church courts regarding this great 
question raised by Campbell’s preaching, was the 
absence of the Church’s most distinguished divine, 
the then celebrated Professor of Theology in the Me- 
tropolitan University. It is not needful to assign any 
reason why he declined to be a member of the Assembly 
in which Campbell was condemned, as well as in that 
which preceded it; but that given by his biographer 
for his having stood aloof from those who took part in 
that controversy is such that, unless constrained to 
record it by some paramount necessity, it ought, for the 
reputation of that great man, to have been concealed.* 
If a month’s careful reading would have been indis- 
pensable to enable him to form a right judgment, what

opinion can be formed of the competency of those hun-

caution, the indecision, the modesty, as it may be

engaged in keen controversies. . It was now manifest 
that these were but of trifling importance, and that 
when the great fundamentals of the faith were in 
hazard, they were one in sentiment, and could act 
harmoniously !”’ 

The proceedings in the Assembly were such as the 
above extract indicates. The defences and appeals 
were heard; Mr. Campbell, Mr. Story, Mr. Wylie, and 
Mr. Carlyle speaking from the bar on the one side— 
Drs. Graham, Fleming, Barr, and Hill, and Mr. H. J. 
Robertson (now Lord Benholme) on the other. Dr. 
Cook, as soon as the pleading was over, moved that Mr. 
Campbell be deposed. The Rev. Lewis Rose, minister

of Nigg (now of Tain) proposed in vain the milder sen- 
tence of suspension. 

Dr. Campbell, the defendant’s father, then spoke :— | 
“IT have in my hands,” said he, “a letter from a most 
respectable individual in the parish of Row, well known’ 
to many in this house,* accompanying a petition from 
many of the parishioners on behalf of their minister. 
Lhe petition is signed by 420 individuals, 150 of whom 
are heads of families, composing (if you except Dis-

It was stated from the bar that Mr. C.’s parish was 
against him. I have read these papers that the house 
may see this to be untrue. Moderator, I am the oldest 
father at present in this house: I have been forty 
years a minister in the Church. It'is gratifying to my 
feelings to state what I have now done, and it ought to 
be gratifying to yours—for you should be glad to hear 
that any one of your brethren has been useful in his 
parish, and is beloved by his people. 

“ A great deal was said from the other side of the 
house about dealing leniently with Mr. Campbell. 
Now I would just ask where is the leniency if you 
go into the motion on the table, and cut him off, brevi 
manu, from the Church? You have not done Mr. C. 
justice in attending to what has been this day laid 
before you. You have heard him this day in his own 
defence, and he has told you that he just teaches that 
‘God so loved the world that He gave His only-begotten 
Son, that whosoever believeth in Him should not perish, 
but have everlasting life ;’ and with regard to universal 
pardon, he has told you that he just means by it ‘that 
sinners may come to God through Jesus Christ as to a

178 SENTENCE OF DEPOSITION. 


reconciled Father.’ Now I am sure there is none among 
us all who has anything to say against this, And with 
regard to Assurance, what he says is no more than this 
-—that a sceptic is no Christian,—that doubting God is

row;’ and I can say that I never heard any preacher

of heart and life. It was certainly what I never ex-

do not stand here to deprecate your wrath. I bow to 
any decision to which you may think it right to come.
