ashamed to be the father of so holy and blameless a son.” 

These simple and affecting words of remonstrance 
uttered, the sentence of deposition was carried by 
119 to 6. 

So ended the “ Row Case,” in its day so notorious; 
to our days not barren of blessings, of which it is seldom, 
perhaps, acknowledged to be the source. The theolo- 
gical thought and teaching to which Mr. Campbell gave 
the first open impulse, and which his deposition did not 
check or deaden, has perceptibly modified the theology 


_ of Scotland. A more direct living and personal appli- 


cation of the Gospel than Scottish preaching commonly 
offered, and as the only true warrant and source of this, 
an exposition of the Atonement which should evolve a 
deeper moral and spiritual meaning than could be found 
in the popular doctrine, had become a felt necessity. 
Anselm’s scheme of “Satisfaction,” elaborated by the 
cold and legal logic of Owen’s rigid theories, was one 


EFFECT ON SCOTTISH THEOLOGY. 179 


in which surely few human souls could find rest or life. 
Nor was a final stand-point likely to be reached in the 
theory—a reaction from this—which regarded Christ's 
sufferings as merely an exhibition of Divine justice— 
a vindication of the consistency of God’s character as 
the moral governor of the world. The thoughts and 
the experience of many had either passed beyond these 
dogmas into a region where their influence was lost, 
or were seeking eagerly some clearer light than they 
could throw. These Mr. Campbell was the first to 
meet, and they received from him that direction which 
is now leading thinking men generally to regard the 
Fact of the Atonement as greater than any theory of 
it; and to judge that, since so great and fundamental a 
fact must doubtless contain the elements of many doc- 
trines, no one of which can exhaustively explain it, he 
will deal most wisely with it who connects it most 
closely with his life, and who, believing Christ himself 
to be the Atonement in whom God and man are atoned, 
reconciled, made one—seeks to be one with Him in 
living the life of sonship. The sentence which the 
Church pronounced upon the teaching which had this 
tendency, though its first result was to scare many from 
her portal, could not extinguish the spiritual necessities 
which that teaching satisfied, nor blind men’s eyes to the 
fact that the Scriptures and the Confession enfolded a 
much fuller doctrine and freer gospel than the clergy 
had been wont to draw fromthem. It is not too much 
to say, that whatever of fresher thought and more 
catholic sympathy goes with us in our search for 
truth, and obtains expression in our pulpits at this 
day, owes no small portion of its origin to the 
variously-reflected influence of the earnest words 
which, in 1831, incurred the charge of heresy. The 
x2 


180 EFFECT ON SCOTTISH THEOLOGY. 


same rigid submission to the mere letter of a logical 
system, is not now looked for, and is not now professed, 
as it was thirty years ago. These years, while they 
have developed clearer views of the relations of God 
and man, and less formal and forensic expressions of 
the great truths of the Christian faith, have surely also 
enforced the lesson that uniform identity of doctrine is 
not needful to unity of moral and spiritual life; and 
that a Church’s creed may stand some diversity of expo- 
sition, without peril to her existence or her usefulness. 
They have helped to teach the lesson, which 1s thus 
expressed by one who represents well the more liberal 
and enlightened spirit which is now quickening the 
Scottish Church, “that men cannot absolutely fix in 
their feeble symbols the truth of God—that it is ever 
bursting with its own free might the old bottles in 
which they would contain it, and that, consequently, 
according to that very law of progress by which all 
things live, it is impossible to bind the conscience by 
any bonds but those of God’s own wisdom (word) in 
Scripture—a spiritual authority addressing a spiritual 
subject—a teacher not of ‘the letter which killeth, but 
of the spirit which giveth life.’”* And that this has 
been taught, and in some measure learnt, is not indis- 
tinctly ascribable to the “Row Case.” Each cause 
must have its martyr; and it was Mr. Campbell’s 
destiny to bear the brunt of the first deliberate 
encounter between the forms of a doctrinal system 
which, emptied of their living meaning, had lost their 
strong hold over men’s consciences, and the deeper 
earnestness and spirituality which met new wants and 
aspirations with a new and diviner teaching. He 


* Very Rev. Principal Tulloch— Leaders of the Reformation.” 


MR. STORY'S REFLECTIONS ON THE SENTENCE. 181 


suffered—but theology gained thereby, and advanced 


steadily towards a broader, more tolerant, more spiri- - 


tual expansion than it had known before. 

But while this has been so, and while we can now 
clearly enough perceive this result; it need not sur- 
prise us that those of his own time who deemed Mr. 
Campbell’s deposition a grievous wrong and injustice, 
should be less ready to recognize any blessing as flow- 
ing from it, than to trace the judgments which they 
regarded as appointed to avenge it. The retribution 
of denied truth and of broken unity seems, according 
to their view, to have fallen since 1831 upon the 
Church of Scotland. Mr. Story, for one, could not 
but see this Nemesis in the subsequent events in the 
Church’s history. In the fragment of biography which 
has already afforded us a quotation, he goes on to say, 
in allusion to the self-congratulations of the Assembly 
on the unanimity displayed by the ‘“‘ Moderate” and 
“Evangelical” parties in the deposing of Mr. Campbell: 
It might certainly have been expected that the Church 
should now have rest from her controversial travails, 
her ministers showing such unity of spirit, so bound 
together in the bond of peace; stmfe had ceased 
throughout all her borders. I now write nearly 
thirty years since that crisis, when that proclama- 
tion of peace was issued in a moment of enthu- 
silastic triumph. But alas! it proved but as the 
utterance of a false prophet, and many were con- 
strained to yield to sad forebodings, thinking that 
the Great Head of the Church had indeed been sorely 
wounded in the house of His friends,—even that He 
had been crucified afresh in the denial of the great 
truth that He had “tasted death for every man;” and 
fearful words came into their memory,—“ Had Zimri 


182 THE NEMESIS. 


peace who slew his master?” Since that time has 
there been peace? In that very year the Voluntary 
Association of Anti-Church-and-State Dissenters was 
formed. A furious controversy ensued between those 
who had been foremost in the condemnation of what 
were called “the serpent doctrines of Row,” and the 
most distinguished leaders of that body. Without 
pronouncing any judgment on the merits of that con- 
troversy, it may be said of the next that arose, that 
in a fierce internecine warfare (not about any great 
principle of the doctrines of Christ, but about a 
matter of mere ecclesiastical arrangement), the two 
parties in the Church encountered each other year 
after year as in a death struggle for the superiority, 
and at last those who had been most active in the 
deposition of Mr. Campbell, and in the condemnation 
of his doctrines, casting him out of the Church of his 
fathers as an unclean thing, were themselves self- 
deposed,—dissenters from the Church of which they had 
deemed their brother to be unworthy. Little was the 
result foreseen when that proclamation was issued in 
the Assembly Hall in 1831. But, in their position as 
they journeyed triumphantly in 1843 from it to Tan- 
field, there were thoughtful men who saw in that 
movement, the Nemesis of the fatal decision which 
denounced as pestilent heresy that which, apart 
from all peculiarities of expression, was but a repe- 
tition of the announcement of the Angel to the 
Shepherds, ‘Fear not, I bring you glad tidings of 
great joy.’ ” . 

It might not be uninteresting to contrast with this 
summary decision in condemnation of Mr. Campbell’s 
teaching, the proceedings in a similar controversy 
within one of the most prominent of the Dissenting

bodies in this country, as detailed by Dr. Cairns* in 
his life of the Rev. John Brown, D.D. We have

Church, which was preferred against Mr. Campbell’s

‘“‘ His friend and fellow-sufferer,” says Dr. Cairns, 
“in the atonement controversy t (Dr. Balmer), as is

before he arrived at the conclusion that the Atone-

There is no record of Dr. Brown’s having experienced

Arminians, but among Calvinists themselves. The 
following extract may be here inserted :—‘ The 
Arminian controversy not only raised the old Pelagian 
question [that God willed the salvation of all men 
equally by the death of Christ], but also the question 
between Godeschalcus and his opponents [that our 
Lord Jesus Christ was not crucified and put to death 
for the redemption of the whole world, but only of 
those who are saved (i.e., the predestinated)]. The 
Synod of Dort which determined the first question

left the other in a great measure an open one. The 
Gomarists were bent on its being decided, but the 
English deputies had sufficient influence to prevent 
this. John Cameron, whose extensive and accurate 
learning and critical acumen do honour to his country 
and age, taught that while the elect are by an effectual 
and irrevocable calling saved through the death of 
Christ, Christ died for all men, with the intention that 
they might be invited and called to repentance ; and 
that when so invited and called, it arises from them- 
selves alone, and the hardness of their heart repelling 
the means of salvation, that they are not saved. 

At present this doctrine, with modification, 1s held by 
the great body of Evangelical Protestants on the con- 
tinent of Europe, of the Congregationalist and Bap- 
tist Churches in this country, of the Congregational 
Churches in New England, and of both the great divi- 
sions of the Presbyterian Church in America; for it 
is quite a mistake to suppose that the doctrine of a 
limited or general atonement, as understood among 
Calvinists, is one of the points of difference between 
these two bodies. * . . . With regard to the 
passages cited [from various authors on the Atone- 
ment], Dr. Brown remarks that it would perhaps not 
be easy completely to reconcile them with each other, 
or with some of the statements in the symbolical 
books. In these, we believe, will be found statements 
as strong as either the friends of an exclusively par- 
ticular, or of a general reference of the Atonement 
would wish to make.”

LETTER TO PRINGLE. 185 


gives will be interesting to readers engaged with this

The discussions in the U. P. Synod resulted in an

To return,—after the deposition, Mr. Story wrote to 
Pringle, “I have, as you would see, been in the midst 
of theological warfare. I believe that to me the 
brethren can do nothing, nor are they much inclined, 
for I hold the truth of the Standards, although, I 
freely declare that many expressions in those Stan- 
dards are not the best possible for that truth. Those 
controversies have revealed to my mind the nakedness 
of this unhappy Church. I had no conception that 
there existed so little of primitive Christianity—of the 
very first principles of the doctrine of Christ, among 
men of high reputation both for their learning and 
their piety. Even admitting Campbell to have held 
the errors for which he is condemned, I should have 
expected some sympathy with his holiness, but that 
very thing, the singular sanctity of his life and 
manners, seems to have more than anything else 
stimulated in many the fiercest animosity. But for 
the present let his cause rest with his God.” 

Again, he says, “I feel very much that I am severed 
from all men, and am anxious to find in God habitually 
all my salvation. I understand they could not find 
fault with what I stated from the bar, and yet I 
concealed nothing, which persuades me more and 
more of the reality of the device of Satan in the 
invention of words not Scriptural, to express the truth 
of God.”

186 MR. Mc LEAN’S CASE. 


The deposition. of Mr. Campbell was not the only 
exercise of authority on the part of the Assembly of 
~ 1881. It also expelled from the Church Messrs. 
McLean and Scott. The former of these gentlemen 
had in 1827 been ordained to the charge of the Scottish 
Church, London Wall.* After some years of useful 
labour there he was presented to the Church and 
Parish of Dreghorn, in Ayrshire. His settlement, 
however, was objected to, on the ground that he held 
false doctrine regarding our Lord’s human nature. 

The case came for final decision before this As- 
sembly, which unanimously agreed to deprive him of 
his licence and of all right to administer religious 
ordinances within the Church of Scotland. 

What Mr. Mc Lean’s opinions were will be learnt best 
from his own words:—He wrote to a friend, “I certainly 
believe and constantly teach that our blessed Lord, the 
consubstantial Son of the Eternal Father, did unite the 
divine nature to ruined, fallen, sin-accursed human 
nature, that He might make full atonement for trans- 
gression, and reconcile us unto God. I believe and 
teach that His human nature was the same as my own 
in all its essential properties ; and whilst I believe and 
hang my dependence upon the blessed truth that He 
was entirely and always without sin, I believe that this 
blessed fact was owing, not to any essential difference 
in the qualities and properties of His nature from the 
qualities and properties of mine, but only and certainly 
to the fact of the hypostatical union of the divine and 
human nature in his person, and the energizing pre- 
sence of the Holy Ghost, who dwelt in Him from the 


* The “Charge” delivered to Mr. M‘Lean on this occasion, by Mr. 
Irving, is one of the finest and noblest of the latter's utterances—wise— 
‘\iberal—comprehensive—lofty in thought, and grand in expression, 


~ 


MR. SCOTT'S CASE. 187 


moment of His conception in the womb of the blessed 
virgin, by whose aid also He performed all His works 
and actings in the human nature. And so firm is my 
belief in this essential article of the truth, that, by the 
grace of God, I will never alter, cancel, or deny it; no, 
not for all the kingdoms of the world and the glory of 
them.” 

Mr. Scott’s case was different. He had for some 
time acted as Mr. Irving’s assistant in London, when 
he received a call to become the minister of a con- 
gregation at Woolwich. On appearing before the 
Presbytery of London to undergo the necessary exami- . 
nations prior to ordination, he declared his inability 
to subscribe the Confession of Faith, on the ground that — 
he differed from some of the statements it contained. 
The Presbytery, of course, refused to ordain him, and 
intimated to the Presbytery of Paisley, at whose hands 
he had received licence, the declaration which Mr. 
Scott had made. This declaration being repeated on 
his being summoned before the Presbytery of Paisley, 
it was held to be equivalent to a resignation of his 
licence, and his name was struck off the list of licen- 
tiates. Against this Mr. Scott appealed, but the 
Assembly concurred with the Presbytery, and he was 
finally deprived of his licence.* 

In both these cases Mr. Story was deeply interested, 
especially in that of Mr. Scott, whom he had known 
intimately from his boyhood, and to whom his words 


* Dr. Hanna—(“ Life of Dr. Chalmers,” vol. iii. chap. 15)—states 
that Mr. M‘Lean and Mr. Scott were deprived of their licence for the

on him our ‘fallen nature.’” This was not the case as regards Mr. 
Scott. His opinions on this point were neither declared nor brought in 
question. He was dislicensed simply because he announced that the 
Westminster Confession was not the Confession of his Faith. 


188 LETTER TO MR. STEWART KER. 


and counsels had been of no slight value. He had a 
warm fellow feeling with those thus called to suffer for 
conscience sake, as he believed it only too: probable that 
he should have ere long to suffer in like manner. But 
though he anticipated libel and deposition, he did not 
feel called on to withdraw from the Church ; he would 
not quit his post unless he were driven from it. “It 
is very clear,” he writes, “that I am not called upon 
to resign, for they have not yet denied the Lord that 
bought them. They have condemned Campbell as 
holding the doctrines condemned in 1720, which I 
also condemn; and M‘Lean as holding Bourignianism, 
which I also condemn. They have condemned truth as 
if it were error, but not the truth as truth.” 

His thoughts on the whole conduct and issue of the 
three cases before the Assembly, are fully given in the 
following letter to his brother-in-law, Mr. Stewart Ker, 
then at Rio Janeiro: “My most beloved Stewart,— 
How often have I so addressed you, the mutilated sheets 
scattered up and down my sanctum can testify. Is it that 
T have little to say? I rather fear that often I have so 
much that it is vain to attempt what I desire, to throw 
my heart’s thoughts into yours. My Stewart, what a 
retrospect since we knew, since indeed we saw each 
other! for do I not turn from our private affairs to the 
state and prospects of the Church of our absent Lord. 
You will have heard before this reaches you of the 
decisions of the Assembly. With regard to Scott 
there could be no other judgment anticipated, although 
the manner they came to it cannot command the 
sympathy of any who look upon the Church as the 
pillar and ground of the truth, and any portion of the 
Church to be so regarded, only in so far as it recog- 
nizes the supreme and exclusive authority of the Holy 


LETTER TO MR. STEWART KER. 189 


Scriptures in all matters of faith and practice. Any 
licentiate of the Church of Scotland appearing at the 
bar of the supreme justiciary to avow that he held 
doctrines contrary to the standards could scarcely ex- 
pect any other result than a declaration that, if so, he 
could no longer be recognized as entitled to preach 
the gospel with their sanction. The Assembly, how- 
ever, decided recklessly, not looking beyond the 
standards. They ought to have said, as a court deli- 
berating under the eye and authority of the Lord Jesus 
Christ, the opinions you entertain are contrary to the 
Holy Scriptures, and therefore you can no longer be 
entrusted with the preaching of the gospel. The 
proposal on the part of Scott to reason the matter with 
them I have never been able to regard, although 
apparently reasonable, as in reality practicable. For 
every year there might be individuals travelling to the 
bar of the Assembly, challenging it on some other 
point or dogma, so that in fact there would be no 
other alternative than either to dispense with the Con- 
fession altogether, or to have incessant polemical dis- 
cussions upon each or all of its doctrines. 

‘“With regard to M‘Lean, while I could not but observe 
in the Assembly that condemned him a total ignorance 
of the true character of our Lord, I cannot acquit him of 
the guilt of departing from Scripture phraseology in the 
expression of truth. The truth of our Lord’s humanity 
is sufficiently expressed by the statement that He is a son 
of Adam as really as you and I are, the son of a sinful 
woman, Himself sinless, that thus He was in sucha 
condition as enabled Him entirely and perfectly to 
sympathize with all our feelings at all times, and in all 
circumstances of joy or sorrow, of trial or temptation. 
But when the words ‘consubstantial and inherent 


190 LETTER TO MR. STEWART KER. 


holiness,’ ‘holiness of nature,’ and ‘ holiness of con- 
dition,’ &c., are introduced at the bar of a court, 
where the jealousy of heresy is excited and exasper- 
ated, you must see that it is quite possible a judgment 
may be come to without any accurate conception of 
the merits of the question at issue. An unrighteous 
judgment I grant you, but unless the words of the 
condemned witness for the truth were in all respects 
fitted to convey right conceptions of truth, the Church 
in the act of Assembly is not to be held as condemning ° 
or casting out the truth, which I perceive is maintained 
by a great majority of the orthodox believers in our 
Lord’s humanity. 

“With regard to our beloved Campbell, similar 
observations may be made. It is not true,—it is not 
found in any declaration of Scripture, that all the sins 
of all mankind without exception and without distinc- 
tion are forgiven; although it be true that the object 
of faith presented to all in the gospel 1s God in Christ 
reconciling the world to Himself, not imputing unto 
men their trespasses. He is not forgiving any in his 
sins so as to deliver him from judgment, and, there- 
fore, although intended to express a true thing and a 
most blessed reality in the revealed character of God, 
the proposition alluded to, and for holding which he 
has been condemned, is not a sound form of words. 
It is true that Campbell has explained what he meant, 
but that was imperfect. He ought to have done as 
day after day I entreated him, at once disavow the 
expressions, and express his resolution never to use 
them but as Scripture warranted, provided he was 
permitted under the protection of the Church to preach 
to every sinner the forgiveness of sins, declaring that 
nothing prevented his return to God but his own 


DECLINE OF HOSTILE FEELING. 191 


unbelief of God’s love. When they prosecute me, and 
if they do so, to deposition, it must be for the truth as 
it is in Jesus, for I have not erred in expression, 
having confined myself to God’s own declaration by 
the mouth of the prophets, and apostles, and His Son 
Jesus Christ. The Presbytery purpose to prosecute 
me. A motion has been made to appoint a committee 
to consider whether any steps ought to be taken 
against certain officebearers within the bounds of 
the Presbytery, thereby including Mr. Dunlop, one of 
the Row elders, and myself. In a year or two, there- 
fore, it is probable I shall be a wanderer like your own 
Emperor ;* and be thankful, my brother, that the 
thorough persuasion of having only the truth to con- 
tend for has, by the grace and tender mercy of God, 
prevented me from having even one momentary feeling 
of anxiety about the matter. I have no feeling as 
regards myself, but I mourn over a deluded and faith- 
less Church.” 

This was written in June. Shortly after, a return 
of his former malady aggravated by mental anxiety 
and bodily fatigue laid him aside for many weeks. 
When he appeared at the Presbytery in September, 
in recovered health, the motion to which he refers 
above was quietly allowed to lapse. ‘There was 
not a whisper,” he writes, “about the motion on 
the table; so when they themselves said nothing, of 
course no obligation of any kind was imposed upon me 
to ask what they meant to do. As my way was clear, 
and as I had no doubt in my own mind of the part 
that remained for me to perform, I receive as the 
intimation of the will of God this singular conclu- 


* Of Brazil. 


192 CLOSE OF THIS SECTION OF HIS HISTORY. 


sion to their proceedings.” Thus died away the last 
threat of ecclesiastical censure of his teaching or inter- 
ference with his usefulness, the last menace of exile 
from his beloved Gairloch, the dread of which had 
during these months of warfare and of sickness been 
very present to his mind. In one of his letters he 
says, pathetically, ‘The shores of the Gairloch are 
exquisitely beautiful at present, and, indeed, seem 
more so than ever. Whether it be that the impression 
on my mind, that soon I shall be far away from them, 
adds to their interest and loveliness, I know not, 
but I am often reminded of the words of the Canticles 
as I survey them, ‘O turn away thine eyes, for I am 
sick of love.’ ” 

Having now followed to its conclusion the external 
history of the Row Case* in as far as it affected the 
subject of this memoir, we must go back to trace the 
growth of another movement of @ less direct and 
simple character, associated in the common notions 
and language of our time with the name of the Rev. 
Edward Irving. |

the Atonement will be found in his latest work, “ Nature of the Atone-

CHAPTER V. 


MARY CAMPBELL—‘ GIFTS”—IRVING. 


Dorine the progress of the Row controversy, began, 
as has been indicated, a religious movement of a widely 
different nature. Its source was not to be found in 
any new dogmatic views, or in any novel expansion of 
Christian life, but in the professed revival of an aged - 
belief and restoration of primitive gifts. Its idea was 
restoration, not development. Its growth was unex- 
pected and abnormal. Nothing in the religious con- - 
dition of the time can be assigned positively as its root 
and cause; unless, indeed, we accept as such, the ten- 
dency, common to all periods of change and excitement, 
whether religious or political, to rush from the extreme 
of repression to the extreme of licence,—the tendency 
which, in the political world, has produced the Red 
Republican; in the religious, the German Anabaptist 
and the English Quaker. 

From the level of a time in which, after long stag- 
nation, religious thought was beginning to move freely, 
suddenly emerged a society claiming direct intercourse 
with and inspiration from Heaven, and the power of 
working superhuman works. The philosophy of its 
origin remains to be propounded: this was the simple 
fact. 


In tracing the facts on which these claims were 
9] 


\ 


194 MARY CAMPBELL. 


based, the path which we must follow, while it leads us to 
the stately porch of the Church which asserts her special 
claim to the titles “ Holy, Catholic, and Apostolic,” 
sets out from the humble threshold of the house at Fer- 
nicarry, which we have already heard of as the home 
of Isabella Campbell. 

In Isabella’s Memoir, reference is made to a pecu- 
liar sorrow of her sister Mary’s, in which she was 
called to sympathize. This was the death of a young 
man to whom Mary was engaged to be married, and 
with whom she had intended to go abroad on a mission to 
the heathen. His death frustrated the accomplishment 
of her design, but did not abate her desire to carry it 
out; and her mind continued to dwell upon it with 
intense interest, hopeless though the project seemed. 
She was in ill health, and exhibited obvious symptoms 
of the disease which had carried off her saintly sister.* 
Her malady, however, was not of a nature to render 
society dangerous, or impossible to her. And society 
she had in abundance. The interest excited by Isa- 
bella’s Memoir, drew many to Fernicarry, to see the 
sister, whose name was so often in its pages; and that 
interest, at first attached to the memory of the one, ere 
long transferred itself to the living presence of the 
other. She was a woman of great personal attractions, 
had a beautiful face, and soft eyes, with drooping lids, 
which she seldom raised. She was very clever, and, 
considering her obscure circumstances, was well in- 
formed. Her character, however, lacked the moral 
strength of Isabella’s, and her enthusiastic imaginative 


* Not the ordinary tubercular consumption, but the formation of 
large abscesses on the lungs, which from time to time burst, and in 
doing so reduced the patient to extreme weakness. During the inter- 
vals she would rally, and enjoy comparatively good health. 


HER MISSIONARY SPIRIT. 195 


mind was not so strictly controlled as might have 
been desired, by keen and clear instincts or per- 
ceptions of nght and wrong. There was in her, 
in fact, much of the nature and disposition which 
have, from age to age, furnished the Church with 
mystics. 

A young, beautiful, not highly educated, and withal 
excitable invalid, could not but suffer a certain distortion 
of the morale of her nature, and be led—insensibly it 
may be—towards the borders of delusion and vanity, 
when she found herself the cynosure of the eyes of a 
large portion of the religious public, and beheld some 
company of its pilgrims ever and anon resorting to her 
shrine. Among those who thus came to Fernicarry, were 
some whose minds were much engaged with the idea to 
which, at the time, Mr. Irving’s teaching had directed 
public attention, viz., that bodily disease was the direct 
infliction of Satan, and that, therefore, faith and prayer, 
and these only, should be employed as the means of 
deliverance from it; and that moreover, by the due 
exercise of these, the power of effecting miracles of 
healing and other wonderful works would be restored 
to the Church—a power hitherto kept im abeyance, 
because of the Church’s faithlessness. 

The subject of missions was also profusely talked 
about in Mary’s sick room, and the idea of a due pre- 
paration for evangelistic work was more or less bound 
up with the new notions regarding the restoration of 
spiritual gifts. To Mary herself, the central point in 
all the discussions and speculations that went on around 
her, was the preaching of the Gospel to the heathen. 
A small band of intended missionaries gathered at 
Fernicarry—funds were collected—clothes provided— 


and prayer was made to God continually that their way 
02 


f 


196 HER ASSOCIATES, 


might be directed according to His will. This was in 
the winter of 1829-30. 

To Mrs. Campbell, whose eldest son had for months 
past been on his death-bed, the constant stream of 
visitors which the discussions and arrangements about 
this projected enterprise conducted to Fernicarry, 
became excessively trying. She frequently complained 
to her minister of the hardships to which the ceaseless 
influx of Mary’s admirers and coadjutors subjected her. 
At last, on one occasion, when he found her on a bleak, 
cold day rinsing clothes at the “burn,” near the house, 
she told him that though she never went to bed 
** the day she rose,”’ yet, so great was the burden cast 
on her by these visitors, that she was utterly unable to 
give the attention that was indispensable to the comfort 
of her dying son. There were, she said, at that 
moment two young men in the house, one of whom had 
been there for some days, and there was no hint of his 
much longed-for departure. Mr. Story at once went 
in, and on entering the little parlour, he found the 
pair, of whom she had spoken, comfortably seated 
beside the fire with books and writing materials on 
a small table before them. Stung into quick wrath 
at the contrast between their comfort and Mrs. 
Campbell’s forlorn washing operations under the 
wintry sky, he at once told them that they ought 
immediately to be gone; that they had no right to 
oppress their hostess and idly eat the bread of charity, 
as they were doing, instead of attending to their 
ordinary avocations. To this, one of them starting 
up and stamping his foot, replied— Get thee behind 
me, Satan ;” and Mr. Story remaining unmoved by this 
exorcism, the other proceeded to explain that they were 
in their present position by the Lord’s command, in 


AND THEIR PROJECTS. 197 


order to be prepared for His work, and that it was an 
honour to Mrs. Campbell to be permitted to minister to 
the Saints. 

Why then, said Mr. Story, if preparing for mission- 
ary work, were they not acquiring a knowledge of the 
languages in which the Holy Scriptures were written, 
and of the people to whom they meant to proclaim the 
Gospel. No preparations of that kind, he was answered, 
were needful; and unbelief alone could suggest the 
thought of such carnal preliminaries. 

If so, pursued the inexorable minister, why not cross 
over to Cowal? There were thousands of Gaelic-speaking 
people there, who kuew very little of the Glad Tidings : 
why not try their virgin powers on them? To sucha 
rash experiment, it need hardly be said, they felt the 
Lord did not call them. 

On Mr. Story’s going from them to Samuel Campbell’s 
room, the sick man reiterated the complaints which 
had been made by his mother, and he added, that his 
rest was night after night entirely destroyed by the 
continual talking and psalm-singing in his sister's 
chamber above him. 

To her Mr. Story related all that had passed, and 
urged upon her that it was her obvious duty to send 
away these foolish young men, and to discourage 
the renewal of the visiting and noise, which were 
so grievous to her mother and dying brother. The 
letter which is subjoined was received a few days 
after he had thus spoken to her. It not only gives 
her views regarding the matter alluded to, but dis- 
closes the strange state of feeling and of belief, in 
which she was waiting for a new revelation from 
God. The whole letter is inserted, not because of 
any intrinsic merit it possesses, but as unfolding 


198 LETTER FROM MARY CAMPBELL. 


the faith and expectation of this woman who—let 
it be borne in mind—was the first to exhibit the 
phenomenon which was asserted to be “speaking with 
tongues ;” and of whom, two years after this, Irving 
could write to Mr. Story, from London—“ She is a 
saint of God, and hath the gift of prophecy.” The 
letter bears no date, but must have been written early 
in the year 18380. 

‘My dear Mr. Story,—I have not been at liberty to 
attempt writing to you until the present moment. I 
bless God who does not now suffer me to write when [ 
please, or to whom J please, but when He pleases and 
wHaT He pleases. There is Now to me a delightful 
depth of meaning in the words being BIDDEN and 
FORBIDDEN by the Holy Ghost, and I fear taking one 
single step in anything unless I see the pillar of fire 
or of cloud moving before me. The Lord from hence- 
forth ever keep me thus dependent upon Himself, and 
so I shall be to the praise of the glory of His grace in 
Christ Jesus ! 

“You remember me saying to you that I felt per- 
suaded God was revealing to me by the Spirit the 
state of mind many of his children were in, and the 
particular temptations Satan was about to ply them 
with. Well, I am more fully convinced that it is the 
ease, and I see plainly from the Scriptures how such 
things may be. We are there told, that we know not 
things we should pray for as we ought, but the ‘ Spirit 
maketh intercession for us, with groanings which 
cannot be uttered.’ ‘The Spirit searcheth all things, 
yea the deep things of God.’ ‘The Spirit maketh 
Intercession for the saints according to the will of 
God.’ 

‘Well then, if the Spirit of God dwell in me, and 


Sr ST NS 


LETTER FROM MARY CAMPBELL. 199 


I pray in the Spirit, I must know by the things which 
He teaches me to ask, for those for whom I pray, what 
state they are in. Nor does this view rob God in the 
least of the prerogative He justly claims as His, (‘I 
the Lord search the heart,’) because it is His Spint, 
(and not mine, which only knoweth the things of men). 
Nevertheless, if His dwell in me, it maketh intercession 
for the saints according to His will. 

“JT was reading last night the history of Elisha, in 
connection with this very subject, and it has tended 
mightily to confirm me in the opinion, that were we 
filled with the Holy Ghost as we ought, we would 
know what is passing in men’s hearts, just in the same 
way as the Man Christ Jesus, by having the Father 
dwelling in him, knew the thoughts of those who were 
resisting the blessed truths which He spake. I think 
it is very striking, what the servants of the Syrian 
monarch answered him, when in the anguish of his 
heart he said to them, ‘ Will you not show me which 
of us is for the King of Israel?’ And one of the 
servants said, ‘None, my Lord, O King! but Elisha, 
the prophet that is in Israel, telleth the King of Israel 
the words that thou speakest in thy bedchamber:?’ 
2 Kings vi. 11, 12. Now, who could tell Elisha these 
things but the Spirit of God? What is recorded in 
the thirty-second verse of this chapter is also very 
striking: ‘ But Elisha sat in his house (and the elders 
with him), and the king sent a man from before him, 
but ere the messenger came to him, he said to the 
elders, See how this son of a murderer hath sent to 
take away mine head? look when the messenger 
cometh, shut the door; is not the sound of his 
master’s feet close behind him? This passage not 
only convinced me, that if we have the Spirit of God, 


200 LETTER FROM MARY CAMPBELL. 


we will know what things are about to happen, but it 
also shows me how very anxious God is that we 
should give the glory of such things to his own Syrit, 
by the care he takes to inform us that Elisha foretold 
the evil that was determined against him, ere the mes- 
senger that the king sent had arrived. I am aware 
it may be said to me that Elisha was a prophet, that 
God spoke specially to him, and recalled certain things 
unto him; but that, seeing that I am not, it is pre- 
sumptuous to suppose that God would speak to me 
as he spoke to him, or discover things to me or to 
any one else of God’s children as He did. But, I 
reply, I see no reason why He should not. We pos- 
sess the character of those upon whom the Lord hath 
promised to pour out in the last days his Spirit and 
‘they shall prophesy.’ 

“T cannot, my dear Mr. Story, leave off this subject 
without informing you of a remarkable circumstance 
which has just taken place. About a week ago, it was 
strongly impressed upon my mind that I should speedily 
receive a letter from an unknown person to whom the 
Memoir had been blessed, and last night (a night to me 
of very high communion) the impression became so 
very strong, as to constrain me to inquire at the Lord 
concerning this matter, which I had scarcely done 
until the Spirit bade me to ask it from the Father in the 
name of Jesus, and believe that I received it, and I 
should have it, which I did; yea, so little did I doubt 
in this thing, that I gave thanks to my Father for 
having blessed me with this letter. Judge then of my

suppose that there will not be enough for him and

And what could anyone say of the man who did this ? 
That he was expecting too much,eor that his most un- 
bounded expectations would not be realized? The 
eloquence of the unlearned fisherman drew forth from 
the multitude glory to God; and were such eloquence 
heard again in our world, men would again be com- 
pelled to ascribe the glory of it to God. But indepen- 
dent of the dishonour done to God, in not expecting 
from him all the armour that we need to fight his 
battles—there is another consideration that prevents me 
from saying to anyone to study, in order to qualify 
himself for the work of the Lord. And it is this—the 
time is short. I expect to see the Redeemer on earth 
long before one (though he were to commence his studies 
immediately) could be ready or fit, according to the 
judgment of men, for entering into the field of Christian 
labour... * * . * 

“TI am much afflicted before God, because His people 
will not believe that this day is so near at hand; but 
their unbelief will not hinder it, thanks be unto God! 
The language of the Apostle to the primitive Chris- 
tians, ‘but ye are not in darkness, that that day 
should overtake you unawares,’ is not applicable I fear 
to the Christians of the present day. Oh! that they 
would cry aloud to the unbelieving world, to repent and 
give God glory, ‘because the acceptable year of the 
Lord’ is about to close, and ‘the day of vengeance 
of our God’ to commence. I look for great tribulation 
shortly, and no cessation of it until the Lord come.

blood, however, lie at owr door; let us beseech them

and He who is now a ‘ refuge’ from the storm, shall be

Soon after this singular letter was written, the 
looked-for gift of tongues was supposed to be granted. 
On a Sunday evening in the month of March, Mary, 
in the presence of a few friends, began to utter sounds 
to them incomprehensible, and believed by her to be a 
tongue such as of old might have been spoken on the 
day of Pentecost, or among the Christians of Corinth. 
This was the first manifestation of the restored “ gift,” 
—for such it was imagined to be. She desired to 


THE M‘DONALDS AT PORT GLASGOW. 205 


ascertain what the tongue was, in order that she might, 
if strengthened to do so, repair to the country where 
it was intelligible, and there begin her long-contem- 
plated labours. By and by she announced that she 
believed it to be the language of a group of islands in 
the Southern Pacific Ocean; but, as nobody knew the 
speech of the islanders, it was impossible either to 
refute or corroborate her assertion; and, for the present 
at least, she was unable to proceed in person in quest 
of the remote savages, whose mother tongue she held 
had been revealed to her. 

While she at Fernicarry was engrossed with her new 
linguistic powers, a company of devout persons at the 
dull little town of Port Glasgow—some fifteen miles ~ 
away—were daily meeting to unite in prayer for a 
special outpouring of the Holy Spirit. Among these 
was a pious family named M‘Donald, consisting of two 
brothers and three sisters, one of the latter of whom 
had for eighteen months been in very bad health. 
These good people, along with some others, had been led 
to pray for and to expect the restoration of “ spiritual 
gifts” to the Church, by a sermon on the nature of 
the “ Charismata” of the Corinthians, preached by 
Mr. A. J. Scott. In a Memoir of the two brothers 
published in 1840,* after an account of some peculiar 
manifestations of God’s presence vouchsafed to the 
invalid sister, we are told (p. 107), that “for several 
days had been so unusually ill, that I quite 
thought her dying, and, on appealing to the doctor, he 
held out no hope of her recovery, unless she were able 
to go through a course of powerful medicine, which he

Robert Norton, M.D. London: J. F. Shaw, Southampton-row. 1840. 


206 GIFT OF “ HEALING” AT PORT GLASGOW. 


acknowledged to be, in her then case, impossible. Mrs. 
and myself had been sitting quietly at the bed- 
side, when the power of the Spirit came upon her. She 
said, ‘ There will be a mighty baptism of the Spirit 
this day,’ and then broke forth in a most marvellous 
setting forth of the wonderful works of God; and, as 
if her own weakness had been altogether lost in the 
strength of the Holy Ghost, continued, with httle or 
no intermission, for two or three hours in mingled 
praise, prayer, and exhortation. 

“ At dinner-time, James and George came home as 
usual,* whom she then addressed at great length, 
concluding with a solemn prayer for James, that he 
might at that time be endowed with the power of the 
Holy Ghost. Almost instantly James calmly said, ‘I 
have got it.” He walked to the window, and stood 
silent for a minute or two. I looked at him, and 
almost trembled, there was such a change upon his 
whole countenance. He then, with a step and manner 
of the most indescribable majesty, walked up to ’s 
bed-side, and addressed her in those words of the twen- 
tieth Psalm, ‘ Arise, and stand upright.’ He repeated 
the words, took her by the hand, and she arose 
when we all quietly sat down and had our dinner. 
After it, my brothers went to the building-yard, as 
usual, where James wrote over to Miss Campbell, com- 
manding her in the name of the Lord to arise. The 
next morning, after breakfast, James said, ‘I am going 
down to the quay, to see if Miss C. is come across

* Their trade was that of shipbuilders, which they steadily and

MARY CAMPBELL’S RESTORATION TO HEALTH. 207 


result showed how much he knew of what God had 
done, and would do for her; for she came-as he

The following is Mary’s own account of her recovery, 
also embodied in Mr. Norton’s book :—‘“ On the Satur- 
day previous to my restoration to health I was very ill, 
suffering from pain in my chest and breathlessness. 
On the Sabbath I was very ill, and lay for several hours 
in a state of insensibility. Next day I was worse than 
I had been for several weeks previous (the agony of 
Saturday excepted). On Tuesday I was no better. On 
Wednesday I did not feel quite so languid, but was 
suffering some pain from breathing and palpitation of 
my heart. Two individuals who saw me about four 
hours before my recovery, said that I would never be 
strong—that I was not to expect a miracle to be 
wrought upon me. It was not long after until I 
received dear brother James M‘Donald’s letter, giving 
an account of his sister’s being raised up, and com- 
manding me to rise and walk. I had scarcely read the 
first page when I became quite overpowered, and laid 
it aside for a few minutes; but I had no rest in my 
mind until I took it up again and began to read. As 
I read, every word came home with power; and when 
I came to the command to arise, it came home with a 
power which no words can describe; it was felt to be 
indeed the voice of Christ ; it was such a voice as could 
not be resisted. A mighty power was instantaneously 
exerted upon me: I felt as if I had been lifted from 
off the earth, and all my diseases taken from off me at 
the voice of Christ. I was verily made in a moment 
to stand upon my feet, leap and walk, sing and rejoice.” 

These occurrences being bruited abroad, naturally 
excited great wonder and dispute. Many were the

208 DR. CHALMERS’ INQUIRIES. 


inquiries, criticisms, denunciations called forth by 
novelties so unprecedented. 

Amid the general excitement, Mr. Story looked on 
calmly and earnestly—desirous only to sift the matter 
thoroughly, and to reach the real truth. 

On the 24th April, Dr. Chalmers writes to him :— 
“Do give me, if you can with pleasure to yourself, your 
information and views about the gift of tongues said to 
be now descending on people in the West. Could you 
transmit a copy of the handwriting by Mary Campbell 
alleged to be in an unknown language?* I have 
written to Mr. Campbell (of Row) on the same subject.” 
To this request Mr. Story despatched the following 
reply, which conveys with sufficient distinctness his 
own impressions of the phenomena inquired into :— 

**Keppoow, April, 1830. 

“My dear Sir,—For several days I have been just 
upon the point of writing you about the memorable 
things that are passing before me, so that you may 
conclude I very willingly answer your letter. Often I 
have visited and spoken to Mary Campbell as a dying 
believer, and every medical man that saw her declared 
that she could not possibly recover. Even still Dr. 
Bryce, who has been in attendance upon her and her 
brother, persists in denying that she is well; and in 
proof of what he conceives to be a true judgment of 
her case, said to me t’ other day, that he has known 
hectic patients rally and manifest extraordinary strength, 
even the very day before their death. He is quite sure

she will kill herself. For three or four months she

she was persuaded that God was to raise her up, and

divine life, was uppermost in her mind—the speaking 
about the Gospel among the heathen. You observe I

people as well as ministers, women as well as men, are

greater jealousy manifested by you and others who, 
from your station, are much in the eye of the Church,

delusion, it is one of the most cunning Satan ever 
devised. Many here say it is of the Devil, while even 
some impartial people of the world I have heard

LETTER TO DR. CHALMERS, 211 


* The individuals who thus testify are very holy per- 
sons. Should the gift be conclusively ascertained, I 
see only, for my own part, what would to me be in 
perfect accordance with the past manifestations of 
God’s power. Ever since Mary Campbell became a 
Christian, her desire has been to make known the 
Gospel: for six years such has been the passion of 
this holy young woman. Many others have imbibed 
the same spirit; and if some can scarcely see Scrip- 
tural authority for such a movement as her going forth 
on this errand, yet such a gift (of tongues) would 
obviously annihilate all objection from preconceived 
opinions ; and the fact of a numerous band of young 
persons of both sexes going forth with the Gospel so 
sanctioned, 1s well fitted to reprove our dead Church. 
But while many considerations conspire, In my mind, 
to efface any obstacle to the willing belief of what is 
alleged as having been, and still is, manifested by God 
in Mary and others, I feel that I am called upon to be 
especially jealous in my scrutiny, seeing that it involves 
such weighty and important consequences. I am very 
anxious to have the matter fully and thoroughly inves- 
tigated; and with this view, I was just on the point of 
writing you, as well as Drs. Gordon and Thomson, 
when your letter arrived. If a delusion, the more 
speedily it is given to the winds, the better. If it be 
a reality, then one conclusion I know you will at once 
feel to be utterly unavoidable. Before I conclude I 
have the prospect of seeing Mary, and of receiving the 
characters you are naturally so desirous to see. For 
my own part, I recognize in none I have seen the signs 
of any language I know, and therefore to me they are 
not evidence; but many have seen her note them down,

212 MR.S. INCLINED TO BELIEVE IN THE “GIFTS.” 


self were unconscious of the exertion. Both in speaking 
and writing she describes her words and movements 
as in every respect independent of her own volition.” 

From this letter it is evident that the writer of it 
had no preconceived idea about spiritual gifts, which 
would have hindered his recognizing them and acknow- 
ledging their reality, had he considered himself entitled 
to do so; and also that, though he had not at this time 
in any way tested the phenomena which claimed to be 
the restored gifts of the early Church, his belief in the 
pious character of those who exhibited them inclined 
him, if not to adopt their views concerning them, at 
least to regard them without suspicion. He was 
endowed with so large a portion of that most excellent 
of all gifts, the charity that “thinketh no evil,” that 
suspicion or imputation of unworthy motive was in any 
case foreign and abhorrent to his nature. ‘“ The irre- 
sistible logic of facts,” howgver, did not permit him to 
remain long favourably affected towards this new reli- 
gious movement, because it undermined his confidence 
in those who were assuming a prominent part in its 
direction. 

The nature and result of his further experience and 
observation will be best learned from the correspon- 
dence that follows. To understand it aright itis needful 
to state that, after her “miraculous” restoration to 
health, Mary Campbell had (in 1831) married a young 
man named W. R. Caird, whase acquaintance she had 
made on his coming to Fernicarry (from Edinburgh, 
where he was a clerk in a writer’s office) to see Isa- 
- bella’s home. With him she went to Mr. Irving’s, in 
London, and then to Brampton Park, where Caird 
acted for a short time as a lay missionary in the em- 
- ployment of Lady O. B. Sparrow. Lady Olivia desiring 


MARY CAMPBELL IS MARRIED. 213 


by-and-by to engage some other agent, he and his wife 
quitted Brampton for Albury Park, and became the - 
guests of Mr. Drummond. After some time Mrs. Caird 
visited Scotland. Again and again she passed and 
repassed Rosneath Manse, but never called. At length, 
one Saturday, Mr. Story, who had gone up to Fernicarry 
to visit her mother, saw on the shore a group of elegantly- 
dressed women, conspicuous among whom he recognized 
Mrs. Caird. A few years before he had seen her in 
that house hard by, a destitute girl, and had heard her 
declare that the Eternal Spirit had bidden her rise 
from her sick bed to go forth to evangelize the heathen, 
else she and her father’s house should be destroyed, 
and that speedily. A sufficiently vivid contrast; not 
without painful suggestions to him, and rendered still 
more vivid by the fact that where he saw Mrs. Caird . 
walking “in silk attire,” one of her sisters, clad in the 
coarsest garb, had not long before been seen loading 
a dung-cart in the adjoining field. That day he had 
no opportunity of private conversation with her. An 
interview which he subsequently had with her, in the 
presence of Lady Harriet Drummond, with whom she 
was staying at Greenock, occasioned the following 
letter :-— 

“My dear Mary,—You could not but see that the 
conversation yesterday unavoidably became in an espe- 
cial manner personal to you. It was painful to me at 
the time and has not ceased to beso. I had no doubt, 
that unless some feeling, not as it ought to be, had 
existed in your mind, you would not have been so long 
in the country without having seen me. And I could 
not but acknowledge a leading of Providence in my at 
length meeting you as I did, on the very day on which 
I had been reading over the alleged declarations of the 


214 MR. STORY’S LETTER TO MRS. CAIRD. 


Holy Ghost by you several years before. Then you 
heard a voice, that unless you rose and proceeded 
without delay to declare the Gospel to the heathen, you 
and your father’s house should perish. After the lapse 
of years I met you returned, and not one heathen man 
or woman had ever received the minutest fragment of 
God’s Gospel from -your lips. The command was 
God’s, or it was not. If it was, what did I see but 
one in the spirit of Jonah refusing to obey? If it was 
not, how necessary was it to the glory of God, and the 
interests of truth, that your sin and delusion should at 
once be confessed before Him and all the people. You 
have either spoken in His name when He gave you no 
sanction, and thus sinned profanely and presumptu- 
ously: or if upon your own averment it must be held 
true, that by you the Eternal Spirit spoke, then your 
conscience must be habitually defiled by resistance to 
His will, and you, until now, must have been sinning 
against the clearest hght. . . . I wished much to 
have seen you for some time alone, but, as this you have 
carefully avoided, I must judge of appearances, and in 
faithfulness and love would I, as your former pastor and 
friend, press upon you, as you shall answer before the 
Lord Jesus at His coming, to ponder what I set before 
you as the words of truth and soberness :—-You must 
remember that at the time of your marriage I warned 
Mr. Caird, that if you spoke the truth, and believed 
what you wished others to believe, he had no liberty or 
sanction to marry you, unless prepared to go forth as a 
missionary. I saw then, in the theory that he had 
formed of first witnessing to the Roman earth, the 
principle that would gradually undermine the autho- 
rity of the revelation you professed to have received. 
Of all persons I ever knew, you are liable to be excited

by the presence of others, and originally having a

Christianity must be generated by the excitement and 
the apparatus of the present day, when in a group of 
Christian people, no testimony was lifted up for God, 
no gratitude expressed to him, even by the ‘ angel’* 
present, who could not but know, without any new 
revelation, that your present condition, comfort, and 
consideration were traceable directly as a stream to its 
fountain, to your Christian profession. You owe every- 
thing to your reputation for Christianity. Is your 
husband a blessing ? He would never have heard of 
you, but for your Christianity. Are Lady Harriet’s 
society, patronage, and bounty, blessings? Would you 
have enjoyed one of them but for your Christian repu- 
tation ? Look at any seamstress, labouring early and 
late for a miserable subsistence, and at your present 
condition, your very servant arrayed in silken attire, 
and so long as you have a being be covered with shame 
and confusion of face for the silence of that moment, 
when, more perhaps than at any other crisis of your 
personal history, it became you to testify for God in 
the presence of those whom you were thus beguiling 
and preventing from giving to Him due glory. Now, 
what then occurred has impressed me more and more 
deeply with a conviction of the false and unscriptural 
notions you must now have of holiness. Surely mere 
absence of outward crimes, mere conformity to outward 
observances, the mere crucifixion of the flesh when 


* This gentleman subsequently became a clergyman of another 
Church. 


218 EXHORTS HER TO A TRUER RELIGION. 


that is easy, or renouncement of Mammon’s service 
among worldly people,—these cannot constitute holi- 
ness. The knowledge of God, approbation of all He 
does, obedience to all He enjoins, thankfulness for all 
He gives, trust in Him for all we need,—that is holi- 
ness; knowledge of what He is, of necessity precedes 
and accompanies all that follows. I know well the 
soul-destroying error which Satan engrafts upon the 
truth, that whosoever is in Christ is safe and holy, and 
the antinomian contempt which it induces of all the 
various moralities of life, as if the Christian life were 
a tune played upon one string, and not a grave sweet 
melody, most glorious in the ear of him whom it is 
raised to glorify. . . . In regard to your thorough 
persuasion of being still possest by a Divine Power, I 
beg you to advert to what must be my conviction of 
the force of your argument, ‘is it likely that a person 
walking in all holiness and nearpess to God, with a 
good conscience, should be so deceived ?’ Your silence 
already alluded to was an index to the kind of holiness 
you meant. Did that indicate your advancement in 
the knowledge of God, obedience to His word, thank- 
fulness for His gifts, trust in Him for what you 
needed? O1 was it a proof that since we last met 
you had been walking worthy of God who hath called 
you to His kingdom and glory ? Where a fundamental 
error regarding holiness exists, there can be no spiritual 
health. 

*“O! I can imagine Isabella,—indeed I have her 
countenance vividly before me, and I do not mistake 
the expression of astonishment, sorrow, pity, disap- 
pointment, and holy indignation with which it seems to 
say, with many sighs and tears, ‘O Mary! Mary! why 
are you silent?’ The certainty of your being under 


PLEADS WITH HER. 219 


the influence of some fatal error was not disproved by 
your silence, and the ‘utterance in power, which I 
witnessed, left the question just as it was. The 
manner of it exhibited nothing beyond human power 
or the workings of human fancy; but in saying this 
I do not implicate your sincerity, for it has been 
often shown how unconsciously people have been the 
dupes of the workings of their own mysterious fancies. 
The matter of it expressed nothing beyond what I was 
propounding. In so far as it went, it corroborated my 
teaching regarding holiness ; while, at the same time, 
it was the utterance of an accommodating spirit, being 
artfully framed to induce those present who hesitated 
about adopting what I said regarding union with 
Christ as productive of holiness, to reject the light, by 
making them suppose that any difference of opinion 
was identical with dissolution of that union. 

“J thus plainly express myself, and you must 
understand that I regarded the ‘utterance’ as artfully 
framed, by whatever energy prompted. It confirmed 
what I taught, yet was fitted to induce rejection of the 
truth, should that disturb the harmony of those who 
were united in the belief of its own preternatural 
origin and authority, or who might regard difference 
of opinion identical with dissolution of union with 
Christ. 

“But I must have done, and in conclusion, with 
much affection as well as prayerful anxiety, 1 com- 
mend you to God. May He save you from going 
astray and teach you what it really is to be holy. 
You have been led into temptation, and I fear that 
you have not, with knowledge of the temptation, 
prayed ‘deliver us from evil.’ You have rushed, not 
without warning, into the midst of the evil. You have 


220 LETTER FROM MR. HENRY DRUMMOND. 


thrown yourself upon the billows, nor are you unwil- 
lingly carried along. Where you are I know not, but 
of this I am persuaded, you are not where Isabella 
left you. A few months’ residence under the roof 
where Isabella so glorified God, while your husband is 
in America, might yet restore the faded lineaments of 
the meek, and lowly, and thankful mind of Jesus. 
A most painful crucifixion I have no doubt you would 
find it to be, but you know how much in the days of 
your adversity your poor mother laboured for you as 
hard as a slave; and I am sure He who framed our 
social duties could not but rejoice to see in youa 
desire to compensate in some degree, by your presence 
and filial reverence and attentions, for what she so 
willingly and patiently did and bore for you.” 

To this letter Mrs. Caird herself vouchsafed no reply, 
but it did not lack an answer. The following missive 
proceeded from the pen of the versatile gentleman who 
in his own person combined the diverse functions of 

‘ country gentleman at Albury Park, banker at Charing 
_ Cross, licensed jester of the House at Westminster, 
and Apostle of the “ Holy Catholic Apostolic Church ” 
' everywhere. 

“19 June, 1834. 

‘** Sir,— Mrs. Caird, in the absence of her husband, 
has put into my hands, as her pastor, a letter from 
you to her, in which you charge her with professing 
religion from mercenary motives, making a gain of 

" godliness. Partly through Mr. Irving, partly through 
Lady Olivia Sparrow, and partly from my own know- 
ledge, I can prove the charge to be as false as it is 
base; and it comes with peculiar bad grace from a 
man who notoriously holding the opinions which drove 
Mr. John Campbell out of the Church of Scotland, 


REPLY TO MR, DRUMMOND. 221 


has contrived to mask them so as to retain his stipend. 
I shall give the letter into Mr. Caird’s hands en his 
return, and leave him to deal with it as he thinks fit. 
. Tam, &c., Henry Drummond.” 

To this apostolical rescript, Mr. Story replied: 
‘** Sir,—I have received your letter in reference to what 
I had written to Mrs. Caird, and have to say I have 
read it with much sorrow on your account, because of 
the painfully unchristian irritation of feeling under 
which you write, and on poor Mary’s account, because 
of the irresistible proof, by the way in which she has 
received my letter, of her having erred far more widely 
from the simplicity and truth of the Gospel than I had 
even feared. She has received too many evidences of 
the feeling with which I ever regarded her to be able so 
to misapprehend my intention in writing to her as you 
appear to have done, and knowing what that must 
have been, she could not so little understand my 
meaning as to draw the conclusion to which you have 
come. She knows that I did not accuse her of making ~ 
a profession of religion for purposes of gain, but that 
I charged her with guilt for refusing to give glory 
to God for the abundant mercies with which he had 
loaded her, and for consenting, by her silence, to 
receive glory for sacrifices which she had never made. 
She knows this, and I fear she felt that I charged her 
justly, but that she has been unwilling to confess her 
fault, and has sought relief from the burden thus 
brought on her conscience by endeavouring to receive 
from you such an impression of my meaning as she 
could sit easily under, conscious that such accusations 
as you suppose me to have made were wholly untrue. 
I am glad you purpose to preserve for Mr. Caird my 
letter, and I shall not cease to pray that the Lord may 


‘ 


R22 CORRESPONDENCE WITH MR. CAIRD. 


so reveal to him the truth it contains as that it may 
yet be blest to the end for which He knows it was 
written. I do not refer particularly to the railing 
accusations which you bring against me. I know that 
God himself alone shall judge, and so I feel it a light 
matter to be judged of man. Tell Mary she has my 
love, my pity, and my prayers; remember me re- 
spectfully to Lady Harriet; accept my best thanks for 
much kindness long since received but still gratefully 
remembered, and with most sincere desire for the 
present and eternal well-being of your soul, I am, &e. 
Robert Story.” 

On the return of Mrs. Caird’s husband from Canada, 
he also despatched a letter in reply to that which had 
been in his absence addressed to his wife; but itis of 
such a style that it cannot be introduced here. He 
characterizes Mr. Story’s communication as an “ angry 
and ungodly tirade,” “base and blasphemous words,” 
&c., and expresses his astonishment that he should have 
written as he had, to one “‘ of whom he knew so much.” 
With no small forbearance and kindness of tone, while 
with equal firmness and dignity, Mr. Story replied to 
Mr. Caird: I give an extract: “It is just because of 
the much I do know, that I so write; the much for 
which in better days I have given thanks, and over 
which, since she assumed the character of a prophetess, 
I have not ceased to mourn. For on what in all that 
period can my mind rest that would give it joy? Is 
it the first exhibition of speaking with tongues to 
confirm in their sinful and dishonest idleness some 
foolish boys? Is it the speaking in power to com- 
mand their stay in her poor oppressed mother’s house? 
Is it the agony of suffering which a patient sufferer 
endured through the noise and riot which night after 


CORRESPONDENCE WITH MR. CAIRD. 223 


night disturbed his rest, and which was called the ex- 
pression of spiritual joy ? Is it the blasphemous appeal- 
ing to God to decide by lot, that which was no sooner 
decided than set aside? Or the fearful things trans- 
acted in the chamber of the dead?* Or the two 
persons, each inspired as they said by the spirit of the 
Lord, directly contradicting each other in my own pre- 
sence? Or the prophecies which I heard and which 
were written to me, that have never come to pass? 
O, my brother, what have I not known, and what 
ought I not to have written that others might know, 
and be hindered from dishonouring the high and holy 
Lord God Almighty, by attributing to the workings of 
His Spirit what would disgrace the common sense and 
feeling of degraded and fallen man ?” 

The correspondence did not end here, but from a 
discussion regarding spiritual gifts, it was immediately 
converted by Mr. Caird into one about money. It isa 
subject which it would have been more grateful to my 
feelings to have passed over in silence; yet it seems 
necessary it should be touched upon, as Mr. Story’s 
conduct throughout the transaction to which the corre- 
spondence (which I should be sorry to reproduce here) 
refers, illustrated strikingly the self-forgetful generosity 
and warmth of his heart; and because his judgment 
of the moral condition of the possessors of the spiritual 
gifts was largely influenced by the insight which this 
correspondence gave him. 

I shall not, however, introduce into these pages 
dedicated to the memory of one who was ever only too 
generous and forgiving, churlish and ungrateful recol- 
lections, farther than it is necessary I should do. It

224 THE THREATENED LAWSUIT. 


will be sufficient to say that Mr. Story had constituted 
himself the almoner of the destitute Campbell family, 
and had allotted to them the proceeds of the first 
edition of Isabella’s memoir (6001.) ;* and that now Mr. 
Caird, either at their instigation or his own, presumed 
to interfere with Mr. Story in the management of 
this charity; and at last so far forgot himself, as to 
threaten a lawsuit, if certain discourteous and unwar- 
rantable demands which he made were not complied 
with. Probably no lawyer ever before had such a case_ 
placed in his hands by such a client: the case—prose- 
cution of the bestower of a charity by the recipients 
of the same, or by one professing to act on their 
behalf; the client—a man pretending to speak and act 
by the direct power of the Spirit of Almighty God.t+ 

It would be useless and tedious to enter into the 
odious details: suffice to say, Mr. Story maintained his 
ground, and Mr. Caird finally abandoned his lawsuit. 

Basely as Mr. Story was thus treated by those for 
whom he had done so much, his kindness to them did 
not cease. Their door had been shut against him; but 
learning from their neighbours that Mrs. Campbell in 
her last illness was in want of the nourishment an 
invalid requires, he caused supplies to be sent to her 
anonymously from Helensburgh. 

We have seen that at the first he was inclined to 
look unsuspectingly on the imagined spiritual gifts. 
Like many others at that time, he was disposed to 
belteve that a crisis in the Christian Dispensation 
was approaching, and that the signs of the latter

days of which Joel had spoken, and which Peter .

still more deeply, that any higher development of the 
Church was to be looked for, not in the display of 
supernatural powers, but in the growth of a diviner 
life—in the increase of love. The spiritual gifts of 
the Corinthian Church had co-existed with error and 
imperfection: the Apostle had warned the members 
of that Church to regard the possession of these 
as altogether secondary to the possession of the 
far more excellent gift of Charity; for Prophecies, 
he said, shall fail, and Tongues shall cease, and 
Knowledge shall vanish away, but Charity never 
faileth.* Any one, therefore, earnestly waiting for a 
better day of purer and more perfect Christian life, 
could not but shrink from acknowledging its dawn 
in a disorderly manifestation of alleged gifts of the | 
Holy Spirit, on the part of persons in whom its best 
and greatest gifts seemed most lamentably a-wanting. 
If, indeed, after a silence of eighteen hundred years, 
the Spirit had returned to the Church in great power, 
might not its quickened energy have been expected to 
display itself in freer and fuller operation than of old 
—less fettered by the imperfection of its human 
organs? But alas, the Divine power was less distinct, _

. And he, for one, in whose parish the unknown voice

.~Campbell went to Helensburgh, where, in the summer 
of 1830, meetings were constantly held, amid much 
wonder and excitement, in which persons who were 
believed, and probably believed themselves to be, under 
the influence of the Spirit, spoke in tongues, pro- 
phesied, interpreted, &c. To these meetings envoys 
came from Edinburgh and London, who, réturning to 
their respective congregations, inaugurated similar 
scenes in both these cities. The result was in 
Edinburgh, the removal of the Rev. Walter Tait, 
from Trinity College Church, and the formation 
under him of a congregation connected with the new 
body ; and in London, the series of sad and extra- 
ordinary meetings and “ manifestations” in Regent 
Square Church, which at length were the efficient 
cause of Mr. Irving’s expulsion from that edifice, and 
from the pale of the Church of Scotland.

IRVINGS ENTHUSIASTIC CREDULITY. 227 


Church he loved so well and had served so faith- 
fully. The end was lamentable, but the events 
which led inevitably to it were lamentable too. It 
was sad to see a lofty mind wasting itself on the 
vague uncertainties into which modern interpreta- 
tion delights to beat down the inspired utterances 
of the ancient Hebrew prophets; and a pure and 
heroic spirit, instead of patiently waiting upon God, 
hurried on and worn out in the feverish excitement 
of a restless and bewildering supernaturalism. 

The spectacle was not one to allure a mind as 
earnest and rational (needful counterpoise to his im- 
pulsive heart and fancy) as Mr. Story’s, within the 
circle of the gifted or possessed. That he should be 
drawn within it was earnestly desired by many who 
loved him and considered that beyond that circle there 
was little, if any, truth or safety.* 

Irving wrote to him from time to time letters full of 
high enthusiasm, exhorting him to hold not back. 
‘Oh, Story,” he says on one occasion in 1882, “thou 
hast grievously sinned in standing afar off from the 
work of the Lord, scanning it like a sceptic, instead of 
proving it like a spiritual man. Ah, brother, repent, 
and the Lord will forgive thee. I am very much 
troubled for you;” and again, “ Dear Story, you keep 


too much aloof from the great work of the Spirit which . 


is proceeding beside you. Draw not back, brother, 
but go forward. The kingdom of Heaven is only to 
be won by the brave. Keep your conscience unfettered 
by your understanding. Farewell.” And then in the 
same letters, sad proof of how he had thrown aside the 


* For years his name was specially mentioned in the prayers of at

228 THE VISION OF THE BURNING SHIP. 


fetters of his own understanding, he would adduce 
evidences of the Spirit’s speaking in the Church— 
*‘ prophecies” and declarations which it was impossible 
seriously to believe, and which the lapse of a little 
time was sufficient to refute and disprove. 

Others besides Irving besought him to join himself to 
the spiritualists, and sometimes the solicitations and 
arguments were more ludicrous than impressive: “ And 
now, beloved sir,” wrote one, “I warn you of your danger 
if you remain in Babylon—flee out of her, I beseech 
you. On Tuesday night I had a dream which ap- 
peared to me very striking, and I cannot but regard 
it as a call from God to you. The dream was this, I 
saw you most distinctly flying from a ship which was 
on fire, and you yourself much burned and blackened 
in the face, and your eyelashes singed; but you had 
escaped with your life, and had your clothes hanging 
over your arm, and I was reproving you for your 
unfaithfulness; and thus did-I awake with a deep 
impression of what I had seen in my dream, and 
told it to my wife. I pray now that the Lord Him- 
self may give you the true interpretation of it.” 
While there is in this a quaint and Bunyan-like 
simplicity, there is also curious evidence of the 
unquiet seeking after signs and wonders, and of the 
tendency to invest the baldest natural facts with 
hidden spiritual meanings, which marked that period 
of religious fermentation. 

The immediate interest and activity of the new 
movement gradually passed away from the scene of 
its first workings to other and more prominent spheres, 
and for some time previous to Irving’s death their 
chief centre had been London. 

In the end of 1834, Irving came down to Scotland,

to die, deposed from his Mother Church,* diseased in 
body, broken down in heart and mind,—a worn out, 
wasted man. Mr. Story met him in Glasgow, and 
still Irving pleaded earnestly with him that he would 
enter into the Ark of Safety from those things that 
were coming on the earth. “I seldom bow my - 
knee in prayer,” said he to Mrs. Stewart Ker; “but 
I pray to God that Story may be brought to see 
the truth as I see it.” The last time they met, 
Mr. Story, entering at length into his reasons for 
doubting the work of the Spirit in the “H. C. A.- 
Church,” told him of the many circumstances which 
had led him to distrust the originator of the move- 
ment, and those immediately connected with her: 
the narrative in no wise shook Irving’s deep-rooted 
confidence in the reality of the Spirit's manifestation. 
“Ah, brother,” said he, “you have seen the short- - 
comings of the brethren in private; but I have heard 
the voice of the Eternal Spirit in the midst of the great 
congregation.” It was after this interview that Mr. S. 
wrote the letter, of which the following is an extract. 
“Oh! my beloved brother, what in this whole matter 
has given me so much pain, is the infallible proof I - 
have received thereby of the backsliding of one whom 
I so confidently set forth to the Church, as possessed of 
a like spirit with her departed sister. From the time 
she conceived that she had become the recipient of 
supernatural powers, she lost much of what I had wit-

IRVINGS DEATH. 233 


into that Presence where the mysteries of the spiritual 
life would be at last unveiled, and where turmoil and 
uncertainty would no longer vex his soul. He died on 
the 8th of December, 1834. It had been prophesied - 
that he should not die at this time, but the prophecy 
did not bind the hand of death. ~ 

He was laid to rest amid the solemn gloom of the 
crypt of Glasgow Cathedral. It has been idly reported 
again and again, repeated lately even in such a respect- 
able work as Dr. Cunningham’s Church History of 
Scotland,* that a band of his followers stood around 
the grave expecting him to rise from the dead. 
No such display of rebellious credulity mocked the - 
consignment of his honoured dust to its venerable 
tomb. If any expectation so profane was entertained, 
it was not expressed; and of those true and mourning 
friends who stood by, thinking not of a present 
resurrection, but of one to come, when all the Saints - 
departed shall be raised, one of the very last to quit 
the resting-place of the “man greatly beloved,” was 
Mr. Story. 

Irving passed away, but the religious movement _ 
which, though chiefly associated with his name, had not 
begun with him, and had not owned his direction or 
control, was not checked or hindered by his removal. 
During the twenty-seven years which have elapsed since - 
he was called hence it has developed into a perfection of 
organization and an energy of Christian life of which, 
in his lifetime, it was only giving the early promise. 
The Church which has grown out of it has continued 
to meet and satisfy, with ever-increasing fulness, the 
spiritual wants of the many, who can find rest and life
