GROWTH OF THE “H.C. A. CHURCH.” 235 


founding the numbers of the elect with the numbers 
of the saved, limits redeeming grace neither by the 
font nor by the grave. Its unity being a unity of spirit, 
not of subscription, it may enjoy within a diversity of 
opinion, the counterpart of its charity towards those 
who are without. Its worship strikes the imagination 
of the educated beholder as the very worship celebrated 
by Tertullian or by Hippolytus. That venerable pres- 
byter, those ardent young evangelists, those black- 
vested deacons, that ancient anthem, and still more 
ancient incense-burning; the homily, the hymn, the 
utterance and the eucharist,—transport us in sacred 
fancy to an antiquity beyond the reach of Romish or 
other rites. And yet how fitting is it to all the wants 
of to-day. That unique confession of the sin of schism, 
that prayerful remembrance of the dead, that earnest 
recognition of a Present Curist, that all-pervading 
devotion without doctrine, does it not give shape and 
language to the aspirations of the soul alienated from 
all Churches, yet quite unable to do without them? It 
may well be so, for this ritual is the product of many 
minds, trained in diverse schools, united in their belief 
of a Divine mission, and searching, under the impression 
of a Divine command, up and down Christendom for the 
scattered vessels of the sanctuary, to find and to rebuild. 
And the priesthood! Consisting of men of various 
sects and vocations, not all of them separated from 
those vocations, it may not exceed the priesthood of 
other Churches in devotion to prescribed duty ; but its 
functions certainly appear at once more comprehensive 
and more precise, every man having a special work, 
and the entire work including ministration to every 
want or circumstance of humanity ; the daily service of 
the altar and the pulpit, the visitation of the sick, 


286 MR. STORY’S RELATION TO IT. 


the relief of the distressed, the absolution of the 
burdened.” 

Such has been the development from the germ of 
Mary Campbell’s utterances at Fernicarry. 

Throughout his life Mr. Story counted amongst the 
members of the “ Holy Catholic Apostolic Church” 
many of his dearest friends. With not a few of them he 
held constant and unreserved communion; he knew 
well their belief, their hope, their principles ; he rejoiced 
in the earnest self-sacrifice and devotion which marked 
much of that Church’s life ; when opportunity offered, he 
joined readily in its worship; but the Divine origin and 
* inspiration, and special guidance, which it claimed for 
itself, he having known its beginning and heedfully 
scanned its progress, never could admit. The Church 
in which he had been baptized and ordained appeared 
to him, despite the many errors, which he deplored, in 
its Ecclesiastical and in its Christian life, to be the 
branch of the True Vine, which was best fitted to 
supply the needs of his native country. Nothing 
which occurred in the times of which we have been 
speaking, or in those that followed, prevailed to estrange 
from it his affection, or to paralyze the whole-hearted 
energy with which he continued to labour in its cause— 
a workman that needed not to be ashamed. 


CHAPTER VI. 
PERSONAL HISTORY. 


Tue chief interest of Mr. Story’s life in these years 
was absorbed in the events related in the three preced- 
ing chapters. 

Apart from them his personal history was calm and 
uneventful, always influenced and coloured more or 
less by his connection with those transactions. 

His four children were born during the years 1880-5; 
and of these, the first and third were early taken. Of 
the first he writes to Pringle, in August, 1830 :— 
“Before he completed his eighth week the blessed 
babe was translated to glory. After death his little © 
frame assumed an aspect of most exquisite beauty ; 
although we kept the precious dust for several days, 
every hour that intervened beheld it more beautiful ; 
the eyelids would never shut, but when the pressure of 
the finger was removed they always retired and left 
about half of the pupil unveiled, disclosing that 
heavenly azure so brilliant, and so beaming with life, 
that without other means of judging, we must have 
concluded that he was only enjoying a refreshing 
slumber. I carried him in my own arms to the tomb, 
and there felt what it was to lose a first-born, an only 
son.” The trial of this loss was not a little aggravated 
by the intrusion of one of the believers in the “ gifts” 


240 VISITS TO ENGLAND. 


followed, and in 1836 Mr. Story went up to visit him. 
From Northampton, where he met the warmest wel- 
come, he went to see Lady Olivia Sparrow, at Brampton 
Park, and thence returned home by Liverpool. 

About two years later he again visited England. He 
enjoyed these trips extremely, continually picked up 
new acquaintances ahd formed new friendships, and 
entered with keen zest into the pleasures of travel and 
society. His health, moreover, required them, his fre- 
quent rheumatic and bilious attacks rendering cessation 
from work and change of air and scene necessary. 

In 1838 his health was seriously impaired, so that he 
had to give up all duty; and during his period of rest 
and absence, his place was supplied by his friend, the 
Rev. Francis Wylie, now the excellent minister of 
Elgin. His English tour on this occasion included 
Northampton, where he was interested in meeting, 
among other new acquaintances, both lay and clerical, 
the Hon. Mr. Spencer, a new pervert to Rome, since 
well known as Father Ignatius, and the Rev. Sir Henry 
Robinson, of the Established Church; and Leaming- 
ton, where he visited his friends, Sir William and Lady 
Maxwell, of Monreith. Here he met one who, for the 
rest of her life, was to him a very dear and valued 
friend. The high appreciation and sincere regard he 
ever after entertained for her express themselves in the 
first mention he makes of her name; and those who 
knew Lady Matilda Maxwell will not wonder that these 
sentiments were early formed and lastingly retained. 
‘Have you heard of Lady Matilda Bruce? Of high 
intellect, devoted piety, with an open and ingenuous 
disposition. She crossed to the Maxwells when she 
heard of my arrival, and we have been a good deal 
together. She is a great friend of Erskine and 


VISIT TO IRELAND. ~ 241 


‘Chalmers, and has great largeness of heart ;” this latter 


was ever a very special term of commendation with 
the writer,—the possession of a heart that could open 
wide, and take in many—covered from him a multitude 
of sins. 

In this summer he also paid his first visit to Ireland. 
He went thither to visit the friend who has kindly con- 
tributed to these pages a letter which, while it expresses 
its writers warm regard for the subject of this me- 
moir, does not tell that the latter ever reciprocated 
that affection with a most fatherly interest and love. 
Mr. Goold was at this time residing on the Hill of 
Howth, on that side of it where the broad Bay of Dublin 
rolls in front, and the blue hills of Wicklow rise beyond. 
The sunny slope of the hill, above the waveworn cliffs, 
where he had pitched his tent, needs no warm Irish 
welcome to make it a delightful resting-place; but the 
Irish welcome was not awanting. ‘‘ You may believe,” 
writes he, “they were most happy to see me. ‘ To 
whom,’ said Fred, ‘ shall we be kind, if not to you? and 
as to C., she will make a Pope of you.’”’ 

The following are a few extracts from his letters at 
this time. Here is an incident in his host’s pastoral 
experience :—‘ A Roman Catholic at died of 
cholera. Not one of the wretches, not even the priest, 
would approach the dead body. F. and another person 
buried him; and, because he read the funeral service 
over the corpse, they swore they would murder the 
‘bloody parson’ for having made a Protestant of him. 
He had to sleep every night with loaded pistols beside 
him.” ‘Mr. Nangle, the Achil clergyman, has been 
with us after service. His labours have been greatly 
blessed among the poor Irish. I am happy to have 
met him; for I shall thus have a kind of personal 


242 INCIDENTS OF IRISH VISIT. 


interest in the Achil mission.” ‘ What strange things 
happen in this country, which could not occur in any 
other. Last evening the prayers of the Church (Mr. 
V.’s) were requested for the Rev. Mr. J., dangerously 
ill, when Mr. J., in the course of the service, entered 
the Church, and worshipped among those who were 
praying for him. Mr. Y. declared afterwards that he 
would not allow him to be prayed for again till he was 
really dead.” ‘On Monday there was a prayer- 
meeting at Merrion Avenue, which I attended, when 
there were about 200 people assembled. I offered up 
one of the prayers, at Mr. Nixon’s earnest request.* 
We are to be there on Sunday, when Dalton, from 
Wolverhampton, is to preach a missionary sermon; and 
on Tuesday, Joseph Wolff is to be with Mr.N. On 
Thursday I attended a visitation of the Archbishop in 
St. Patrick’s—a very sorry affair; the clergy amounting 
to perhaps seventy or eighty.” ‘I addressed a few 
Irish Protestants on the cliffs of Howth, last evening, 
for a considerable time. We had between forty and 
fifty of a congregation. About a dozen English fisher- 
men, Methodists, came from Howth, across the hill, 
and conducted the music—a very fine set of men; and . 
it was exceedingly interesting to see their hardy, sun- 
burnt visages while they sang with such earnestness, 
and with no small art, the praises of God.” 

In all his absences from home, it is half amusing to 
observe together his hearty relish for the change and 
excitement of new scenes and new society, and the 
warmth and sincerity of his constant reminiscences of 
his own fireside. The day seems a blank in which 
he has no letter from the Manse; when the longed- 


* Rev. R. H. Nixon, Incumbent of Booterstown Church, near Dublin, 


THE BOOKSTALLS. 243 


for despatch has come, then every burden seems 
lifted from his spirit. “I went along the street 
making verses to you in my mind,” he writes one day, 
after a satisfactory account has been received from 
home. “Mrs. Carpenter has to keep her husband 
from buying likewise,” he writes, at another time, 
naively conscious of a purchasing frailty, especially 
im respect of books, and which his generally slender 
resources made it highly impolitic to indulge. But 
an old bookstand presented to him irresistible attrac- 
tions ; if he could not buy, he at least would handle 
and brood over the ancient, tarnished volumes. “I 
kept at a respectful distance from the bookstands,’ 
he says in one letter, proud of his self-command. 
This tendency leading him, as it did, to linger in all 
sorts of dingy and grimy corners when in town, often 
was a severe cross to the friend who chanced to be with 
him at the time the bibliomania seized him. It was 
not at all a pleasant pastime—as one of his Irish friends 
used to represent—to have to loiter among the dubious 
purlieus of the Quays and Four Courts while he was 
poking about among the bookstalls, and conversing with 
the voluble and ragged Irish gentlemen who presided 
thereat. He occasionally, however, was rewarded with 
a bargain; and he gradually acquired a large collection 
of old and curious editions—some of them of consi- 
derable value. His library was extensive, and was 
especially rich in the works of the older English 
divines. The range of his reading was at all times 
wide and varied, and his taste classical and refined. 

It is interesting and instructive to observe how con- 
stantly both in presence and absence he bore his parish 
and people on his heart before God, how devotedly 
his thoughts turned to them, how, when he was laid 

RB 2 


244 “THE PASTOR ALWAYS.” 


aside from work and separated from his flock, he like 
Paul “night and day prayed exceedingly that he might 
see their face and might perfect that which was lacking 
in their faith.” Instructive too, to note how decidedly 
and openly he bore himself everywhere and always, as 
the Christian minister. Many in travel, or in any pro- 
miscuous company are inclined to keep their minis- 
terial character out of sight. But he was, to use the 
language of Irving’s charge to Mr. McLean, “ the 
Pastor always, less than the Pastor never.” No meal 
was ever begun in his presence without a blessing 
invoked upon it; no evening ever closed without an 
“evening sacrifice.” ‘He is a marvellous man, he’s 
always praying,” was once said of him by one who 
thought that the forms of prayer and thanksgiving 
were perhaps too constantly insisted on; but to him 
they were not forms, they were the living expression 
of the “ deep religion of a thankful heart.” No pro- 
fanity or misconduct ever passed him without a sharp 
rebuke, enforced with a tone and look of such inherent 
authority as few cared to question or disobey. 

In August, 1839, he lost his second son, aged six 
years. ‘“ He longed to depart,” says his father, “to be 
with Jesus: he frequently wished me to pray, espe- 
cially that God might take him to himself before the 
morning. There was not one thought or word but was 
consistent and rational to the last, and in holding out 
his hand for his mother to kiss he expired.” The 
consolations, which he felt to abound towards himself 
in the bereavements he endured, are expressed in a 
letter written long afterwards to a friend, mourning 
the death of an only son. “I cannot,” he writes, 
“help, in a sentence alluding to what I felt an all- 
availing consolation when in bitterness for my own 


FAMILY BEREAVEMENT. 245 


first-born. I was reading the words ‘ God spared not 
his own Son,’ a light flashed upon the word spared. 
Yes, thought I, that word was chosen as the symbol of 
the sacrifice that God’s fatherly heart made and felt, 
when he gave up his Sonto die. He knows, exclaimed 
I, what I feel; He once had a dead Son. The Infinite 
from that blessed moment seemed to be in my presence, 
actuated by fatherly sympathies, swathed as it were in 
a human heart, afflicted in my affliction; the incompre- 
hensible Jehovah manifest to me indeed in flesh, 
the fountain of my fatherly nature, and bathing so to 
speak my fatherly sufferings in his fatherly sympathies. 

. . . I ecommit your sorrowful household to the 
consolations of Him who knows alone how to give the 
garment of praise for the spirit of heaviness, and a 
name to the bereaved better than that of sons or 
daughters.” 

His family circle after this bereavement, remained 
unbroken to the close. 

It were unfitting here to draw aside the veil which 
encloses the sacred household memories; but surely 
no home was ever the centre of happier associations 
to those who knew it, than his; none could be visited 
with more pleasure and remembered with kindlier 
affection. “He was one,” to use the language of a 
friend and neighbour," “ who, the more intimately he 
was known was the more highly esteemed, whose 
Christian character shone forth with especial bright- 
ness to those who were admitted to his familiar inter- 
course, who saw him in his house at home, and in his 
personal conduct of himself.” +

2416 THE “ ENFANS PERDUS.” 


To more than one that genial home was the Sacred 
Point in their horizon to which their “hearts untra- 
velled,” ever returned with the assurance of finding 
there help, and light and sympathy, such as they could 
find nowhere else. 

There were a few ill-starred beings whom he called 
his “enfans perdus.” These persons were perpetually 
travelling from Jerusalem to Jericho, and getting sadly 
knocked about by the way. He was the Good Sama- 
ritan ; and his were the oil and wine that helped to 
rehabilitate them, and his own house was the inn to 
which their friendly footsteps were guided. 

The “facile princeps” of this broken band was 
the Rev. R. B. He had at some former period held 
a charge in the Synod of Ulster, and being a person 
of headstrong temper had immersed himself in a sea 
of Irish troubles, from which several years did not suf- 
fice to extricate. him. During his struggles to disen- 
tangle himself from Ireland, and yet obtain his “ rights” 
from the Hibernian Presbyterians, he came within Mr. 
Story’s ken; and for years after Mr. Story was his refuge, 
the Manse his cave of Adullam. He wandered about 


W. Dunn, of Cardross :—“ And over a man of finer, more graceful 
mould and form—over a more genial friend and companion—over one 
whose face and presence threw more light, and cheerfulness, and 
vivacity into a social circle—over a kinder and more loving heart— 
over one with larger and wider sympathies, sympathies with all 
humanity, with all creatures and created things, enjoying with a keener 
relish everything enjoyable on earth, with a quicker ear and eye to all 
sweet sounds and sights of beauty, with a merrier laugh and a more 
exquisite sense of humour, and a readier tear—over one who took into 
his bosom so many and so diverse persons, of all variety of tempera- 
ment, of all sects and denominations, and had room and space for each, 
and a special nook for all unfortunates and outcasts, cast adrift on 
life’s voyage—over a man who had seen more of human life in all its 
aspects, in the hall and in the cottage, and who had such store of 
anecdotes and illustrations gathered from books and men—the grave 
of a country Churchyard has seldom closed.” 


THE “ENFANS PERDUS.” 247 


Scotland as a preacher, and occasionally in a case of 
extreme need officiated in that capacity at Rosneath, 
till increasing age and eccentricity forbade his being 
admitted to the pulpit. Whenever a forlorn ring of the 
door bell was heard late of a winter’s night, “ This is 
B.,” was the unanimous and almost always the correct 
prognostication. He came when he pleased and stayed 
as long as he chose, and was by no means bashful in 
making known his wants while an inmate. Mrs. Story 
was not quite so tolerant of his ways as her husband, 
and could not be brought to believe that hospitality 
required that B. should one day eat all the eggs in 
the house, raw, to cure a slight hoarseness, or the 
next be permitted to recline on the drawing-room 
sofa with his shoes on, and with a small mound of the 
best bound books in the room under his head for a 
pillow. She consequently was held in less esteem 
than the minister himself; a fact which B. took his own 
way of notifying. One summer we were at Bridge of 
Earn, and as usual he turned up; he was visiting in 
the neighbourhood, and considerately came to see his 
friend, Mr. Story. “ My friends Mr. and Mrs. Robert- 
son, of Henhill,” said he, “ were just talking about you 
last night, Mr. Story, and they thought you were 
very like one of the Patriarchs.” ‘Indeed, Mr. B., 
and which of the Patriarchs am I like?” ‘ Well, 
we agreed it was Isaac. You see he was a fine, mild, 
benevolent old gentleman; and then you know” (with 
a vixenish glance at Mrs. Story) “he had a real birky 
of a wife.” 

Poor B., he betook himself at last to medical studies, 
with a view to becoming a medical missionary; but 
on his way to Paris to walk the Hospitals there, he 
broke down in London, and all his money melted away 


248 . THE TWO GREEKS, 


as he lay sick in some obscure lane about Smithfield. 
Utterly destitute and forlorn, he wrote to the Duke 
of Argyll, and told him he wanted to get to Rosneath, 
to die there. The Duke had him sent safely down by 
rail—he died in a day or two, and was buried in the 
spot he chose, under the shadow of the large laurel, 
beside the old Church. And then, strange to say, one 
who in other years had known this queer, lonely, help- 
less creature, and true to his earlier image had always 
loved him, came to ask where he was laid, that she 
might raise a stone to his memory. What broken 
and twisted chains of love must lie scattered up and 
down the world, waiting for the time that shall rivet 
them anew ! 

Another protégé, to whom Mr. Story was much 
attached, was a negro, sent home from Rio, by Mr. S. 
Ker. He was baptized Robert Story; and when after 
a pious and useful life, his end came, Mr. Story laid 
his head in his grave in Rosneath Churchyard, and 
wrote his epitaph. 

During the Greek War of Independence, Joseph 
Wolff sent home to Irving’s care two Greek lads, 
whom he had rescued from danger and privation, and 
whom he wished to have educated in Scotland. Irving 
sent them down to the patriarchal guardian, whom 
in a subsequent letter Joseph Wolff calls “our in- 
comparably excellent, and kind-hearted friend, Mr. 
Story.” 

He proposes their coming in a characteristic letter. 
“Joseph Wolff, my much esteemed friend, and Lady 
‘Georgiana Wolff also, my much esteemed friend, have 
given me another proof of their esteem, by sending me 
two Greeks—the one a Deacon (Parthenius), the othera 
youth of thirteen or fourteen (Pavolo), son of a mer- 


THE TWO GRELKS. 249 


chant, in Cyprus, whom the Turks beheaded. The 
Deacon is about twenty-four years old, and brings 
excellent testimonials from one of the Bishops of 
Cyprus, upon whom he has been accustomed to wait; 
for the Deacon in the Greek Church is nearer to the 
Deacon in our Church, than either in the Latin or 
English Church. Well, these two Greeks has Joseph 
Wolff sent, wholly entrusted to me, so that I am to 
them as father and guardian, and provider, and every- 
thing, which also I am right happy to be, for they are 
come purely for the acquisition of knowledge. 

Wolff's positive instruction to me, is to send them to 
Scotland that they may be educated there, and after a 
while return to their native land to instruct their coun- 
trymen, both as teachers and preachers ;—not to take 
Orders with us; but as it were to obtain the style and 
character of our Scottish Church, and return with 
it to their own land. This I very much approve, 
and by the blessing of God, poor though I am, yet 
rich in faith by His grace, I will take upon myself 
the responsibility of all their charges till they return 
to their native Cyprus again. At once and imme- 
diately it occurred to my wife and me, that the 
proper place to station them in was your village, 
and your parish school the proper school for them, 
and you, the proper pastor.” 

The two Greeks accordingly came down, and were 
boarded with Mr. Dodds, the schoolmaster, much to the 
horror of one conservative old gentleman in the parish, 
who declared that Mr. Story was going to overrun the 
countryside with Greeks and Barbarians, and that there 
would not be a fence left in the place—his hobby being 
dykes and hedges. The Deacon had a flowing beard 
which, with a view to Scotticizing himself more tho- 


250 THE TWO GREEKS. 


roughly, he wished to get rid of. After considerable 
persuasion, the village barber was prevailed upon to 
perform the necessary tonsure; but when the operation 
was ended, the Deacon’s glory was gone. His chin was 
the merest bracket for his face to rest on—the diaconal 
gravity and comeliness had perished with the beard. Mr. 
Story enjoyed, as he enjoyed all that moved his keen 
sense of humour—the wonder and, speculation caused 
in the quiet parish by the advent of these foreigners. 
He used to give with great effect the narrative of their 
first visit to Portkiln, where old Mrs. Campbell, after 
regarding them with a look of benevolent perplexity, 
turned to him at last with the practical query, “ And 
what can the worthy gentlemen eat?” and on finding 
that their gastric powers were not different from those 
of Scotchmen, she stuffed them with good things, 
till Paul laying his hand impressively on his stomach 
declared, ‘“ non possible more.” The Deacon was not 
a popular character : after a time he returned to Cyprus. 
Paul was a universal favourite; after acquiring the 
English language and a good foundation of education 
at Rosneath, he studied medicine at Edinburgh, and 
eventually, discharged for many years the duties of 
Medical officer, in Murray’s Lunatic Asylum, near 
Perth ; after leaving which, he also went back to 
Cyprus. | 

These are but a few of the many clients for his 
sympathy and aid and counsel, who from time to time 
found their way to his door. That door, one of his 
friends used to say, was like the heart of its owner, 
ever standing wide open to receive all who chose to 
enter. All were welcomed with a kindly courtesy 
which, while it expressed at once a genial hospitality 
which no formality stiffened or restrained, yet had not 


HOSPITALITY. 251 


a little of the dignity and grace of an age of statelier 
manners than our own. 

However dreary a bore the guest might prove himself 
to be, he was taken leave of with a certain reluctance, 
there was a kind of lingering dislike to say farewell, 
even toa bore. There is one example of his thoughtful 
consideration of others which occurs in a letter written 
during absence from home, and which, perhaps, should 
be extracted, as it concerns a class of most deserving, 
yet too often neglected persons—the unattached and 
unendowed licentiates. He says, “I can only write 
about some necessary things, and first about the 
preacher. Let him have a fire in his room lest he 
should be inclined to sit up, and let the glasses be set 
down at supper, as he may incline to. take something 
after the fatigues of the day. Ever remember I am 
anxious that the preachers be particularly attended to, 
for they are performing a very important, and neces- 
sary, and obliging duty, and they are generally in cir- 
cumstances which make them feel the more about the 
attention paid to them. . .. . P.S.—If the 
preacher goes off by the early boat, be sure that the 
servants get him some coffee.” 

One lingers over these traits of the nature, open, 
and generous, and loving, which expressed itself so 
vividly in every word and look, and which endeared 
him so closely to the many who called him friend. To 
them, these few reminiscences are not needed to recall 
the daily aspect and home life of one, whose presence 
diffused a geniality—a kindliness—an atmosphere of 
love around him as he moved, that seemed to bathe 
his path in constant sunshine. To others they must 
appear but cold and feeble outlines, hardly indicating 
at all the characteristics which they should illustrate. 


252 RETURN TO MORE PUBLIC MATTERS. 


It will be best to go on without longer delay to matters 
of more public interest; and to point out the position 
occupied and the part taken by the subject of this 
memoir, in those important passages of Scottish 
Church History, which culminated in the secession of 
1843. 


PART ITI. 


1834—1846. 


PART III, 


1834—1846. 


CHAPTER I. 


PATRON AGE—VETO—GROWTH OF “ NON-INTRUSION” 
PARTY. 


WuHen the Church of Scotland was finally established 
on its present basis after the Revolution of 1688, the 
method adopted for the settlement of ministers in 
vacant charges was election by the joint suffrages of 
the heritors and the Elders of the parish. In 1712, 
this system was overturned, and lay patronage substi- 
tuted in its stead; and ever since 1712 lay patronage 
has been the difficulty and the bugbear of the Scottish 
Church. It has been found all but impossible to 
adjust the respective rights of the patron and the 
people in a manner satisfactory to both parties, and 
the effort to do so has cost the Church no small 
labour. During the latter part of last century and the 
earlier part of this, all cases of disputed settlement 
commonly ended in the reclaiming voice of the people 
being overruled in the General Assembly by the 
votes of the “Moderate” party in that Court. “It 
often happened,” * says Dr. Somerville, “that the call 


* Somerville’s “ Life and Times, 1741—1818,” page 77. 


256 PATRONAGE. 


in favour of the presentee consisted exclusively of the 
names and signatures of the patron and non-resident 
heritors; but I do not recollect a single instance 
within the time of my attendance on the General 
Assembly, of any call in the favour of a presentee 
having been rejected or set aside.” This, no doubt, 
was a state of matters fraught with evil; it engendered 
dissent ; it impaired the usefulness and respectability 
of the Church. Its evils, however, were such as the 
extending education of the people, and the increasing 
strength of public opinion would gradually have re- 
moved by leading the people to a wiser choice, and 
the patron to a more respectful attention to their rea- 
sonable wishes. But even while these good influences 
were beginning to operate, the solution of the vexed 
question of patronage was precipitated by the action of 
the Church itself. 

The Reform excitement of 1831-32 fostered agita- 
tion for ecclesiastical reform no less than political. 

The people became more bent than ever on obtaining 
power in the Church—now that they had gained a cer- 
tain power in the State. The popular party in the 
General Assembly found its ranks swelled by a number 
of lay members, who, unlike their predecessors before 
1832, held opinions decidedly opposed to patronage. 
Without the Church, the Dissenters formed a Volun- 
tary Anti-State-Church association ; within her pale, a 
strong anti-patronage party began to develop itself. 
The best and largest section of the popular party, 
however, did not seek the abolition of patronage, but 
the amendment and regulation of it, so as at once to 
preserve the salutary rights of the patron, and to give 
effect, as far as possible, to the deliberate wishes of 
the people. With this view the law, known as the 


THE VETO LAW. 257 


Veto Law,* was passed in the Assembly of 1834 by a 
majority of 46, the popular party now finding them- 
selves, for the first time, unquestionably in the as- 
cendant. The law carried with it the approval of the 
highest legal authorities; it was recommended by the 
overpowering eloquence of Chalmers; it seemed a 
generous concession to the popular claims, and it ap- 
peared to be most skilfully adapted to preserve, by 
slightly modifying it, the patronage which extreme 
reformers insisted should be overthrown. 

The same Assembly admitted to the full status and 
functions of the parochial clergy, ministers of chapels 
of ease, or chapels “ quoad sacra,” as they were called, 
who hitherto having no parochial charge or endow- 
ment, had been held unqualified to sit in Church 
Courts. 

These acts were not passed without strong opposi- 
tion from the Moderate party ; but having been passed 
by a majority of the Assembly, they were adopted as 
law, and acted upon. 

For some time the Veto Act, when put in force, was 
not disputed; but a case soon arose to test its lega- 
lity—the famous case of Auchterarder. The presentee, 
Mr. Young, was vetoed ; he appealed to the civil Courts 
against the Presbytery’s right to give effect to the 
people’s Veto by refusing to “take him on trials,’ t 
and the decision of the Court of Session and of the 
House of Lords was, that the Presbytery had no right 
to give effect to the Veto—the Veto Act being an Act 


* The veto law gave to a majority of male heads of families in any 
parish—being communicants—the right to reject a presentee, abso- 
lutely, without reason given. 

+ J.e., to subject him to the usual examination, in order to test his 
fitness for receiving ordination to the charge. 


258 THE AUCHTERARDER CASE. 


which it was incompetent for the Church to pass 
without the concurrent sanction of the State, which 
concurrent sanction had not been secured. ‘This was 
the simple legal decision {apart from any consideration 
of the desirability or excellence of the Veto Law), that 
it had been passed illegally, was therefore null and 
void, and could not justly be acted on. The same 
judgment was at a later period pronounced on the Act 
admitting ministers of chapels to the status and func- 
tions of ministers of parishes. Their admission may 
have been, as the popular party maintained, a desirable 
step, but it was an illegal one. In enacting both these 
‘Measures, each involving civil rights, the Ecclesiastical 
Court had exceeded its proper jurisdiction ; neither 
could be in any. way binding without the assent of the 
Legislature. Such an interdependence of the two 
powers in the State, the Civil and the Ecclesiastical, 
is inevitable in an established Church. 

Into the dreary history of the conflict between the 
rival jurisdictions of the General Assembly and the 
Court of Session which followed the Auchterarder 
case, itis not our province to enter. ‘Those who wish 
a detail of the whole will find it in the magniloquent 
pages of the “Ten Years’ Conflict,” by the Rev. Dr. 
R. Buchanan, where they will be entertained by a 
good deal of clever description and special pleading, 
and amused with the calm self-complacency with which 
the good Doctor assumes that he and his party are the 
only true Church of Scotland, and their principles the 
only principles a Christian man should, could, or 
would hold. A fairer and more judicial narrative of 
the leading points, and examination of the principles 
involved, will be found in the work of Dr. Turner.* 


* “The Scottish Secession of 1843.” Rev. A. Turner, D.D. 


THE CONFLICT THAT ENSUED. 259 


That during these ten years much was done by the 
Civil and the Ecclesiastical Court that was fitted to 
embitter the contest, few will deny. The authority of 
each was stretched to, perhaps beyond, the extreme 
limit, and the mode in which the law of patronage was 
enforced in certain cases exhibited the utmost abuse of 
which that law was susceptible. The cases of Marnoch 
and Culsalmond, especially, seem discreditable to 
almost all concerned in them, and can be regarded only 
with shame and disgust—shame, that in a Christian 
and National Church such cases should occur, and 
disgust with those for whose behoof the rigour of the 
law was so unhappily exerted. 

But one must not forget that general principles of 
equity are not affected by particular instances of hard- 
ship, and the fact of the law sanctioning the hardship 
does not justify us in any attempt to gain a lawless 
liberty. 

At the same time the Government of the country 
cannot be acquitted of heavy blame for having indo- 
lently or timidly neglected to adopt safe and reasonable 
measures, to regulate the action of the law, and give 
peace to a distracted Church. Opportunity after oppor- 
tunity of an equitable adjustment of the question of 
patronage was suffered to slip by, until at length the 
question had lapsed from a demand for “ Non-intru- 
sion” * into a demand for “ Spiritual Independence ;’’ 
the popular party had become a revolutionary party, 
and the claims of the Church, as represented to the 
House of Commons, were such as the Constitutional 
Legislature of this land could not possibly grant. 

And then came the end, the secession of the revolu- 


* That is—a demand that no minister should be settled in a parish 
in defiance of the people’s wish.

o 


260 MR. STORY'S VIEW OF THE VETO ACT. 


tionary party from the Establishment and the creation 
of the so-called “‘ Free” Church, in 1843. 

During the long contest and the harassing disputes 
which were thus terminated, Mr. Story joined but little 
in the strife; but when he did speak and act in the 
Church Courts, he took an independent part. From 
the peculiar position which his connexion with the 
Row case had given him among his brethren, he could 
not have complete sympathy with either party in the 
Church. While he felt the political correctness and 
necessity of the course adopted by the party called 
“Moderate,” there was much in their traditions, in 
their habits of thought, in their views and practice as 
ministers, that separated between him and not a few of 
them. In what was really true and earnest in the life 
of the party called “ Evangelical” he cordially rejoiced, 
while he recoiled from the fatal tendencies of the 
policy they adopted, a policy which, from the first, he 
condemned as subversive of the peace and safety of the 
Church, and of the wise and salutary union between her 
and the State. 

On his return from the Assembly of 1834, in which 
the Veto Law was passed, he stated from the pulpit 
that the Church had adopted a measure which he could 
foresee was to produce much trouble and dispeace; a 
new power had been put into the hands of her mem- 
bers, their possession of which he regarded with alarm, 
and as the only protection against the abuse of that 
power was to be found in the character of those who 
were to wield it, he should consider it in future his 
duty to be even more strict than he hitherto had been 
in determining who were to be admitted to the Lord’s 
table, since admission thereto was to involve the heavy 
responsibility implied in the power of “ vetoing.” 


THE VETO ACT, UTOPIAN. 261 


He thus early detected where the danger of the Act 
lay, and took the only possible precaution. 

In an ideal state of society the Veto Law would have 
been unnecessary, but only in such would it have acted 
fairly. Had all the “male heads of families being 
communicants” in Scotland, been the too perfect 
characters they were assumed by the advocates of the 
Veto to be, it might have been safe to entrust them 
with the irresponsible right of absolutely rejecting any 
clergyman presented by a patron to a living; but being 
as they were, “men of like passions with ourselves,” 
often very ignorant and very prejudiced, they were not 
in every case, if in any, fit recipients of such a power. 
One case in which the Veto was exercised occurred in 
the Presbytery of Dunbarton, under Mr, Story’s own 
eye. “And what is your objection to Mr. 2” 
was asked of one of the congregation, ‘‘ Div’ ye think,” 
replied the high-minded “non-intrusionist,” ‘that 
we're gaun to hae a minister that wears a sark collar 
like thon?” The minister was then, and is still, an 
able and excellent clergyman. Mr. Story besought the 
people to be sure that in exercising the power which 
the Act of Assembly gave them they were led by the 
Spirit of Christ ; but the shirt-collars carried the day, 
and the presentee was vetoed. 

The Auchterarder case was decided in the House of 
Lords on the 2nd of May, 1839. By the highest legal 
tribunal in the empire the ilegality of the Veto Act 
itself, and of all proceedings arising out of it, was thus 
finally and positively decided. The Church herself 
had appealed the case to the civil court. She was 
bound to bow to its deeision. True, the Veto Law had 
been passed with the counsel and concurrence of high 
legal functionaries; but this did not invalidate the 


264 HE CONSIDERS THE 


rior courts. I will not come as a member to your 
General Assembly. I will not form part of the govern- 
ing body of an Established Church which, with no 
invasion by the State of any of her holy and inherent 
rights—in defence of no sacred principle—but for a 
matter of mere ecclesiastical polity, has set herself up 
in an attitude—for so it is, gloss it as you will—of 
dogged defiance, of virtual disobedience to the declared 
law of the land. I will not, by my presence even, be 
responsible for a line of policy which I believe is at 
variance with all her own best interests, and which, in 
a few years, will leave ber a Church, no doubt (nobody 
ever denied she would still be a Church, if she had not 
a shilling of endowment left; and it needed not yes- 
terday’s declamation to prove it), but which in my con- 
science I believe has already rung out her knell as the 
Established Church of Scotland.” 

Having so said, Lord Dalhousie walked out of the 
house, the Rev. William Cunningham* coarsely calling 
after him, “ Sic transit gloria mundi.” ‘“ The step was 
rash,” wrote Mr. Story, who spent part of his time 
during the sitting of Assembly, at Dalhousie Castle. 
“Had he been less straightforward, open, and single- 
minded, he would have been more prudent.” Rash as 
it was, however, it showed only too plainly how rapidly 
the revolutionary party were outrunning the sympathies 
of calm and statesmanlike advisers—how surely they 
were becoming involved in the eddies of a mere popular 
agitation. 

The only active part Mr. Story took in the business 
of this Assembly, was to move that a day be appointed 
for fasting and humiliation for the sins and afflictions 
of the Church; but his motion was unsuccessful : those

CHURCH'S KNELL IS RUNG. 265 


who considered themselves the “ Evangelical,” did not 
love to have such proposals made by any but them- 
selves. 

‘“‘Blunder will succeed to blunder,” he writes, “ until 
they acknowledge more decidedly than has ever yet 
been done, that neither ministers nor people can form 
any just idea of the ‘Headship of Christ,’ or how his 
sovereignty ought to be exercised in the government 
of his Church, until crucified together with him they 
have acquired the divine art of carrying about his 
dying.” 

What, in the whole agitation, most grieved and sad- 
dened him, was the tone of mere political excitement— 
of mere worldly strife—which pervaded the counsels of 
the Church. The “ headship,” or absolute sovereignty 
of Christ over his Church, was professedly the point in 
dispute; but the mind of Christ seemed to have been 
scared away by the clamour and bitterness of the dis- 
putants. He felt that the unity of the spirit was broken 
—that the sacred name was profaned by these fierce con- 
tendings about what were, in truth, as Lord Dalhousie 
had called them, “ matters of mere ecclesiastical polity.” 
On one occasion, in one of the Church courts, he gave 
indignant expression to the feeling. ‘‘ How dare you,” 
he broke in, amid some miserable wrangling about the 
“crown rights of the Redeemer,” &c., “how dare you

speak of the Lord that bought you as if he were the

“Look round you,” he wrote at another time to a 
gentleman near him, “ at the people among whom you 
dwell, and surely you cannot possibly judge it to be a 
counsel of the Spirit of God that you should hazard 
the very existence of the Church in its present form, 
rather than not bestow upon them an absolute and 


266 THE AGITATION SPREADS. 


uncontrolled discretion in the election of their pastors. 
Yet, because this is assumed to be the mind of God 
respecting the whole population, all those contentions 
have deluged our unhappy time. The adoption of the 
Veto was the opening of the fatal valve, and now who 
can prevent the rushing forth of those bitter waters ? 
I from the beginning opposed and denounced the Veto, 
as essentially unrighteous in principle, and therefore 
necessarily unscriptural. Independent of its unright- 
eousness, it had this additional evil—it deprived Pres- 
byteries of their judicial prerogative, and forced them 
to act according to the people’s capricious will at a most 
important stage of the process in the settlement of 
pastors. And mark here how marvellously the Divine 
will has been expressed ;—that which they wrongfully 
resigned to the people, is the very power of which the 
majority complain the civil courts have deprived them. 
They degraded their own judgment seat, abandoned its 
duties, countenanced the infidel maxim, ‘ Voz popult 
voz Dei,’ and now they cry to the people to deliver 
them from oppressions which their very flatteries of 
those people have provoked.” 

The agitation rapidly spread and gathered strength. 
Never were popular demagogues more successful in 
stimulating the enthusiasm of the multitude, than were 
the revolutionary party in their advocacy of the 
‘““Headship of Christ.” The halls in which the 
General Assemblies and the Commissions* met, 
were at every meeting crammed with excited and 
tumultuous crowds, congregated to cheer the popular . 
declamations to the echo. 


* The Commission, or “ committee of the whole house,” of the

DR. CHALMERS’ “ REEL OF BOGIE.” 267 


The unfortunate cases of disputed settlement which 
occurred in the years from 1838 to 1842, certainly 
afforded ground enough whence to appeal to the 
passions of the many, while they dismayed and dis- 
couraged the efforts and prayers for peace of the few. 
The state of the Church grew worse and worse, dis- 
union, disaffection, wrath and malice and evil speaking 
Were increasing on every side, while the love that 
cannot dwell in unquiet chambers, where its calm 
sovereignty is slighted, seemed to have fled away to 
seek elsewhere its home. The following extract from 
a letter written during the commission of March, 1840, 
gives a glimpse of scenes that were too frequently 
repeated. In order to render intelligible the allusion 
to the “ Reel of Bogie,” it must be premised that the 
majority of the Presbytery of Strathbogie had been 
suspended from the performance of ministerial duty 
by the commission of December, 18389, because they 
had in spite of the people’s veto, and in compliance 
with an injunction of the Court of Session, taken 
Mr. Edwards, the presentee to Marnoch, on trial for 
ordination. The Presbytery of St. Andrews in a 
similar case had acted in a like manner, choosing to 
obey the law as interpreted by the Court of Session, 
rather than to give heed to the standing order of the 
Church recently enacted, which ruled that no action 
should be taken in any case of disputed settlement 
until the matter had been referred to the General 
Assembly. Dr. Chalmers, in demanding that the 
Presbytery of St. Andrews should be dealt with as 
that of Strathbogie had been, made very merry over 
the picture of these two Presbyteries being “ partners 
in a dance of mazy and multiform confusion”—deserib- 
ing the former as coming forward to play her part in

LORD ABERDEEN’S INTERVENTION. 269 


go to Kilsyth on Tuesday,” to preach to the people 
there, among whom at the time there was a very 
earnest spirit of revival and enquiry. ‘I spoke,” he 
writes from Kilsyth, “‘to two hundred people that were 
collected in less than an hour’s time. I was told by 
several that my address had aroused and quickened 
them. To-day I addressed the factory people, in 
which I had great enjoyment, and they were deeply 
impressed.” 

In the early summer of this year a brighter day 
seemed about to dawn upon the troubled Church. 
The great and good Earl of Aberdeen, no less devoted - 
to his mother Church than distinguished as a states- 
man, introduced a Bill in the month of May for the 
purpose of adjusting the difficulties with which she 
was harassed. The Bill secured to the people—in 
place of an absolute and unreasoning veto—the right 
of objecting to any presentee, and of stating their 
objections ; and to the Presbytery their constitutional 
prerogative of examining these objections, and giving 
them whatever effect they were judged to deserve. 
One would have thought this was ample security 
against intrusion, but no—the Bill was indignantly 
rejected by a majority of eighty-seven in the General 
Assembly, and its author branded as a traitor to the 
Church’s glorious Head—as trying to hurl the Re- 
deemer from His throne, and ,to tear the crown from 
His brow. He consequently withdrew the measure in 
the House of Lords before its third reading. 

The Duke of Argyll next stepped forward to the 
rescue. In May, 1841 (the strife of parties in Scot- 
land having in the intervening year grown fiercer and 
fiercer), he laid a Bill on the table of the House of 
Lords, which was in its principle identical with the 


270 THE DUKE OF ARGYLL’S BILL. 


Church’s Veto Law. It differed from the Veto Law 
chiefly in this, that it extended the right of Veto to all 
male communicants above twenty years of age; and it 
provided that the Veto should be set aside in any case 
in which it could be proved to have been exercised on 
account of mere factiousness or prejudice. By a 
majority of 125, the General Assembly which met on 
the 20th May, approved of and adopted the Bill. It 
was destined, however, never to reach a second read- 
ing. The Whig Ministry fell in June, and Parliament 
was dissolved. When the Legislature met after the 
new elections, the reins of government were transferred 
to the hands of Sir Robert Peel, who had no sympathy 
with the demands for “spiritual independence” urged by 
the clamant majority of the Assembly. A Mr. Camp- 
bell, who was one of the advocates of the revolutionary 
party in the House of Commons, brought forward a 
measure identical with the Duke of Argyll’s in April, 
1842, but after some delay and opposition it was 
finally thrown out. The subject was not a favourite 
nor a well understood one in the House, and the ques- 
tion of a noble lord, overheard in the lobby, indicated 
only too truly the spirit in which it was approached : 
‘What the are we to do with this d Scotch 
Kirk of yours ?” 

Meantime, there had been an attempt made by Sir 
George Sinclair, a well:known member of the Church, 
to effect a pacific arrangement, which had at first pro- 
mised well, but eventually been thwarted by the oppo- 
sition of the extreme party. Sir George had, in 
September, 1841, proposed to add to the bill which 
Lord Aberdeen had withdrawn, a clause which should 
conciliate that party’s support. The object of the 
clause was to provide that, in the event of a strong and 


PROGRESS OF THE AGITATION. 271 


continued opposition to a presentee, the Presbytery, 
though, in their judgment, the reasons of that oppo- 
sition might not be satisfactory, should give effect to 
it, lest by disregarding it, they should “ preclude the 
prospect of the presentee’s usefulness in that particular 
parish.” The “ Non-Intrusion Committee” agreed to 
accept the bill with this clause, and their approbation 
included that of the party in the Church whom they 
represented ; and had not Parliament been prorogued 
early in October, the bill, with this addition, would 
have been reintroduced and passed into law. Unfor- 
tunately, by the time Parliament reassembled, and 
when the Government was willing to carry the measure 
through, the revolutionary tide on which they had 
embarked had carried the ‘‘ Non-Intrusion Committee ” 
far beyond the point of any constitutional adjustment 
of their grievances. Accordingly, in April, 1842, they 
deliberately repudiated the arrangement which they 
had professed themselves willing to accept in October, 
1841: * while in the Assembly which met in May, a 
resolution condemning patronage as a grievance which 
ought to be abolished, was carried by a majority of 
69—a majority, swelled by the influence of those who, 
in 1834, had introduced the Veto law with the view, (to 
use the words of its introducer,) “ undoubtedly of pre- 
serving, not of putting down, the right of patronage.” 
While the revolutionary agitators were thus rushing 
on to the consummation of their wilful and headstrong 
policy; a middle party emerged, composed in part of 
those who had been inclined to seek either the legali- 
zation of the Veto law, or some other constitutional 
means of satisfying the demand for an amendment of 


* See Dr. Turner’s “Scottish Secession,” chap. xi. 


272 THE “ FORTY.” 


the law of patronage, but who could not conscientiously 
advance to the point the extreme Non-Intrusionists 
had now reached; in part of those who, from the 
first, had condemned and lamented the Veto and its 
bitter fruits. 

Among this party, and in that section of it known 
as the Forty,* Mr. Story now took his stand. It was 
& position exposing those who held it to all the vitu- 
peration and abuse of the Non-Intrusionists, hurled 
even more vehemently at them than at “the old 
Moderates,” because their opponents could not but 
perceive that in the middle party, and specially among 
the Forty, there were not a few on whose earnestness, 
piety, and sincerity, they could cast no slur, and who 
In spirit were far removed from indifference or Eras- 
tianism. The old Moderates, such as Dr. Cook, Dr. 
Mearns, Dr. Bryce, &c., were their long-established 
foes; but this new battalion was made up of different 
men, whose opposition was less expected, and less 
easily flouted with the old reproach of Moderatism. 

Mr. Story and Mr. Wylie had in former days, in the 
same Synod which now held the Forty, defended their 
friend the minister of Row against the charge of heresy. 
Such an opportunity of adding the “ odium theolo- 
gicum ”’ to the already bitter elements of strife, as this 
fact afforded, was not to be let slip by so keen-witted 
a controversialist as the Editor of the “ Witness.” 
‘Where now,” he asks in a leader,t devoted to the 
pounding of the obnoxious party, “are the gibbering 
disciples of Row and Gareloch—the men who, some 
ten or twelve years ago, were busy working unseen

CORRESPONDENCE. 273 


miracles and speaking unknown tongues? Scattered - 
over many a devious track—some deep in the delusions 
of Puseyism—some reposing supinely in the infalli- 
bility of Rome—some foremost in the ranks of infi-

Those portions of Mr. Story’s correspondence which 
have been preserved are so scanty as to offer no ex- 
tracts very clearly indicative of his views and feelings 
during this time ; but the excerpts which follow from 
letters written to him by a well-known dignitary of 
another communion, with whom he corresponded on 
Church matters, and with whose opinions he cordially 
apreed, will help to throw some light on his own stand- 
point, and on the reasons which had guided his course 
throughout the whole contest, now hastening to its ill- 
starred close :— 


“My dear Story,—1. Dr. Chalmers says that the 
ureconcilable difference between your Church and mine 
is, that you hold Christ to be the head of the Church, 
while we hold the Queen. If this have any meaning, 
it must mean either that we hold the Queen to be the 
head of the Church in the same sense in which you hold 
Christ—that is, that we believe our Bible, sacraments, 
and orders, to be true and effective, because they are 
sanctioned by the Crown; or that you hold Christ to 
be the head of the Church in the sense in which we 
hold the Queen—that is, that you believe Christ 
ought to, and that he does personally, alter your cere- 
monies and formularies, and exercise certain nghts of 
patronage, according to the expediency of the case. 
That is, that either you or we are actually insane. 

Py 


Q74 CORRESPONDENCE. 


“2. In all this question, it should be kept in mind 
that there is the common law of Christianity, which is 
contained in the positive commands of Scripture, and 
the bye-laws which each National Church makes for 
itself. If the Veto, or popular election, be the common _ 
law of the Church, then every established Church 
throughout Christendom is in rebellion against its 
Divine Head; if it be only a bye-law, then, though it 
may be wrong in any member of your National Church 
to disobey it, it may be very right for him to disapprove 
of and regret it. 

“3. It must be remembered, that the Church of 
Scotland is the clergy and laity of your communion 
within the kingdom of Scotland; that the laity, using 
their right and interest, are not bound to act simply 
in courts called ecclesiastical, but may act by courts 
of law, or of Parliament, and have done so without 
challenge in innumerable cases. 

“4, That in a Missionary Church it would be 
absurd to maintain the right of the heathen, who are 
to be taught to choose their teacher; that a partially 
informed, or partially sanctified people, can be only 
partially qualified to choose their pastor; that, there- 
fore, the absolute right of choice is not a fundamental 
right, but can only be termed right when it is proved 
to be expedient. In any parish where it can be proved 
that the majority are truly pious, thoroughly acquainted 
with the doctrine, and practically obedient to the law 
of Christianity, I will allow they have an equitable 
right to the choice of their own minister, but not to 
dispose of a stipend to which they do not contribute. 

“So long as ‘no patronage’ is treated, not as a rea- 
sonable and expedient polity, but as the assertion of 
Christ’s authority, it must be opposed by conscientious 


CORRESPONDENCE. 275 


and well-informed men, as false, oppressive, and 
fanatical. 

“5, As the supreme government of Jehovah over 
the affairs of men, does not make Queen Victoria an 
usurper, so neither does the acknowledged headship 
of Christ render it absurd to acknowledge the head- 
ship of a minister in his own parish, of a Bishop in 
his diocese, of a General Assembly, or of a Court of 
Session. Nay, even the Pope, when he claims to be 
the visible head of the Church, usurps, not upon the 
authority of Christ, but upon the rights of his fellow- 
Christians. Temporal powers (Courts of Session, 
Parliament, the Queen in Council) violate the rights 
of the people when they meddle in ecclesiastical 
matters otherwise than as the conjoint constitution 
of the Church and State warrants. Thus I think that 
the attaching the penalties of premunire to a refusal 
to elect a Bishop recommended by the Crown, is mani- 
fest tyranny; for the very form of election implies a 
constitutional right to elect, and this is practically 
taken away by the penalty. 

“6. It is allowed that in Scotland a civil process, 
that of induction, is conjoined with a spiritual, that of 
ordination: it is therefore equally competent for the 
civil courts, as for the ecclesiastical, to say they ought 
to have the supreme authority in the matter. .In 
England ordination is entirely in the hands of the 
Bishop, and has no reference to induction. Induction 
supposes a previous ordination, and can be compelled 
by a civil power. Surely no man can say that such a 
separation is contrary to the headship of Christ; and 
if your Church could alleviate the bitterness of the 
strife in which she is engaged by such a separation, she 


would do well to make it. These are someof my poor 
t Z 


a" mim 


276 CORRESPONDENCE. 


thoughts upon the matters in question. As to the 
temper and language with which the controversy is 
carried on, I only say that the loudest blusterer for the 
headship of Christ, if he have not charity, 1s but a 
sounding brass and a.tinkling cymbal.” | 


‘My dear Story,—Really the rheumatism had little 
to do when it set upon spoiling your hand; at present 
it has really reached the ne plus ultra of unintelligi- 
bility. . . . . . . As to the Church Catholic, 
I believe that the Apostles fixed a complete system of 
government, part of which was temporary, part per- 
petual; and as their immediate successors retained 
only the three orders, I think there is ahigh probability 
that those only were to be continued. I see in the 
Acts, that the government of the Church was not 
purely hierarchical, but that the Christian laity sat in 
council with the inspired Apostles, and that the decree 
of the council of Jerusalem ran in their name, as well 
as in that of the Apostles. I see that Cyprian, the 
oracle of High Churchmen, and really a good and great 
man, considered the approbation of the people essen- 
tial to his acts of discipline, and that in his age the 
concurrence of the laity and provincial bishops was 
requisite for the election of bishops. I see that as 
soon as the empire was acknowledged to be Christian, 
the Emperor, though not a Christian himself, did, as 
legal representative of the whole Christian laity, preside 
over the Council of Nice. From all this I infer that no 
government of the Church can be agreeable to the will 
of Christ, in which the judgment both of clergy and 
laity is not consulted. In Voluntary Churches this is 
effectually done by vestries, sessions, &c.: often, so as 
to tyrannize over members who boast of their indepen- 


SS SP ig ay eye 


CORRESPONDENCE. 277 


dence. In Established Churches it is far better man- 
aged by the general control which the chief magistrate, 
i.e., not the Queen personally, but the legislature, exer- 
cises over the Church. In England, the constitutional 
view is, that the State of England is the mass of the 
English people considered in relation to their temporal 
interests and organization ; while the Church of Eng- 
land is the same mass considered in relation to its 
Spiritual interests. When our fundamental laws were 
framed, dissent was unknown and uncontemplated. 
In your case the matter was very different. The Kirk 
was a predominant sect, which made a contract with 
the State, as an external and adverse body, The 
power which the State exercises in England is, there- 
fore, I believe, of divine right,—so long as the people 
of England consent to consider the legislature as their 
representative in spiritual as well as in temporal 
affairs. In Scotland, it is merely the result of a com- 
pact or series of compacts, embodied in the statute law 
of the country. The meaning of these compacts can 
be explained only by the sworn interpreters of the law. 
If the Church, which is in Scotland a body quite dis- 
tinct, from the State, finds the compact incompatible 
with its duty to God, let it renounce the wages of 
iniquity, and if the State be willing, make a new 
arrangement for the future. But really, to break the 
existing compact, make no intelligent demands for a 
new compact, and lay upon the State the onus of 
finding out what would satisfy the conscience of the 
Church, which has at least three very distinct con- 
sciences, is too bad. Can you make anything out of 
this very syllabical sketch of my views of the Regale? 
. .. I can see no end to the controversy but the dis- 
solution of your Establishment; for I cannot imagine

THE CONVOCATION. 279 


the first of which, subscribed by 422 ministers, detailed 
the supposed grievances of the Church; and the second 
of which, subscribed by 354 ministers, proposed as the 
remedy for these, an appeal to Parliament, and in the 
event of its rejection, secession from the Establishment. 
There can be no doubt that many adhered to the second 
of these, in the fond belief that the declaration of their 
determination to quit the Church, would coerce the 
Government into submission to their demands. 

This, however, was a most deceiving calculation. 
The Government stood firm, and when the Church’s 
“Claim of Rights” was brought before the House of 
Commons by Mr. Fox Maule in March, 1843, it was re- 
ceived by the ministry with a direct negative, and the 
proposal for a committee of inquiry rejected by a large 
majority. The revolutionary party thus saw their own - 
chosen alternative before them: they had pledged 
themselves to secede, and right or wrong, the pledge 
must be redeemed. 

But they did not act precipitately. The Established 
Church was Egypt and Babylon, but still it was a com- 
fortable shelter until they could complete all their 
preparations for their journey to the land flowing with 
the milk and honey of spiritual independence. Accord- 
ingly the intending seceders proceeded to collect funds, 
to prepare meeting-houses, to organize societies, to 
harangue and agitate in every parish in Scotland, with 
the view of securing a large following, and providing a 
well-filled treasury.* For the two or three months 
that they remained in the Church after the secession 


* An old woman in Rosneath being urged to contribute to the fund 
for the seceding ministers, replied, “I hae heard o’ some that went oot 
lang syne, and they were tell’t no to tak’ purse or scrip wi’ them, but 
ye’re a hantle wiser—ye send the purse and the scrip afore ye.” 


280 MEETING AT ROSNEATH. 


was irrevocably resolved on, they were certainly, 
though in the Church, not of it, and only. continued 
within its pale for the purpose of dealing their mother 
at the last a more deliberate and fatal blow. 

Rosneath was of course not overlooked by the 
zealots, who were careering through the country as 
deputies of the Convocation, with the view of enlight- 
ening the people on the duty of committing the sin of 
schism, and abandoning the Church of their fathers. 

In many parishes the deputies were not received 
by the parish minister, and not allowed to deliver 
their harangues in the Church. Mr. Story, how- 
ever, hospitably entertained the two who were sent 
to shed their light on the darkness of his flock, 
and admitted the one who was to speak, to his 
pulpit, which he had been careful never to desecrate 
by any such discourses on Church politics as formed 
the staple of too many of the Sabbatic orations of the 
Nonintrusionists. , 

In intimating the meeting on the previous Sunday, 
he said: “ You are aware the subject to be discussed is 
one I have always refrained from bringing before you, 
because, being set to watch for your souls as one who 
must give account, I feel that I would have sinned 
against them, had I led any man to believe that the 
conclusions to which he came in such a matter could at 
all affect his eternal salvation, or take the place of that 
inquiry which I am bound ever to press on each of 
you, viz., ‘ What 1s God to you, and what is your con- 
dition before God ?’” His jealousy of the mixture of 
any lower principles or questions with the all-important 
subject of eternal salvation led him, it cannot be 
doubted, to preserve a silence on the points in dispute 
which was injudicious. His abstaining from speaking 


MEETING AT ROSNEATH. 281 


to his people about the existing controversies did not 
keep them out of the whirlpool of popular excitement, 
but only left them a prey to the fiery declamations and 
seductive misrepresentations of the ‘agitators. 

The orator who addressed the meeting at Rosneath, 
gave a fair enough representation of the state of 
matters in the Church, but concluded with the some- 
what startling, though on like occasions very usual 
announcement, that he was sure no Christian man who 
heard what he had said could be in any doubt as to 
the duty of seceding; then waving his hand towards the 
minister whose pulpit he occupied, he added, “ But yet 
there are some, my brethren, who, though they profess 
to reverence the headship of Christ, say, ‘No, we 
won't go, we'll stick by our Manse, our Glebe, and our 
Stipend.’ ” When he had finished, Mr. Story rose, and 
after adverting briefly to what they had heard, said: 
‘““And now, brethren, one word about your own 
conduct in this matter. It is everywhere creating dis- 
quietude and dispeace, and many, alas, who think they 
are doing God service, are only by their hatred and 
malice, and envyings, and evil speakings, crucifying 
the Lord afresh, and putting Him to an open shame. 
Let it not be so with you; let every man be thoroughly 
persuaded in his own mind, for whatsoever is not of 
faith is sin; but let none judge or condemn his brother. 
Where God has not seen fit clearly to reveal his mind, 
men must be allowed to differ; and surely those for 
whom the Lord died, while they unite in praising God 
for this common salvation, should not allow minor 
differences to separate them, and should not be guilty 
of rending the seamless garment of their Lord. For 
myself, brethren, I have from the beginning disapproved 
of, and on every fit occasion opposed the steps that

THE SECEDERS’ ANIMOSITY. 283 


the fiddle amid the burning of Rome. The seceders 
jested over the downfall of the Church. 

As the time of the Assembly drew on, Mr. Story 
looked forward with ever-deepening apprehension to 
the evil days which he saw coming upon the Church. 
The estrangements, the malignings, the suspicions 
which were so rife weighed down his spirit with a 
sense of personal calamity. He saw in the conflict 
and its issues only a distracted society and a degraded 
Christianity. His whole soul revolted against the 
language used by the one party against the other, and 
against the base and ungenerous insinuation so broadly 
made and so industriously circulated, that “ filthy 
lucre” was the only tie that bound the clergy who 
refused to secede, to their mother Church. One 
example of the perverted state of feeling which these 
dissensions engendered, he often afterwards referred 
to as having given him at the time much pain. He 
attended a meeting held in Glasgow in March, 1848, “to 
form a young men’s Evangelical Church of Scotland 
Society.” An “Evangelical” orator, since renowned, 
no less as a philanthropist than as a preacher, in a 
speech garnished with ribald jests levelled at the con- 
stitutional party in the Church, took occasion to say, 
‘“‘I saw a person the other day who said to me, ‘ You 
speak of pulling down the Establishment?’ ‘Yes,’ said 
I, ‘I do, just because I have a regard for it I would pull 
it down. I have a regard for the dead body when the 
spirit is gone,* and just because I have a regard for 
it, I seek a burial-place for it. Better to lay the

stirring up the people to secede, had filled the whole 
land fuller than ever, of murmurings and disputings, 
enmity and discontent. In the Presbytery his motion 
for an Overture to the Assembly for the repeal of the
