DESIGN OF THE CONVOCATIONISTS. 285 


Veto law was carried: in the Synod his motion con- 
demnatory of the Convocation was after a long discus- 
sion defeated. 

There can be no doubt, that up to the last, many 
who seceded were under the delusive impression that 
they would not be required to leave the Church. Their 
idea was, that the threat of a large secession would force 
the Government to alter its determination even at the 
eleventh hour. ‘“‘ Nothing but the extension of our 
front will now save the Church as a national Establish- 
ment,” wrote a well known member of the revolutionary 
party to Mr. Story, urging him to join the movement. 
He wrote in reply: “I am fully aware of the difficulties 
which are before me by reason of the peculiar position 
I occupy, standing with a few others on a narrow 
isthmus between two tumultuous and hostile par- 
ties, but the difficulties do not affect my judgment 
of duty. It would be far easier for me to go with you 
than to remain, but I think I have the mind of Christ 
in resolving to remain at my post, however difficult to 
maintain it, and faithfully to watch over the flock I 
have solemnly vowed to feed with the blessed truth of 
God.” 

It has been represented that the Government was 
convinced that the secession would be a very small 
affair, and that it was unmoved by the mutterings of the 
coming storm, because it believed the tempest would roll 
away without bursting. But the Government had fully 
weighed the alternatives; and her Majesty’s advisers 
had come to the almost necessary conclusion that a 
secession, however formidable in numbers and influence, 
would be a lesser evil than the concession of the claims 
of the revolutionary party. A noble lord, who, though 
not a member of the Cabinet, enjoyed the confidence of 


286 ‘‘ FREE” KIRK SELF-PRAISE. 


those in office, wrote to Mr. Story: “ Rest assured, there 
will be no change in the Government's determination. 
We have now only to face the result, whatever it 
may be. I agree with you in thinking that many 
underrate the amount of secession which will take 
place. Great calamities and troubles will follow on all 
this. I deplore them, and would, if I could, have 
averted them. But while fully estimating the calamity 
and very sincerely lamenting it, I do not for one 
instant doubt as to the propriety of the decision 
expressed by Government against ‘the Claim of 
Rights.’* Great calamity may follow the rejection of 
it, but evils ten thousand times greater would have 
been the inevitable and irremediable consequence of 
its admission and adoption.” 

It has been the custom of “ Free” Church writers 
and orators to descant upon the glorious self-sacrifice of 
those who in 1843 forsook the Establishment, and on 
the mercenary meanness of those who adhered to it. 
One might have expected, that men making a great 
sacrifice for conscience sake would have been generous 
enough to believe in the honesty and conscientiousness 
of those who did not feel called upon to make that 
sacrifice. One might have expected from those who, as 
the popular and godly party, enjoyed at once so much 
of human favour and Divine grace, some little sympathy 
with the griefs and difficulties of those who believed 
God’s message to them was, “Be watchful and 
strengthen the things that remain that are ready to 
die,” as well as the jubilation and self-praise which 


* The document embodying the demands of the dominant party in 
the Church, drawn up by Mr. Dunlop (now Mr. Murray Dunlop, M.P., 
for Greenock), and brought before the House of Commons by Mr. Fox 
Maule (now Earl of Dalhousie). 


PAINFULNESS OF MR. STORY'S POSITION. 287 


signalized their own obedience to the call which they 
declared was addressed to them, “Come out from 
among them and be ye separate.” 
But sympathy with the feelings, or respect for the 
motives of the remanent section of the clergy never 
was expressed. Gibes and scorn, misrepresentation 
and calumny were their portion, and to their lasting 
credit, be it recorded, a portion accepted meekly and 
without retaliation. There were many who remained, 
to whom it would have been easier to secede, to whom 
it was harder to stay behind amid reproach and thank- 
less neglect than to go out amid a tumult of popular 
applause. “ It would be far easier for me to go than to 
remain,’ said Mr. Story. With the leaders who went 
out, such as Chalmers, Welsh, and Gordon (apart 
from their views on Church polity) he had far more in 
common than with the leaders who stuck by the good 
old Church. To remain broke the ties of many a 
friendship, severed from his discipline and teaching half 
his flock (almost all of whom would have gone with him~ 
had he gone), bound him to defend what he believed to 
be, though a righteous, a ruined cause, and to fight 
amid obloquy and sorrow and heartsickness a losing 
battle, for at the time of the Secession his impression 
was that the days of the Church as an Established 
Church were numbered. Still he could not forget his 
ordination vow ; he would not throw down beneath the 
people’s feet one of the Church’s solemn functions. 
When he, and all the clergymen who took a prominent 
part in the affairs of the Church, were ordained her 
ministers, there was no Veto law, no popular election, 
no claim of spiritual independence. He and they, so 
far from repudiating the interference or denying the 
jurisdiction of the civil magistrate, had signed the “Con- 


288 HIS OBLIGATION TO REMAIN. 


fession of Faith,” which declares * that he “ hath autho- 
rity and it is his duty to take order that unity and 
peace be preserved in the Church, that the truth of 
God be kept pure and entire, that all blasphemies and 
heresies be suppressed, all corruptions and abuses in 
worship and discipline prevented or reformed, and all 
the ordinances of God duly settled, administered, and 
observed. For the better effecting whereof he hath 
power to call synods, to be present at them, and to pro- 
vide that whatsoever is transacted in them be according 
to the mind of God.” Having subscribed this declara- 
tion, he could not now see it to be his duty to forsake 
the Church because the civil magistrate had, in virtue 
of the powers which this very declaration assigns to 
him, refused to sanction the Church’s high-handed 
defiance of the law of the land. He could not hold 
himself to be released from the life-long obligations 
of his ordination vow, because a dominant party in 
the Church had come into collision with the courts 
of law; nor could all the eloquence of Nonintrusion 
platforms banish from his memory and his conscience 
the solemnity of the oath by which he had sworn, as he 
should answer to God most High, and in the presence of 
the flock which the Church in God’s name and by God’s 
authority had entrusted to his care, that he promised to 
submit to the Church’s government and discipline, and 
“to concur with the same, and never endeavour directly 
or indirectly the prejudice or subversion thereof,” and 
that “according to his power he would maintain the 
unity and peace of the Church against error and schism, 
notwithstanding of whatsoever trouble and persecution 
might arise, and that he would follow no divisive 


* “ Confession of Faith,” chap. xxiii. 8. 


HOPE OF BETTER DAYS. 289 


courses from the present established doctrine, worship, 
discipline, and government of the Church.” * 

This vow he and all the fiery partisans of popular 
election and spiritual independence had, on their taking 
holy orders, sworn to keep; he, for one,‘ believed that 
to quit the Church, at whose hands he had received his 
ordination, in her day of danger and trial, would be 
nothing less than to forswear himself, and to commit 
the sin of schism. : 

It would have been well for the people of Scotland, 
if all the ministers of their National Church-had been 


able with as prayerful an earnestness as his to arrive 


at this conviction, and with as unshrinking a fidelity to 
hold to it. 

The day, we may hope, will yet come, when, moved 
by the charitable impulse of arevived and strengthened 
unity, the nation shall join in deploring the evil coun- 
sels that wrought the desolation of its Church, and 
when our beloved Zion shall once more be called by 
all “ Sought out, a city not forsaken.” + And when that 
day comes, if indeed it ever dawn, surely we or our 
children shall look back with wonder to the inconsis- 
tency which led men, many of them good and some of 
them wise, to convulse a country and to rend a Church 
in defence of no vital doctrine, of no sacred principle, 
but of a vague theory which ere a few years had 
elapsed had utterly broken down in practice.{ Perhaps 
the delusive mist will then be scattered which still 
wraps in its folds the Secession of 1843, and which 
softens its outlines and magnifies its bulk. And if so, 


* From the Ordination Service of the Scottish Church. 
+ Isaiah Lxii. 12. 
t See a proof of it in “ the Cardross Case.” 


290 TRUE ASPECT OF THE SECESSION. 


if the visionary charm is dispelled and the bare truth 
clearly seen, he who may have time or will to scan 
heedfully the troubled page in the stormy history of 
Scottish Presbytery which bears the chronicles of the 
“Ten Years’ Conflict,” will probably conclude that 
the close of that contest left the Established Church 
no less a free Church than the body that forsook her, 
and that whatever additional privileges the seceding 
phalanx believed they gained by their movement, 
were dearly purchased at the cost of violated peace, 
broken unity, estranged love, and dishonoured vows. 

The “Free” Kirk Secession has won to itsélf 
enough of the world’s honour and applause, but let it 
be remembered that it erected itself on the ruins of a 
venerable Church’s peace and order. Art lent her aid 
to portray the scenes and heroes of the memorable 
struggle and protest ; Fiction threw her charmed mantle 
over the romance of the great self-sacrifice ; and the 
religious world, inflamed with a contagious enthusiasm, 
forgot that when the seceders marched in pretended 
triumph from S. Andrew’s Church to Tanfield Hall, 
they had broken the constitutional law, which as citi- 
zens they were bound to revere; they had violated the 
solemn vows, which as clergymen they had sworn to 
keep; they had rent their Lord’s mystical Body, whose 
unity it should have been their heart’s desire, as 
Christians, to preserve. 


CHAPTER II. 
THE SECESSION AND THE SECEDERS. 


THe General Assembly of 1848 met on the 18th of 
May. This was the day chosen by the leaders of the 
revolutionary party as that on which they should 
formally sever themselves from the Church of their 
fathers, and consummate the schism on which they 
had long before resolved. In strict honour and con- 
sistency they had been bound to secede as soon as the 
Legislature refused to recognize their “Claim of 
Rights ;” but they had selected the time for their 
Exodus, not with the view of escaping as soon as pos- 
sible from the Egyptian bondage of the Establishment, - 
but of maturing their plans, and surrounding with the 
highest possible dramatic effect and moral éclat the final 
act of their divisive courses. 

To make their secession, however, what they fondly 
termed it—a Disruption, it would have been necessary 
that ere they quitted the Church, the General Assembly 
should be duly constituted, its moderator chosen, its 
roll of members made up. The seceders knew that if 
these ordinary and regular steps had been taken, they 
would have found themselves in a decided minority in 
that court, in which they had for nearly ten years been 
dominant. This was owing to various causes: to the 


conviction of many who had up to a certain point acted 
u 2 


292 THE WELL-TIMED EXIT. 


with the revolutionists, that their final step would be 
a criminal and unjustifiable one,—to the exclusion from 
the Church Courts by a recent decision of the Court of 
Session,* of ministers who officiated in chapels, and who 
had no legal parochial status (and by whom the majority 
had been chiefly made up),—to the growing activity 
and organization of the parties that advocated consti- 
tutional measures. The seceders, therefore, although 
they waited from the 10th of March till the 18th of 
May for the consummation of their schism, did not on 
the 18th of May wait from three o’clock till four, to allow 
of the Assembly being constituted in the usual way; but 
at once tendered their protest against its being consti- 
tuted at all, and being regarded as a true representative 
of the Church of Scotland; and having done s0, with- 
drew to the place of meeting that had been prepared for 
their reception at Tanfield. 

_ The copious historian of the “'Ten Years’ Conflict,” 
in his graphic description of the Seceders’ Exodus, 
would have his readers to believe that when Dr. 
Welsh, as Corypheus of the retreating throng, 
bowed, ere retiring, to the Queen’s Commissioner 
(Lord Bute), he in so doing symbolized “the Church 
of Scotland’s farewell to the State.” It was not 
so; only the wilfullest misrepresentation could so 
describe it. The Church of Scotland remained united 
to and co-ordinate with the State; a minority, in many 
ways respectable and influential no doubt, but still 
@ mere minority, of her clergy seceded, and in so

excellence the Church of Scotland, that it is due to

numerical ground it had at that time for arrogating this

with the consciousness of a heavy loss, and the anxie- 
ties of a troublous future. Every good man’s heart 
could not but bleed at the spectacle of a Church so rent 
and shattered—so grievously wounded by her own 
children. “Oh my heart is sad,” wrote Mr. Story, 
“to think of this terrible crisis—so vast a multitude of 
ministers and people, all Dissenters. The breach will 
never be healed in our time; yet these evils must be 
faced, in the spirit of the Lord Jesus Christ.” In 
another letter he says: “ An event has thus occurred, 
which must affect future generations in a way none can 
calculate. May God overrule it for good—let this be 
the subject of our constant prayers. . . . The 
business after the party left was mere routine; there 
was no contest for the moderatorship,* no vote taken ; 
and under the circumstances I saw no just necessity 
of saying anything at thattime. . . . The Queen’s 
letter to the Assembly is unhappily drawn up, and there 
are some expressions about obedience to the law, 
which will have very evil effects. It is not so favour- 
able as the private ‘letter of Smollett,} or the public 
speech of Lord Aberdeen, led one to expect. . . 

A is unhappy, but he did not go off with the

resolutions he regarded as the greatest blunder he ever

went with the rest—I suppose he did.” It may be 
observed, in passing, that both these dubious individuals 
were soon among the mostflaming advocates of the seces-

made by the revolutionists, in abandoning the Church. 

Again Mr. Story writes, on May 21st, “ There will 
be no difficulty in showing how. different I and some 
others are from the Moderate section of the Assembly. 
I feel that, with the exception of Adam Tait,* I have 
little sympathy withany init. . . . . Iam weary 
of the hotel; and so many things have occurred to take 
away all pleasure from this visit, that I shall never 
think of this Assembly but with deepest pam. . . 
Amid the forms of delusion that encircle one, it 1s a 
consolation to see God’s own arm stretched forth, on 
which we can rely and realize the peace and confidence 
which pass all understanding. Pray, pray for us.” 

The reader will be at no loss to understand the 
meaning of the first sentence in the above extract. 
Between the writer of it and the bulk of the old Mode- 
rate party, there could be, as has been indicated in the 
preceding chapter, but little sympathy; and though 
compelled by his views of duty to adopt the course of 
action which they adopted, the reasons and principles 
which led him so to do, were often widely different 
from theirs. He was a constitutional Churchman, and 
therefore his policy generally agreed with that of the 
Moderate party; but he was in the truest sense an 
evangelical theologian, and a most earnest and devoted 
minister, and therefore his doctrine and his practice 
were of a somewhat loftier standard than was commonly 
sought by the “ Moderates.” 


. Adam Duncan Tait, Minister of Kirkliston, then as now 
rst justly respected ministers of the Church. 


MR, STORY'S STATEMENT OF HIS PRINCIPLES. 297 


The statement of his own principles, as respected 
the points in which they differed from those of the 
‘“* Moderates,” he found opportunities of making during 
the discussions regarding the repeal of the Veto law, 
and the “‘reponing” of the deposed ministers of Strath- 
bogie. He spoke frequently, but one can gather from. 
the reports but little of the substance and meaning of 
what he said. ‘I am absurdly and incorrectly re- 
ported,” he says on one occasion. Again, when he had 
moved that the ecclesiastical censures with which the 
Strathbogie ministers had been visited should not be 
removed until the Church had come to a decision 
regarding the repeal of the Veto law, under whose 
influence these censures had been pronounced, he says: 
“The Strathbogie ministers are reponed. ‘My motion 
was supported only by fifteen. My speech, they say, 
was a good one; but speaking rapidly, as I preach, the 
reporters do not catch the words.” 

It is in connection with what Mr. Story said on this 
subject that his name is introduced in the closing 
chapter of the “'Ten Years’ Conflict,” apparently with 
no other object than to level a sneer at him as one of 
the “Forty.” His motion was defeated—his views 
were overborne, and yet, “‘ faedum dictu visuque,” he did ~ 
not forsake the tyrannical Church of his fathers, but 
actually considered it his duty to adhere to the engage- 
ment which, at his ordination, he had assumed, that he 
should “ follow no divisive courses,” and that he should 
submit himself ‘“ humbly and in the spirit of meekness 
to the superior judicatories of the Church.” Of course, 
from Dr. Buchanan’s enlightened view-point, such a 
man can be regarded as only a spiritless slave. “ Mr. 
Story and his friends,” says the Xenophon “a little 
scratched,” of the “Free” Kirk, “might make wry

“DEED OF DEMISSION” ACCEPTED. 299 


separate themselves. He gave them credit for sin-

were with them to a great extent, but with them in their 
final step of secession he could feel no sympathy what- 
ever.” 

His advocacy of delay was unsuccessful,—the se- 
ceders’ “‘ Deed of Demission” being at once accepted 
and acted upon; but the pleading was characteristic. 
His large charity begat the hope that a brotherly deal- 
ing with those who had gone might yet induce them to 
return. His heart yearned after them still as brethren 
beloved. Amid all the uproar of the popular demon- 
strations in Tanfield Hall, he hoped the still small 
voice of Christian love might perhaps yet be heard, 


* Great as this was, it should be fairly estimated. The burden of the 
sacrifice has fallen on those who have said least about it—on the mass of 
the lay members of the Church, and on the country ministers. The chapel 
ministers who went out, instead of losing (in a pecuniary sense), gained 
by going—their yearly income as “free” ministers being considerably 
more than what they commonly had within the Establishment. The 
great principle stated by Dr. Chalmers in the Convocation (Nov. 1842) 
was that “the ministers of the most opulent parishes agree to share 
alike with the ministers of the poorest parishes in Scotland.” It is 
notorious, however, that the town ministers have, in not a few cases, 
larger stipends than they had before they left the Church. Though 
they draw an equal sum from the “Sustentation Fund,” it is amply 
supplemented, while the poor pastors in the country have their bare 
pittance and not a farthing more. Dr. Guthrie said, and that truly, in 
one of his speeches before the secession, “It would not be doing us in 
the metropolis any temporal injury to turn us out. Why, there’s Dr. 
Candlish, who by being forced to be a voluntary minister, would treble 
his stipend.” And ata meeting long after the secession, a country 
minister of the “Free” Kirk said, and with no less truth, ‘“ We are 
compelled from year’s end to year’s end to occupy every Presbytery 
with money affairs, and instead of going to the pulpit with the rich 
blessings of Heaven in our hands, there to proclaim them free as the 
air we breathe, to carry on an eternal wrangle and reproach with the 
people about their own niggardly supplies. . . . . I could weep 
tears of bitterest agony over such a loss of character, and such an 
obacuration, if not total blighting, of one’s holy ministry ; and I for 
one will be a partner in such a hideous bankruptcy no longer.”—From 
a speech by the Rev. J. Purves, at Jedburgh, 16th Feb., 1858. 


v 


300 SPIRIT OF THE SECEDERS. 


recalling the men who had struck the causeless blow 
to a gentler and purer spirit, and leading them to look 
on the mother whom they had wounded, and to mourn. 
But the hope died away as soon as it arose: the schism 
was irreparable; the most sanguine soon saw that 
between the Church and the schismatics there was a 
great gulf fixed that widened day by day. There was 
no possibility of ‘Christian communion—of brotherly 
dealing between the members of the two opposing 
hosts. When the Assembly rose, and when the gather- 
ing at Tanfield Hall dispersed itself, the greatest of all 
the secessions from the Scottish Church was an irre- 
vocable fact. 

The Seceders retired to their respective districts, to 
apply themselves with all their might to the upbuild- 
ing of the unsightly fabric of their schism, and to the 
downpulling of the venerable Church of the Reforma- 
tion.* The Church turned in humble dependence on 
her Divine head to her painful, yet through His 
blessing, not hopeless task. She had to organize 
her reduced and weakened forces—to do all in her 


* “Some supposed they ought to recognize the Scottish Establish- 
ment and themselves as being two sections of the same Church, and 
that the one ought not to interfere with the other. But the principle 
on which they proceeded was, that they ought to go to the whole popu- 
lation of Scotland, to tell them why they had separated themselves 
from the Established Church, and to tell them farther that they ought 
to see their duty in the same light. . . . . . They had also 
brought prominently before the view of the legislature, of statesmen, 
and of the people of the land, that the strong principle which led them 
to separate from the Establishment would also lead them to oppose those 
who remained in the Establishment, and to desire the downfal of that 
Establishment. It would be folly in them as honest men to say other- 
wise. The very circumstance of their separation and protest, implied 
that they would aim at its downfal now. . . . Their position was 
that of hostility to the Establishment, for it could not be disguised that 
‘“s Scottish Establishment, and virtually the English Establishment 

were mountains which would need to be removed, that God’s 
m might be advanced.”—Dr. Candlish, 6th September, 1843. 


SPIRIT OF THE SECEDERS. 301 


power to strengthen the things that remained that 
were ready to die—to repair the shattered walls—to fill 
up the vacant places, and to efface as best she. could 
the marks of contest and disorder, which those who 
had deserted her in her time of trouble had left behind 
them. 

It would be unbecoming and uncalled for to occupy 
the reader’s attention with the details of a history now 
happily much forgotten. It is not needful to illustrate 
this narrative by reviving now the aged bitternesses 
and contendings of the year of the secession. Yet it ~ 
is due to the many good and self-sacrificing men who 
adhered to the Church, to remember that although 
their praise has not been sounded, nor their experience 
chronicled, no slight pain and suffering were theirs. 
It was not easy patiently to bear the rupture of 
hallowed ties, the severance of valued friendships, the 
secession of congregations long faithfully tended, the 
neglect and reproach and contumely, which often were 
their lot. It needed some patience—some Christian - 
temper to put up with such words, as these of the 
abusive organ of their self-constituted foes, “ Let the 
parish minister be regarded as virtually the one excom- 
municated man of the district, the man with whom no 
one is to join in prayer, whose Church is to be avoided 
as ad impure and unholy place, whose addresses are 
not to be listened to, whose visits are not to be re- 
ceived, who is everywhere to be put under the ban of 
the community :”—or with those of one of the leading 
divines of the new sect: “The ministers and people 
of the Free Church should proceed substantially on 
the theory that the provision for preaching and ordin- 
ances, by the residuary Establishment, is not to be 
taken into account at all, or held in any sense as ; 


302 SPIRIT OF THE SECEDERS. 


provision of the means of grace for the people. We 
ought to proceed to provide for the wants of the whole 
people, as if the Established Church had no existence 
whatsoever—a Church which had the light, and by its 
own act extinguished it to its own everlasting shame :” 
—or yet, again, with these of another of its reverend 
oracles: ‘ Whatever support the cause of religion may 
receive in Scotland, must be derived from the Free 
Church. The idea of the residuary Establishment 
doing anything valuable towards promoting the salva- 
tion of souls, is perfectly ridiculous.’”* - 

Such were the utterances of the chief of the pro- 
phets; it is not to be supposed that these and like 
hints for godly and brotherly walk and conversation, 
were lost upon the mixed multitude of their followers. 
Even a much smaller sect, animated by as bitter a 
spirit of antagonism, would have been a grievous hind- 
rance and annoyance to the Church; as it was, the 
seceders were in numbers strong enough, and in ani- 
mosity keen enough, to embroil the whole land, and 
sow dispeace throughout its length and breadth. It is 
not asserted that there was no retaliation—no evil 
feeling on the other side; but no one will be so misled 
by partisanship as to deny that the silence and forbear- 
ance were, with very rare exceptions, exhibited by 
the Church; and that the invective and malevolence 
were, with very rare exceptions, exhibited by her 
assailants. 
When Mr. Story returned home from the Assembly, 

he found the schism had been accomplished in his 
once quiet and united parish,—half his people had 
ceased to acknowledge him as their Minister—half his 


* Culled from orations by Drs. Candlish and ‘Cunningham. 


THE SECESSION IN ROSNEATH. 303 


congregation had left the Church where they and their 
fathers had worshipped God. Few things in his life 
smote his heart with a keener pang than the spectacle 
that met his eye, as he looked on the Sunday morning 
after his return, from his study window to the hill 
behind the Manse. Formerly the brae would have 
been dotted with groups wending their way towards the 
Parish Church ; now other groups were there travel- 
ling in the opposite direction, making their way to the 
School House, on Loch-Longside, where the emissary 
of the “ Free ” Kirk was to discourse. 

The people of the parish were in a state of great 
excitement ; party spirit ran high. The secession was 
proclaimed there, as elsewhere, the “great religious 
movement ;” yet to the impartial on-looker, it seemed 
rather to be, in its character and results, a great irre- 
ligious commotion. “If the agitation shall lead to the 
conversion of any from the error of their ways,” wrote 
Mr. Story, “I shall be thankful for it all;” but the 
spiritual blessing of so much ecclesiastical contention, 
he found it hard to trace. 

It may be worth while to look at the working of the 
‘reat religious movement’ in a country parish,—to 
take one ordinary example of what went on throughout 
Scotland. 

We get some insight into it from the following letter 
to the late Duke of Argyll :—“ Only a few months ago 
our once peaceful parish was, in the confession of all, 
one of the most orderly parishes in Scotland, with a 
higher standard of discipline than was to be found else- 
where. Now all bonds of good order are dissolved, and 


the drunkard reproved for his excess finds a satisfactory ‘ 


justification of his conduct in telling me that he has 
become a seceder. The widowed mother bewails that the 


304 LETTER TO THE DUKE OF ARGYLL. 


only restraint which controlled her self-willed boy is 
removed; the husband and wife, the parent and child 
are set in determined opposition to each other. Fear- 
ing that my speaking to them on the subject might 
lead them into the fatal error of supposing that the 
view they took of such a matter could at all affect their 
standing before God, I have ever abstained from men- 
tioning it to them, and thus I fear have unwisely given 
an undue advantage to those who, with secret but 
untiring diligence, were actively working on them. 
The most unscrupulous statements I find have been 
made, and one which has wrought more successfully 
on the best disposed than any other, is the declaration 
that, in the event of my death or removal, a French- 
man,—‘a wild Irisher, —‘a Roman, —or a drunkard, 
might be put in my place, and that no power was 
left them to object; and that unless they join the Free 
Church, and get a Church built now, they never can 
get one again; nor even if one were in the parish, could 
they be admitted members of it afterwards, unless going 
now. All this seems so utterly absurd, that one would 
be inclined to laugh at it; but I cannot number the 
instances in which I have found it the mainspring of 
action. ..... The bedside of the sick and dying is 
not spared from the intrusion of those who distract 
the weakened mind with their vehement discussions : 
Episcopalians, and even poor ignorant girls from the 
Highlands, are beset, time after time, to allow their 
names to be put down, that the list of adherents may 
be swelled. One young man told me that his overseer 
had ‘never kept off him, night or day, till he put his 
name down, adding, ‘they are, may be, no all so 
stupid as me, but I canna’ understand a word about 
it; but every man in the work is down as well as me.’ ” 


REASONS ASSIGNED FOR SECEDING. 305 


The main point with the promoters of the secession 
seemed to be here, as elsewhere, not, in the first 
instance, to make the poor people understand the 
question, but to make them quit the Church. After 
they had seceded came the explanation of the reason 
why.* ‘The parish was beset by reverend lecturers 
from Glasgow and elsewhere, who harangued their 
edified auditors upon the “ principles of the secession.”’ 
. Sometimes their orations were rendered more level to 
the rustic intellect by the interpolation of anecdote and 
gossip, as the clown of the circus comes tumbling into 
the ring, between the grander feats of horsemanship, for 
the delectation of the gallery. And, on one occasion, 
a reverend individual, who had charmed his audience 
by the number and piquancy of his nasty stories about 
the “ Moderates,” wound up by saying that after all 
this he couldn’t very well offer a prayer, so he would 
just pronounce the benediction, and let them go. 
Truly a very great “religious movement” this 
secession ! 

The reasons which the “ Evangelical” parishioners 
of Rosneath, thus enlightened, gave for joming the 
‘“‘ popular and godly” party were sometimes odd enough. 
“I’m no gaun to bide in a Kirk where I canna’ get 
preevileges,’ + said one extremely profane swearer. 
“ Div’ ye think Ill stay in a Kirk where I’m tell’t 
that if I dinna believe the Almichty’s my father I’m 
nae Christian?” was the protest of another. ‘ And 
what will you do, John?” was asked of the coachman 


* “T dinna blame the folk that gang, and that ken naething aboot 
it, puir bodies,” said an intelligent parishioner ; “but I blame them 
that are gaun’ roun’ pittin’ their names intil a book, as if they were 
listin’ a regiment for the Queen.”

of a gentleman, to whom the local secession owed not

Sometimes in the cottage debates on the all-en- 
grossing subject, the dry Scotch humour of the dis- 
putants lighted up the vexed question with a gleam of 
wit and common sense, that would have rather discom- 
fited some of the orators on Non-intrusion platforms. A 
favourite figure of speech with the schismatics was 
the representation that they were the “corn,” and 
their degraded opponents in the Establishment, the 
“chaff.” “ Aweel,” said one old woman on hearing 
the oft-repeated assertion, “ may be sae, but I’m feared 
ye maun be some o’ the licht corn o’ Egypt, for I ne’er 
heerd tell in my time o’ corn that flee’t awa, and cauf 
that bided ahint.” ‘“ Bonny on their conscience,” 
exclaimed another, on Mr. Story saying to her that 
he hoped those who went were obeying that inward 
monitor, “ If ye kent them as weel as I dae, ye wadna 
say muckle aboot their conscience. Ony way, con- 
science is an ill guide wi'’oot the Scriptur’; nae doot 
it was conscience that sent Saul gallopin’ awa’ to 
Damascus.” 

Sometime after the secession, one of the parishioners 
who had not been at Church for some time, accosted 
a lady whom he met: “I’m coming to the Kirk noo, 
Mrs. ; I’m that mad at and his calves, that 
I’m coming tothe Kirk noo.” * “ What have the calves

LOOSENESS OF “ FREE” KIRK DISCIPLINE. 307

and he says in it, that we’re worshipping golden calves

greetin’ at ae end o’ the hoose, and M. greetin’ at

‘Will ye gar M. gang to the Kirk?’ ‘No,’ says I, 
‘Tl no gar her. Wad ye like me to gar ye gang wi’ 
her to the new Kirk ?’ ‘Iwad na gang for yer garrin, 
says the wife. ‘ Weel then,’ says I, ‘ T’ll no gar her gang 
wi’ you to the auld Kirk; she'll gang to her Kirk, and 
you'll gang to your Kirk; and I'll gang to no Kirk till 
sich time as ye keep peace in the hoose.’ But I’m that 
mad at and his calves that I’m coming noo.” 

Such are a few homely illustrations of the aspect 
of the schism, and the nature of its working, taken 
from the incidents that marked it in a country parish 
which, more than most others, had been the home of 
order, peace, and unity, before the disastrous days 
when the dogmas of “ Non-intrusion” and ‘ Spiritual 
Independence ” shed their malign influence, “ as killing 
as the canker to the rose” over the uninformed minds 
of the people. 

One of the features of the movement most grievous 
to Mr. Story’s spirit, was the utter relaxation of 
all discipline and ecclesiastical authority among the 
separatists. All the persons excommunicated in the 
Church joined the secession—all were admitted to 
the Communion of the “Free.” No wonder he 


should have said, referring to one specially flagrant 
x 2 


808 VISIT TO. DR. LANDSBOROUGH. 


example of this demoralizing laxity: “It is impos- 
sible to hear appeals to the people from all 
quarters to come out of a corrupted, and to join a 
pure Church, and not to feel strongly the utter amper- 
tinence of such pretensions and arguments.” Equally 
odious and still more saddening to his loving nature 
was the rabid sectarianism which poisoned the very 
springs of Christian life. It is with a tone of rest and 
relief that he writes from Stevenston, whither he had 
escaped from his parish, for a day or two, to pay a 
quiet visit to his old friend Landsborough: ‘I found 
dear Landsborough true and kind as ever, and my 
heart seems to yearn towards him with increased affec- 
tion because of these troubles. Thoroughly persuaded 
in his own mind—thoroughly honest, he can put con- 
fidence in the honesty of his friend, although he sees 
not eye to eye with him; and so our intercourse is 
unfettered as ever. He had a prayer meeting after I 
arrived, and I had great delight in uniting my suppli- 
cation for God’s blessing with his. Coming out he 
was arrested by a group of ladies; I walked slowly 
on, and he joined me laughing heartily. One of them 
had said, ‘If this were not a Free Church, I would 
say that was Mr. Story of Rosneath.’ Poor woman, 
she could not credit her own senses. Alas, alas! God 
says if two or three shall agree about any one thing 
and ask it of Him, He will hear and answer them: 
the Devil says, ‘Though you agree about fifty things, 
if you differ about this one you must not pray together,’ 
and men believe him. What a monstrous delusion.” 
Mr. Story had always felt that he was capable of 
undertaking the charge of a more populous parish than 
Rosneath ; and now that a considerable portion of his 
flock had withdrawn from his ministry, it became with 


THOUGHTS OF LEAVING ROSNEATH. 309 


him a serious question whether or not he ought to 
remain where he was, when so many large parishes 
were left without a pastor. Moreover the old cordiality 
of his relations with the people of Rosneath was neces- 
sarily so much impaired, that he felt little disinclina- 
tion for a change. The first parish offered to him was 
Stony-kirk in Wigtonshire. Sir James Graham wrote 
to him that “in consequence of the high character 
which he had received of his piety and usefulness in 
his sacred calling, he was disposed to place him in 
charge of the parish of Stony-kirk.” This was 
followed up by a memorial from 102 heads of families 
and members of the Church in the parish, begging 
him to become their minister. On visiting the place, 
however, he found that the charge would be too heavy, 
and the parish too large for him: he therefore declined 
the presentation.* The parish of Monifieth in Forfar- 
shire was also proposed to him. It was near Mr. 
Erskine’s home, Linlathen, and had many attrac- 
tions. He went to inspect it; and among other matters 
he mentions, in a letter giving his impressions of the 
place: “I should be neighbour to an old class-fellow, 
who sat on the same form as I in the Greek class, but 
who is the very extract of miserable old moderatism, 
—I should not be surprised if he got up the old howl 
of Rowism.” After these and some other projects of 
change of sphere, he seems to have made up his mind 
to stay in Rosneath. 

We have but little of his correspondence at this 
time,t and so cannot trace his feelings as he gradually

ADDRESS TO THE PEOPLE, 811 


desire and prayer for every one of them is, that they 
may be saved; and God is my record, how earnestly I 
longed after them in the Gospel, how willingly I would 
spend and be spent in their service, but they desired

to be set over them, I feel it is not consistent with the

his ministry, though I shall willingly co-operate with

that can be used to separate you from it has been used 
within the last six months, and that in your peculiar 
circumstances, temporal considerations* were fitted to 
give these arguments additional weight; and I there- 
fore conclude, that those who have had strength so long 
to resist, are not likely to yield now. 

“I am confident, that had you seen reason to judge 
as others do, that the Church is apostate and forsaken of 
God, you could never again have partaken of sealing

ordinances within her pale; and I doubt not that you

by prayerful and zealous discharge of our various

may desire the sincere milk of the Word and grow 
thereby.’ I thank God the lessening of your num- 
bers has in no wise diminished my willingness to 
labour for your good, and if but one soul among you 
could be saved, I would not consider the labour of a 
lifetime too great to be expended for that end. Now 
that you are fewer in number, I shall be able to see 
you in private more frequently, and though much of 
my strength has been spent among those who have 
gone away, I am thankful to God that at present he 
vouchsafes me such a measure of bodily health, as 
justifies me in assuring you that at any hour of the 
day or watch of the night, as in former years, I shall 
be ready to minister to you in my Master’s name, amid 
your anxieties and bereavements, your calamities and 
SOITOWS.” 


“ FREE” KIRK INTOLERANCE. 313 


It was the man that could speak thus of those 
who went and to those who stayed, yearning over 
both with an apostolic love and desire for their souls’ 
salvation, who was sneered at as a ‘“ Residuary,” 
an “ Erastian,” a “dumb dog who dared not bark ;” 
who, according to the oracular “ Witness,’ having 
been once a “ gibbering disciple” of the Row doc- 
trines, and now sunk even to a deeper depth, was to be 
‘“‘the one excommunicated man of the district,” and to 
be “put under the ban of the community.” These 
were the tones of the voice that was not to be listened 
to any more—with whose words in prayer no one was 
to join*—though never a Sunday passed that it did not 
with a half-faltering utterance implore God’s blessing 
for those ‘‘who once were with us, but who now have 
gone forth from us.” This faithful and unwearying 
ministry of which he speaks, and whose minutest 
services, even down to the weekly visitations and 
meetings for prayer, not all the distractions of the 
secession were suffered to interrupt, was the ministry 
which, according to Dr. Cunningham, “it was per- 
fectly ridiculous to suppose could do anything valu- 
able towards promoting the salvation of souls.” A 
stranger full of “‘ Free”’ Kirk intolerance and zeal, was 
to be regarded now as the only true Minister of Christ 
in the parish, and Mr. Story and his services of five 
and twenty years were to be ignored, and treated as a 
farce or a nullity; though it was his ministry that had 
made the parish and the people what they were, and 
though still from many a death-bed, when the excite- 


* This injunction was faithfully carried out in Rosneath. The

314 THE SCANDALOUS SPIRIT SUBSIDES. 


ment of Church politics was forgotten in the presence 
of the eternal realities, eager voices with their failing 
breath called on the old minister to return and speak 
again to those who had forsaken him in their days of 
health and strength. 

But it is irksome and painful to dwell on these remi- 
niscences of a time full of all uncharitableness; it is 
pleasanter to remember that gradually the scandalous 
spirit died down, and though the old unity, peace, and 
concord never were restored, the violence of opposi- 
tion and the acerbity of estrangement passed away. 

And he who had suffered most from the intrusion of 
schism and sectarianism into the fold he so long had 
watched with a shepherd’s care, was able to bow with- 
out repining to his Master’s will, and to go on in his 
diminished round of labour as heartily, as faithfully, with 
as unrelaxing an authority and diligence as in days gone 
by—cheered by seeing almost everywhere throughout 
Scotland the Church gradually recovering from the 
effects of the blight that fell on her in 1843, and the 
seceders left without even a plausible pretext for 
representing themselves as other than they really were, 


-a sect, strong and aggressive, but a sect only. 


But the strongest and amplest days of his life were 
near their close. The still and silent shadows were 
beginning to lengthen from the West. He was not 
again to move prominently before the public eye 
on the arena of the Church. His parish had ever 
been his dearest concern; he was in future to be 
but little occupied, save with it and its interests, 
and with the interests of that wide circle of friends, 
whose hearts from every quarter of the globe turned 
to him with an unforgetting reverence and affection. 
It has happened to other men, less richly gifted, to 


LATER YEARS OF MR. STORY’S MINISTRY. 315 


take a much more notable place in the eyes of the Church 
and of the world, but to none to stand higher in the 
hearts of a larger number of good Christians, and to win 
a deeper love and veneration from all sorts and condi- 
tions of men. Life’s autumnal years as they passed 
over his reverend head, seemed only to shed upon it a 
more Apostolic glory, as if that crown laid up for him 
amid the fadeless splendours were already descending 
on his brow. Age left no mark of care or sorrow, 
dimmed not the lustre of the dark eye, quenched not 
the quenchless energy of heart and mind, the vivid 
sympathy with all that was noble, beautiful and true in 
human life,—but only cast the charm of an ever-grow- 
ing kindliness and geniality and fatherly benignity 
around voice, and look, and manner. So he fared along 
“with God’s benediction upon him,” still as of old, ever 
to all his people the pastor and the friend, still the 
faithful teacher and monitor, the vigilant “‘Episkopos ” 
set over them to uphold God’s law, and rule His 
Church. 

As time rolled on, his beautiful peninsula gradually 
lost the likeness of what it once had been; it was 
brought nearer to the great noisy town-worlds of the 
West ; yet still, as changes passed upon it, as the sum- 
mers came and went bringing always some new human 
influx to these placid shores, he moved through all, 
the “man of God”—the patriarchal father and 
bishop of the place; and not a few of those whose 
fancy led them to pitch their summer tents by the 
Gairloch, found ere they left it, that some deeper influ- 
ence than that of those lovely outlines of lake and 
hill had reached their hearts and swayed them, ~ 
and knew that the well-spring of what was truest and 
purest in their nature, owed the unsealing of its 


316 CORRESPONDENCE WITH DR. CHALMERS. 


source to him,—to that good life, to that earnest 
ministry. 

We here close that section of his history in which 
the most marked object is the calamity of 1843; but 
before finally quitting this period, it may be interest- 
ing to the reader to peruse the subjoined correspon- 
dence between Mr. Story and Dr. Chalmers, which 
took place in 1846. It explains itself and requires no 
comment. 


MB. STORY TO DR. CHALMERS. 


“My dear Dr. Chalmers,—I did not hear of your 
being in this parish until you had left it. Your 
intended visit, indeed, had been spoken of for some 
time before, to which not only the associations of 
former times, but recent events, seemed to give no 
small importance in the eyes of not a few of the 
people. As last year you had expressed your regret 
at not having time to call, I doubted not if you came 
I should see you once more where you had often 
conferred on me happiness, which I delighted to 
think of. 

“It was, therefore, with peculiar pain, I frankly 
confess to you, I heard from the son of our dear old 
friend Irving that he had met you at the quay when 
waiting for the steamer. I would have rejoiced to 
recelve you under my roof for howsoever brief a time, 
nor’do I think you would have denied me that pleasure 
had you not had some urgent reason for withholding 
it, since so (apparently at least) inconsistent with your 
avowed principles. 

“1 had just been reading your letter on Christian 
Union, and had heard that at Old Kilpatrick you had 
expressly declared that you had never cut an old 


MR. STORY TO DR. CHALMERS. 317 


friend on account of Free Churchism, but that you 
had yourself been subjected to that incivility. I could 
therefore attribute your passing and repassing the Manse 
only to some reason personal to myself; nor could I 
avoid concluding that I must, in some way unknown 
to me, have given you offence sufficient to justify a 
departure from your rules of intercourse with those 
who differ from you in matters of ecclesiastical policy. 
It is true I am little worthy of your friendship; but 
you once permitted me to enjoy it ; and if the admira- 
tion and love I have never ceased to bear you could 
have warranted or secured its continuance, I should 
not have had occasion thus to address you, or to regret 
that its ordinary expressions should have been with- 
held. Ido indeed regret this for reasons other than 
personal; and allow me to add a few words explanatory 
of my meaning. 

“You must often, with all good men, have deplored 
how the schism of the time has harassed and torn 
asunder the most quiet localities, accompanied with 
such asperity and bitterness of feeling as could alone 
be traceable to him whose works our blessed Master 
came to destroy. Now, the evils which some of the 
younger brethren have thus occasioned can only be 
remedied, under God, by those whose names have mofal 
weight with the community. Your letter seemed to 
me pregnant with the very anodynical virtue required 
by the times, and I read it with the profoundest satis- 
faction. But, alas! I cannot but express to you how 
thoroughly it has been neutralized by the’ example 
you have set to the people around me. How entirely 
different from those in your letter are the ideas and 
obligations of union which it suggests. I myself cer- 
tainly cannot reconcile them ; and, as example is more 


318 MR. STORY TO DR. CHALMERS. 


effectual than precept, I anticipate sorrowfully the 
power which, despite your precious views, the nature 
of your visit here will exercise over corrupt minds. 
Stumbling-blocks it will not fail to erect in the way 
of even the well-disposed, who are weary of carnal 
contentions, and yearning after the blessedness there 
is in the interchange of the offices of civility and 
love. It will, besides, have a ruinous influence upon 
those who already attach undue importance to a mere 
outward position, to their being seen of men as anta- 
gonists to eyil which they abhor and repudiate. 

“IT cannot help, with all respect and deference, 
saying, that, in this locality especially, you ought to 
have weighed well how you should be seen of the 
people. The character of the schism here also you 
would have done well to have investigated, for we 
stand in a peculiar relation to each other, involving 
solemn responsibilities. You introduced me to the 
pastoral charge of those who have abandoned my 
ministry, and before supplementing all other reasons 
for their doing so by such a sanction as your example 
affords, you ought to have had some knowledge of 
those reasons. I have no hesitation myself in stating, 
that whatever may be their condition now, of which I 
sfy nothing, the religious element was scarcely dis- 
cernible in the great majority of the dissentients. 
The very fidelity with which my Great Master enabled 
me to discharge the duties of the ministry to which 
you introduced and recommended me, stimulated their 
secession. Many of a name to live, and of a specious 
Christianity, could not endure my true sayings. The 
excommunicated, without an exception, fled from dis- 
cipline; the young catechumens, from whom the 
Communion was withheld, because of their inade- 


MR. STORY TO DR. CHALMERS. 319 


quate knowledge, accompanied them, and increased 
the number of those who were in search of freedom. 
Often I have said, that though all should go away, 
I would not speak ‘smooth things,’ nor ‘ prophesy 
deceits,’ nor cease to tell them that nothing was 
Christianity but the mind of Christ, and that such a 
mind could be in none but those who continually 
carried about His dying. 

“Now, my most dear and venerable father, can you 
wonder, having had so long the charge of the souls 
of the people around me, and not ceasing still to feel 
deeply interested in the spiritual condition of those 
who have fled from me, that, apart from all personal 
reasons, I should have been constrained to regret that 
we had not seen each other, to interchange the ordinary 
courtesies of life, instead of recoiling from, or repudi- 
ating all future intercourse ? 

“From the publicity given to your movements by the 
press and other means, your having passed the Manse 
was widely known; and such individuals as I have 
alluded to would be only too ready to conclude that all 
was well with them, and that by separating from the 
Church of their fathers, they had done what would 
cover a multitude of sins, that at all events they had 
followed that which in the esteem of one so great and 
worthy seemed the most important of all movements. 
In the mere outward thovement they would be led 
only the more confidently to rest satisfied, while by any 
deportment of yours, which should seem to justify such 
satisfaction, I felt you had done much to weaken my 
hands in the exercise of an uncompromising ministry, 
for the success of which, I still remember that nearly 
thirty years ago you so vehemently and lovingly 
prayed. May I also add, that it has considerably 


320 MR. STORY TO DR. CHALMERS. 


damped the joy I had in hoping that the interest 
excited by the plans of Chnstian Union* would put to 
shame those uncharitable and wrathful feelings and 
expressions with which our Great Head could have no 
sympathy; for there are many of both parties, who I 
fear will more readily vindicate their exclusiveness by 
your example, than fan into life their charities by 
your catholic counsels . . . I would not have written, 
had I not the prospect of being in town in a week 
hence, and I should not like to be there without seeing 
you; while, having these feelings, I could not, unless I 
had expressed them, have thought of even the possi- 
bility of our meeting without pain. I have only to 
add, that with undiminished regard and affection, and 
with most earnest prayers for God’s blessing on your- 
self and those dear to you, 
“J remain, my dear Doctor, 
“Your old and obliged friend, 
‘* RoBERT Story.” 


DR. CHALMERS TO MR. STORY. 
Edinburgh, 14th October, 1846. 

“My dear Sir,—Both the proximate and real cause 
of my not calling upon you was, that I had not time. 
It was altogether an unforeseen visit that I made to 
your peninsula, and of which I knew nothing till the 
few minutes before I set sail for it. 

“But even though I had known of it for weeks 
before, I would not have felt that any calculation was 
necessary, and certainly would not have made any 
calculation on the principles set forth in your letter. 
I should not have been restrained from visiting 


* At this time the “Evangelical Alliance” was in vogue. 


DR, CHALMERS TO MR. STORY. 321 


Rosneath, nor from taking my own view of it from the 
consideration that it would make the people who left 
your minjstry feel themselves all the more right in 
having done so. What I felt to be right for myself, I 
surely cannot feel to have been wrong for them. You 
cannot surely suppose that I should look on the prin- 
ciple for which I and the other ministers of the Free 
Church relinquished our endowments, to be any other 
than a principle of the gravest importance, or that 
what we felt to be a good and urgent principle for 
ministers should not be a good and right principle 
for laymen also. There would be infinitely more of 
civility on the part of the Free Church ministers to 
those of the Establishment, were it not for the con- 
struction that so many of them are anxious should 
be put upon it, which is plainly that the difference be- 
tween us is of no consequence,—a conclusion which, if 
good, would stultify ourselves, and place us in the false 
and foolish position of having left the Establishment 
for a mere bagatelle. 

“‘ My view is clearly as follows,—that we should not 
drop the courtesies of life to the ministers of the 


Establishment any more than we should to Roman. 


Catholic ministers, if we happen to fall in with them. 
But on the other hand, as it is our duty to protest 
against the errors of Popery, and refrain from giving 
any countenance to the delusion that this is a 
trifling matter, so it 1s our duty to testify against the 
errors of Erastianism, and withhold all countenance 
from the delusion that this too is a corruption of no 
consequence. I confess that with myself, they are in 
like estimation as grievous departures from the purity 
and authority of the truth as it is in Jesus. By the 


one a power has arisen on-ecclesiastical ground claim- 
Y 


822 DR. CHALMERS TO MR. STORY. 


ing a co-ordinate, or rather a paramount authority to 
that of the Bible. By the other a power alike anti- 
scriptural has arisen upon the civil ground. And I 
confess to the same zeal and determination against 
both. Had the Evangelical Alliance framed such com- 
mittees as I recommended, these are the sentiments I 
should have given forth in them, but not without 
pointing to the only way, though at present a very 
hopeless one, by which an adjustment of our differences 
is at all possible. 

“If after these explanations, you will have the 
goodness to call upon me, be assured not only of a 
courteous, but also of a cordial welcome, and a welcome, 
too, pronounced as of old in the words of Quarle, 
‘Sweet Phosphor, rule the day.’ * 

“J am, my dear Sir, 
‘Yours with great regard, 
“ THomas CHALMERS.” 


MR. STORY TO DR. CHALMERS. 


“My dear Dr. Chalmers,—It has been a great relief 
to me to learn that want of time was the real reason 
of your not having called at the Manse. Had my 
people seceded for the reasons you did, I should never 
have expected you to do anything to lead them to 
suppose they were wrong in having done so, but I

MR. STORY TO DR. CHALMERS. 323 


think this could scarcely have been effected by the 
interchange of any civilities between us. Liver since 
the adoption of the Veto principle, our difference upon 
the mere point of ecclesiastical order had been so 
manifest, that no one who cared at all to attend to the 
matter could be in doubt as to the true character of 
such civilities, which during the progress of the struggle 
had not been interrupted, and which the mere issue of 
that struggle could afford no good reason for abandon- 
ing. Had I found it possible to see ‘eye to eye’ with 
you in the point of Church policy, of course I should 
not now have been in the Manse of Rosneath ; because 
it was otherwise, my position is in no respect different 
from what it was when I had such enjoyment in seeing 
you here, and I do not, feel that I have been guilty of 
anything that should separate any man from me, because 
I have reverenced my own judgment and conscience 
more than his. 

But the secession of my people was not such as 
yours—of the question which divided the Church they 
were as a body greatly ignorant. I knew to my griet 
that too many of them were ignorant of a far more 
important question ; and I felt I could not dare to say 
a word that would have led any of them to believe they 
could glorify God by any decision in such matters, 
until they had solved the momentous point of their 
relationship to Him—what God was to them and they 
to God. I therefore never thought of instructing them 
in the Church question, lest I should lull their con- 
sciences asleep ; and I am very certain that at the period 
of the secession not half a dozen among them were in 
circumstances to give an intelligent answer on the sub- 
ject. One of the best informed gave me as a reason, 
that ‘ We were told in Scripture how that kings and

324 MR. STORY TO DR. CHALMERS. 


queens were to be nursing fathers and mothers to 
the Kirk, and he thought the Queen had not been 
very kind to them.’ And certainly the cleverest 
and best-informed woman among them all said, in 
reference to the transaction which pledged many to 
secede and hurried away the rest, ‘ It was the blackest 
day that ever happened in Rosneath, for the men 
left home not knowing what it was they were sent 
for, and when they came back they had all their names 
down, and as for us we just sat at home for days and 
grat.’ ‘The movement was in truth notoriously occa- 
sioned by other considerations than arose from know- 
ledge of the merits of the question. To myself none, 
so far as I remember, but the individuals referred to, 
ever gave any reason for seceding but my own strict- 
ness of doctrine and discipline; and from strangers 
then resident in the parish, I learned that the general 
answer to the question ‘ Why have you seceded ?’ was, 
‘the minister was ower strict and the factor was unco 
keen ot.’ 

“So flagrant was their ignorance, that long after 
the secession had been consummated, lectures were 
delivered to show them why they had gone out. I still 
feel therefore, from my knowledge of these and other 
circumstances connected with the history of the seces- 
sion in my parish, that it would have been well in you 
to have avoided what might tend to soothe faithless or 
unenlightened consciences among the people, or lead 
any to suppose that all was well with them merely 
because their outward position was changed, and they 
had ceased to attend the ministry of the pastor you 
had introduced and recommended to them. 

I do assure you as you so kindly permit me, it will 
give me the greatest pleasure to wait upon you, not

DR. CHALMERS TO MR. STORY. 
‘Morningside, Edinburgh, 19th October, 1846. 


“My dear Sir,—I shall be happy to see you at your 
convenience. But to anticipate and save the necessity 
of any argument when we meet, let me just state, in 
reply to your letter received this morning, that whereas 
you seem still to lay stress on my having introduced 
and recommended you to the parish of Rosneath, I 
was not then aware of what you must permit me to 
call your Erastianism, else I should not have done it. ~ 
So much for the vindication of my own consistency. 

“Qndly. In regard to your people, I cannot but still “ 
think it better that they should attend a non-Erastian 
rather than Erastian ministry; and what I thus say 
frankly to you, I will say frankly to all, I will not be 
restrained from saying to you next summer when I 
expect to spend a month in your parish. 

“8rdly. In most cordial agreement with your own 


326 MR. STORY TO DR. CHALMERS. 


views, I will as frankly and fearlessly state that it 1s 
not their Free Churchism which will save them, and 
will do my uttermost to dissipate the illusion of which 
you seem to stand in dread, and of which, I think with 
yourself, that there is great danger. And now, my 
dear sir, if with these principles laid down as clearly 
and plainly as I can, you can still keep up the cordiality 
of our past acquaintance, which I can assure you I 
can do, after that we have come to an explicit under- 
standing of each other, though not with each other, it 
will afford me great pleasure to meet you both here, 
and at Rosneath, where, if God be pleased to spare me, 
I expect to be in the course of next summer. 
“T am, my dear Sir, 
‘Yours very truly, 
“'THomas CHALMERS.” 


MR. STORY TO DR. COHALMERS, 


“My dear Dr. Chalmers,—I cordially concur with 
you that it is far better we should so understand each 
other as to render controversy when we meet (which I 
hope will be soon) quite unnecessary. To avoid this 
in the same candid spirit which yours evinces, I may 
just say in regard to the points it refers to, that what- 
ever obligations may be held to be involved in having 
recommended another to the ministry, they certainly 
must be fully cancelled by his teaching any error; but 
in regard to my alleged Erastianism, I cannot feel that 
IT have done anything that would, if known at the time, 
have prevented you from so honouring me. For in 
the sense of my being Erastian now, we were surely 
both Erastian then; we had both entered the Church 
of Scotland when under the bondage of patronage, 


MR. STORY TO DR. CHALMERS. 327 


nor did either of us dream that the Church could do 
whatever she chose irrespective of the law of her 
Establishment. 

“It cannot be doubted that patronage includes what- 
ever elements of Erastianism were or are developed 
in her policy, and never did the Church practically 
enjoy such freedom as now, or have under sanction of 
law such power of judging of the rights and privileges 
of the people. I rejoice to hear that you are, God 
willing, to be for so long a time my parishioner, and 
that you do cordially agree with me that the Chris- 
tianity of the people is to be tested by something 
deeper and more important than what is called ‘ Free 
Churchism,’ and that you will have no hesitation in 
showing them that by a mere change of outward posi- 
tion they have not gained all they need. In regard to 
the preference of one ministry to another, I believe 
that whatever may be incumbent upon the head of a 
party as a teacher knowing truth and standing in the 
Divine counsel, you are bound to look deeper than 
outward distinctions; and I believe, had the voice of 
Eternal Wisdom been heard amid the partial preferences 
of Corinth, it would not have uttered ‘ go to Paul,’ or 
‘go to Cephas,’ but ‘ receive the Gospel from whatever 
lips, and woe be to him who does not feel it to be the 
wisdom and power of God unto his salvation.’ With 
the profoundest respect, and in hope of having soon 
the pleasure of waiting on you, 

“‘T remain, my dear Dr. Chalmers, 
“Your most affectionate and obliged, 
“ RoBERT STory.’’* 


* Shortly after this they met at Morningside, Dr. Chalmers bursting

PART IV. 


1849— 1859. 


PART IV. 


1849—1859. 


CHAPTER I. 
AUTUMNAL—LAST YEARS. 


Tue last decade of the life whose interests and 
experiences we have traced so far, was one over which 
rested a calm autumnal light,—light not as of summer 
in its golden strength and fulness, but of the mellow 
tranquil harvest-time, with its pensive stillness and 
gentle wane. 

In the beginning of the winter of 1849-50, no one 
would have deemed that he of whom I have been 
speaking, carried about with him at the very centre 
and citadel of life the seeds of a fatal malady. His 
eye was as bright as ever, his voice as strong and 
cheery, his step as elastic, his activity as manifold. 
But the inevitable hour was close at hand, which was to 
bid him pause in his varied labours, and to tread more 
slowly, and speak with bated breath thenceforward. 

The attack was sudden. “I was hurrying to the 
boat, rather late as usual,” he writes to Mr. Stewart 
Ker, “‘and began to breathe with difficulty. This 
increased even when I slackened my pace; I became 
insensible and fell to the ground. I saw my hat in 
one direction and my cane in another, that was my 
first perception, the last had been a hurried glance of 


oT 


332 FIRST ATTACK OP ILLNESS. 


the Arrochar Hills. JI rose and thought I was pretty 
well, and proceeded to Greenock according to my first 
intention, lest I should alarm Helen by returning from 
such a cause. I went to Aunt Mary’s to fulfil my pur- 
pose of calling for Canadian Fairrie, her brother-in-law, 
and there became sick again, and sent for the doctor. 
He considered the catastrophe had arisen from over 
action of the heart. I arrived home pretty well, but 
my face a little nearer the colour of my hair than 
usual. I have preached since and done all my ordi- 
nary duty, but Dr. Scott* considers, with his brother 
leech of Greenock, that I must not walk fast, and by 
no means climb a hill, so that those splendid views 
behind the Manse, which when crossing to see the sick 
and dying on the other side of the hill, often from amid 
the schismatic miasma of these unhappy times, used to 
seem a refreshment and solace from the Eternal Foun- 
tain of all love and beauty and harmony, must be for 
a season foregone by your old friend, once ever ready 
to start with you for Kamtschatka or the Pyramids even 
at a moment’s notice.” 

He shortly after went into Edinburgh to consult 
Drs. Simpson and Halliday Douglas, whose opinion 
left him in no doubt as to the fact of his malady being 
a species of heart disease, rendering all loud speaking 
or violent exercise highly dangerous. Otherwise his 
health was unimpaired; but the contraction and repres- 
sion of his powers of speech and action, necessitated 
by the nature of his disorder, was a sad blow to one so 
enthusiastic in his work. 

“ The doctors,” he says, with a certain pathos in his 
tone, “do not prohibit preaching in my own pulpit, 
but they are peremptory regarding my going from 


“ Dr. Geddes Scott, then of Glasgow. 


FIRST ATTACK OF ILLNESS. 333 


home to do so, especially in a large Church. If they 
are correct, and should no change of symptoms induce 
them to give a larger latitude of professional exertion, 
I must never preach to Paisley’s* or to Wylie’s people 
more. .... H. is wonderfully well; yet I am very 
often reminded of a simile which, more than forty 
years ago, I admired as peculiarly beautiful, in a then 
favourite author, when describing the song of a star- 
ling in a cage. ‘It is the smile of grief, which is 
more dreadful than tears, or like the playfulness of a 
child mid scenes of sorrow.’ She is so cheerful, and 
watches me with the same happy countenance with 
which she ever looked to , although she knew he 
was on his way to another home. You know he died of 
enlargement of the heart; and I have no doubt often a 
thought is with her, notwithstanding all she seems— 
one of those thoughts which lie too deep for tears.” 
Though thus arrested and stricken in the yet full 
vigour of his age, and unclouded brightness of his 
faculties, his spirit, unchecked, unchilled, looked out 
on life with as serene a faith and as warm a love as 
ever. The strong vitality, debarred from its wonted 
exercise, only animated into a higher fervour his wide 
love towards God and man,—his keen interest in all 
that concerned the welfare of the world; and as he sat 
apart in an involuntary stillness and seclusion, his 
thoughts and affections went forth only the more unre- 
servedly to all within the ample circle they embraced. 
The extensive correspondence which he maintained 
with many friends, and touching on many subjects, 
cannot now be recovered, so as to yield us the extracts 
from its records of his thoughts and feelings which we 


* The Rev. Robert Paisley, minister of S. Ninian’s, 


® 


334 LATER CORRESPOS DENCE. 


should wish to have. Only a small and fragmentary 
portion of it is at my disposal, chiefly consisting of 
letters to myself and to his brother-in-law Mr. Stewart 
Ker. 

To me his letters were perhaps too exclusively occu- 
pied with counsel about the subjects and progress of 
my studies at College, whither I had gone in November, 
1849. This was owing, no doubt, to the keen interest he 
took in the development of the only son who had grown 
up to College years under his eye, and all whose studies 
had been under his own immediate supervision. It 
was, however, an evil—one of the evils which almost 
necessarily result from a purely home-training—in 
which it must needs happen that in the mind of one 
so trained, the image of the parent loses somewhat of 
its beauty through being confused and blended with 
that of the preceptor. I remember well, when on the 
eve of my departure for Edinburgh, he was piling beside 
my boxes volume after volume, which he believed would 
be of use in my classical studies, it was mildly sug- 
gested, that if I put all these books in the trunks, there 
would be no room left for clothes. ‘‘ Clothes,” re- 
peated he, in high disdain, ‘let him take his clothes 
in a clothes-basket.” And the slight indifference to 
interest and considerations, to the youthful mind not 
altogether despicable, which was betrayed in this reply, 
breathed more or less through a correspondence which, 
when I review it now, seems, though marked by this 
trivial defect, a model of all wise and pious counsel, 
and a magazine of sound criticism and useful direction 
in study. 

In his letters to Mr. Ker, he speaks with the freedom 
and unreserve of long and intimate friendship on what- 
ever matters, publicor private, are interesting him at the 


PAPAL AGGRESSION. 835 


time. He thus writes at the time of the “ Papal 
aggression,” which startled the Protestantism of Eng- 
land into spasmodic self-assertion in 1850. ‘“‘ What a 
scene of turmoil you seem to have had near you lately 
at Birkenhead—all roused by a feeble old man in the 
Vatican. Certainly if the ‘ Church,’ according to his 
notions, be a remedy for all evils, your fine old 
Spaniard* will have been deeply interested in the 
restoration of England to her place in the heavens. 
What a deeply affecting speech that seemed to me. 
What a fine development of still more important 
views there would have been, had not his mind been 
oppressed by the heavy atmosphere of Iberian super- 
stition. Can you tell me anything more of his his- 
tory? I wish I could get more into the fountain of 
his thoughts. . . . The High Church clergy, I observe, 
did not take any part in your neighbourhood in the 
movement against the ‘aggression.’ The ground occu- 
pied elsewhere by that section of the Anglicans seemed 
to me a very quicksand. The demurring to Papal 
claims, merely because they themselves are of Apos- 
tolical descent, will be of little avail in the struggle: 
to the right of primogeniture they have no claim; and, 
in all minds, when that dogma (the Apostolical Suc- 
cession) has taken root, is the power, if not the inclina- 
tion, taken away to resist the claims of the lineal 
descendant of the most ancient Bishop of Christendom. 
It is a very singular predicament we are in, but such 
as we have richly merited. The Protestantism of many 
heads knocking against each other ought at least to 
learn the power there is in unity. What in the firma- 


* M. Donoso Cortes Marquis de Valdegamas. The speech alluded 
to in the next sentence, is one delivered by him in the Spanish 
Chamber of Deputies, on 80th January, 1850, ° 


336 PAROCHIAL INCIDENTS. 


ment of Prophecy do you consider this movement to 
be, or do you think that it is not of such magnitude 
as to be visible there ? 

“The effect upon the mind of the community is 
certainly a singular sign of the times—more so than 
the Papal act itself. So long, however, as the idea of 
the priesthood so occupies the minds of the Anglican 
Churchmen, and the ministrations both of the Esta- 
blishment and of the Dissenters of all colours are so 
indistinct regarding the foundation of our confidence 
towards God, the Papal power will not lose its hold, 
but rather grasp more firmly what it has of all classes 
of the people. But we shall see. It is possible there 
may be an apparent modification of the claims of Pio 
Nono, but there will be no real retractation, and our 
rulers being themselves without any fixed principle 
will be outwitted. They are without the light which 
is inseparable from uprightness of heart. Lord J.’s 
letter* was a mere ad captandum for the nonce. 
He will not embody it in any legislative measure if he 
can avoid it.” 

Here is a leaf from his pastoral experience. 
“Nothing has occurred more affecting in the history 
of our little parish, I think, since the schism, than 
this, that of the small company of Christians, in 
it, three have departed to their blessed rest since I 
wrote to you. A boy, about twelve years of age, the 
most promising in the whole parish, who, night after 
night, gladdened his mother's ears with the singing of 
hymns and spiritual songs, in about two days’ illness 
of a mysterious kind was translated to his blessed rest. 

“ A youth of about twenty-one, the most handsome of 


* Lord John Russell’s letter to the Bishop of Durham. 


INCIDENTS. 337 


all his- cotemporaries, was carried away by pulmonary 
disease. Aremarkable progress of the Divine life was 
developed in him, and you may easily conceive what 
joy filled me, when, in answer to my question what he 
wished me to thank God for, he began, after some 
moments of deep thought, ‘O thou my soul, bless 
God the Lord,’ and repeated the first sixteen lines of 
that psalm (103rd) as containing the knowledge I 
desired, and the reasons of his sacrifice of thanksgiv- 
ing. The last was the mother of ‘Mary Mackay,’ after 
a lingering malady. Her last words were, ‘My ain 
country, I am going to my home.’ She had long been 
waiting for the Salvation of God. But Stewart, you 
cannot understand what a sore bereavement, in so 
short a time, it is to lose from so small a flock three 
of the goodliest, that so lived in the pastor’s eye; but 
adieu, my heart is full.” And again, “I have just 
completed my parochial visitation, and rest for some 
time is needed and enjoined.* We have had some plea- 
sant visits within the last fortnight. In popped one 
day estimable David, our loving Evangelist.t Scarcely 
had we begun to enjoy him when lo! a form moving 
up from between the two lime trees, with slow and 
reverend paces, a meek and venerable man, yet more 
oppressed with infirmity and sorrow than exhausted 
with the burden of old age—the once renowned from 
afar, the Mackay of Rio Janeiro, the Achates of the 
man of many histories. It was pleasant to see two 
such Gali, their countenances beaming benignity : 
there is a music in the play of expression, and a ‘grave

sweet melody’ was the tune these brethren sung, even

Araby the blest,’ perfamed the Manse, as a beautiful

circle of one days sun entered our habitation? A 
devoted youth, originally a gardener like Paxton, but 
seized by a divine enthusiasm, he longs and pants for 
spiritual achievement amid the infamous dens of 
human vice and misery, and came to entreat that I 
would write to Lord Ashley to procure employment for 
him in London. How rich, you will say, have been 
the effluences of the divinely social spirit within the 
last fortnight, when I add that the voice of Mochrum t 
has been heard for a couple of hours within the walls 
of the old Church, and early and late for a couple of 
days beside my hearth. R. Paisley, too, a few days 
ago, was here, and gave full proof of his continuing to 
sound the depths of the ocean of Truth, and with greater 
intenseness of wonder, still constrained to cry, ‘O, the 
depths !’” 

During the months of June and July, Mr. Story
