LIFE AT HOME. 339 


usually left home; but in the autumn he was always at 
Rosneath in these later years; and day by day and 
week after week the many friends who could make the 
Manse a point in their holiday tours went and came. 
Often within the circle of one day the roll of visitors 
would count a dozen or more, of all ranks, from the 
peer to the peasant—of all sorts and conditions, the 
old companion, the needy suppliant, the hungry tourist, 
the perplexed foreigner, and all—though prudence would 
have often suggested less talking and fatigue—must 
needs be received and entertained with the most ex- 
pansive hospitality and good-will. One day a broken- 
down adventurer whom he had known in better days, is 
there, getting the last words of advice and the last 
instalment of the money that had been collected for 
him, and the letters of recommendation which are to 
secure him a free passage in a steamer for the East, 
where he is to try his fortune yet once more: another, 
there is a party of friends from England whom he 
must conduct at least part of the way up the steep 
glebe, in spite of quickened pulses, to show them his 
favourite view-point ; or again with one or two of those 
who were most at one with him in every thought and 
feeling, he saunters by the Loch and under the plane 
trees, in friendly genial talk about men and books, 
and Church and State, and the now long and varied 
experiences of his pastoral life. 

The picture of him as he moved among us in these 
calm autumns and winters, with step still light, form 
erect, and eye beaming, must be carried vividly even 
yet in manyamemory. It spoke too plainly of the 
pure spirit and the loving heart to be soon forgotten. 
I have found it tenderly and gratefully recalled when I 
little thought it was remembered, and cherished as “a 

z 2 


840 LIFE AT HOME. 


joy for ever,” by those whose hearts his love and 
sympathy had bound to his own. One instance comes 
before me now. When in Rome, four winters ago, I 
discovered that the chaplain of the Prussian Embassy 
was the Rev. Charles Heintz, a German clergyman, who, 
when in Scotland some time before, had been a visitor 
at Rosneath. I found him out in the Palazzo Caffarelli, 
hard by the Capitol, and when I told him my name, 
was received with even more than German heartiness 
of welcome. After a little while he said to me with a 
tear in his kind eye, “ Ah, when you told me your 
name I thought I should have wept, for I remembered 
my dear Mr. Story, who was to me most like a father 
of all I saw in Scotland, and when he bade me 
farewell did give me a German kiss.” 

The same bright and genial humanity which so 
touched the German’s heart made him enter with a 
warm and ready sympathy and delight into all the 
interests and amusements of the younger circle of 
friends who often gathered at the Manse. He smiled 
with most benignant approval on every expedition or 
picnic on which the party started in the sunny fore- 
noons; and when the day was done, no one listened 
with such relish as he to the anecdotes of its adven- 
tures. Rigidity and austerity which would interfere 
with the natural gaiety of youth he could not under- 
stand, so that his presence always seemed a sanction 
instead of a restraint; and the evening games, the 
music, the dance, or the charade, went on none the less 
merrily because he was by. The “sour and fanatical 
spirit, the aversion to innocent gaiety, the disposition 
to limit the enjoyments of others,” which a recent very 
positive historian maintains are, among other unlovely 
traits, distinctive of the Scottish clergy, will hardly be 


HIS SCOTTISH .THOUGHT AND FEELING. 3841 


accepted as their universal characteristics by those 
who can look back to one whose whole nature and 
bearing offered the stupid charge an unqualified con- 
tradiction. And yet he exhibited to the last, much 
that to such a bold generalizer as the author of the 
“ History of Civilization in England,” would no doubt 
seem to indicate the extremity of superstition and 
fanaticism. For there was in him, blended curiously 
enough with the frankest liberality of sentiment and 
the readiest toleration of all new theologic speculation, 
the strictest adherence to distinctively Scottish modes 
of thought and feeling on points of religious discipline 
and order. 

While no one could hail with more intelligent delight 
the growth in the Church of a more liberal and 
enlightened spirit, or mark with more cordial interest 
the advance which religious thought was making both 
in England and Scotland, under the influence and 
impulse imparted by such writings as those of the 
Hares, of Maurice, and of F. W. Robertson, no one 
clung more steadfastly to the traditions of his Cal- 
vinistic fathers, as to Church discipline and ‘“ Sabbath 
observance,” and all the other forms in which the 
religious strictness of Scotland has expressed itself. 
But with him this was not a mere dogged attachment 
to a dull and lifeless set of modes of thought and 
forms of observance. The old serious religious life 
and feeling of Scotland were very dear to him, though 
to the popular theology under whose shadow they had 
existed he was remarkably indifferent. And this was 
so, no doubt, because all his early religious principles 
and sentiments had come to him through the channel 
of that stern Calvinistic training, which, harsh and 
forbidding as it appears to those who know only its 


e 


342 INFLUENCE OF EARLY CALVINISTIC TRAINING. 


outer aspect, yet has its own moral beauty for those 
who have learned its somewhat austere lessons within 
the sacred threshold of their early home. 

What Newman says of the influence on the Pro- 
testant of his Saxon Bible, may be said, though in a 
less degree, of the influence of his strict Scottish 
training on one who has been brought up within a 
Scottish Presbyterian household. However far, in 
after times, he may travel beyond the utmost goal to 
which those who guarded his youthful years could 
reach, the memory, not scornful or ungrateful, of 
the early influence can never utterly fade away. 
The thought of the calm old home, of the peaceful 
order, of the morning and evening worship, of the 
quiet “‘Sabbath” and its simple service, of the Bible 
he was taught to reverence,—even of the catechism he 
was enjoined to believe, comes back to him like the 
sound of a voice that is still,” or the flush of the last 
sunset light on hills he never more may see. And 
apart from the abiding force of this cherished associa- 
tion, a man who had been obliged to meet and subdue 
so much ill-conduct and disorder as had encountered 
Mr. Story on the outset of his career at Rosneath, 
had an additional reason for reverencing the rules and 
traditions of his fathers. He had found that the stern- 
ness of Calvinistic discipline was needed for the work 
he had to do, and that none gentler could adequately 
influence and restrain those, over whom he was 
empowered to exercise it. ‘Thus both the associations 
of his youth and the experience of his manhood com- 
bined to strengthen his loyalty to that system, which 
now is so frequently and unreasonably denounced as, 
in the main, an ugly symbol of priestly tyranny and 
popular superstition. 


MR. MAURICE. 348 


In him, as in most, if not all pious Scotsmen of 
former days, the reverence for the Church’s discipline 
in all its stringency was so deeply rooted, that if it 
were lost, a real part of their religion was lost with it. 

“The Scottish Church, both on himself and those 

With whom from childhood he grew up, had held 

The strong hand of her purity ;” 
and so to him her moral strictness seemed the neces- 
sary expression of her spiritual life, and all excursions 
of speculation only safe and right when accompanied 
by a devout respect for her times and seasons, rules 
and observances ; and thus one saw in him at once the 
utmost liberalism and impatience of defined limit in 
the region of thought, and the straitest scrutiny of any 
deflection from the established discipline in the region 
of action and observance. | 

I have spoken, a page or two before, of the pleasure 
with which he hailed the writings of such men as the 
Hares, Maurice and Robertson. The sermons of the 
latter were special favourites, and he would generally 
have one or two of them read to him of a Sunday 
evening, often expressing his regret that so many good 
people should deny themselves the profit he found in 
them, merely because they could not say ‘Amen’ to 
everything they contained. He frequently quoted 
apropos of this, and of the suspiciousness of unen- 
lightened dogmatism generally, the saying of Richard 
Baxter, ‘‘ When will men be as anxious to ascertain 
wherein they agree, as they have for so long been to 
discover wherein they differ !” 

With many of the views expressed in the later 
works of Mr. Maurice he could not sympathize. He 
discovered therein an indistinctness, and a tendency to 
deal arbitrarily with Scripture so as to make it square 


344 INCREASED POPULATION OF ROSNEATH. 


with certain theories or convictions, which he could 
only account for by concluding, that Mr. Maurice’s 
opinions were not altogether definite and perfected im 
his own mind. What he thoroughly admired and 
assented to was Mr. Maurice's unequivocal teaching of 
the universal fatherhood and love of God ;—His love 
to all, revealed to all, in Christ the Saviour and Head 
of all. This truth for which he had been himself a 
witness, he rejoiced to see proclaimed clearly and 
boldly by one who must be admitted, even by those 
who differ from him most, to be a bright star among 
the many brilliant lights of the English Church. 


ArT the very time that his strength was beginning to 
fail him, the circumstances of his parish were demanding 
increased exertions. 

The Duke of Argyll opened up for “feus”’ the beautiful 
banks of Loch Long, and a rapid increase of population 
was the immediate consequence. Acre after acre was 
“feued,” and villa after villa rose along the winding 
shore. Where but a year or two before, the narrow 
parish road had rambled along under lonely banks of 
birch and hazel and rowan, with quiet fields of grain 
or pasture on the upward slopes that stretched back to 
the dun moor, and with bright glimpses up the broad 
river to Dunbarton Rock, and down beyond the Cloch 
and past the low hills of Bute to the blue crests of 
Arran, now all the old-world silence and Arcadian 
simplicity of the place were gone. The sound of life’s 
voice and bustle no longer came only in sleepy mur- 
murs across the water from under the smoke of 
Greenock, but drummed and echoed all around. The 
quiet road changed to a vulgar highway, the copses 
fell to give place to stone wall and iron rail, the soft 


NEW CHURCH ON LOCH-LONGSIDE. 345 


green fields were split and parcelled out into a hun- 
dred orderly lawns and gardens encircling “ marine resi- 
dences” of every species of architecture from the earliest 
Egyptian to the latest Glaswegian. 

It was, to those who knew the still Lochside before, 
a desecration, but one quite necessary and inevitable 
when the want of the many competes with the sentiment 
of the few. So the Kilcreggan and Cove shores were 
feued—a population gathered there, for whose pastoral 
superintendence Mr. Story felt himself totally unable: 
his Church was too far off to admit of regular attendance 
there, on the part of these new comers; there seemed 
only one remedy for the dreaded evil of a Churchless, 
priestless community—he would build a Church and 
provide a minister. He accordingly obtained a site 
from the Duke of Argyll, and proceeded to collect 
subscriptions for the building. At first he rather 
solicited subscriptions from a sense of duty than from 
any other motive, but as he went on he warmed to his 
work and entered into it heart and soul. He came 
even, I believe, to have a certain pleasure in being 
refused, since it afforded him the opportunity of bring- 
ing all his artillery of argument and solicitation to 
bear on the refuser, and opposing to each of his reasons 
why he should not give, a stronger reason why he should. 
“Have you met Mr. Story?” asked one gentleman 
of another on the Glasgow streets. ‘“ No.” “Then if 
you meet him, beware of him; for he’s begging, and 
you'll need to give.”* “ How shall I resist the appeal ?” 
wrote another, to whom he had applied byletter. ‘Have 
I the desire to resist the advocate—and that advocate

Story, the beloved?” And thus with most he prevailed, 
and boasted that his little sanctuary on Loch-Longside 
had drawn to itself the goodwill and charity of mem- 
bers of almost all the multifarious sects of Protestan- 
tism, from “ Holy Catholic Apostolic” angels down 
to Baptists and Independents. His advocacy of his 
cause by letter, was as usual greatly impeded by the 
ilegibility of his writing, and thus the point of his 
adroit periods was lost to not a few. “I received 
your letter some time ago,” writes Lord Shaftesbury, 
to take one instance, “ but I could not read either the 
date or the signature, or in fact make out anything. I 
now gather from a study of the context whence and 
what it is; and am delighted to find a reminiscence of. 
my old friend at Rosneath.” Notwithstanding this 
drawback, however, his appeals were eminently suc- 
cessful; without assistance from the Church’s Home 
Mission, and chiefly by his own efforts, the required 
sum was raised, and the foundation-stone of the place 
of worship laid on the 3lst July, 1852. It was 
completed at a cost of more than 1100l., and now 
stands on the hill side, overlooking the Loch—an 
enduring memorial of its founder’s thoughtful care 
and self-sacrificing energy—self-sacrificing in no small 
measure—for beyond doubt the subsequent increase of 
his malady was traceable to the undue exertions he 
made on behalf of this chapel.*

Public House in the Parish ? Are you yourself a total 
abstainer from alcoholics, and smoking and snuffing ? 
Will the new minister be secured to be such ?” 

To this Mr. Story replied, “I use neither snuff nor 
tobacco, and my physicians have prohibited for nearly 
two years the use of stimulants; but I am not an 
abstainer on principle, nor do I think, even if I were, 
that I could secure the minister of my chapel of ease 
being a total abstainer. I have secured along a line of 
about six or seven miles, I believe, of a feued shore, 
the exclusion of all sale of spirits, which you must 
allow implies an immense change upon a district which, 
at the commencement of my incumbency, swarmed 
with illicit distillers. I cannot say that there is no 
whisky sold in the parish. Not many years ago there 
were five licensed whisky-houses: there is now only 
one, at the Ferry, which is kept in the best order, and 
on the whole of the Sabbath is shut. Ours is, as you 
may know, one of the Royal Ferries on the old road 
through the Highlands, which the proprietors are bound 
to keep up. So long, I would beg to state, as physi- 
cians do not unanimously exclude stimulants from the 
medicine chest, and religious teachers have not proved 
their use to be in itself sinful, and so long as weary 
and way-worn travellers may need restoration in such 
places of rest, it would be difficult to found the abso-

the face of the wise man’s counsel, ‘ Give strong drink

unto him that is ready to perish, and wine unto those

“Having put to me certain questions, I felt it needful 
thus fully to answer them, not without hope that you 
might thereby see more clearly how to judge of my 
case. Perhaps it may be interesting to you to know 
that, notwithstanding the religious divisions that exist 
among us, I have found a singular unanimity of sym- 
pathy with me in accomplishing my object. I returned 
yesterday from Glasgow, after a most successful appeal 
to the benevolent, and have on my list of contributors 
members of ten different denominations of Christians— 
a beautiful specimen of the practical result of Evan- 
gelical union. The first on my list is an office-bearer 
of the ‘ Holy Catholic Apostolic’ Church ; the last a 
Baptist; the intermediate space between these points 
being occupied with those varieties wherewith the 
dappled garment Scotch Christianity wears 1s woven. 
It surely would not have been well had they declined 
to assist in supplying the religious wants of Loch- 
Longside, until a pledge had been given to adopt into 
my religious code their respective peculiarities. Would 
it not have implied that, much as they loved truth, 
they loved Plato more? Or had they met in partibus 
infidelium, from their lips no ear would have heard the 
voice of the Gospel, until the distinctive peculiarity of 
one or other of them had been stereotyped as the 
Christianity of them all. I cannot help yielding to 
the temptation of thinking that the questions you put 
to me, do indicate a tendency which I have observed 
in devoted philanthropists, to claim a too unreserved 
subserviency to their own views of the public good, 
as if by no other means it could be in any degree 
promoted. They often thus cause their good to be 


NEW PARISH CHURCH. 349 


evil-spoken of, for then charity even the most vehement 
and large-hearted, embracing all men, seems to dwell in 
one of those narrow circles in which selfish sectarianism 
tries to confine the interchange of their sympathies.” 

While his Church extension scheme was thus pros- 
pering on Loch-Longside, a new Church for his own use 
was also rising by the Gairloch. The barn-like edifice 
which the Vandalism of a former factor and minister 
had erected out of the ruins of an old cruciform Church 
dedicated to the Blessed Virgin, had been pronounced 
unfit for further use, and the heritors had adopted a 
plan for a new parish Church, in the early English 
style, prepared by Mr. Cousin, of Edinburgh. 

The building was finished in autumn, 1853; and on 
Mr. Story’s return from Ireland, where he had spent 
the summer, he looked forward to beginning to officiate 
within the new walls. One of the first things he did 
was to take his Psalm-book and go down to try his 
voi¢e in it, asking his friend Mrs. Hill to form the 
audience. He spoke easily, and was distinctly heard ; 
but it was destined that the voice which had so long pro- 
claimed the glad tidings in the old Church should not 
again lead the devotions of the congregation in the new 
one. Some additional symptoms of ill health induced 
him to go to Edinburgh to have again a consultation 
with the doctors. Their verdict was decisive : his only 
hope of prolonged life lay in cessation from all fatiguing 
duty—he must give over preaching altogether. 


“How dull it is to pause—to make an end— 
To rust unburnished—not to shine in use.” 


He felt it keenly, and still hoped that, by careful 
modulation of voice, and avoidance of excitement, he 
might be able to preach, at least occasionally, in his 
own small Church ; but the doctors were peremptory— 


350 OPENING OF THE NEW CHURCH. 


it was not to be. When he returned from Edinburgh, 
there was a sadness in his eye, although he tried to 
talk lightly and hopefully about his health, which 
told what a struggle it cost him to be resigned and 
cheerful under this new calamity. 

It was in many ways a trying time to him. He 
was harassed by the necessity of having his pulpit 
supplied, while, as yet, he had no regular assistant ; 
he saw around him much that he fain would do, and 
yet he durst not do it; the mere act, too, of trans- 
ferring the services from the old Church to the new, 
was not to be done without many sad and thrilling 
memories being quickened into vivid life, and many 
old associations broken. | 

Most of those to whom he had ministered when he 
first occupied that old ungainly pulpit were sleeping 
now under the shadow of the churchyard trees. The 
friends who had welcomed him at first were gone. 
“ Mr. Robert’ had long been dead; many of his family 
were away across the Atlantic; “ Miss Betsy,” the last 
of his sisters, the high-souled old Highland gentle- 
woman, had died two winters before ; in weird coinci- 
dence, one of the old trees of the Clachan avenue 
falling on the same night. Campbell, who had preached 
there in the time of their early earnestness, could now 
no longer speak within the walls of any Scottish Church. 
Chalmers, who had introduced him there; Irving, who 
had often thundered forth his message, both were gone; 
and the old Kirk was the one visible link between his 
present and their past. 

He had looked forward to having the new Church 
opened by Mr. Caird on the 11th of September. On the 
previous Sunday, however, his old friend, Dr. Muir, of 
S. James’s, Glasgow, had agreed to do duty for him; 


OPENING OF THE NEW CHURCH. $51 


and on coming down on the Saturday night, the Doctor 
was highly indignant to learn that he was to preach 
in the soon-to-be-abandoned edifice. Right or wrong, 
he must get into the new Church. He was told it 
was littered with lime and shavings, and quite unpre- 
pared for public worship—no matter, he would get in. 
Sunday morning found him as determined as he had 
been on Saturday night to preach in the new Church, or 
not at all. In much perplexity, Mr. Story sallied out, 
closely followed by the Doctor, to see what could be done. 
Mr. Dodds, the session clerk, was consulted. ‘“ Quite 
impossible,” was his decision. ‘The Church isn’t clean, 
and we can’t clean it on Sabbath morning.” ‘ There’s 
no more harm in cleaning the Church on Sabbath 
morning than there is in blowing your nose,” retorted 
the worthy Doctor, suiting the action to the word. 
At this juncture, John, the beadle, made his appear- 
ance. ‘‘ Ask the beadle, and see what he says.” The 
beadle, who was much inclined to regard the new Kirk 
with contempt and aversion, as a new-fangled inven- 
tion, was peremptory and positive in his negative. 
“The thing was clean oot o’ the question.” “Can 
you speak Gaelic?” said the Doctor, suddenly turning 
to John, and addressing him in the language of the 
hills, The effect was magical—the spirit of oppo- 
sition was subdued—the Doctor’s point gained, and 
the Church all ready by twelve o'clock. ‘ That 
auld man—I never heerd the like o’ him,” soli- 
loquized John, afterwards half-repenting of his com- 
pliance, “ he wad rule even the minister himsel’.” Dr. 
Muir preached in his own quaint, earnest, impressive 
way; and, in his prayers, implored that the Divine 
blessing might descend and rest on this “neat and 
commodious edifice ;” and when the service was done, 


352 ASSISTAS TS. 


it was a relief to feel that the formal transfer was 
fairly effected, and that the little absurdity and agita- 
tion with which the preparation for it was accompanied, 
had perhaps served to divert the mind from the sadder 
thoughts connected with this change. 

Now that Mr. Story was unable to condact public 
worship, it became, of course, necessary he should have 
an assistant, and his congregation kindly undertook to 
aid him in providing the requisite salary. He would 
gladly have secured the services of the young friend, 
Mr. Campbell (now the minister of Eastwood), who 
had supplied his place during his midsummer tour in 
1852; but Mr. Campbell was by this time engaged in 
organizing the congregation that met in the new Church 
at Craigrownie. After a good deal of inquiry, he was 
fortunate in finding an able and efficient “curate” in the 
Rev. David Rose, now minister of Ferry Port-on-Craig. 

With Mr. Rose and those who succeeded him, 
especially with our friend the Rev. W. L. Macfarlan, 
now minister of Tongland, his relations were most 
cordial and friendly. He expected from his assistants 
no small amount of parish work and no paltry standard 
of pulpit services, and he generally got what he ex. 
pected, so that to have served satisfactorily under him 
was at once a good discipline and a high recommenda- 
tion. “ His example,” wrote one who had done this, 
after Mr. Story’s death, “and the counsels which he 
kindly gave me, have not, I trust, been without a salu- 
tary influence on my life, and it is only now, when God 
has been pleased to take him home, that I fully realize 
the privilege I enjoyed in being so long associated with 
him.” ‘Ican never,’ wrote another at the same time, 
“forget his unwearied interest and heart’s desire for 
the success of his Master’s work. I only trust I may

possess his spirit and thorough devotedness wherever 
my lot may be cast. One other point, the more I knew 
him, became the more impressed upon me,—his gene- 
rous sympathy with his assistant; in him it was as 
pure, as (I grieve to say from the testimony of all I 
have known in similar positions) it was rare.” 

His own warm interest in the welfare of these 
younger fellow-labourers is illustrated in the following 
letter written to one upon his ordination to a parochial 
charge :— 


“My DEAR , The events of yesterday were 
not likely to induce repose during the night watches, 
and after many a searching of heart, I have been con. 
strained to leave my pillow, and for a little to hold 
converse with you, now the minister of so vast a popu- 
lation, from among whom I do pray the Eternal Spirit 
will prepare many souls to meet with you in the great 
day of Christ’s manifestation, in whom your Master and 
Brother shall see with complacency the result of the 
travail of his soul. You know I love you, and you may 
well judge with what deep interest I witnessed the pro- 
ceedings of yesterday; prayerfully did I listen to the 
counsels addressed to you, trusting that in so far as they 
expressed the mind of God, you would not fail to follow 
them. There seemed to me one thing lacking, a dis- 
tinct description of the great message, which first and 
midst and last is the essential element and principle 
fitted to give power and life to your ministrations 
among the people, viz., the message addressed to them 
all without distinction or exception, that God was in 
Christ reconciling them to Himself, not imputing to 
them their trespasses.

- mournful conviction, that the preaching of justification 
by faith had lost its power, and that the mighty. influ- 
ence which in Luther’s days had moved and impreg- 
nated the heart of Christendom seemed now quite 
unknown,—was indeed a phenomenon of a remote tra- 
dition. For many years I have seen and felt that this 
may be greatly attributed to the fact, that although 
the form of his words has been retained, the substance 
of Luther’s teaching has been withdrawn from what is 
called ‘ Evangelical Preaching.’ There is an expres- 
sion of Luther’s somewhere which will explain what I 
mean and justify my conviction. He speaks of men 
now, of all men living under the great and ample 
canopy of God's forgiving love. That very thought, 
taking possession of any soul, is fitted to convert it 
into a heaven of peace and serenity, of confidence and 
joy. O! my dear young friend, what are the cold 
prelections on the nature of faith and justification by 
faith compared with what such words teach of man’s 
condition now, and what he is called to believe con- 
cerning God, if it be as the old German supposes! 
Am [I living under such a canopy? Do I believe 
this? What remains then for me, but in believing 
it to have joy unspeakable and full of glory in the 
very thought of such a mighty wonder of the faithful 
Creator’s redeeming love ? 

“ The light that irradiates that ample canopy is the 
glory beaming from the face of Jesus, the Son in whom 
God dwells. In that light I see all things new. Does 
my seeing them make them new? As well might any 
of your people on the coast say that they created the 
mountains and the waters which spread their magni- 
ficent beauty and grandeur under their eyes, of a 
summer morning. O! my dear young minister, allow

your Paul the aged to express his thankfulness at the 
very thought that you have been led to fix for your 
first Sunday’s sermon upon a passage from the Word 
of eternal life, which affords so happy an oppor- 
tunity of applying the axe to the root of so fatal a 
delusion, which has so neutralized the truth which 
Luther was raised up to emblazon before the eyes of 
Christendom. You have from these words a distinct 
message—as he in his day—to declare to the people of 
——, that they live under the canopy of God's forgiving 
love, that on the Cross and now on the throne God’ 
was and is in Christ reconciled to them, not imputing

excuse for their not now returning to that chief good— 
that source of all blessedness, that fountain of living 
waters. Change the mode of the Spirit’s setting 
forth this great truth: represent God as not imputing 
their sins to those only who believe—O! what clouds 
and darkness would overcast that glorious canopy! 
Away from the canopy, shrinking from its contempla- 
tion, would the eye of the awakened sinner be turned 
towards himself to see whether he believed, for not until 
he discovered that he did so, would he dare to look 
upon that surpassing glory of divine compassion and 
love. How can the doctrine of justification by faith 
transform a dead soul, or delight a miserable, or 
purify a corrupt soul, if it is supposed to be a believing 
in one’s own faith? The ordinary teaching makes a 
man’s own state the object of his faith, and if confidence 
towards God be inspired by what he feels or knows 
about himself, he is his own justifier, and not God—he 
worships himself, not God—he thinks he has reconciled 
God, and not that God has reconciled him, Like Paul 
the aged therefore in the days of old, as he himself

for Christ subject himself to the possibility of

the kinsman of that Babe: even before the words were 
uttered, ‘It is finished,’ the reconciliation was de- 
clared. In the Incarnation itself, between you and 
Jehovah, all barrier is removed. Believe it and have 
rest. But I must close in haste. To God our Father 
committing you. Kver, R. 8.” 


The medical prohibition which forbade his attempt- 
ing to officiate in Church, did not extend to the dis- 
charge of such duties as visiting the sick, or any 
lighter offices requiring but little exertion of voice or 
strength. He even stretched a point on special occa- 


3043 PAROCHIAL WORK STILL PERMITTED. 


sicna, such as the time of aiministering the Sacrament, 
and ventured to say a few words inthe Chureh. On the 
Snnday or the Fast Day previous to the Communion, 
he alwaya went up to the pulpit before the close of the 
service and read a short address to the people. The 
last time he did this was in April, 1959, and with a 
presentiment, perhaps, that he should never address 
his flock again, he said at the close what he had 
never said before—‘ Farewell.” He never failed, too, 
to examine the candidates for admission to the Lord's 
Table personally, and, except on very rare occasions, 
to teach in the Sunday School, which met for an 
hour before service began. To the last he took a 
special interest in the well-being of the “ lambs of the 
flock ;” knew them all by name, and had a kind and 
fatherly way with them that made him always the 
children’s friend. He used to tell of his one day, when 
approaching a cottage, hearing the woman of the 
house holding his advent in terrorem over a refractory 
urchin—* There’s the minister, my man; will ye no 
catch it noo?” and of his indignant reproof, “How 
dare you venture to make me a bugbear to your child ?” 
It was certainly a venture which his voice and look 
would always be sufficient to defeat. 

As he was forbidden to walk far or to ascend hills, 
and as he often wished to do both, the ladies of his 
congregation thoughtfully provided him with the 
means of gratifying his wishes without transgressing 
doctors’ orders. They presented him with a purse of 
sovercigns, requesting him to expend itin the purchase 
of a pony and phaeton. ‘Mrs. W. Robertson,” he 
says, in writing of it to Mr. S. Ker, “ was the organ of 
the good sisterhood, and you would have been glad to 
peruse her kind letter.” Accordingly when he went 


TRANSACTIONS IN HORSE FLESH. ‘359 


to Ireland to spend the summer of 1850, he ransacked 
Dublin for an appropriate “ Shandrydan,” as he called 
it. One was at last found and bought, and then, under 
the auspices of Mr. Bellingham, began a search for 
a pony to correspond. This quest was also crowned 
with success at the fair of Dunboyne, held within a few 
miles of Ravensdale (Mr. Bellingham’s place in the 
county Kildare). A good part of a day was spent in 
inspecting and criticizing the various steeds that were 
pressed on our acceptance by their eloquent and ragged 
proprietors, who, with their long frieze coats streaming 
in the wind, flew up and down with the ponies to let us 
see their paces. At last a beautiful little chestnut was 
chosen, and, after some haggling and a great many 
jokes, bought for 7/7. 10s. , 

It turned out rather an unfortunate purchase ; the 
pony was too light for the phaeton, or rather the 
phaeton too heavy for the pony, so after a time it was 
sold. Another and robuster animal came in its place ; 
but it had not been with us very long before it exhibited 
eccentricities which rendered Mrs. Story extremely 
anxious for its dismissal. It would permit no noise in 
the phaeton; and, on one occasion, when Mr. Story 
and his daughter were driving, and he had taken the 
liberty of putting up his umbrella, the brute first ran 
off, and then lashed out behind so viciously, that the 
occupants of the vehicle had to jump out to escape the 
battery. On a subsequent drive, some slight noise 
being made in the front seat, the splashboard was 
instantly kicked to pieces, and it was at once decided 
that a beast of so touchy a disposition was unfitted 
to remain longer in the minister’s service. Such 
was the end of his last speculations in horse flesh. 
The advance of civilization by and bye introduced 


360 VISITS TO IRELAND. 


hackney carriages to Rosneath ; and, latterly, when he 
wanted to drive, he used to get one of them. 

Almost the only places which he visited in these 
later years were, Edinburgh, occasionally; Carluke, 
generally at Christmas, when he always liked to be 
with his invaluable friends there; Polloc, once or twice, 
to spend a day or two with Sir John and Lady Matilda 
Maxwell; Partick, the residence of Mr. J. M‘L. 
Campbell; the house, in Glasgow or on the coast, of 
his kind friend Mr. Allan Gilmour; Bridge of Earn, 
where he passed some weeks in 1854 and 1856, and 
whence he visited also Mr. Caird at Erroll, and Mr. 
Erskine at Linlathen ; and chiefly Dublin and Ravens- 
dale, where he spent the summers of 1850, 1852, 1853, 
1855. f 

To Ireland he was drawn in the summers when he 
sought change and rest, more strongly than to any 
other place. He found there always entire change of 
scene and of society, complete rest and relaxation, and 
abundance of ever-genial Irish welcome, Irish kindness, 
and Irish wit. His head-quarters were almost always 
Ravensdale, near Maynooth, the residence of his friend 
Mr. Bellingham; whose sister, “ Fanny Bellingham,” 
afterwards Mrs. D’Arcy of Clifden had, on his first 
visit to Ireland years before, inspired him with her 
own generous enthusiasm of love for her countrymen, 
and of longing for their deliverance from the thrall of 
priestcraft and Popery. Dublin, too, had its strong 
attractions when it contained the Goolds, the Arch- 
deacon and his family, who were bound to his heart 
by many a memory, and by an undecaying affection 
since early romantic days in Rosneath, long, long 
ago; and Booterstown, with old Mr. Nixon, the incum- 
bent, and Mr. Hopes, at his beautiful villa in Merrion 


sane 


RA VENSDALE. 361 


Avenue; but Ravensdale was his Irish home, to which 
he always looked forward with pleasant anticipations, 
and which he never left but with regret. There, as 
everywhere, if was his delight to go about, doing good 
as he had opportunity; giving the boys some useful 
lessons; consulting with Mr. Bellingham about the 
management or improvement of his land; ,studymg 
the workings of the Romish faith, or no faith, in the 
peasantry around ; saying a good word, giving a good 
advice, when occasion offered. On the Sunday fore- 
noons, we used to drive through the Carton grounds to 
the httle Church of Maynooth, with its barrel organ 
and scanty worshippers; and, in the evenings, Mr. 
Bellingham gathered a congregation of his Protestant 
neighbours in the large drawing-room, when Mr. Story 
would conduct the service and expound the Scriptures. 
These Sabbatic evenings did not pass without leaving 
behind them an enduring witness. “ We both feel,” 
wrote one who often came to listen, “ our God has 
sent us a message by you, for you have answered many 
fears and doubts which had been gaining possession of 
my mind, and I shall, I trust, be enabled to hold fast 
what I have heard during the last three weeks, which 
truly have been to us a time of refreshing from the 
Lord.” * 

With his sympathetic nature, and ready power of 
interesting himself in everything or anything within 
his reach, he discovered various schools, national and 
other, in the neighbourhood, the inspecting of which

362 IRISH SCHOOLS. 


he greatly enjoyed. Though a stanch upholder of 
the Scottish parochial schools, he cordially approved 
of the Irish national system, and especially on the 
ground that it offered an educational meeting-point for 
Catholic and Protestant alike. 

A story which he picked up on one occasion when, 
through the kindness of the Rector of Maynooth, he 
was asked to join a deputation of clergymen on a tour 
of school inspection, he often used to repeat. It was 
told to him by one whom he was not singular in 
regarding as a fine specimen of the Irish Church—the 
Rev. Edward Thackeray, of Dundalk. Mr. Thackeray 
had one day been present at an examination, when, 
after the ninth commandment was repeated, the ques- 
tion was asked, ‘‘ And what is the meaning of bearing 
false witness ?”’ The boy interrogated paused for a 
reply, but a little girl below him looked so eager and 
intelligent, that turning to her the questioner said, 
“Well, my little girl, what do you understand by false 
witness-bearing?” “ Please, sir, it’s when nobody does 
nothing at all, and somebody goes and tells everybody 
all about it,” was the exhaustive reply. 

Besides visiting schools and Maynooth College, 
whose dirty and disorderly aspect surprised him, and 
where the vice-president, who conducted him through 
the buildings, pointed out a gallery in the Great Hall 
which was intended to serve for an orchestra in the 
event of a Royal visit, he had some pleasant excur- 
sions through the rich green plains that stretched away 
round Ravensdale. 

There was one long drive of forty miles—by Sum- 
mer Hill and Laracor, where he looked into “ Stella’s” 
well—to Trim, with its fine old ruins, beside the pleasant 
winding of the “ bright and tranquil” Boyne. An- 


THE HILL OF TARA. 868 


other, to the hill of Tara, an excursion often recalled. 
The view from the great terraced mound, where an 
upright stone marks the grave of the hero of the battle 
of Tara, where O'Connell, a more recent hero, held 
one of his monster meetings; in the immediate fore- 
ground the old Church, surrounded by weird-looking 
pines; Skreen Castle, somewhat farther off; beyond, 
for miles and miles, a softly undulating plain bounded 
on south, east, and west only by the horizon, 
and on the north by the violet mountains of Morne, is 
of itself a thing to be remembered; but what dwelt 
most in his memory was the crowd of impetuous beg- 
gars who beset him as he walked down from the hill. 
They were all women, and all, according to their own 
averment, widows. ‘This fact was positively contra- 
dicted by a bystander, who affirmed none of them were 
such. Unable to decide the question of widowhood, 
and yet unwilling to go away without giving them 
some charity, he hit upon a plan which he thought 
would insure his alms falling into the hands, if not of 
the bereaved, at least of the orthodox. He announced 
he would give a certain sum to each who could repeat 
the Apostles’ Creed. In an instant he was enclosed in 
a ring of women, each with her native volubility quick- 
ened by her hope of gain, rattling over the words of the 
Belief. It was a queer scene, he standing in the centre 
of the crowd, insisting on order and deliberateness in 
the repetition, and looking almostas intent as the quast 
relicts themselves; the mob of white caps and eager 
faces pressing round him; a man or two looking lazily 
on, and his own party edging off down hill, and inclined 
to get out of the public eye as soon as possible. When 
the ordeal was over, he reflected with much satisfac- 
tion that all the beggars knew the Creed, although 


364 LAST VISITS. 


they did introduce into it the name of the Virgin 
Mary. 

After 1855 he did not revisit Ireland. The slow 
increase of his malady—so slow as from month to 
month to be hardly perceptible—gradually narrowed 
the range of his wanderings, and after that year he 
was never out of Scotland. 

In 1856 he paid his last visit to Bridge of Earn; 1n 
1857 he was almost constantly at home; in 1858 he 
was in Edinburgh for the last time, and while there, 
with his kindest relatives in Picardy Place and the 
. Royal Terrace, was alarmingly ill. The attack, how- 
ever, was subdued, and he returned home, somewhat 
shattered by it, and never to be so far from his own 
hearth again. It was at this time while in Edinburgh, 
and far from well, that the photograph by Ivan Szabo 
was taken, which upon the whole is the best likeness 
we have of him. 


CHAPTER II. 
THE CLOSE. 


As I approach the closing scene, darkened with the 
shadow of Death, I look back wistfully, and see how 
imperfect has been this record. I feel that those who 
did not know him of whom it speaks will gather from 
it but a feeble idea of his character—but a dim picture 
of the man. 

And now it seems as if the curtain must needs fall 
ere all the meaning of the drama is fully told. Sad is 
it too to feel that to him my words have done no justice ; 
that he was far more than all that my human language 
trying to limn the divine idea of the man can succeed 
in portraying. The relation in which I stand to him, 
while it has enabled me to know much that another 
could not have known, has also forbidden my saying 
many things that another might have said : 


‘“ But there is more than I can see, 
And what I see I leave unsaid ; 
Nor speak it, knowing Death has made 
His darkness beautiful with thee.” 


Towards the close there is not much to tell except 
what is still too sacred to his recent memory to be 
told. As the inevitable day drew near, I was far away, 
and I could only gather from the reports of others, and 
from his own letters, how bravely and beautifully he 


368 LETTER TO REV. J. ANDERSON. 


sion of the truth. At all events, it is not to be spoken 
lightly about; and until you have sufficiently ample 
opportunities of judging, express yourself cautiously 
and reservedly about it. At Kilsyth, for example, 
there was a certain amount of reality—greatly mingled, 
I would say adulterated, with excitements which were 
mistaken for religious emotions.” 

The only letter written during the last months of 
his life to another than myself which I have, 1s ad- 
dressed to the Rev. John Anderson, of the “ Free ” 
Church, Helensburgh, who, disregarding the sectarian 
howl of bigotry and prejudice, had crossed to Rosneath 
in Mr. Story’s ‘absence and preached to his people. 
It is dated— 


“ Carluke, July 27th, 1859. 


“My DEAR OLD FRIEND,—Many thanks to you for 
what you have been doing in Rosneath in my absence, 
and for which you merit the grateful acknowledg- 
ment of all whose Christianity is not identical with 
schism, who know and who act upon the conviction 
that nothing else is Christianity but the mind of 
Christ. The paragraph in the ‘Daily Bulletin,’ 
which has been so kindly sent me, deepens the 
satisfaction with which I learned from my friends 
at Rosneath, how the spirit of our Master had 
moved you, having time at your disposal, to cross and 
declare to all denominations the words of truth on 
Sabbath evening. I have no doubt, from the impres- 
sion produced by the services, that evening will be held 
by many in thankful remembrance, while many who 
were not there, and are of kindred spirit with yourself, 
will rejoice that the energy and warmth of your zeal 
for the interests of true Christianity, led you so boldly 


LAST READINGS, 369 


and chivalrously to sap the foundations of those 
earthen partitions which schism has reared to check 
or prevent altogether ‘the interchange of the divine 
offices of brotherly love. You have indeed sown good 
seed upon the waters, the abundant fruitage of which 
will gladden many an eye, while -your name will 
awaken only the more pleasing emotions, as the heart 
of the Church sympathizes in greater tenderness and 
strength of brotherly love with the heart of her risen 
Lord. Hoping soon to see you, and with our kindest 
regards, 
‘Believe me ever, dear Mr. A., 
* Your affectionate old Friend, 
‘ROBERT SToRY.” 


This letter is written in a feeble hand, feebler than 
when he wrote to me, probably because he was less 
guarded in concealing his weakness. He had gone up 
to Carluke, hoping the change of air might reinvigorate 
him, but it was of no avail. From the time of his 
return thence, gradually flesh and heart fainted and 
failed: at times he was a little stronger, but the amend- 
ment was only for a time, and always followed by a 
relapse into greater weakness. Still the mind was 
clear and active, and the wide circle of his interests in 
no way circumscribed. His labouring breath did not 
allow him to speak much, and latterly he was too 
languid to read; but before he sank so low, two of the 
last books he had taken a pleasure in having with him, 
beside his Bible, were his Shakspeare and Dante. The 
last volume he read through by himself was an expo- 
sitory work on S. John by the Rey. D. T. K. Drum- 
mond; and the last read aloud to him was Motley’s 
“ History of the Rise of the Dutch Republic.” For 


370 FRIENDS WITH HIM TOWARDS THE CLOSE. 


some time in 1858 and 1859, he had occupied himself 
with the Memoir of Alan Ker, which is quoted in an 
earlier part of this book, but as the summer of the 
latter year wore on, he had to give it up; he could not 
undergo the labour of arranging his materials or 
recording his thoughts, and so the work was never 
completed. While yet he was able to converse and 
enjoy their presence, some of the friends who had 
been most to him in days gone by came to have some 
last words :— Robert Paisley, Stewart Ker, Archdeacon 
Goold, still to him the “Fred Goold” of other days, 
and various times the friends most intimate and 
trusted, Dr. Wylie and Mr. Campbell. As they 
watched the gradual approach of “the Shadow feared 
of man,” it seemed almost robbed of its terror, and 
grief to be half lost in the forethought of his joy, so 
serene was his peace, so calm his faith, so cheerful his 
endurance. His mortal frame indeed was passing 
through the Dark Valley, but the star of his spirit 
was shining more and more unto the perfect day. Mr. 
Ker brought him a hymn of Paul Gerhardt’s, in some 
lines of which he found great delight :— 


“ For me there is no sentence—for me Death has no sting, 
Because the Lord who loves me shall shield me with his wing. 
Above my soul’s dark waters His Spirit hovers still, 

He guards me from all sorrows, from terror and from ill ; 

In me He works, and blesses the lifeseed He has sown, 

From Him I learn the ‘ Abba,’ that prayer of faith alone ; 

And if in lonely places a fearful child I shrink, 

He prays the prayers within me I cannot ask or think, 

The deep unspoken language, known only to that love 

Who fathoms the heart’s mystery from the throne of light above ; 
His Spirit to my spirit sweet words of comfort saith, 

How God the weak one strengthens who leans on Him in faith ; 

How He hath built a city of love and light and song 

Where the eye at last beholdeth what the heart had loved so long.’ 


His warm sympathy in the interests of others, his 


LAST SCENES. OTL 


deep enjoyment of the beauty of the world around him, 
lived with him to the last. 

All the friends and parishioners who came to inquire 
for him he liked to see, and he welcomed them still 
with his old gracious and winning manner. On each, 
when leaving him, he bestowed, often almost inaudibly, 
his parting blessing, and some received it thus, who 
had been separated from his ministry for many years. 

One who was always specially welcome had one day 
come in at a time when he was too weak to speak, and was 
just going to take some nauseous medicine. He raised 
the glass, and smiling and bowing to her over it, as if 
in old fashion drinking to her health, swallowed his dose. 

The same friend told me of another evening when 
she was with him. ft was early autumn; the candles 
were not yet lit, and the window was unclosed. He 
sat near it, looking out to the Loch. All around was 
deep peace and stillness. Beyond the Loch, which 
was calm as the sea of glass, the pale blue ridge of 
the hills lay glimmering beneath the moon, that walked 
in her brightness through the cloudless sky. A 
broad track of silver lay athwart the water, with here 
and there a spectre-like boat stealing across it noise- 
lessly. Close to the house the dark dense plane-tree 
threw its deep shadow on the lawn. It was the sacred 
hour when the toil and turbulence of life are stilled, 
and the cool and lucid Heavens seem to be nearest to 
the wearied Earth. He looked at the tranquil moonlit 
scene long and lovingly, though with languid eyes, 
then waving his hand toward the shining Loch sighed 
rather than said, ‘“‘ Beautiful! how beautiful!” ‘Many 
a time, and in many a varying mood, had he gazed on 
these hills and waters under the sailing moon, during 


the four-and-forty years now drawing to a close.

372 LAST WORDS. 


Many memories were doubtless with him then, 
perhaps too a fore-splendour of the time when he 
should know all the meaning of the words “ The sun 
shall be no more thy light by day; neither for bright- 
ness shall the moon give light unto thee; but the 
Lord shall be unto thee an everlasting light, and thy 
God thy glory. Thy sun shall no more go down; 
neither shall thy moon withdraw itself, for the Lord 
shall be thine Everlasting Light, and the days of thy 
mourning shall be ended.” 

Meantime the sad tidings that were coming to me 
across the Atlantic urged me to return home. I could | 
not leave my post in Montreal so soon as had been 
hoped, but by the end of November I was free. The 
last letter I had from him was written in broken and 
trembling lines, yet strong and vivid with the old depth 
and fulness of love and of interest in all my concerns. 
He had spoken to me when mentioning the Duke of 
Argyll’s kind intentions regarding the living of Ros- 
neath, of my occupying his place and making good the 
“ defects” of his ministry ; and I had replied, as became 
any one who knew what that devoted ministry had 
been. In reference to this, he says, in his last letter, 
‘“‘I was sorry you attached such a meaning to what I 
said respecting the character of my own ministry and 
“defects.” I had not so much in my mind what might 
be done by any young and energetic successor, as my 
own unprofitableness upon the retrospect of forty years. 
The parish was certainly in a very sad condition when 
I came ; I did not, however, take a sufficiently high posi- 
tion; take your stand as a minister of Christ.” Thus, 
like the great Apostle, counting not himself to have 
apprehended, he pressed toward the mark for the prize. 

I sailed for Liverpool on the 26th of November, 


DEATH. 873 


little thinking that that very day they were laying him 
in his narrow bed. When our voyage was over, on the 
6th December I found a letter awaiting me, which told 
me all was ended, “the hope, and the fear, and the 
sorrow;” and that where I had thought to meet a living 
and genial presence, I was to find only a memory and 
a grave. He had died on the afternoon of Tuesday, 
the 22nd November, after no little suffering uncom- 
plainingly borne,* and in the presence of the only two 
members of his family who were then at home. 

He had gradually sunk to great weakness, inability 
to move, and oppression in breathing, but no cloud for 
@ moment darkened the clearness of his intellect, or 
the serenity of his spirit. ‘‘ When seated in his chair 
it was hard to realize that one whose mind was so 
active, and whose interest in others so great, could be 
so rapidly hastening to the grave.” That morning he 
had an article on Alford’s Greek Testament in one of 
the periodicals read aloud to him, and listened with 
undisturbed attention ; the last thing he had in his 
hand was a Canadian newspaper which I had sent 
him, containing a picture of S. Andrew’s Church in 
Montreal. As he lay much oppressed in the darkening 
afternoon, suddenly he raised his hand as if to point 
upwards, then drew one or two long sighs, and his 
troubled breathing ceased. 

Thus he passed away, and the spirit, purified by 
its suffering and its love, entered within the veil of 
the “ worlds not realized.” 

On the Saturday following, he was carried from the 
blank house standing dreary in the dull November

weather. The service was conducted in the Church 
by his friend and neighbour, the Rev. J. Laurie Fogo 
of Row; and then the coffin was borne shoulder high 
to the grave. He was laid beside hts two boys, George 
and Alexander, in the quiet corner of the Churchyard 
which is overlooked by the window of his own chamber, 
and hard by the old Church within whose walls, now 
deserted and silent, he had spoken during thirty-seven 
years, and where many by his faithful words had been 
led out of darkness into marvellous light. The thorn- 
less rose of Sharon which he had planted above the 
grave of his first-born and first-lost sheds its blossoms 
over his own resting-place, and the shadow of his long- 
loved home deepens beside it when the sun 1s in the west. 

In the parish Church, on the wall of the chancel, a 
monument has been raised to his memory. It is from 
a design by Mr. W. Brodie, R.S.A., and contains a 
beautiful medallion in white marble, from the classic 
and faithful chisel of that distinguished sculptor. It 
bears this inscription :—

EXTRACTS FROM FRIENDS’ LETTERS. 8375 


Of the many letters that were received from those 
who mourned him, there are one or two from which J 
think I may extract a few words. They will be valued 
by many who have read these pages, and the writers, I 
believe, will not dislike that the expression of their 
sorrow and regard should be here associated with his 
memory. 

From Mr. Erskine of Linlathen. 


“Yet a little while! It is indeed a mysterious 
curtain which separates the dead from the living, when 
surely on both sides of it there is such a desire to 
communicate with each other ; but He who is the Lord 
both of the dead and the living doeth all things well, and 
is Himself the living medium through whom we may 
have true fellowship with the whole family in heaven 
and on earth. I can now perfectly reproduce to my- 
self my first impression of him, with his beautiful 
radiant countenance in that lovely country where he 
was standing a faithful witness for truth in opposition 
to many gainsayers. It is now thirty years ago, and 
many are gone who were with us then, and the witness 
which was then borne has been seed which the Lord 
has blest to many.” 

From one of the “Angels” of the “Holy, Catholic, Apostolic” 
Church. 

“‘His memory is very precious to me—he was a 
link in the chain of many blessings. I well remember 
the day at Row, when literally in a corner of the 
room he shut me up, standing before me, pleading 


dark days of nothingness,” need no acknowledgment in imperfect 

words ; still I like to record here our gratitude to that friend of him we

mn after the burial-day, and preached the funeral sermon, the Rev. Dr.

‘The much loved brother now removed—how much

APPENDIX I. 


LerrerR To DONALDSON. 


*¢ Ballindalloch, Nov. 13. 


“** My ever dear and excellent friend,—It is now a fortnight 
since the date of your last invaluable communication, and although 
I have something to offer by way of apology, perhaps it would 
be better to lay aside such ceremony altogether. As, however, 
there is something of romance in my excuse, and as there is in 
it a little adventure, you will forgive me for giving you it in 
detail before we talk of things more important. Last Sunday 
I set off from here in the morning to pay a second visit to one 
of our brethren of , which, from common civility, I could 
not defer any longer, as he is a great reader, has a good library, 
and gives me a complete liberty to the use of his books! I 
intended to have written this letter after I returned in the 
evening, which, if I had been able to accomplish, you would 
have heard from me about a week sooner than you now can 
possibly do according to the regulation of our post. It came, 
however, a deluge of rain, which continued till the Monday 
afternoon ; and I, for the first time since my arrival in the 
Highlands, fairly lost a day. For you may conceive that my 
interest in this country is not sufficiently great to give me a relish 
in the vulgar reports, and that unless something of sentiment 
or speculation enter into the conversation of a whole day, the 
frown is more natural than the smile to me, I stayed till after 
dinner, and the weather clearing a little, being anxioustoget home, 
I bade adieu, contrary to many solicitations of , the 
parson, who undoubtedly is a very honest fellow. He told me 
at our separation, that if I should perish, my blood would not 
be upon his head, and that his conscience was free, whatever 
should be my disaster. Off I started along a rugged mountain 
path, intersected by many a torrent, The first I passed com- 
pletely drenched me, although I went aside to avoid the depth

ecvated alawe the power of the winds, ard was of a deep
