APPENDIX IV. 


CHURCH AT ALBURY. MR. DRUMMOND'S ORDI- 
NATION. 


“y ABury Park, December 28, 1832. 


‘‘ At the commencement of the usual meeting for prayer, on 
Wednesday evening last (26th current), the Lord spoke a 
searching word through Mr. Caird, while Mr. Drummond was 
reading the 33rd Psalm; the substance of it was a warning 
against trifling with God and with sin. Before singing, Mr. D. 
warned the people against coming there without knowing why. 
He saw some who, he feared, were ignorant that the purpose 
of this meeting was, to pray unto the Lord for the outpouring 
of the Holy Ghost, and the revival of all His gifts to the whole 
Church everywhere throughout the world; and unless they 
could join sincerely in this the prayer would be a wavering 
prayer, which the Lord would not hear. After the Psalm Mr. 
D. said there were some amongst them who he knew were very 
anxious concerning the ordinance of the Lord’s Supper, and he 
requested the brethren who might be led to pray to make this 
a subject of special prayer: he then called upon the elder, Mr. 
Bayford, to read and pray. Mr. B. read Luke iv. During 
his prayer, while beseeching the Lord to make known His mind 
regarding ordinances, the Spirit broke forth in Mr. Drummond 
saying, ‘It is the Lord’s will; it is His will that the ordi- 
nances of the Lord’s supper be observed in this Church ; it is 
His will.’ Then the Spirit, through Mr. Caird, called on us 
to rejoice that the Lord had heafff the prayer of the destitute ; 
and said, ‘ Be ye prepared to keep the feast with desire—desire 
ye to do this in remembrance of Jesus ; the Lord will feed the 
hungry, but the rich he shall send empty away.’ Mr. Bay- 
ford concluded his prayer, and Mr. D. desired the Church to 
sing the 36th Paraphrase, which contains the words, last quoted 
by the Spirit. While preparing to sing it the Lord spoke 
through Mr. Taplin, a long time in a tongue, and then said 


110 MR. H. DRUMMOND'S ORDINATION. 


‘Tho Lord ordains, by you who have been called to be the 
Angel of this Church, to feed this people with the body and 
with the blood of the Lord : the meek ones shall be fed, but the 
proud consumed.’ Mr. D. then called on the Church for 
thanksgiving to the Lord for the mercy He had shown, but told 
them to remember we still required the counsel of the Lord in 
this matter, and added, ‘I may give you the bread and the 
wine, and you may press the bread with carnal teeth, and touch 
the wine with your lips, but this is not to have communion with 
the Lord,— it is the Spirit that quickeneth, the flesh profiteth 
nothing ;? and again he called to prayer. The Spirit imme- 
diately spoke through Mr. Caird, saying, ‘Let the Lord do his 
work ; let Him declare all His mind ; let H:s working alone be 
seen in the midst of you.’ The Spirit then, through Mr. Car- 
dale, began to open up the mystery of the body and blood, and 
the proper condition of those who receive it, and with much 
expression of grief, saying, ‘ Mourn ye, because the cisterns are 
broken, and there is no water, the Lord’s people are a grief to 
Him : they area burden to Him; He is pressed, He is pressed 
under them ; there are some among you that believe not ; Jesus 
is angry ; He is angry.’ The Spirit proceeded in prayer, crying 
unto the Lord, ‘O come down unto thy people; O for a living 
cry to ascend unto our God,’ concluding with a comprehensive 
prayer for the whole Church, and for the officers of this Church 
in particular, specifying every one—Pastor, Evangelist, Elder, 
and Prophet. At the close of this prayer Mr. D. again said, 
‘I wish some of the brethren would pray, for I do not clearly 
discern the mind of the Lord in this matter.’ The Spirit, in 
Mr. Cardale, said, ‘Ye do well,’ and continued to plead and 
exhort: it was a mingled utterance of both. When the Spirit 
broke forth in Mr. Taplin, with great power in a tongue, and 
thus said, ‘The Lord commandeth you, you who have been 
called to be an Apostle, to lay hands on the Angel of this 
Church, and ordain him to rule and feed this people—to feed 
them with the body and blood of the Lord; be faithful, be 
faithful, and Jesus will honour you.’ After a short pause Mr. 
Cardale advanced to Mr. Drummond, who was kneeling at the 
desk, and after a prayer, mighty im the Spirit, beginning at 
Creation and going through the manifestations of God unto the 
person, sufferings, and glory of the Lord Jesus; with strong 
a-~'~~ for faith, and that the hand of the Lord alone might be 
both his hands on Mr. Drummond’s head, the latter 
reply absorbed in communion with God ; the Spirit 
lale saying, ‘Be thou filled with the Holy Ghost, 


MR. H. DRUMMOND'S ORDINATION. 411 


and with the Spirit of wisdom and of knowledge, and of a sound 
mind ; be thou of a quick understanding in the fear of the 
Lord : feed and rule this people; be thou faithful unto death 
and thou shalt receive the crown of life, which the Lord hath 
prepared for thee, and for all who love the Lord Jesus.’ Then, 
turning to Mr. Bayford, he blessed him, and spake words of 
encouragement, exhorting him to feed this people, and in so 
doing he himself should be fed. Immediately after this the 
Spirit burst forth in Mr. Drummond in a song, ‘ Glory to God 
in the highest,’ when the Spirit in Mr. Caird took up the same 
strain in the name of the Church, singing the ‘ Doxology,’ in 
which the congregation joined. Then fullowed a remarkable 
prayer in the Spirit by Mr. Drummond, thanking and praising 
the Father for all His goodness and mercy; for His gentle dealing 
with us, not remembering our unworthiness, but putting away 
our sins ; beseeching the Lord with great urgency not to let 
the vessels be looked to or regarded in themselves lest he should 
be provoked to dash them in pieces. He seemed to have great 
entrance into the bosom of Jesus, enjoying, apparently, much 
light shown in the rapidity of the utterance. Mr. Cardale then, 
in a commanding manner, spoke in the Spirit, saying, ‘It is 
the Lord’s will that thou proceed to feed this people with the 
body and blood of the Lord. See thou to it: live for them ; 
watch for them by night and by day, and see that thou give a 
good account of the souls committed to thy trust ; the Lord will 
bless thee in it. The Lord hath ordained thee the pastor of 
this people : He hath cast off the Pastors who have forsaken 
and fouled the waters ; but now He hath appointed them one 
who will give them pure water.’ Then a word to Mr. Bayford, 
charging him to be faithful in teaching this people, and promis- 
ing him ordination in the Lord’s time. Mr. Drummond was 
then looking for a Psalm to sing when the Spirit, through Mr. 
Cardale, ‘Sing 24th Psalm, and let all your hearts be lifted up 
to the Lord.’ The Spirit in Mr. Taplin then, after singing for a 
while in a tongue, declared that Jesus had been in the midst 
of us, that his arms were open to receive us, that we should 
flee into them. Mr. Drummond then again, in prayer, blessed 
the Lord ; praying for the souls of the Pastors, although their 
offices were being set aside ; and, after a few words of exhorta- 
tion to his people, showing that the utterances of the “ ° 
were not decrees but addressed unto faith, and that 

ing to the faith would the blessing be imparted and rx 
concluded by giving his blessing to the congregation.” 


APPENDIX V. 


HINTS FOR THE CONSIDERATION OF HIS GRACE THE 
DUKE OF ARGYLL, RESPECTING THE ERECTING 
OF PROTESTING CHURCHES ON HIS ESTATES. 


22nd July, 1843. 


‘¢ WHATEVER views may be held by landlords respecting a par- 
ticular form of Church government and polity, it is clear that 
without the violation of any Scriptural rule, they may grant sites 
for Churches for the accommodation of those who hold views 
differing from their own ; but while extending to others in this 
manner the full amount of the toleration which they them- 
selves enjoy, they cannot be held bound so to co-operate 
with them, as to injure the system to which they themselves 
adhere, and which they regard as essential to the good order 
and well-being of society. While therefore granting accommo- 
dation to those who differ from them, they must carefully avoid 
offering any inducement that could tend to increase their 
number, which would, of course, just be to counteract those 
views which they themselves desire to uphold. 

*¢ On this account, those who regard the Established Church 
as indispensable to the well-being of the community ; who look 
to it as the only safeguard under Providence of the throne, 
and the judgment seat ; the only barrier against the fiercest 
revolution that ever visited the land, will carefully weigh, 
(while desiring to respect the right of private judgment,) how 
they ought to comply with the wishes of those whose avowed 
object is its destruction. 

‘¢ Where landlords feel themselves called upon to comply with 
such wishes, it is needful to remember that the erection of 
a place of worship for a new sect necessarily becomes the 
nucleus, as it is the symbol, of irritation and divisive feeling 
in any neighbourhood, and that therefore no site should be 


SITES FOR DISSENTING PLACES OF WORSHIP, 413 


granted, but in such a locality as might in this respect be pro- 
ductive of the least possible evil. 

‘¢ In reference to the present times, it may be added, that no 
site should be fixed upon which would excite peculiar or unne- 
cessary annoyance to those who remain in the Established 
Church, and tend to increase and perpetuate angry and evil 
feelings between the two parties ; and that it should have peculiar 
reference to the proportion of tenants and heads of families 
whose continuance in the same locality is more certain, and not 
to that of servants or young people, whose residence may 
change, or who can more conveniently travel to a distance. It 
is needful, also, that the moral effect likely to be produced by 
the residence of the clergyman should be considered ; which of 
course must be greater by placing one in a district where none 
has hitherto been, than by placing two who adhere to different 
systems, where, from their contiguity, that difference would be 
brought continually before the eye of the people. 

‘‘In granting such sites the landholders are bound to consider 
whether or not they are perpetuating a secession which would 
only be temporary ; and whether the circumstances of those 
desiring it render it possible for them for any long period to 
maintain that which they desire to erect.” 


APPENDIX VI. 


EXTRACT FROM A LETTER TO LADY MATILDA 
MAXWELL, 


‘¢ Tue original counsel, ‘It is not for you to know the times 
and seasons,’ as well as that expostulatory query to the 
Apostle ‘What is that to thee?’ was designed for all they 
represented in all coming times. The attitude and condition 
of the Church in reference to her final deliverance from evil, 
alter not nor vary with the vicissitudes of times and seasons, 
but must be always the same in all circumstances—she must be 
for ever waiting for her absent Lord. Howsoever distant in the 
language or measurement of time His advent may appear, affects 
not the designed influence of that great event upon her feelings. 
Its very greatness seems to annihilate and fill up the space 
between the present and the future. The Church rises to the 
power of entering into the meaning of the truth that to her 
great Head one day is as a thousand years, and a thousand years 
asone day. ... . 

** The effect of that great event which took away the sin of 
the world, has not been diminished in its moral power because 
it occurred eighteen centuries ago. Had it occurred only yes- 
terday, would its power have been greater? Or does the 
Church ever dream, that because it is a long past action, she 
has any apology for the decay of gratitude or love? So, 
rightly exercised regarding the Second Advent, she feels that 
were it to occur to-morrow, the same state of mind would 
undergo no essential change which, however distant, it was 
designed to produce. When she thinks of the Cross on 
Calvary, it is not when it was erected, but the Crucifixion itself 
that occupies her mind; and so, of the Lord’s return. The 
time is to her as nothing. She is absorbed in the believing 
contemplation of the glorious event itself. Living in the 


~~" Oe ene LL -—-— - a -T- ~ —_ -—- - - 


+ THE SECOND ADVENT. ‘415 


visions of the Almighty, she is not even tempted to say, ‘ Why 
doth my Lord delay His coming ? 

‘¢The repugnance to receive the predictions of the second 
advent, in a literal sense, I have been accustomed to trace to 
two or three causes, First—The original objection to a literal, 
and the theory of a Spiritual advent was traceable to the 
Gnostic phase of Christianity in the Apostolic times. The 
same Gnostic influence prevails, but more insidiously now. In- 
deed it were difficult to distinguish between those who would 
not admit that Jesus was literally crucified, and those of our 
time who recoil from the idea of His returning to the world 
again, as if that were a degradation of His person, and that 
therefore all descriptions of His so returning must be held as a 
mere Spiritual allegory. Second—-There is another cause, 
secret but powerful, in the state of mind which has ceased to 
use as descriptive of the Church’s state, the term waiting for 
her Spouse and Head. It is very manifest that the conscious- 
ness of guilt must recoil from the very thought of the visible 
advent of the Saviour in glory, and this sufficiently accounts 
for the rejection of the prophecy by the mass of un- 
believers. But there are others who reject it, not so 
much from this cause, but from their not being in the condition 
which enables them to look to His advent as a blessed hope. 
Such are professors of the faith and believers of the Gospel, 
but they have not realized its power. They have but vague 
ideas of what the Son of God has done; they cannot use the 
language of the Apostle, ‘I am crucified with Christ;’ they do 
not carry about His dying, and His life therefore is not in them. 
They cannot say, ‘ My beloved is mine and I am his, for He died 
for me ;’ and is it not manifest that without this knowledge they 
never can love His appearing? If their Christianity has never 
exceeded that form which the earlier Reformers used to call a 
‘doubtsome and general faith ; if, while He died for some, 
they do not know that He died for them ; if thus they do not 
know in whom they believe, how can they endure the idea of 
His speedyreturn? What leads the ungodly to disbelieve the 
reality of a judgment to come, leads professors of the faith to 
entertain the idea of a Spiritual Millennium, in exclusion of the 
reappearance of the King of Glory at its commencement ? Of 
course we must dismiss the idea of a fixed intermediate period 
between the utterance of the prophecies and the coming of the 
Lord. The attitude of waiting for our Lord as at hand, and 
the knowledge that an absolute period of, say 1000 years, would 
intervene before His approach, is utterly incongruous. We may 


416 THE SECOND ADVENT. 


be sure that had it been revealed to the Church that by a Divine 
decree 1000 years should elapse before the Saviour’s return,—in 
whatever way she might have been directed to exercise or de- 
mean herself,—such expressions as urge the necessity of watch- 
fulness would never have been employed; nor would the 
Saviour Himself, before His departure, have used such words 
as, ‘ Watch and pray, for ye know not the day nor the hour,’ 
&e. . 2. | . 
‘¢ Tt is indeed a miserable delusion—the idea of a progressive 
amelioration of society corresponding to the realization of our 
conceptions of millennial blessedness. Our tickets-of-leave, our 
parish schools, our educational legislation, all promising symp- 
toms of the approach of that glorious period! Alas, alas! the 
words of the faithful and true witness confound all such silly 
dreamings. ‘ Verily, when the Son of Man cometh shall he 
find faith on the earth ?’” 


APPENDIX VII. 


EXTRACTS FROM LETTERS TO A FRIEND IN 
ENGLAND. 


“Dec. 8, 1847. 

‘*T remember, many years ago, when, by a remarkable dis- 
pensation of Providence, I had a vivid experience of the aid 
which faith receives from the removal of the visible channels 
of God’s grace and tenderness. It was so ordained by Him, 
that on the communion Sabbath both Mrs. S. and myself were 
so ill that we were not only confined tothe Manse, but in sepa- 
rate apartments on that solemn day—a day of all others, as 
you may believe, a minister desires to be with ‘his people, and 
with those he loves, to eat the body and drink the blood of their 
Lord. The Manse is very near the Church, so that I heard 
from time to time the singing of the Psalms—even the sound 
of the voice of my friend in prayer, as well as in all other parts 
of the service, You may understand, from your longing to be 
with your sister—and longing the more because so near you— 
what I felt when on the one hand, severed from Mrs. 8., who 
was dangerously il]—and, on the other, from my people in the 
sanctuary. I was, indeed, deprived of human solace and 
sympathy—the channels through which we derive so many 
blessings—I was shut up into the presence of the Invisible. 
Nothing was interposed between Him and my faith. Nothing 
to distract or enfeeble. Spirit with spirit on that eventful day 
held converse ; and, even until now, I can trace the issues of 
that interchange of sympathy with which nothing outward, 
or earthly, or even of man could intermeddle. 

**’'You are thus at present shut up into that presence ; and 
in the blessed communion will you find new power of more 
intensely loving your dear sister, and more effectually praying 
for a happy issue to her present affliction. ‘I believe in the 


communion of saints’ are precious words. And at such a season 
KE 


418 EXTRACTS FROM CORRESPONDENCE. 


you doubtless feel that communion to be a great reality. And 
although you see not your sister, those partitions do not 
prevent communion of soul according to the rule—truly our 
fellowship is with the Father and the Son, and our fellowship 
is one with another, because the blood of Christ cleanseth from 
all sin. _In God both dwelling, you dwell in each other’s hearts 
—and receiving one spirit from the Father through the Son, 
you are taught by that Spirit, who knoweth the deep things of 
the will of God, what to pray for for each other, so as to 
receive what you ask.” 

‘‘The right condition of our soul is that which love longs 
‘for, —and the crucifying of the flesh,—the death of the Adamic 
life, of which doubtless bodily sufferings are emblems, is the 
object of all its desires and dealings with us, and however diffi- 
cult and painful to endure, the end thereof, being the formation 
within us of Christ in his perfect manhood, over which God 
rejoices. Whatsoever such love desires may, although grievous to 
the flesh, be hailed as a good and perfect gift, and preparatory 
to a joy unspeakable and full of glory. How often I think of 
your sufferings, but never separated from the end, which so 
manifestly God, who so loves, is accomplishing. I cannot 
grieve, while I cease not to pray that you may lose nothing of 
the good which all your trials have been working together to 
secure. You have been indeed in deaths oft, but in none has 
the sting been found, nor shall it be, prepared, as you are of 
God, to share the universal song, ‘Death is swallowed up of 
victory.’ ” 


** Feb. 28, 1850. 

‘‘T had gone to Edinburgh to consult the physicians there 
respecting my own health. I had fallen down once insensible 
on the road, and had nearly done so again, and therefore felt 
it right to consult the first medical authorities regarding the 
cause. They have decided that my heart is affected ; that I 
must live a life of perfect repose ; never preach from home, nor 
ascend an eminence, d&c., &c., but avoid all excitement, all 
labour that would fatigue ; in short, that the prolongation of 
life depends upon adhering to these and similar rules which 
they laid down for me. I feel very thankful that they have 
not as yet prohibited preaching to my own people. It is a 
solemn thing t6 know, rather unexpectedly, that I thus carry: 
about with me as it were a sentence of death, in a sense some- 
what more emphatic than the mere knowledge of our mortality 
pronounces, They consider my malady as beyond the reach of 


EXTRACTS FROM CORRESPONDENCE. 419 


medicine. What a deep sympathy I have with many of the 
contents of your letter, and how readily I turn from the con- 
fessed powerlessness of all human skill to that Great Phy- 
sician of whom you speak. It is possible that it may not be 
with me as the physicians think ; but I have no other desire 
than this, that, whether living or dying, God may be glorified 
by me and in me; and that without carefulness I may abide 
the appointment of His most gracious providence. How He 
does mingle mercy with judgment.” 


‘IT may mention to you the case of a dear old friend of mine 
who has lost his only daughter. By this post his letter arrived. 
We have not heard of each other for a dozen years, and on the day 
of his daughter’s death he wrote me a long letter. ‘ You will not 
wonder,’ says he, ‘that I choose this moment to write to you 
after so long and, perhaps to you, inexplicable silence, because 
you know how the heart resorts to the feelings and consolations 
of friendship when amid suffering and sorrow, as it likewise 
makes me apply with greater earnestness to the highest source 
of comfort.’ After describing all the endearing qualities of his 
daughter, he ends by expressing his belief that their earthly 
happiness would have been too great if she had been spared to 
them. God doeth all things well. 

‘‘It is rather interesting that the only time I ever heard 
him preach, nearly forty years ago, he made a quotation which 
I remember, though I do nothing else about the sermon, 
‘Sweet are the uses of adversity.’ 


‘<I remember once when by the pillow of a dear friend in 
great bodily agony, and which her physicians in attendance 
were confident must end-in death, she cried — * Terrible, 
indeed, this! Once I could not have borne—I must have 
died in bearing it; but now I feel as if coming to me 
through my blessed Head it has lost its peculiar anguish, 
and it brings strange power of endurance with it.’ And it 
was even so. She recovered; and to lead a strange life of 
almost all variety of pain and sorrow—but she had power given 
her from time to time to repeat the testimony. * 

The rumours of an American war alarmed our dear old friend 
a little, but these have died away, and the speedy close of our 
Indian war will have an excellent effect upon our Transatlantic 
kinsmen, so that by the blessing of God we may now 
anticipate without doubtfulness, a long tranquillity. But 
alas! in some other form than war of nation against nation 


420 EXTRACTS FROM CORRESPONDENCE. 


will antagonisms of human passion develop themselves. The 
sectarian spirit of religionists is very violent, although there is 
an outward aspect which is very imposing, as if all were 
breathing for union. Many godly and holy men undoubtedly 
are, but I fear exceedingly that other elements than pure 
Christian love do mingle in those promptings of that great 
coalition at present attracting attention. The tract of Mr. 
Raikes I have enjoyed much, and it breathes a beautiful 
spirit. Were that spirit to predominate we would soon see more 
than a mere outward and nominal union of professing Chris- 
tians. It is manifest there can be no real union but through 
union with Christ, and that can only be through belief in His 
love. No apparent union, therefore, ought to be attempted. 
but through the assumption of that principle. Each member, 
therefore, of that confederacy* ought to profess that the God- 
Man loved him and died for him—and so believing, he has 
received power to love and be united to his brethren. But 
alas, the voice may still be lifted up, ‘ Who hath believed our 
report?’ May I mention that a few days ago, when presiding 
at an ordination in the parish of Luss, I took those words 
for my subject of discourse, in which under the similitude of 
a proclamation to the rebellious subjects of some province in 
any of this world’s empires, I set forth the character of the 
Gospel, and the various reasons which led men to reject it ; 
having first shown that there can be no preparation for receiv- 
ing the Gospel—that no previous attainments are necessary— 
and that nothing can justify any—howsoever they may have 
sinned,—for rejecting it. I was thus enabled to prove that 
unless my audience were in the enjoyment of the peace and 
joy it brings, they must be acting like those rebels, who, not- 
withstanding the proclaimed pardon of their sovereign, still 
maintain their attitude of hostility to his person and govern- 
ment. Think over in your own mind what various reasons 
might sway with rebels against an earthly king—so as to pre- 
vent them availing themselves of his mercy—and you will see 
asin a glass, the working of the hearts of the rebellious in 
whom the Lord God desires to dwell. That day you were 
brought very vividly to my remembrance, for I had with me 
that beautiful case for my bands, which is to me so very pre- 
cious a memorial of your kind regards towards me. Often 
thus, at interesting services, will you be present to my mind’s 
eye, as well as when no outward thing is needful to remind 
me of Lewisham.” 


* The “ Evangelical Alliance.” 


EXTRACTS FROM CORRESPONDENCE. A421 


(To another friend, when her brother was il.) 

‘© Meanwhile cast your burden on Him who is a friend that 
sticketh closer than any brother ; who is indeed a brother, too, 
born to be with us in our adversities. ‘I will not leave you 
comfortless,’ are precious words for all bereaved and suffering 
members of His family. The first disciples felt their power and 
truth. The last will testify they are fresh and mighty as when 
first uttered. 


‘*Poor B.’s case is to me very affecting too—most deeply 
so. I can enter somewhat into her feeling. I remember well, 
as if it were but yesterday, in early life, just the year I went 
to college, what agony I endured when suddenly bereft of a 
brother whom I loved, and who loved me, We slept 
together,—he was my oldest brother, and therefore I was 
young in comparison ; but I remember the last morning we 
slept on the same pillow being awakened with his saying 
to me, ‘O! how I love you.’ He went to bathe in a loch 
near my father’s house. Some accidental circumstance pre- 
vented my going with him, else I doubt not we should have 
died together. The occupations of college could not prevent 
him being ever present to my mind, and he was night after night 
in my dreams. One day, in the South Bridge, I met a gen- 
tleman so like him, that I started, and a cold tremor,thrilled over 
my frame. I for a moment thought it was himself. A hand- 
‘some youth of imposing appearance—for my brother was noted 
as the most handsome of his age in the district; or, indeed, 
wherever he happened to be. I followed him. I never left 
him till I ascertained where he lived, and day after day I used 
to watch his movements, for he seemed to walk at a certain 
hour, and I kept always near to him that I might feast my 
eyes with a living resemblance to my brother, who was slumber- 
ing in the sepulchre of his fathers. One day I missed him, 
and never met him more. Who he was, or what became of 
him, I know not; but a strange blank in the streets was 
occasioned by his absence, And often afterwards in my 
dreams, I strangely thought that my brother had been alive 
again, and had a second time died.” 


‘¢ When I think of your protracted and agonizing sufferings, 
which I often do, I do earnestly pray that you may enter more 
and more into the depths of the blessed experience of your 
oneness with the once agonized Saviour. He ‘was made perfect 
through suffering.’ The statement is full of meaning,—espe- 


422 EXTRACTS FROM CORRESPONDENCE. 


cially reveals to us the great mystery of divine love and com- 
passion in this, that to secure to us the consolation of His 
sympathy, He perfected himself by suffering. Truly to sym- 
pathize, we ourselves must be sufferers. This none can doubt ; 
and we all feel that expressions, howsoever fervid, symbols too of 
true feeling from those who have known no changes, who have 
not been afflicted of God, do not convey to us the soothing 
virtue there is in what proceeds from the lips of those whose 
spirits have known anguish and sorrow. I remember well, 
many years ago, when there were some cases Of severe suffering 
among the people, when attempting to administer consolation, 
I was met different times with an apology for impatience or 
murmuring, in the form of such an observation as this—‘ But 
ah, you have never known pain or suffering yourself; we have 
heard you say you always enjoyed good health.’ When after- 
wards I was seized with a malady, occasioning intense suffering 
for many months, and this afterwards had arrested the atten- 
tion of all, I was prepared with an answer to my surviving 
patents : ‘ You said I had never suffered ; but you cannot say 
that now.’ ‘O sir,’ replied an old man, ‘we heard your 
sufferings were far greater than what I used to complain of ;’ 
and I remember well how much more he seemed to feel the 
expressions of my sympathy. I had been made perfect for that 
duty, so to speak. My qualifications as a sympathizer no more 
were incomplete. The more objective knowledge I had pre- 
viously, I now had acquired the subjective knowledge and 
experience that brought me truly in contact with the Spirit of 
the man. He felt I could be afflicted in his afflictions; having 
the same humanity—the same capacity of endurance—the 
words I uttered had a power, as they really had also a signifi- 
cancy they had not before. And is it not so, that our ever 
Blessed Father, knowing what our hearts needed in our suffer- 
ings and sorrow, thus perfected for us His Son, that we might 
not only believe that He himself knew all that concerned us, 
and as a Father pitied us; but that His Son, one with Him- 
self in our very nature, in this humanity we wear, suffered, 
that His sympathy might be felt by us in all its power— 
in its highest power of consolation ?” 


_ © The transubstantiation of the Papacy is the perversion of 
@ great truth, which it has hid by its carnality from the eye and 
heart of God’s suffering people. Everywhere, according to 
that fiction, may the perishable element of bread be converted 
into the true body of the glorified Redeemer ; but what is the 


EXTRACTS FROM CORRESPONDENCE, 423 


truth? Even that by the infinite presence and omniscience of. 
the God-Man, he is felt in the believer’s heart in its trials, 
and griefs, and anguish as humanly present—present as the 
very flesh and blood of the harassed and perplexed sufferer, 
and bearing with him his burdens ; and this is the zest of the 
consolation amid the severest endurance, that he deprived it of 
its sting, and the sufferer is led with thankfulness to testify, 
that ere what he feels has come to him, the Ever Blessed 
Jesus, the brother born for adversities, has extracted what no 
power of patience could have endured in tranquillity. 

‘¢% %* ‘When hanging on the Cross, He saw through the 
remotest depths of future time ; and He saw of the travail of 
his soul, and was satisfied for the knowledge of this, that he 
was then and there extracting the poison from all suffering, 
and all agony, that his people, until He should come again in his 
glory, would be appointed to endure in the place of their pil- 
grimage. Now, my most dear sister, whenever those sharp 
spasmodic pangs rack your feeble frame, may your experience 
be that there is no bitterness in them; that Jesus has taken 
away ; and he is in you effecting what he died for—the patient 
endurance of your common Father’s will ; and to whisper from 
your heart’s depths the mystery (although the ear only which, 
as one with you, he has given you, can hear it), as each suc- 
cessive agony drops, so to speak, from the cup His Father has 
mingled, ‘this is only a varied expression of His invariable 
Love” * * *” 


‘¢ She was, dear girl, much tried by her visit to ———-. I fear, 
however, that the direct effect designed by God, through that 
dispensation to her soul, was somewhat prevented by circum- 
stances having rather forced upon her attention our unhappy 
schism ; for with all the miseries of their physical condition, 
the schismatic spirit rages with extraordinary virulence in the 
Highlands and Islands. Her gentle spirit, however, has no 
sympathy with the fierceness of zeal, which in some districts 
characterizes both parties. Alas, alas! what wounds our 
Blessed Brother does receive from those who profess to honour 
Him. A most godly man from another parish, who for thirty 
or forty years has been distinguished for his eminent sanctity 
came day before yesterday to see Mrs. Story, and I was struck 
with an illustration he used to describe the spirit of the time. 
He is a man in lowly life, but one of those who so resembles 
the children of a king, that I am always inclined to think old 
is a prince in disguise. ‘O what would you think,’ said 


424 EXTRACTS FROM CORRESPONDENCE. 


he to me,’ if you saw a crowd of people around the body of a 
man, and one time trying to hold up his head, then seeming to 
fix a crown upon it, and all the while lashing and wounding, 
even sometimes fiercely kicking, His body.’ The figure was 
new to me at the moment ; and alas! when I thought of all I 
knew and had witnessed, there seemed great truth in his graphic 
images of these unhappy times. O were we indeed to honour 
the Head of all principality and power, our attitude ever were 
the bearing about his dying, that his life might be in us. No- 
thing in the wide world of professing Christendom can the 
eye of the Eternal rest upon with complacency, but what 
resembles his own most blessed Son,—His meek and lowly, His 
loving and holy mind. O! that we could ever remember this, 
alone or in society, in joy or in sorrow, within the gates of 
Zion or in the dwellings of Jacob. Day after day I am con- 
strained to mourn, and when I think that he is in my very being 
—my root—the very substance of my spiritual life, that all things 
that pertain to life and godliness are in Him, and that He has 
been given to me, that He is in me,—I am humbled to the 
dust, and constrained to cry, ‘O wretched man that I am, to 
return so little to Him who has given me so much.’ ” 


THE END.

‘In the present instance we have most appropriately one of the deepest thinkers of the present 
day making the Platonic Dialogues as intelligible in an English garb, to the English reader, as 
they are in the original to himself and the comparatively few scholars. . . . The Dialogues are 
rendered additionally intelligible, and, indeed, interesting to the English reader, by copious 
explanatory passages thrown in parenthetically here and there, and sufficiently distinguished 
from the translated portions by being unaccompanied by the marks of quotation which distin- 
guish the translation throughout. In addition to this, the translation itself merits high praise ; 
while by no means the least valuable portions of the volume are the ‘Remarks’ at the conclusion 
of each Dialogue.” —Gentleman’s Magazine. t 

‘‘So readable is the book that no young lady need be deterred from undertaking it ; and we are 
much mistaken, if there be not fair readers who will think, as Lady Jane Grey did, that hunting 
or other female sport is but a shadow compared with the pleasure there is to be found in Plato. 
. . . The main questions which the Greek master and his disciples discuss are not fit simply 


.or theses in Moral Philosophy schools; they are questions real and practical, which concern 


Englishmen in public and private life, or their sisters or wives who are busy in lowly or aristo- 
cratic households. Questions of right and wrong . . . of the virtues which children in National 
Schools ought to be taught, and the training which educes the best qualities of body as well as 
inind,”—A thenceum.
